My godmother lived in a stately house nestled in the quiet, old-fashioned town of Bretton. Her family had been rooted there for generations—so much so that they bore the name of their homeland: Bretton of Bretton. Whether this was mere chance or a legacy from some distinguished ancestor who once shaped the town’s destiny, I never knew. As a child, I visited Bretton about twice each year, and each time, I found myself drawn to its calm charm.

The house itself was a haven—spacious rooms with high ceilings, polished furniture arranged with care, wide windows that let in soft daylight, and a balcony overlooking a street that seemed frozen in time. On Sundays and holidays, the air hummed with stillness; the cobbles were clean, the shutters neat, and the world outside felt gently paused. I adored it all.

I was the only child among adults in the household, and though Mrs. Bretton was reserved by nature, she took quiet notice of me. A widow when I first met her, she had lost her husband—a physician—while still young and strikingly beautiful. Though no longer youthful when I knew her, she retained her elegance: tall, poised, with dark hair and a complexion glowing with health. Her eyes, black and bright, sparkled with warmth and wit. People often lamented that her son hadn’t inherited her coloring; his eyes were blue, sharp even as a boy, and his long hair shimmered golden in sunlight. But he carried her features, her strong teeth, her height, and most importantly, her unshakable health and steady spirit—qualities more valuable than wealth.

In the autumn of the year ——, I was staying at Bretton. My godmother had come personally to fetch me from the relatives where I lived permanently. I sensed something was amiss—though I couldn’t quite name it. There was an undercurrent of sorrow, a hush beneath the usual peace. And so, I welcomed the change of scenery, eager to leave behind the quiet unease that clung to my home.

Time passed smoothly under my godmother’s roof—not swiftly, but steadily, like a river flowing through a sunlit valley. My visits reminded me of Christian and Hopeful resting beside a gentle stream, where green trees lined the banks and lilies bloomed all year. There was no drama here, no sudden turns—just serenity. And I cherished it. I didn’t seek excitement; when it came, it felt almost intrusive.

One day, a letter arrived that clearly startled Mrs. Bretton. At first, I thought it might be from home and feared the worst. But nothing was said to me, and the shadow lifted quickly.

The next day, returning from a long walk, I entered my bedroom to find it transformed. Alongside my French bed in its shaded alcove stood a small crib draped in white. Beside my mahogany chest of drawers sat a delicate rosewood chest. I stopped, stared, and wondered.

“What could these things mean?” I murmured.

The answer came easily: another guest was expected.

At dinner, the truth was revealed. A little girl would soon join me—the daughter of a distant relation of the late Dr. Bretton. She had recently lost her mother, though Mrs. Bretton added that the loss wasn’t as great as it seemed. The woman—Mrs. Home, as she was called—had been lovely, yes, but flighty and careless. She’d neglected her child and worn down her husband. Their marriage had been incompatible, and they had parted ways peacefully, without legal turmoil.

Shortly after, Mrs. Home overexerted herself at a ball, caught a chill, developed a fever, and died quickly. Her husband—Mr. Home—was deeply sensitive and shattered by the news. He blamed himself, convinced his own harshness had hastened her death. Grief had weakened him, and doctors urged travel as a cure. In the meantime, Mrs. Bretton offered to care for the child.

“And I hope,” she added, “this little one won’t inherit her mother’s silliness. I wouldn’t want her to be a frivolous flirt like the woman who married Mr. Home.”

She spoke kindly, yet with firmness. “Mr. Home is sensible, in his way—though not very practical. He loves science, spends half his life in a lab conducting experiments—something his wife could never understand. Honestly,” she confessed, “I wouldn’t have liked it myself.”

When I asked more, she told me Dr. Bretton once said Mr. Home’s scientific mind came from a maternal uncle—a French scholar. His lineage was mixed: French and Scottish. He had relatives still living in France, several of whom bore noble titles and wrote *de* before their names.

That evening, at nine o’clock, a servant was sent to meet the coach carrying our new guest. Mrs. Bretton and I waited in the drawing-room. John Graham Bretton was away visiting a schoolfriend in the countryside.

She read the evening paper; I sewed. Rain pounded against the windows, wind howled outside.

“Poor child,” she whispered now and then. “What weather for her journey! I wish she were safe here.”

Just before ten, the bell rang. I rushed downstairs. In the hall lay a trunk and a few bandboxes. A nursemaid stood nearby, and Warren, the footman, held a shawled bundle in his arms.

“Is that the child?” I asked.

“Yes, miss.”

I reached out to peek, but the bundle was turned away.

“Put me down, please,” a small voice said as Warren opened the drawing-room door. Then, with quick hands, the child unpinned the shawl and shrugged it off.

The figure that emerged was tiny, yet perfectly formed—slight, straight, and graceful. She tried to fold the shawl, but it was too large for her small hands.

“Give it to Harriet, please,” she said. “She can put it away.”

Then she turned to Mrs. Bretton.

“Come here, little dear,” Mrs. Bretton said softly. “Let me see if you’re cold. Come warm yourself by the fire.”

The child stepped forward. Without her shawl, she looked like a doll—her neck delicate, her curls soft and fine. Seated on Mrs. Bretton’s lap, she seemed almost unreal.

Mrs. Bretton chafed her hands and feet, speaking gently. At first, the child watched with a solemn gaze, but then a smile broke through.

Mrs. Bretton, usually reserved—even with her beloved son—kissed her forehead. “What’s your name?”

“Missy.”

“But besides Missy?”

“Polly. Papa calls me Polly.”

“Will Polly be happy living with me?”

“Not always. Only till papa comes home. Papa is gone.”

She shook her head slowly.

“He will return—or send for me.”

“Will he, ma’am? Do you know he will?”

“I think so.”

“But Harriet thinks not. Not for a long while. He’s ill.”

Her eyes filled with tears. She pulled her hand from Mrs. Bretton’s and moved to step down.

“Please, I wish to go. I can sit on a stool.”

She was allowed to descend. Carrying a footstool, she placed it in the deepest corner of the room and sat quietly.

Mrs. Bretton, though authoritative in serious matters, often yielded in small things. She let the child have her way.

“Take no notice for now,” she whispered to me.

But I did notice. I saw her rest her elbow on her knee, her chin in her hand. Then, from her pocket—hidden within the ruffles of her dress—she drew a tiny handkerchief. And I heard her weep.

Not loud, not openly—but softly, secretly. A faint sniff, a quiet tremor. Other children cry without shame; this one hid her sorrow.

Mrs. Bretton didn’t hear. Good thing.

Soon, a voice from the corner: “May the bell be rung for Harriet?”

I rang. The nurse arrived.

“Harriet, I must be put to bed,” the child said. “You must ask where my bed is.”

Harriet nodded—she already had.

“Ask if you sleep with me, Harriet.”

“No, Missy,” she replied. “You’ll share this young lady’s room.”

The child didn’t move. But I saw her eyes turn toward me.

After a moment, she rose from her corner.

“I wish you good night, ma’am,” she said to Mrs. Bretton.

Then she passed me without a word.

“Good-night, Polly,” I said.

“No need,” she answered. “We sleep in the same room.” And she vanished.

I heard Harriet offer to carry her upstairs.

“No need,” she insisted. “No need, no need.” And her small steps climbed the stairs, slow and weary.

An hour later, when I went to bed, I found her still awake. She sat upright, propped by pillows, her hands folded neatly on the sheet. Her posture was unnervingly composed—more like a grown woman than a child.

I said nothing for a while. Just before turning out the light, I said, “You should lie down.”

“By and by.”

“But you’ll catch cold, Missy.”

She picked up a small garment from the chair beside her crib and wrapped it around her shoulders.

I left her alone.

In the darkness, I listened. She was crying again—quietly, carefully, as if afraid to make a sound.

At dawn, the sound of water trickling caught my ear. There she was—perched on a stool near the washstand, struggling to pour water from the ewer into the basin. It was heavy; she couldn’t lift it easily.

I watched her wash and dress—tiny, silent, determined. Clearly, she wasn’t used to doing this alone. Buttons, strings, hooks and eyes—all presented challenges. But she faced them with quiet persistence.

She folded her nightdress neatly, smoothed the sheets, then retreated into the corner, hidden by the white curtain.

I raised my head slightly to see what she was doing.

On her knees, her forehead resting on her hands, she was praying.

A knock at the door.

She jumped up.

“I’m dressed, Harriet,” she said. “I dressed myself, but I don’t feel neat. Make me neat!”

“Why did you dress yourself, Missy?”

“Hush! Speak low—for fear of waking *the girl*.”

(Meaning me.)

“I dressed myself to learn—before you leave me.”

“Do you want me to go?”

“When you’re cross, I’ve wanted you to go many times. But not now. Tie my sash straight. Please make my hair smooth.”

“It’s straight enough. What a particular little thing you are!”

“It must be tied again. Please.”

“There. Now, when I’m gone, you’ll have to ask that young lady to dress you.”

“On no account.”

“Why? She’s very nice. I hope you’ll behave well, Missy.”

“She shall dress me on no account.”

“Comical little thing.”

“You’re not combing straight through my hair. The line will be crooked.”

“Ay, you’re hard to please. Does that suit?”

“Pretty well. Where should I go now that I’m dressed?”

“I’ll take you to breakfast.”

“Come, then.”

They walked to the door.

She stopped.

“Oh! Harriet, I wish this was papa’s house! I don’t know these people.”

“Be a good child, Missy.”

“I am good—but I ache here,” she said, pressing her hand to her chest. “Papa! papa!” she moaned.

I stirred, sat up.

“Say good-morning to the young lady,” Harriet instructed.

She said, “Good-morning,” and followed her nurse out.

Harriet left the next day to visit friends nearby.

When I descended, I found Paulina—she called herself Polly, but her full name was Paulina Mary—seated at the breakfast table beside Mrs. Bretton. A mug of milk stood before her, a piece of bread in her hand. But she wasn’t eating.

“How we shall win this little one over,” Mrs. Bretton said to me, “I don’t know. She eats nothing. And by her look, she hasn’t slept.”

I said I believed kindness and time would help.

“If she takes a liking to someone here,” Mrs. Bretton replied, “she’ll settle. But until then… I don’t know.”

Days passed, and it became clear that the child in the house was not likely to take kindly to anyone around her. She wasn’t wilful or disobedient—she was too quiet for that—but she carried an air so heavy with melancholy that no one could remain unaffected by her presence. Her stillness was profound, a kind of sorrowful silence that seemed to drain the warmth from every room she entered. No grown soul exiled far from home had ever worn such visible longing on their face as this small girl bore upon hers. In her young features, I saw the shadow of deep homesickness, as if time had already aged her beyond her years.

I, Lucy Snowe, do not claim to be overly sensitive or given to flights of fancy, yet whenever I opened a door and found her sitting alone in some corner, head cradled in her tiny hand, I felt the space grow cold and still—as though the room were not merely empty but haunted. And then, on moonlit nights, when I woke and saw her figure, pale and ghostly in her nightdress, kneeling rigidly in bed, whispering prayers like a fervent saint or a child lost in devotion—I could not shake the feeling that something unsettling stirred beneath the surface. Her words, when they came at all, were barely audible: “Papa… my dear papa…” That refrain echoed through her silent hours, revealing a mind narrowed to a single thought, a heart fixed on one image. It was a monomania, I thought—a curse more painful than any outward rebellion, because it was invisible, internal, unrelenting.

What might have become of her, had this quiet torment gone unchecked, I cannot say. But fate intervened. One afternoon, Mrs. Bretton gently lifted Paulina from her usual spot in the corner and placed her on the window seat, instructing her to watch the passersby and count how many ladies walked down the street in five minutes. The child sat motionless, eyes half-lidded, hardly paying attention—until I noticed something strange in her gaze. A sudden shift took place within her eyes: the dullness vanished, replaced by flickering light; her brow smoothed, her expression brightened. A spark ignited in her face—something fierce and expectant.

“It _is_!” she whispered, and without another word, she was gone.

Like a dart, she shot from the room. How she managed to open the front door, I never knew—perhaps it was left slightly ajar, or perhaps Warren, obedient as always, had done her bidding without question. From the window, I watched her dash halfway down the street, her black frock fluttering behind her, her tiny braided apron flapping like wings. Just as I turned to tell Mrs. Bretton that the child had fled into madness and must be brought back immediately, I saw her caught mid-run—snatched up by a man who stepped forward from the crowd. He wrapped her in his cloak and began walking back toward the house, carrying her as though she were a treasure.

I expected him to hand her over to a servant and depart, but he did not. He came inside. After a brief pause downstairs, he ascended the stairs. His entrance confirmed he was known to Mrs. Bretton. She recognized him instantly—her greeting was warm, yet tinged with surprise, even alarm. Her manner suggested rebuke, and in response, he said simply, “I could not help it, madam. I found it impossible to leave the country without seeing her myself—to see how she settled.”

“But you may unsettle her,” she replied.

“I hope not,” he answered. Then, turning to the child now standing before him, he asked gently, “And how is papa’s little Polly?”

She leaned onto his knee, gazing up with wide eyes. “How is Polly’s papa?” she replied.

It was not a loud scene, nor dramatic in sound—but it was deeply emotional. There was no outburst, no tearful embrace, only a quiet intensity that pressed heavily on the observer. When passion surges openly, one often feels relief in mockery or disdain. But this was different—this was a tenderness held in check, a love so deep it refused to show itself except in silence. It weighed upon me like a burden.

Mr. Home was a stern-looking man—his face sharp, his forehead knotted, his cheekbones high and pronounced. His features spoke of Scotland, yet his eyes held emotion, even now, as they softened with the sight of his daughter. His voice, when he spoke, carried a northern lilt that matched his bearing. He was proud, yes, but also plain—homely in a way that made his dignity feel earned, not assumed. He placed his hand gently on her head.

“Kiss Polly,” she said.

He kissed her.

I longed for her to cry out, to break the stillness with some burst of feeling—anything to ease the tension. But she remained calm, absorbed, almost trance-like. She had what she wanted—everything she needed—and seemed content in that moment.

Though she bore none of his features, she was clearly of his blood. Her mind, shaped by his, mirrored his depth and restraint. Mr. Home possessed a quiet strength, a self-command that belied whatever turmoil stirred beneath. He looked down at her and said, “Polly, go to the hall. You’ll find my greatcoat on a chair. Put your hand in the pockets—you’ll find a handkerchief. Bring it to me.”

She obeyed without hesitation. She returned swiftly, holding the cloth in her small hands. He was speaking to Mrs. Bretton and did not notice her return. So she waited—then, with careful precision, she opened his fingers, slipped the handkerchief into them, and closed his hand slowly, one finger at a time. He did not look at her, nor acknowledge her touch. Yet later, he lifted her onto his knee, and she nestled close. They sat in silence, neither speaking nor looking at each other, yet both, I sensed, were at peace.

At tea, Paulina continued her quiet attentions. She directed Warren where to place the chairs. “Put papa’s chair here,” she instructed, “and mine near it, between papa and Mrs. Bretton. _I_ will hand his tea.” She sat down and beckoned him to join her. “Be near me, as if we were at home, papa.” As he passed his cup, she intercepted it, stirring the sugar herself, adding cream with solemn care. “I always did it for you at home, papa. Nobody could do it as well—not even you.”

Her efforts were absurd, really. The sugar tongs were too large for her hands, so she used both. The silver cream-ewer was too heavy; the bread-and-butter plates, the cup and saucer—all taxed her small strength. But she persisted, lifting, handing, serving, miraculously avoiding breaking anything. I found her behavior intrusive, almost meddlesome. Yet her father, blind to everything but his affection, welcomed her ministrations. “She is my comfort,” he murmured to Mrs. Bretton, and she smiled, understanding. For she had her own “comfort”—a larger, more distant one—currently absent, and thus could sympathize with his attachment.

That evening, the second “comfort” arrived. I knew the day had been set for his return. We sat by the fire after tea when Graham joined us—though he didn’t so much join as disrupt. His arrival caused a stir, and since he had fasted, refreshments were quickly prepared. He greeted Mr. Home warmly, old friends reuniting. At first, he paid no attention to the child.

After finishing his meal and answering his mother’s questions, he turned to the hearth. Opposite him sat Mr. Home, with Paulina beside him. To call her a *child* felt inadequate—she was more like a miniature adult, dressed in mourning black and white chemisette, perched on a high chair beside a toy work-box of polished wood. In her hands, she held a scrap of handkerchief, pretending to hem it. The needle, clumsy in her grip, pricked her skin again and again, leaving tiny red marks across the cambric. She winced once or twice when the point dug deeper than intended, but she kept working—silent, focused, utterly serious.

Graham, at sixteen, was handsome in a way that hinted at mischief. His hair was a soft auburn, wavy and full, his build supple, his smile easy and clever. He was spoiled, whimsical, and entirely unafraid of being bold. After watching her silently for a while, he turned to his mother.

“Mother,” he said, “I see a young lady present whom I have not yet been introduced to.”

“Mr. Home’s little girl, I suppose you mean,” she replied.

“Indeed, ma’am,” he corrected, “I consider your phrasing most uncivil. Miss Home, I should certainly have said—when referring to the gentlewoman I have in mind.”

“Now, Graham,” Mrs. Bretton warned, “I won’t have that child teased. Don’t imagine I’ll let you make sport of her.”

Undeterred, Graham continued. “Miss Home, might I have the honour of introducing myself? Since no one else seems inclined to do so—your slave, John Graham Bretton.”

She looked up. He rose and bowed with perfect seriousness. She put down her thimble and scissors, stepped carefully down from her perch, and curtsied with astonishing gravity. “How do you do?” she said.

“I am in fair health,” he replied, “only somewhat fatigued from my journey. I trust, ma'am, you are well?”

“Tor-rer-ably well,” she declared, with a hint of pride.

She attempted to climb back onto the high chair, but realized it would require effort—and in front of a stranger, she refused aid. So she chose a low stool instead. Graham drew his chair closer.

“I hope, ma'am,” he said, “that you find my mother’s house a convenient residence?”

“Not par-tic-er-er-ly,” she replied. “I want to go home.”

“A natural and admirable desire,” he agreed, “though I shall do my best to oppose it. I intend to extract from you some of that rare commodity called amusement—one which, I regret to say, my mother and Mistress Snowe fail to provide.”

“I shall have to go with papa soon,” she said. “I won’t stay long.”

“Yes, yes,” he said, smiling. “You’ll stay with me. I have a pony you can ride, and countless books with pictures.”

“Are _you_ going to live here now?”

“I am. Does that please you? Do you like me?”

“No.”

“Why?”

“You’re queer.”

“My face, ma'am?”

“Your face and everything about you. You have long red hair.”

“Auburn hair, if you please,” he corrected. “Mamma calls it auburn, or golden. All her friends agree. But even with my ‘long red hair’”—he waved his mane with a flourish—“I cannot possibly be queerer than you, your ladyship.”

“You call me queer?”

“Certainly.”

(A pause.)

“I think I shall go to bed.”

“A little thing like you should have been in bed hours ago,” he said. “But I suspect you stayed up waiting to see me?”

“No, indeed.”

“You certainly wished to enjoy my company. You knew I was coming home and waited to catch a glimpse of me.”

“I sat up for papa, not for you.”

“Very good, Miss Home. I shall be your favourite soon—preferred even to papa.”

She bid Mrs. Bretton and me goodnight. She hesitated for a moment, wondering whether Graham deserved the same courtesy—then, suddenly, he scooped her up in one hand and held her aloft above his head.

She saw herself reflected in the mirror above the fireplace—the suddenness, the boldness, the sheer disrespect of it all struck her.

“For shame, Mr. Graham!” she cried. “Put me down!”

Once back on her feet, she added, “I wonder what you’d think if I treated you the same way—lifting you with my hand” (she raised her small fist dramatically) “as Warren lifts the little cat.”

And with that, she turned and left.

Mr. Home stayed for two days. During his visit, he refused to go out, choosing instead to sit quietly by the fireplace all day long—sometimes silent, sometimes exchanging light conversation with Mrs. Bretton. Her talk was just right for a man in his fragile state: not too emotional, not too distant, but thoughtful and warm, with a gentle maternal tone that suited their age difference perfectly.

Paulina, meanwhile, was both joyful and quiet, busy yet watchful. Her father often lifted her onto his lap, where she would stay until she sensed—real or imagined—that he was growing restless. Then, with soft precision, she’d say, “Papa, put me down; I shall tire you with my weight.”

And so, like a tiny burden, she’d slide down to the rug, settling herself beside his feet on the carpet or a low stool. There, her white workbox and her red-spotted handkerchief came into play. This handkerchief was meant as a keepsake for “papa,” and it had to be finished before he left. Thus, the demands on her delicate needlework were great—she managed only about twenty stitches in half an hour.

The evening brought Graham home from school, returning to his mother’s care. His arrival added a spark of life to the household—a vitality heightened by the familiar drama that always unfolded between him and Paulina.

From the moment he arrived, Paulina had maintained a distant, almost proud air, a reaction to the slight she felt when he first appeared. When he spoke to her, her usual reply was, “I can’t attend to you; I have other things to think about.” When pressed to name them:

“Business.”

Graham tried to win her attention by opening his desk and displaying its treasures: seals, bright wax sticks, penknives, and a collection of engravings—some vividly colored—gathered over time. The temptation wasn’t entirely without effect: her eyes, though hidden behind her embroidery, would flicker toward the table, drawn by the scattered pictures. One etching slipped to the floor—a child playing with a Blenheim spaniel.

“Pretty little dog!” she exclaimed, delighted.

Graham said nothing. Soon, slipping from her corner, she crept closer to examine it. The dog’s large eyes, floppy ears, the child’s hat and feathers—everything was irresistible.

“Nice picture!” she declared.

“Well—you may have it,” said Graham.

She hesitated. The desire to own it burned strong, but accepting would mean surrendering her dignity. No. She placed it back and turned away.

“You won’t have it, then, Polly?”

“I would rather not, thank you.”

“Shall I tell you what I’ll do with it if you refuse?”

She half-turned, listening.

“Cut it into strips to light the taper.”

“No!”

“But I shall.”

“Please—don’t.”

Graham, undeterred by her plea, took scissors from his mother’s basket.

“Here goes!” he announced, making a show of menace. “Right through Fido’s head—and splitting little Harry’s nose.”

“No! _No!_ NO!”

“Then come to me. Come quickly, or it’s done.”

She paused, lingered—but stepped forward.

“Now, will you have it?” he asked, standing before her.

“Please.”

“But I shall want payment.”

“How much?”

“A kiss.”

“Give the picture first into my hand.”

As she said this, her expression shifted slightly—doubtful, perhaps untrustworthy. He handed it over. She fled, a debtor, darting to her father and taking refuge on his knee. Graham rose, feigning anger, and followed. She buried her face in Mr. Home’s waistcoat.

“Papa—papa—send him away!”

“I’ll not be sent away,” said Graham.

Still hiding her face, she held out her hand to keep him off.

“Then I shall kiss the hand,” he said. But at once, it curled into a tiny fist—and delivered payment in a small coin that was no kiss at all.

Graham, ever clever like his young rival, pretended to be thoroughly discomfited. He threw himself onto a sofa, resting his head against the cushion, appearing wounded. Paulina, seeing his silence, peeked at him. His eyes and face were covered with his hands. She turned on her father’s knee, gazing at him anxiously. Graham groaned.

“Papa, what is the matter?” she whispered.

“You had better ask him, Polly.”

“Is he hurt?” (groan again.)

“He makes a noise as if he were,” said Mr. Home.

“Mother,” Graham suggested weakly, “I think you should send for the doctor. Oh my eye!” (Silence returned, broken only by sighs.)

“If I were to become blind…” he mused.

His tormentor could not bear the thought. She rushed to his side.

“Let me see your eye. I didn’t mean to touch it—only your mouth—and I didn’t think I hit so _very_ hard.”

Silence answered her. Her features trembled. “I am sorry—I am sorry!”

Then came emotion, faltering, and finally tears.

“Have done trying that child, Graham,” said Mrs. Bretton.

“It is all nonsense, my pet,” Mr. Home insisted.

And Graham lifted her again—she punished him once more, pulling his hair while calling him “The naughtiest, rudest, worst, untruest person that ever was.”

On the morning of Mr. Home’s departure, he and Paulina spoke privately in a window alcove. I heard part of their exchange.

“Couldn’t I pack my box and go with you, papa?” she whispered, earnestly.

He shook his head.

“Would I be a trouble to you?”

“Yes, Polly.”

“Because I am little?”

“Because you are little and tender. Only strong, grown people should travel. But don’t look sad, my little girl—it breaks my heart. Papa will soon come back to his Polly.”

“Indeed, indeed, I am not sad, scarcely at all.”

“Polly would be sorry to give papa pain, wouldn’t she?”

“Sorrier than sorry.”

“Then Polly must be cheerful—no crying at parting, no fretting afterward. She must look forward to meeting again and try to be happy in the meantime. Can she do this?”

“She will try.”

“I see she will. Farewell, then. It is time to go.”

“_Now_?—just _now_?”

“Just now.”

She raised quivering lips. Her father sobbed—but she did not. After setting her down, he shook hands with everyone present and left.

When the street door closed, she dropped to her knees beside a chair, crying out—“Papa!”

It was low, long—a cry like “Why hast thou forsaken me?” For several minutes, I saw her endure deep agony. In that brief span of childhood, she experienced emotions some never know. It was in her nature: such moments would return if she lived. No one spoke. Mrs. Bretton, being a mother, shed a few tears. Graham, writing at his desk, looked up and watched her. I, Lucy Snowe, remained calm.

Left alone, the child handled her sorrow as none else could—fighting an unbearable feeling, and eventually, slowly, taming it. That day, she accepted no comfort; nor the next. Afterward, she grew quieter, more still.

On the third evening, she sat on the floor, worn and subdued. Graham entered and gently lifted her without a word. She made no resistance—instead, she nestled into his arms, as if drained. When he sat, she rested her head against him. Within minutes, she fell asleep. He carried her upstairs to bed. I was not surprised when, the next morning, her first question was, “Where is Mr. Graham?”

It happened that Graham was not joining breakfast—he had exercises to complete and had asked his mother to send tea to his study. Paulina offered to bring it. She needed to be busy, to care for someone. The cup was entrusted to her—for, though restless, she was careful. As the study faced the breakfast room across the hall, I watched her.

“What are you doing?” she asked, pausing at the threshold.

“Writing,” said Graham.

“Why don’t you come take breakfast with your mamma?”

“Too busy.”

“Do you want any breakfast?”

“Of course.”

“There, then.”

She placed the cup on the carpet, like a jailer passing water to a prisoner, then retreated. Soon, she returned.

“What will you have besides tea—something to eat?”

“Anything good. Bring me something particularly nice; that’s a kind little woman.”

She returned to Mrs. Bretton.

“Please, ma’am, send your boy something good.”

“You shall choose for him, Polly. What shall my boy have?”

She selected the best item on the table. Shortly after, she came back with a whispered request for marmalade—which wasn’t there. Once obtained (Mrs. Bretton refused them nothing), Graham soon praised her to the heavens, promising that when he had a house, she would be his housekeeper—and possibly, if she showed talent, his cook. When she didn’t return, and I went to check on her, I found Graham and her sharing breakfast—she standing at his elbow, eating alongside him, excepting the marmalade, which she delicately refused, perhaps fearing it seemed she had fetched it for herself as much as for him. Such subtle instincts she constantly displayed.

This bond, once formed, was not easily broken. Time and circumstance seemed to strengthen it rather than weaken it. Though different in age, gender, and interests, they somehow found endless things to say to each other. Paulina, I noticed, revealed her true character only in Graham’s presence. With Mrs. Bretton, she was obedient, even dull—sitting silently at her feet, learning, sewing, drawing figures on slate, never showing originality or spark. I stopped watching her then—she was uninteresting. But the moment Graham knocked in the evening, a transformation occurred. She would rush to the top of the stairs.

Her greeting was often a reprimand or threat.

“You haven’t wiped your shoes properly on the mat. I shall tell your mamma.”

“Little busybody! Are you there?”

“Yes—and you can’t reach me. I’m higher up than you.” (Peering between the banister rails; she couldn’t look over.)

“Polly!”

“My dear boy!” (a term she adopted from his mother.)

“I am fit to faint with fatigue,” Graham declared, leaning against the wall. “Dr. Digby has quite knocked me up with overwork. Just come down and help me carry up my books.”

“Ah! You’re cunning!”

“Not at all, Polly—it’s fact. I’m as weak as a rush. Come down.”

“Your eyes are quiet like the cat’s, but you’ll spring.”

“Spring? Not at all. It isn’t in me. Come down.”

“Perhaps I may—if you promise not to touch, not to snatch me up, not to whirl me round.”

“I? I couldn’t do it!” (Collapsing into a chair.)

“Then put the books down on the first step, and go three yards off.”

Done. She descended cautiously, eyes fixed on the supposedly frail Graham. Of course, her approach revived him instantly—spasms of energy erupted. Playtime was guaranteed. Sometimes she’d be angry; sometimes it passed smoothly, and we’d hear her say, as she led him upstairs: “Now, my dear boy, come and take your tea—I’m sure you must want something.”

It was amusing to see her beside Graham during tea. Alone, she was still and quiet; with him, she became fidgety, officious, endlessly attentive. I often wished she’d mind her own business—but no—herself was forgotten in him. He was more than the Grand Turk in her estimation. She’d gradually gather all the plates before him, and even when everything seemed within reach, she’d find something new: “Ma’am,” she’d whisper to Mrs. Bretton, “perhaps your son would like a little cake—sweet cake, you know—there is some in there” (pointing to the cupboard). Mrs. Bretton usually disliked sweet cake at tea, but the request persisted: “One little piece—only for him—as he goes to school: girls—like me and Miss Snowe—don’t need treats, but _he_ would like it.”

Graham liked it well, and almost always got it. To his credit, he would have shared it with her—had she allowed it. But insisting would ruffle her for the evening. Standing by his knee, monopolizing his talk and attention—that was her reward, not a slice of cake.

She adapted swiftly to whatever interested him. One might think the child had no mind of her own, that she existed solely through another. Now that her father was gone, she clung to Graham, sensing his emotions as her own, living through his life. She learned the names of all his schoolmates instantly, memorizing their traits as told by him. A single description sufficed. She never confused identities. She’d talk for hours about people she’d never seen, imagining their faces, manners, temperaments with astonishing clarity. Some she mimicked—like an under-master Graham disliked, whose quirks she caught and repeated for amusement. But Mrs. Bretton disapproved and forbade it.

They rarely quarreled—but once, a rift occurred, deeply wounding her.

On Graham’s birthday, he invited friends—boys his own age—to dinner. Paulina was eager for their arrival; she’d heard much about them. After dinner, the boys stayed in the dining room, laughing loudly. Passing through the hall, I found Paulina sitting alone on the lowest stair, staring at the polished door where the hall lamp reflected. Her brow was furrowed in thought.

“What are you thinking about, Polly?”

“Nothing particular—only I wish that door were glass, so I could see through it. The boys seem very cheerful, and I want to join them. I want to be with Graham, to watch his friends.”

“What stops you?”

“I feel afraid—but may I try? May I knock and ask to come in?”

I thought they might welcome her as a playmate, so I encouraged her.

She knocked—too softly at first. On a second try, the door opened. Graham’s head appeared—cheerful, but impatient.

“What do you want, you little monkey?”

“To come to you.”

“Do you indeed? As if I’d be bothered with you! Off to mamma and Mistress Snowe, and tell them to put you to bed.”

His red-haired head and flushed face vanished—the door shut firmly. She was stunned.

“Why does he speak so? He never spoke like this before,” she said, horrified. “What have I done?”

“Nothing, Polly. Graham is busy with his friends.”

“And he likes them better than me! He turns me away now they’re here!”

I started to console her, ready with wise words. But she stopped me—plugging her ears at my first sentence, then lying flat on the mat, face against the floor. Neither Warren nor the cook could move her. So she lay there until she chose to rise.

That evening, Graham forgot his irritation and reached for her as usual. But she pulled away, her eyes flashing. She refused to say goodnight, refused to look at him. The next day, he ignored her—she became stone-cold. The day after, he teased her, asking what was wrong. Her lips stayed sealed. He couldn’t truly be angry—his feelings were too uneven. He tried soothing, coaxing: “Why are you so angry? What did I do?” Eventually, tears answered him. He petted her, and they reconciled. But the incident marked her. Afterward, she never sought him, followed him, or begged for his notice.

Once, I told her to deliver a book to Graham in his study.

“I shall wait till he comes out,” she said proudly. “I don’t want to make him get up to open the door.”

Graham had a favorite pony, often ridden out. From the window, she always watched his comings and goings. Her secret dream was to ride it around the courtyard—but she would never ask. One day, she went down to watch him dismount. Leaning on the gate, longing shone in her eyes.

“Come, Polly, will you have a canter?” he asked, half casually.

I suppose she thought he was too casual.

“No, thank you,” she replied, turning away with cool dignity.

“You’d better,” he pressed. “You’ll like it, I’m sure.”

“I don’t care a fig about it,” she said.

“That’s not true. You told Lucy Snowe you longed to ride.”

“Lucy Snowe is a _tatter_-box,” she muttered. (Her imperfect speech was the least precocious thing about her.) And with that, she walked inside.

Graham came in soon after and remarked to his mother: “Mamma, I believe that creature is a changeling. She’s a perfect cabinet of oddities. But I should be dull without her. She amuses me far more than you or Lucy Snowe.”

“Miss Snowe,” Paulina said to me later (now used to chatting with me at night in our room), “do you know on what day I like Graham best?”

“How could I possibly know such a thing? Is there one day when he’s different from the others?”

“To be sure! Can’t you see? Don’t you know? I find him most excellent on Sunday. Then we have him all day. He’s quiet, and in the evening—so kind.”

This wasn’t entirely untrue. Church and Sunday routines kept Graham calm. Evenings, he’d claim the couch and call for Polly.

Graham was not like other boys. Not all his joy came from action. He could sit still, reflect, read—though he rarely commented on what he read. Still, I’ve seen him lost in thought, absorbed. He had a taste, even instinctive preference, in his choices. Occasionally, a quiet mood would settle over him.

With Polly nearby, kneeling on a cushion or the floor, soft murmurs began—quiet, not loud, yet clear enough to catch. I overheard snatches of their talk. Something deeper than daily routine seemed to soothe Graham into gentler thoughts.

“Have you learned any hymns this week, Polly?”

“I’ve learned a very pretty one—four verses long. Shall I say it?”

“Speak nicely—don’t hurry.”

She recited—or half-sang—in a small voice. Graham corrected her delivery, teaching her how to express it. She learned fast, imitated well, and above all, wanted to please him. She became a quick student. Hymns led to Bible readings—perhaps a chapter. Corrections were rare; she read simple narratives well. When the subject moved her, her voice gained depth, emphasis—remarkable.

Joseph cast into the pit. Samuel called. Daniel in the lions’ den—these were favorites. Especially Joseph’s story stirred her.

“Poor Jacob!” she’d whisper, lips trembling. “How he loved his son Joseph! As much,” she once added, “as much, Graham, as I love you. If you were to die…” (She reopened the book, found the verse, and read.) “I should refuse to be comforted, and go down into the grave to you mourning.”

With those words, she gathered Graham into her arms, pressing his long-haired head close. The gesture struck me as oddly bold—like seeing a wild animal, half-tamed, fondled too freely. I didn’t fear Graham would harm her, but I worried she might provoke a careless, impatient push—one worse than a slap. Yet these displays were usually met with passive acceptance. Sometimes, a quiet wonder at her devotion would glimmer in his eyes. Once, he said: “You like me almost as if you were my little sister, Polly.”

“Oh! I _do_ like you,” she said. “I _do_ like you very much.”

But my time for observing her was short. Barely two months after her arrival, a letter came from Mr. Home. He was settled among his relatives on the Continent. England was distasteful to him; he had no plans to return for years. He wished his daughter to join him immediately.

“I wonder how she’ll take this news?” Mrs. Bretton said, reading the letter.

I wondered too. I decided to deliver it myself.

I found her in the drawing-room—her favorite place when alone, where she could trust herself not to touch anything or soil it. She sat on a couch, half-hidden by the drooping curtains near the window. She seemed content. Her tools of occupation surrounded her—the white workbox, scraps of muslin, ribbons collected for doll’s clothes. The doll, neatly night-capped and gowned, lay in its cradle. She rocked it to sleep, fully believing it could feel and dream. At the same time, her eyes scanned a picture book open on her lap.

I stood before her, holding the letter. She looked up—not startled, but calm, composed.

After leaving Bretton—just a few weeks after Paulina’s departure, unaware that I would never return to its quiet streets or walk again through its familiar lanes—I made my way home, having been away for six months. One might suppose that I was overjoyed to be back among family and the comfort of familiar surroundings. And perhaps that assumption does no harm. Let it stand unchallenged. After all, who is to say what lies beneath the surface? Still, I must confess: though I could pretend contentment, I did not feel it. The years that followed were not ones of peace, but of quiet endurance—like a ship anchored in still waters, lulled into silence by calm skies and gentle breezes. Yet even such serenity can hide a storm beneath the surface.

I had imagined myself drifting through life like a vessel at rest, sails furled, drifting on a glassy sea. But fate has a way of stirring the depths. Though I longed for stillness, I was destined for upheaval. I remember now—vividly—the cold, the terror, the struggle. In dreams, I still taste salt on my lips, feel the icy grip of waves pressing against my chest. There was a storm—not one night, nor one day, but many. Days without sun or stars. We threw ropes overboard with our own hands. The wind howled like a wounded beast. Hope vanished. The ship went down. The crew perished. And yet, I said nothing. To whom could I speak? Mrs. Bretton was gone from my life—cut off by circumstances beyond my control. Her fortune, once substantial, had dwindled; her son Graham had taken up a profession, and both mother and son had moved to London. No one remained to turn to. I was alone.

And so, when Miss Marchmont sent for me, I went—not with joy, but with resignation. She lived near us, a woman of means, but crippled by rheumatism for twenty years. She had not walked in decades. Her world was confined to a single room, upstairs, where her drawing-room adjoined her bedroom. I had heard whispers about her—her eccentricities, her sharp tongue, her solitary nature—but I had never met her until now.

She was old, lined by time and pain, her hair silvered, her eyes deep-set and watchful. There was a gravity in her presence, born not just of age, but of long suffering. She sat stiffly in her chair, wrapped in shawls, her hands clenched tightly as if holding onto something unseen. When I entered, she looked at me with a gaze that seemed to pierce through my mourning dress, my tired face, my hollow eyes. She knew I had lost much.

Her companion—a woman who had cared for her for years—was soon to marry. Miss Marchmont had heard of my loneliness, my displacement, and decided I might take her place. After tea, we sat together by the fire. She spoke plainly.

“It will not be an easy life,” she said. “I require constant attention. You will be confined. But compared to what you’ve known lately, it may seem bearable.”

I thought of it. It should have seemed bearable. But it didn’t. The idea of spending my youth in this close room, watching her suffer, enduring her moods—while the world outside continued, full of color and motion—filled me with a quiet dread. My heart sank. Then rose again. For though I feared the burden, I refused to imagine it worse than it was. I wasn’t given to fantasy. I saw things as they were.

“I doubt I’ll have the strength,” I said softly.

“That’s your concern,” she replied. “You look worn out.”

She was right. In the mirror, I saw a ghost—pale, thin, dressed in black, a woman who had already begun to fade. But inside, I felt something still alive. Not hope, perhaps, but a stubborn pulse of existence. I believed the blight was only skin-deep.

“What else do you plan?” she asked.

“Nothing clear. But perhaps something will come.”

“Then try your way first. If it fails, mine is open to you—for three months.”

That kindness surprised me. I thanked her. As I spoke, a spasm seized her. I moved quickly, applying compresses, doing exactly as she instructed. By the time she calmed, a strange closeness had formed between us. I saw in her endurance a strength I admired. She saw in my care a quiet devotion. She called for me again the next day—and then again, and again.

We grew acquainted. Her temper flared sometimes—sharp, sudden, like lightning—but never cruel. It was more like a mother scolding a child than a mistress reprimanding a servant. And beneath the anger, there was reason. Even in fury, she was logical.

Gradually, I began to understand her. Her sternness masked a depth of feeling. Her solitude had shaped her, but not broken her. I found myself drawn to her—not because I loved her, but because she needed me, and I, in my own way, needed to be needed.

One evening, she spoke of the past. Her voice softened, and for the first time, I glimpsed the woman she had once been—young, passionate, full of dreams. She told me of Frank—her lover, her soul’s companion, the man she had expected to marry on Christmas Eve thirty years before.

“I waited,” she said, her eyes distant. “The snow fell gently. The moon was bright. I saw him ride up the path—then… something wrong. A horse, trembling. A man holding it. I ran out. On the lawn lay a body—dragged, bruised, breathing faintly. It was Frank. He was dying.”

She described it with chilling clarity. How she carried him in. How he whispered, “Maria, I am dying in Paradise.” How he died in her arms at dawn, on Christmas morning.

“And that,” she said, “was thirty years ago. I have lived since in sorrow. I am not good. I am not kind. But I have loved—truly loved—once. And that love, though gone, remains my deepest truth.”

She paused. Then turned to me.

“Lucy,” she said, “what do you think? Can I be forgiven for loving him so much, so long? Have I failed God?”

I could not answer. Words failed me.

She smiled faintly. “Very well. We must accept what we cannot change. We must strive to bring joy to others. Tomorrow, I shall begin by trying to make *you* happy. I will do something for you—something lasting.”

She closed her eyes. Her head ached from speaking too much. “Go to bed. It’s two o’clock. You’ve stayed late. I’ve kept you awake selfishly. But go now. I shall sleep well.”

I left her, retiring to my small closet within her room. The night passed quietly. In the morning, she was found still—peaceful, calm, her face serene. No pain. No struggle. Only silence.

Her sudden cheerfulness, her renewed spirit, had been the sign. A final surge of life before the end. The stroke came swiftly, cutting short a life long burdened by suffering.

And so, Miss Marchmont passed. Not in grief, but in quiet release. And I, who had come seeking refuge, found myself standing at the edge of a new chapter—one I had not chosen, but which now claimed me.

With my mistress gone and no one left to guide me, I found myself once more alone in the world, adrift without a purpose or place. It was a time when my nerves, already strained by grief and loss, felt the weight of uncertainty pressing down upon me. I confess I did not look well—thin, pale, with eyes that seemed sunken from too many sleepless nights. I carried the weary air of someone who had watched through long vigils, or labored beyond their strength, or perhaps simply wandered too far from home. But though I looked worn, I was not destitute. Miss Marchmont had not lived long enough to fulfill her promise of kindness on that final night, yet after her passing, her second cousin—the heir—paid my wages promptly. He was a man of sharp features and narrow brow, his face etched with the lines of thrift, and later I learned he was indeed a miser, a stark contrast to the generous woman whose memory still warmed the hearts of those she had helped. So I held fifteen pounds in hand, a body still capable of work, and a spirit, though shaken, not broken. Compared to others, I might have been said to stand in a fortunate position. Yet fortune brought no comfort; instead, it only deepened the ache of uncertainty.

It was a day marked by silence and decision. The next week would see me leave this quiet house, but I had no new shelter waiting. In desperation, I turned to the only person I could trust—a former nurse of my childhood, now the housekeeper at a grand estate nearby. I spent hours with her, speaking softly, listening closely. She offered comfort, but no clear path forward. As twilight fell, I left her, walking two miles back under a sky bright with frost and stars. The night was cold and still, the fields silent, untouched by village lights or human voices. No moon rose to guide me—only the dim trail ahead, lit by scattered stars. And then, above the northern horizon, something stirred: the Aurora Borealis, a shimmering veil of light dancing across the heavens like a ghostly banner. It filled the sky with color and motion, strange and beautiful, as if nature herself were whispering secrets.

I did not fear it. Instead, it stirred something within me—something bold, unyielding. The crisp wind carried strength into my lungs, and a thought came, clear and strong: *Leave this place. Go out into the world.*

*Where?* I asked myself.

The answer came swiftly. From this quiet corner of England, I could see, in my mind’s eye, what I had never seen with my own: London. Not just a city, but a possibility—an escape, a future.

The next morning, I returned to the hall and spoke again to Mrs. Barrett. I told her of my plan. Though she was cautious, even skeptical, she did not call me mad. She was a woman of sense, and I had always carried myself with a calmness that masked my inner turmoil. That reserve had served me before—once allowing me to act boldly without drawing suspicion, to achieve things others might have dismissed as dreams.

As she slowly voiced her concerns, preparing orange peel for marmalade, a child burst into the room—laughing, joyful. It was Charles Leigh, son of a girl I had known in school. Once, she had been dull and plain, in a lower class than mine. Now, married and mother of two, she was transformed—kind, radiant, gentle in her bearing. I recognized her not at all. Nor did she recognize me. I did not try to remind her. What difference did it make?

But when she addressed her nurse in French—poorly spoken, with an accent so thick it echoed our school days—I noticed the woman was foreign. The boy chattered beside her in the same tongue. Later, Mrs. Barrett told me the nurse had come from abroad with her young mistress two years prior, after a trip to the Continent. She was treated almost like a governess, walked with the baby, taught the child French, and lived comfortably—just as many Englishwomen did in foreign homes. “And,” Mrs. Barrett added, “there are plenty of them.”

I filed that thought away, like a scrap of cloth saved for a future seam. Then, before I left, she gave me the address of a respectable old inn in the City—one her husband had once visited. My uncles had stayed there too, she said.

So I went to London. It was not such a daring leap as it may sound. Fifty miles separated us—no great distance, especially in those days. My funds would carry me there, sustain me for a few days, and bring me back if I found nothing worth staying for. I did not think of it as a gamble, nor as a flight into danger. More like a short holiday—rest for overworked thoughts, a breath of fresh air in a life that had grown too tight.

Fifty miles, yes—though now, looking back, I can hardly believe how much the world has changed. My hair, once dark and resilient against time, is now white, covered by a cap as pale as snow beneath snow.

I arrived in London late, on a wet February night. The rain fell hard, the streets were black and slick, and the air thick with unfamiliar sounds. I know my reader does not care for poetic descriptions of first impressions, and truly, I had none to give. There was no time for wonder, no mood for awe. I stepped off the coach into a city that felt like Babylon—vast, chaotic, indifferent. The voices around me—cabmen, porters, strangers—spoke English, yet chopped it up in ways I’d never heard. It sounded alien, clipped, harsh. Still, I managed to understand enough to get my trunk and myself to the inn.

How strange it all felt! First time in London. First time in an inn. Tired, chilled, lost. No experience, no advice, no map—but still, I had to act. I trusted common sense. But common sense, too, was frozen, uncertain. Only necessity drove it forward. It paid the porter—though I was badly cheated, and I did not scold it too harshly. It asked for a room. It called for the chambermaid. And when she appeared—proud, smart, her waist tightly bound, her cap neat, her speech laced with a sneering elegance—I did not flinch. I kept my composure. I remembered she was only a servant, though she acted like a queen.

She looked me up and down, as if I were a country fool. Her tone rebuked me. Her clothes mocked mine. But I said nothing. I waited. And slowly, she softened. Perhaps she thought I was a maid at first. Then she saw my manner—quiet, steady—and hesitated between condescension and respect.

I ate a little, warmed myself by the fire, and finally entered my room. Only then, as I sat on the bed, leaning my head and arms on the pillow, did the full weight of my situation crash over me.

What was I doing here? Alone in London? With no friends, no prospects, no certainty of tomorrow? Where had I come from? Where was I going? What would I do?

Tears welled up, hot and fast. I wept silently, drenching the pillow, my hands, my hair. A long, dark silence followed—full of sorrow and fear. Yet even in that storm, I did not regret my choice. A quiet conviction rose above the doubt: it was better to move forward than to stay behind. A way would open—narrow, perhaps, difficult—but it would open. That belief calmed me. It let me breathe. I whispered my prayers, blew out the candle, and lay down.

Then, just as I closed my eyes, a deep, resonant sound rolled through the night—low, powerful, echoing. Twelve times it rang. On the twelfth toll, I knew it: the bell of St. Paul’s Cathedral.

“I lie in the shadow of St. Paul’s,” I whispered.

And in that moment, I felt less alone.

The morning of March first dawned with a quiet strength, as if the city itself were waking from a long sleep. I rose early, pulled back my curtains, and there—above the rooftops, almost level with the clouds—rose the dome of St. Paul’s, dark blue and solemn in the foggy light. In that moment, something stirred deep within me. My spirit, long bound by routine and silence, seemed to stretch its wings, unburdened for the first time. A sudden warmth flooded through me: *this* was life—the real thing—beginning at last. That morning, my soul grew as fast as Jonah’s gourd under the sun.

“I did well to come,” I whispered, dressing quickly but carefully. “I can feel the pulse of London beating around me. Who would live their whole life in quiet corners, letting their mind rot in obscurity? Only a coward.”

Refreshed and composed, I descended to breakfast. The waiter—a man with silver hair and twenty years of service—entered with his tray. We spoke for ten minutes, and in that time, he became more than a servant; he became a familiar. I mentioned my uncles, Charles and Wilmot, who had once stayed here. He remembered them clearly, with respect. When I told him I was their niece, his manner shifted instantly—from cautious to courteous. He said I looked like Uncle Charles. Mrs. Barrett had often said the same. Now, I felt seen. No longer would I be met with hesitation or indifference.

Outside my window lay a narrow, peaceful street. Few people passed—nothing alarming, nothing overwhelming. I felt safe enough to venture forth alone.

After breakfast, I stepped into the world. Joy surged in my chest. To walk alone in London! It felt like an adventure. Soon I found myself in Paternoster Row, a place steeped in history. I entered a bookseller’s shop run by Mr. Jones, a dry, serious man behind his desk. I bought a small book—too expensive, really—but one I meant one day to send to Mrs. Barrett. For a moment, standing there, I felt both insignificant and radiant. He looked at me as if I were someone remarkable. And perhaps I was.

That morning, I lived more than I had in years. At St. Paul’s, I climbed to the dome. From there, London unfolded: the river winding like a ribbon, bridges arcing over it, churches rising like memories. Westminster stood ancient and proud; the Temple Gardens sparkled under the spring sun. Above it all, a pale blue sky stretched wide, softened by a thin veil of haze.

Descending, I wandered without plan, carried by a sense of freedom. I drifted into the heart of the city—the Strand, Cornhill—where the hum of commerce and movement filled the air. I crossed busy streets, not trembling, but bold. To move through this chaos alone—and to do so without fear—was a triumph. Later, I saw the West End, the parks, the elegant squares. But I loved the city best. There, life was real: urgent, loud, purposeful. The West End offered pleasure; the city offered meaning.

By two o’clock, I was faint and hungry—truly hungry, as I hadn’t been in years. I returned to my modest inn, where I ate simply: a joint and vegetables. They tasted better than any meal Miss Marchmont’s cook had ever prepared. Afterward, I lay on three chairs, weary but content. I slept, then woke, and thought—deeply—for hours.

My mind was clear, my resolve strong. I had nothing left to lose. The past was a wasteland I could no longer endure. If I failed now, who would care? If I died far from home—I had no home, really—then who would mourn?

I could suffer. I had learned to endure. Death held no terrors for me. I had faced it before, calmly. So I made a decision.

That evening, I asked my friend the waiter about ships sailing to Boue-Marine. Time was short. I needed to board that night. “Better take your berth now, ma'am,” he advised. I agreed. I paid my bill generously—more than I should have—and he smiled faintly, as if amused by my extravagance. Then he called a coach.

He warned the driver to take me directly to the wharf—not to leave me to the watermen. But the driver ignored him. Instead, he dropped me into a crowd of shouting boatmen.

Darkness pressed in. The coach sped away. The men lunged for me and my trunk. Their curses cut through the night. One grabbed my luggage. I waited. When another reached for me, I stepped forward sharply, shook off his hand, and boarded a boat. “Place the trunk beside me—just there,” I said firmly. The boatman obeyed. He became my ally.

The river was black as ink, lit only by scattered glimmers from buildings and ships. We glided along, passing vessel after vessel. By lantern-light, I read their names: *The Ocean*, *The Phoenix*, *The Consort*, *The Dolphin*. Then—*The Vivid*. That was mine.

As we neared her, I thought of Charon rowing souls across the Styx. The wind chilled my face. Rain fell from midnight clouds. Two rough rowers cursed beside me. Yet I felt neither fear nor sorrow. I was alert, alive. “Why?” I wondered. “Why am I not afraid?”

Then, suddenly, *The Vivid* emerged—white and sharp against the black water. “Here you are!” shouted the waterman. “Six shillings!”

“You ask too much,” I replied.

He refused to let me aboard until I paid. A young steward leaned over the rail, grinning. To stop the show, I handed over the money. Three times that day I’d paid crowns when I should have paid shillings. But I told myself: *It’s the price of learning.*

“They’ve cheated you!” the steward exclaimed when I stepped on deck.

“I knew it,” I said, and walked below.

In the ladies’ cabin, a stout, striking woman stood waiting. She eyed me suspiciously. “It’s unusual,” she muttered, “to board at this hour.” Her tone was cold.

“I’m already on board,” I replied. “Show me my berth.”

She complied—reluctantly. I removed my bonnet, arranged my things, and lay down. The hardest part was over. For now, I could rest. Until we reached port—then came the next chapter. But I couldn’t think ahead. Exhausted, I slipped into a half-dream.

All night, the stewardess talked—loudly, incessantly—to her son, who resembled her perfectly. They quarreled, made up, argued again. She claimed to write a letter to her father, reading aloud passages as if I weren’t there. Some spoke of a younger sister, Charlotte, on the verge of a reckless romance. The son mocked her words. She raged. They were odd, defiant, full of noise and pride.

Near dawn, she turned to talk of the “Watsons”—a family of passengers expected soon. “They’re worth a fortune to me,” she declared. “Every time they cross.”

At sunrise, the ship stirred. Passengers arrived. The Watsons—two men, two women—were greeted with fanfare. They were wealthy, confident. The women wore bright flowers, velvet cloaks, silk dresses—far too fine for a damp packet deck. The men were short, fat, vulgar. The oldest, the husband, looked like an oil barrel. Yet the young wife—beautiful, dazzling—laughed wildly, carefree.

I watched her. Her laughter felt hollow—like madness disguised as joy. Then, without warning, she tripped toward me, offering a camp-stool with a smile too wide, too bright. I declined politely. She danced away, heedless.

Beside her, a young girl arrived with a gentleman—her father, I later learned. She wore a simple print dress, a plain straw bonnet, a large shawl. Her look was quiet, dignified. Her father scanned the passengers, then glanced at me—displeased. He kissed her goodbye and left. The ship set sail.

Foreigners marvel at English girls traveling alone. They call it daring. Some say it’s masculine. Others blame education and religion. But this girl—Miss Ginevra Fanshawe—was different. She paced the deck, looking with disdain at the Watsons, then approached me.

“Do you like sea voyages?” she asked.

I admitted I’d never been on one. “So it will be new,” I said.

“Oh, how lovely!” she exclaimed. “I envy you. First impressions are always sweet. I’ve done this so many times, I’m completely *blasée*.”

I couldn’t help smiling.

“Why are you laughing?” she demanded, a little testy—but oddly endearing.

“Because you’re too young to be bored by anything.”

“I’m seventeen,” she retorted.

“You don’t look sixteen. Do you like traveling alone?”

“Bah! I don’t care. I’ve crossed the Channel ten times. But I never stay alone long—I make friends.”

“I doubt you’ll make many here,” I said, nodding toward the noisy Watsons.

“Not those awful people,” she snapped. “They should be steerage. Are you going to school?”

“No.”

“Where then?”

“I don’t know—only as far as Boue-Marine.”

She stared, then shrugged. “I’m going to school. Oh, I’ve been to so many foreign schools! And yet I’m still an ignoramus. I know nothing—except I dance and play beautifully. And I speak French and German… sort of. But I can’t read or write them well. Once they asked me to translate a page of German into English. I couldn’t. Papa was mortified. He says M. de Bassompierre—my godfather—has wasted all his money.”

She paused. “And I’m terrible at history, geography, arithmetic. I write English terribly—spelling, grammar, everything. And I’ve forgotten my religion. They call me Protestant, but I don’t know what that means. I used to be Lutheran in Bonn—dear Bonn!—where every nice girl had a admirer. ‘Schönes Mädchen,’ they’d say. I was so happy there.”

“And now?” I asked.

“Oh! At—_chose_,” she said.

Later, I learned “_chose_” was Villette, capital of Labassecour. A word she used whenever she forgot the real name—like a French habit she’d picked up.

“Do you like Villette?” I asked.

“Fine. The locals are dull and rude. But there are nice English families.”

“In a school?”

“Yes.”

“A good one?”

“Oh, no! Horrible. But I go out every Sunday. I ignore the teachers, the students, the lessons—send them _au diable_. And I get on just fine.”

She yawned. “You’re laughing again.”

“No—just thinking.”

“Tell me where you’re going.”

“Where fate leads me. My goal is to earn a living wherever I can.”

“To earn?” she gasped. “Are you poor?”

“As poor as Job.”

She paused. “Bah! How sad. But I know poverty. We’re poor at home—papa, mamma, all of us. Papa’s Captain Fanshawe, on half-pay. He’s noble, but our connections aren’t rich. Only my uncle De Bassompierre helps—educates us girls. I have five sisters, three brothers. We’ll marry older men with money. Augusta married Mr. Davies—he’s yellow from the fever in India, but rich. She has a carriage. We think she did well. That’s better than ‘earning a living.’”

She blinked. “By the way—are you clever?”

“No. Not at all.”

“You can play, sing, speak several languages?”

“Not at all.”

“Still, I think you are clever,” she said, yawning again.

“Shall you be sea-sick?”

“Will you?”

“Oh, terribly. I already feel it. I’ll go below. And I’ll tell that fat, hateful stewardess exactly what I think. *Heureusement je sais faire aller mon monde.*”

She disappeared.

For the rest of the afternoon, I remained alone on deck. I recall the calm, even happiness I felt—despite being adrift, uncertain, without a home. As the poet says:

> Stone walls do not a prison make,
> Nor iron bars—a cage.

So too, danger, loneliness, uncertainty—these are not true prisons, so long as the spirit is free and the mind engaged. So long as Liberty flaps her wings, and Hope lights the way.

I didn’t grow sick until after Margate. Then, the sea breeze felt divine. The waves heaved like giants. Seabirds soared. Sails gleamed on the horizon. The sky loomed quiet and vast. In my thoughts, I saw Europe—distant, golden, dream-like. Sunlight painted the coast in gold. Towns, towers, forests, rivers—all etched in light. Over it all, a rainbow arched from north to south—soft, grand, promising.

But then I dismissed it. Or perhaps let it stand—as a lesson:

Day-dreams are delusions of the demon.

Eventually, sickness overtook me. I stumbled below.

Miss Fanshawe’s berth was next to mine. During the storm, she tormented me with selfishness. She complained endlessly. The Watsons, equally ill, were stoic compared to her. I saw, then, the truth: some people—fair, fragile, careless—cannot endure hardship. They sour like spoiled wine. A man who marries such a woman must promise her perpetual sunshine.

Finally, furious at her constant whining, I said sharply, “Hold your tongue.”

It shocked her. But strangely, it brought her peace. And she liked me better for it.

As night fell, the sea grew rougher. Waves crashed against the hull. Furniture toppled. We all grew worse. Miss Fanshawe groaned, “I shall die!”

“Not yet, honey,” the stewardess said. “We’re nearly in port.”

And indeed, within minutes, the storm calmed. By midnight, we docked.

I was sorry. Yes, truly sorry. My brief reprieve was over. The hard work began again. On deck, the cold air and dark sky seemed to scold me for daring to be here. The lights of the foreign town glimmered like watching eyes. Friends welcomed the Watsons. A family took Miss Fanshawe away. I—no one came for me.

Yet I must go somewhere. Necessity doesn’t wait. As I gave the stewardess her fee—more than she expected—she surprised me with kindness. She not only directed me to a quiet, respectable inn, but summoned a commissionaire to escort me—though not my trunk, which had gone to customs.

And so I walked, alone, into the unknown.

The next morning, I awoke with a renewed sense of strength and clarity. The weariness that had clouded my thoughts the night before had lifted; my mind felt sharp, alert, and unburdened. I dressed quickly, the routine bringing a quiet rhythm to my nerves. Just as I finished, a knock came at the door. “Come in,” I called, expecting the chambermaid—only for a rough-looking man to step inside without ceremony.

“Give me your keys, Miss,” he said bluntly.

I blinked. “Why?”

“Gif!” he snapped, snatching them from my fingers before I could protest. “All right! You’ll have your trunk soon.”

Relief washed over me when I realized he was from customs. The moment passed, but the memory of his abruptness lingered. I didn’t know where to find breakfast, but I decided to go downstairs anyway—though slowly, deliberately. As I descended the wide staircase, I noticed something I’d missed the night before: this wasn’t just an inn. It was a grand hotel. High ceilings soared above me, painted walls glowed under the daylight streaming through large windows, and marble steps, though bare and slightly grimy, reflected the light like polished stone.

And yet—my room had been tiny, sparsely furnished, almost invisible compared to the opulence surrounding it. A strange thought struck me: how did servants everywhere seem to know exactly who I was? Not by appearance, not by speech, but by some instinctive calculation—how little I had, how insignificant my presence. They sized me up instantly, silently placing me in a category too small to matter. I saw it plainly now: they all knew, without asking, that I was no one of consequence.

It was odd—and unsettling—but I refused to let it crush me. I kept my spirits up, even as I walked into the great hall below, bright with skylight and bustling with strangers. I found the coffee room eventually, though I trembled as I entered. The silence of solitude pressed on me. I wished I knew if I was making the right choice—yet I couldn’t turn back. There was no turning back. So I sat down at a small table, and when a waiter brought breakfast, I ate, though my appetite was poor.

The room was full of men—no women among them. Still, no one stared. A few glanced my way, but only briefly. Perhaps they assumed I was eccentric—just another Englishwoman lost in foreign lands. I accepted it quietly, like a truth beyond dispute.

After breakfast, I faced the next question: where now? An inner voice whispered, *Go to Villette.* It came from a half-remembered phrase Miss Fanshawe had tossed off casually as she bade me farewell: “I wish you’d come to Madame Beck’s. She has marmots—children, I suppose—you might look after. She needs an English governess—or did two months ago.”

Who Madame Beck was, or where she lived, I didn’t know. I’d asked, but the question slipped away in the rush of parting. I assumed Villette was her home. Forty miles away. I knew it was a gamble—a desperate one—but what else was there? In the vast emptiness I’d left behind, any hope was worth grasping, even a shadow.

I arranged passage on the diligence, secured a seat, and set out with nothing but that faint thread of possibility. Before you judge me reckless, reader, consider where I began. I had nothing to lose. Only the chance to gain something.

Though we traveled slowly, the day held its own charm. The landscape was flat and sparse—fields tilled like kitchen gardens, pollard willows lining the roads, canals winding like sluggish green serpents. The sky was gray, the air damp and still. Yet amid all that dullness, my spirit stirred. My imagination bloomed, and my heart warmed.

But beneath it all, a silent anxiety waited—like a beast coiled in the dark. I heard its breath. Felt its pulse against mine. It never moved, but I always knew it was there, waiting for nightfall to spring.

I hoped we’d reach Villette before dark. I wanted to arrive in daylight, not shrouded in shadows. But our progress was slow. Fog thickened. Rain fell in steady sheets. By the time we reached the city outskirts, darkness had settled—thick and tangible.

We passed through a gate guarded by soldiers, then left the muddy road behind, rattling over a rough, flinty pavement. At a bureau, the diligence stopped. Passengers disembarked. My first task: retrieve my trunk.

I stood back, watching carefully. I’d tied a green ribbon to the direction card so I could spot it easily. One by one, packages were removed. Boxes, bags, parcels—everything came down. Then, nothing. No green ribbon. No portmanteau.

I searched the vehicle. The oilcloth cover was lifted. Every last item was gone—umbrellas, hats, cloaks, bandboxes. But mine was nowhere.

I couldn’t speak. I had no words in French. The world around me chattered in a language I couldn’t understand. What could I do?

I approached the conductor, touched his arm, pointed to the trunk, then to the roof. I tried to ask with my eyes. He misunderstood—grabbing the wrong trunk and lifting it onto the carriage.

“Leave that alone!” a voice said in clear English. Then, sharply in French: “What are you doing? That trunk is mine.”

I turned. The sound of my own country’s tongue brought warmth to my chest. “Sir,” I said, not noticing his face, only the relief in my voice, “I cannot speak French. Could you please ask him what happened to my trunk?”

He hesitated—half surprised, half uncertain whether to interfere. But he stepped forward. “Do ask him,” I urged. “I would do the same for you.”

I don’t know if he smiled, but he spoke gently—calm, not harsh. “What kind of trunk was yours?” I described it—the size, the shape, the green ribbon. Then he turned to the conductor, and a storm of French erupted. I understood none of it, but I felt the force of his questioning.

When he returned, he said, “He claims he was overloaded. He admits he took your trunk off after you saw it loaded. He left it behind at Boue-Marine with other parcels. He promises to send it tomorrow. You’ll get it here by the day after.”

“Thank you,” I whispered. My heart sank. But what now?

He looked at me, perhaps seeing the fear in my eyes. “Do you have friends here?” he asked.

“No,” I replied. “I don’t know where to go.”

A pause. Then, as he turned more fully into the lamplight, I saw him clearly. Young. Handsome. Distinguished. A gentleman—perhaps a lord. His face was noble, open, kind. Not arrogant, not cold. He looked strong, yet gentle.

I turned to leave—knowing I had no right to expect help from someone so far above me.

“Was all your money in the trunk?” he asked.

I breathed easier. “No. I have enough in my purse—about twenty francs—to stay at a quiet inn until the trunk arrives. But I’m completely lost. I don’t know the streets, or the places.”

“I can give you the address of such an inn,” he said. “It’s nearby. With my directions, you’ll find it easily.”

He tore a page from his notebook, wrote a few words, and handed it to me. I felt a deep gratitude. I wouldn’t have doubted him—not for a second. There was honesty in his eyes, integrity in his tone.

“You should follow the Boulevard and cross the park,” he continued. “But it’s too late and too dark for a woman to walk through the park alone. I’ll walk with you this far.”

He started forward, and I followed—through the rain, through the darkness. The Boulevard was empty, the path slick with water. Trees dripped. The park was black, impenetrable. I couldn’t see him, only hear his footsteps. Yet I felt no fear. I would have followed that calm tread anywhere—into eternal night, if need be.

When we reached the other side, he said, “Now, walk straight down this street until you see steps. Two lamps will mark them. Descend them. Below lies a narrower street. Follow it to the end—you’ll find your inn. They speak English there. Your troubles are nearly over. Good night.”

“Good night, sir,” I said. “Thank you—truly.”

We parted. And as I walked on, I carried with me the image of his face—the kindness in it, the strength. It stayed with me long after.

The city loomed ahead—grand houses, towering buildings, palaces or churches, I couldn’t tell. I passed a portico when suddenly two men emerged from behind the columns. Mustached, cigar-smoking, dressed like gentlemen but with coarse manners. They spoke insolently, and despite my fast pace, they matched it—following me for a long stretch.

At last, I met a patrol. They turned the men away. But the chase had thrown me off course. When I regained my composure, I realized I’d lost my bearings. The steps I was supposed to find—I’d already passed them.

Panic fluttered in my chest. I couldn’t go back. Not past those men. But I had to retrace my steps. I wandered, trembling, until I found a worn flight of stairs. I assumed it was the right one. I descended.

Below lay a narrow street. No inn. I kept walking. Then, in a quiet, clean lane, I saw a light glowing over a doorway. A large house, taller than the others. This must be it.

I hurried forward—my knees weak, my breath ragged. But it wasn’t an inn. A brass plate read: *Pensionnat de Demoiselles—Madame Beck.*

My heart leapt. A hundred thoughts surged through me. But I didn’t think. I didn’t plan. Providence seemed to say: *Stop here. This is your place.*

Fate took hold. I rang the bell.

While I waited, I forced myself not to think. I focused on the wet stones beneath the lamp, counting them, tracing their shapes. I rang again.

The door opened. A maid in a neat cap stood before me. “May I see Madame Beck?” I asked.

She hesitated—then nodded. I suppose she assumed I was a teacher, coming for work. She let me in.

I found myself in a cold, glittering salon—porcelain stove unlit, gilded ornaments, polished floor. A clock struck nine. Silence. I sat, staring at the white folding doors, waiting.

Then—“You ayre Engliss?” a voice said beside me.

I jumped. I hadn’t expected anyone. But there was no ghost—only a plump, motherly woman in a shawl and nightcap.

“Yes,” I said. “I am English.”

And immediately, we began talking—though neither of us truly understood the other. She spoke rapidly in French. I answered in English. We made a terrible noise—she with her loud, rolling vowels, I with my careful words. We achieved little.

She rang for help. A woman arrived—small, brisk, with a bold manner. She’d studied in an Irish convent and claimed to speak English well. Though she butchered it, she listened.

I told my story: how I’d left my country seeking work, how I’d do anything honest—nursing, cleaning, serving—anything that wasn’t shameful. I would be useful, I promised.

Madame Beck listened. Her face remained unreadable—no pity, no curiosity. She studied me, weighing every word. Then a bell rang.

“Voilà pour la prière du soir!” she said, rising.

Through the interpreter, she told me to return the next day. But I couldn’t. I couldn’t face the dark streets again.
