So I spoke directly to her—calmly, firmly. “Madame, I assure you, your interests will be better served by hiring me tonight. I will work fairly, fully. And if you employ me, it would be best for me to stay here. I have no one in Villette. I don’t speak the language. How can I find lodging?”

She paused. “But you have no reference?”

“None.”

She asked about my luggage. I told her it would arrive tomorrow.

Then, a man’s footsteps echoed in the vestibule. He approached the front door.

Madame Beck turned. “Who goes out now?” she asked.

“M. Paul,” the teacher replied. “He came to read to the first class.”

“The very man I want to see now. Call him.”

The teacher ran to the door. M. Paul entered—a small, dark man with spectacles.

“Mon cousin,” Madame Beck began, “I need your opinion. You know physiognomy. Read that face.”

He fixed his glasses on me. His expression was stern—determined to see through me.

“I read it,” he said.

“And what do you say?”

“Many things.”

“Is she good or bad?”

“Of both kinds,” he replied. “But may one trust her word?”

“If you’re negotiating something important?”

“She wishes to work here. Tells a tale of honesty. Gives no references.”

“She’s a stranger?”

“An Englishwoman, as one can see.”

“She speaks French?”

“No.”

“She understands it?”

“No.”

“One may speak plainly in her presence?”

“Certainly.”

He studied me longer. Then, finally: “Engage her. If goodness prevails, the action will bring its reward. If evil—well, madam, it will still be a good deed.”

With a bow, he left.

And so—by God’s grace—I was hired that very night. I did not have to step back into the lonely, hostile dark. I had a place. A purpose. A shelter.

Delivered into the care of the maîtresse, I was led through a long, narrow passage into a kitchen that felt both foreign and oddly clean—though it held no fireplace or oven. It wasn’t until later that I understood the great black furnace dominating one corner served as their cooking heart. A quiet sense of relief washed over me when, instead of being left alone in this strange space, I was guided further into a small inner room called a “cabinet.” A cook, dressed in a short jacket, petticoat, and wooden sabots, brought my supper: meat of an unknown kind, served in a sharp yet pleasant sauce; chopped potatoes seasoned with something mysterious—perhaps vinegar and sugar; a tartine of bread and butter; and a baked pear. Hungry and weary, I ate with gratitude.

After evening prayers, Madame Beck came to inspect me once more. She asked me to follow her upstairs. We passed through a series of tiny dormitories—once nuns’ cells, I would learn later—and then into the oratory, a low, dim room where a pale crucifix hung on the wall and two candles flickered in silence. At last, we reached a chamber where three children slept soundly in tiny beds. The air was thick with warmth from a stove, and laced with a strong, unexpected scent—like smoke mingled with spirits, unmistakably the smell of whisky.

Beside a table, where a candle burned low, sat a woman fast asleep. She wore a garish striped silk dress beneath a coarse apron, her clothes mismatched and absurd. On the table beside her lay a bottle and an empty glass. The scene was startling, yet Madame watched it all without expression—no smile, no frown, not even a hint of disapproval. She didn’t wake her. With calm authority, she pointed to a fourth bed and indicated it was mine. After extinguishing the candle and lighting a night-lamp, she slipped through a half-open door into her own spacious, well-appointed bedroom visible beyond.

That night, my prayers were all thanks. How far I had come since leaving London just two days before—without protection, without plan, only hope clinging like a thread. Yet here I was, sheltered, fed, and assigned a place under a roof that felt both alien and strangely safe.

I was a light sleeper. In the deep silence of the night, I woke suddenly. All was still—but a white figure stood in the room. Madame, in her nightdress, moved silently across the floor. She visited each of the three sleeping children, then approached me. I feigned sleep, but she studied me closely. A quiet drama unfolded. She sat on the edge of my bed for what seemed a quarter of an hour, watching my face. Then she leaned closer, lifted my cap slightly, turned back the border to reveal my hair, and examined my hand resting on the sheets.

Next, she turned to the chair at the foot of the bed, where my clothes lay folded. As she touched them, I opened my eyes cautiously—curious to see how far her scrutiny might go. And go it did. She inspected every article of clothing. I sensed her intent: to judge me by my garments—my station, means, habits. Her hands moved methodically. She turned my pocket inside out, counted the coins in my purse, opened my small notebook, read its contents aloud to herself, and pulled out a plaited lock of Miss Marchmont’s grey hair. Then, with special attention, she took the keys to my trunk, desk, and workbox, and carried them into her own room.

I rose quietly in bed and followed with my eyes. Those keys, reader, were not returned until they had left an impression in wax upon her dressing table—a record of their shape, a silent testament to her vigilance. Only then were my belongings restored, carefully folded and placed back in order.

What conclusions did she draw? Were they favorable? I could not tell. Her face remained unreadable—a mask of stone in the moonlight. But I knew, in that moment, that this was not England. This was a world governed by watchfulness, discretion, and control.

The next day revealed more about the woman who had occupied the room before me—Mrs. Sweeny. She had presented herself as an English lady of fallen fortune, claiming to be from Middlesex and speaking with a pure London accent. Madame, trusting her own methods of discovery, hired her without hesitation—just as she had hired me. Mrs. Sweeny became the nursery governess for the three children.

But soon enough, the truth emerged. She was not English at all—she was Irish. Her true station, I couldn’t say. She claimed to have raised the son and daughter of a marquis, though I suspected she’d been little more than a servant or nurse in some noble household. Her speech was thick, layered with affected Cockney mannerisms. Yet she possessed a wardrobe of suspicious grandeur—stiff silks ill-fitting her frame, lace-trimmed caps, and most notably, a real Indian shawl, a “véritable cachemire,” which Madame admired with genuine awe. That shawl, I believe, was her only claim to dignity. Without it, she would have vanished from the pensionnat in two days. With it, she ruled the household—commanding respect even from the teachers and servants, and momentarily bending Madame’s will.

But when she learned I was to take her place, her true nature erupted. She rose against me with full force. Madame observed this with perfect composure—so still, so unshaken, that I felt compelled to remain calm myself. For a brief moment, Madame stepped out of the room. Ten minutes later, a police officer stood among us. Mrs. Sweeny and her belongings were removed. Not a word was spoken in anger. Madame’s brow never twitched.

All of this—the summons, the arrest, the expulsion—was settled before breakfast. The room was cleaned, aired, scrubbed of every trace of her presence—even the lingering scent of whisky, that subtle clue to her downfall, erased completely. By the time Madame emerged from her chamber, radiant as dawn, she was already pouring her first cup of coffee with serene detachment.

By noon, I was summoned to assist Madame with her morning routine. (It seemed my role would blend governess and lady’s maid.) Until midday, she stayed in her wrapper, shawl, and soft slippers. One might wonder how an English headmistress would view such informality.

Dressing her hair proved difficult. She had abundant auburn locks, untouched by grey, despite her forty years. Seeing my confusion, she said gently, “You’ve never been a femme-de-chambre in your country?” Taking the brush from my hand, she arranged her hair herself, guiding me without harshness. In other tasks, she directed and helped with equal grace, never showing impatience. Note: This was the only time I ever dressed her. From then on, Rosine, the porter, took over.

Once attired, Madame Beck appeared shorter and fuller-figured, yet graceful in her own way—proportioned, balanced. Her complexion was fresh, rosy but not flushed; her blue eyes calm and observant. Her dark silk dress clung to her form with French precision. She looked well, though modestly so—bourgeoise, indeed, in every sense. There was a harmony in her bearing, yet her features offered contrast: her forehead high but narrow, expressing intelligence and kindness, but no breadth; her eyes watchful, serene, yet never stirred by passion. Her mouth was firm, lips thin—hard, even grim at times. She reminded me of Minos, the ancient judge of souls, clad in petticoats.

Yet in time, I saw she was more than that. Her name was Modeste Maria Beck, born Kint—though she should have been Ignacia. She was charitable, doing much good. Her rule was mild, though firm. I heard she never scolded Mrs. Sweeny, despite her drunkenness and neglect—yet dismissed her the moment it suited her. Similarly, she never openly criticized her staff—yet teachers and masters came and went with quiet regularity, replaced without explanation.

Her school was both a boarding house and a day institution. Over a hundred day pupils attended, while about twenty boarded. She managed them all—four teachers, eight masters, six servants, three children—while maintaining harmony with parents and visitors. She did it all without haste, without strain, without visible effort. She had a system, and it worked perfectly.

“Surveillance,” “espionage”—these were her guiding principles. The reader has already seen a glimpse: turning my pocket inside out, reading my private notes, testing my keys. She knew people deeply, and used them wisely. She wouldn’t hesitate to use the dirtiest tool for a dirty job—discarding it afterward like a spent shell. But when she found someone pure and reliable, she guarded them like treasure, wrapped in silk and cotton wool.

Yet beware: trust her too far, and you risked becoming her secret enemy. Interest was her compass—the driving force behind every decision. I’ve seen her feelings appealed to, and smiled at the attempt. No one ever swayed her through emotion. To try was to provoke her antipathy. It reminded her of her own emptiness. She gave charity generously—to classes, to causes—but rarely to individuals. She supported large-scale philanthropy, yet felt nothing for personal sorrow. Even Christ’s agony in Gethsemane, his death on Calvary, could not move her to a single tear.

Still, she was remarkable. Capable, wise, relentless. That school was too small a stage for her talents. She ought to have ruled a nation, led a turbulent assembly. No one could intimidate her, irritate her nerves, or outwit her. She combined the roles of prime minister and chief of police. Wise, firm, faithless; secretive, clever, impassive; vigilant, inscrutable; sharp-minded, yet emotionally dead—yet always impeccably composed. What more could one ask?

Let me clarify: I did not grasp all this in a month, nor even in half a year. At first, I saw only the outward glow of a thriving school. Here was a large house filled with healthy, lively girls—well-dressed, many beautiful—learning easily, without strain. Lessons were gentle, play allowed, exercise encouraged. Food was plentiful and good. No pallid faces, no frail bodies. Holidays were frequent. Sleep, washing, meals—all given ample time. Her methods were liberal, rational, healthful. Many an English schoolmistress might do well to emulate her—if only parents would allow it.

Behind the house lay a garden, and in summer, the girls lived outdoors among roses and fruit trees. Under a vast vine-covered arbor, Madame would sit in the afternoons, summoning classes to sew and read. Masters came and went, delivering lively lectures rather than rigid lessons. Students made notes—or didn’t—as they pleased, knowing they could copy others’ if needed. Monthly outings, Catholic holidays, and occasional country walks with *gaufres*, *vin blanc*, milk, *pain bis*, or butter rolls and coffee—life seemed pleasant. Madame appeared kind, the teachers tolerable, the girls cheerful, perhaps a bit boisterous, but full of life.

This was the enchanted view from afar. But soon, the distance would vanish. I would be pulled down from my nursery perch, forced into deeper contact with the world of the Rue Fossette.

One afternoon, I sat upstairs, teaching the children English while stitching a silk dress for Madame. She entered quietly, her brow furrowed in thought, her expression distant. She sat opposite me, silent for several minutes. Désirée, the eldest, read aloud from Mrs. Barbauld’s essays, translating as she went. Madame listened.

Then, without warning, she asked, almost accusingly: “Miss, were you a governess in England?”

“No, Madame,” I replied, smiling. “You’re mistaken.”

“Is this your first attempt at teaching—my children?”

I confirmed it was. She fell silent again, but when I reached for a pin, I caught her studying me—measuring, judging, weighing. She had already examined my things, my papers, my past. But from that day, for about two weeks, she tested me anew. She listened at the nursery door, followed me at a distance, hiding behind trees when we walked. Once satisfied, she made her move.

One morning, she approached me abruptly, pretending urgency. “I’m in a dilemma,” she said. “Mr. Wilson, the English master, hasn’t arrived. He may be ill. The class waits. Can you give a short dictation exercise? Just to keep the lesson going?”

“In classe, Madame?” I asked.

“Yes—in the second division.”

“Sixty pupils,” I said, already retreating into my usual cowardice. I knew the number. I feared the task. My instinct was to hide, to avoid responsibility. If left to myself, I might have spent twenty years teaching infants the hornbook, sewing dresses, making frocks—content in my sloth. Not because I loved it, but because it meant no heavy burden, no trial. Avoiding pain felt like peace. I lived in two worlds: one of thought, rich with imagination; one of reality, limited to bread, work, and shelter.

“Come,” she said, as I bent over a child’s pinafore. “Leave that.”

“But Fifine needs it, Madame.”

“Fifine must wait. I need you.”

My days were now fully occupied—filled with purpose and steady effort. Between teaching others and studying myself, I had little time to spare. It was satisfying, this sense of progress: no longer idle or stagnant, but actively shaping my mind, sharpening it through constant use. The world around me offered a broad experience—one not confined by narrow limits. Villette is a city of many peoples, and in Madame Beck’s school, girls from nearly every European nation mingled freely, each bearing the marks of her own station in life. Equality was deeply rooted here; though not formally republican, Labassecour lived almost as if it were. At the desks of Madame Beck’s institution, noble daughters sat beside those of humble birth, indistinguishable by outward signs—except that often, the commoner girl carried herself with greater openness and kindness, while the aristocratic one wore an air of polished insolence, masked beneath a delicate veneer of deceit.

In the blood of these young ladies, there was sometimes a quick French spark mixed with sluggish northern temperament. And sadly, this vitality seemed to manifest itself not in noble impulses, but in a slick ease with which flattery flowed—and fiction, too. Their manner was light, lively, even charming—but empty of sincerity, devoid of heart.

Yet let fairness be said: the native girls of Labassecour had their own kind of hypocrisy—though coarser, more blatant, less likely to fool anyone. When a lie was needed, they delivered it without hesitation, untroubled by conscience. No soul in Madame Beck’s house—from the kitchen maid to the headmistress—would blush at deception. To invent was not seen as virtue, but it was considered among the least serious of sins. “I have lied several times,” became a routine confession every month. The priest listened without surprise, absolved without reluctance. But miss mass? Read a novel instead of scripture? That was another matter—those were true transgressions, met with penance and rebuke.

Still unaccustomed to this way of life, still learning its hidden currents, I managed well enough in my new role. After the first few tense lessons—delivered amid danger, like standing on the edge of a volcano rumbling beneath my feet—I found the storm seemed to quieten, at least as far as I was concerned. My desire for success was strong. I could not bear the thought of failing, not because of my own shortcomings, but due to the willful disobedience and indifference of others. Many nights I lay awake, plotting how best to gain control, how to win influence over these restless spirits. I saw clearly that I could expect no help from Madame Beck. Her principle was to remain beloved by the pupils, no matter what cost to justice or comfort for the teachers. To seek her support during any crisis was to invite dismissal. She took only what pleased her—what was sweet, agreeable, flattering—while demanding that her subordinates endure the harsh duties that brought disfavor.

So I must rely on myself alone.

First, it was evident that force would never work. These girls could not be driven. They required patience, gentle handling. A calm, respectful demeanor won their regard; a rare, sharp jest could even delight them. But long mental labor, heavy demands on memory or attention—they rejected outright. While an English girl of average ability might quietly accept a difficult task, a Labassecourienne would laugh, throw it back, and declare: “Heavens, how hard! I don’t want it. It bores me too much.”

A wise teacher would take the assignment back without argument, carefully simplify it, return it with clear explanation—and then apply sarcasm with boldness. They would wince, perhaps, but they did not resent such mockery—provided it was not sour, but hearty, spoken plainly so all could read it: a reflection of their own ignorance, laziness, weakness. They’d protest over extra lines of homework, but never over a wound to their pride—little as they possessed, it had been trained to submit, and even welcomed the firm pressure of a strong hand.

As I grew more fluent in their language, able to wield its sharper idioms with skill, the older, brighter girls began to respond to me—not with affection, exactly, but with a certain respect. I noticed that whenever one of them felt a stir of honest shame or worthy ambition, she was already half-conquered. If I could make her ears burn under her thick hair, all was well. Soon, bouquets began appearing on my desk each morning. In return, I sometimes walked with a few favored students during breaks.

During these conversations, I once ventured—without planning—to correct some of their twisted notions of morality. Especially, I spoke about the wrong of lying. In a moment of candor, I added that between falsehood and missing church occasionally, I saw the lie as worse. The girls were taught to report everything I said to their priests. An unexpected consequence followed.

Something invisible, unnamed, slipped between me and my most attentive pupils. The flowers kept coming, but conversation ceased. Whenever I walked the paths or sat in the garden bench, a student would approach my right side—only for a teacher to appear instantly at my left. And Madame Beck, silent as a shadow, would materialize behind me, swift and sudden as a breeze.

Once, a pupil who had received small kindnesss from me leaned close and said: “Mademoiselle, what a pity you are a Protestant!”

“Why?” I asked.

“Because when you die—you’ll burn straight into Hell.”

“Do you believe that?”

“Certainly. Everyone knows it. Besides, the priest told me.”

Isabelle was blunt, odd, childlike. Then, in a whisper: “To save your soul up there, it would be better to burn alive down here.”

I laughed—it was impossible not to.

Have you forgotten Miss Ginevra Fanshawe? If so, allow me to reintroduce her—now a thriving pupil at Madame Beck’s school. She arrived in the Rue Fossette just days after my own arrival, meeting me with no great surprise. There was something remarkable in her—she moved through life with the effortless grace of a duchess, utterly unconcerned, completely nonchalant. Her emotions were thin, fleeting—her likes and dislikes spun from gossamer. Yet one thing was solid, enduring: her selfishness.

She was not proud, and—being as good-natured as I was—she quickly made me her confidante. She teased me endlessly with complaints: the salt fish on Fridays, the hard eggs, the soup, the bread, the coffee—all beneath her taste. She called the staff despicable, simply because they were foreigners. For a while, I endured her criticisms with patience, but eventually, tired of repetition, I snapped back—something I should have done earlier. A firm reprimand always suited her.

Even longer I suffered her demands for help with sewing. Her wardrobe was elegant, well-stocked with fine clothes—but not with undergarments or stockings, which she neglected. She hated needlework, so she dumped heaps of hose and linen onto my desk, expecting me to mend them. When it became clear this would become unbearable, I finally told her plainly: she must mend her own things.

She wept, accused me of abandoning our friendship. But I stood firm, letting her cry out her tantrum.

Despite her flaws—many, and none particularly noble—how lovely she was! On a sunny Sunday morning, dressed in pale lilac silk, her golden curls falling over her white shoulders, she looked radiant. Sundays were holidays spent with friends in town. Among these friends, she soon hinted, was one who wished to be more than friend.

By glances and whispers, by the glow in her eyes and the lift of her step, it became clear: she was being courted—perhaps loved—with deep passion. She called him “Isidore.” Not his real name, she said—just a title she chose for him, since his actual name was “not very pretty.” Once, when boasting of his devotion, I asked if she returned his love.

“Comme cela,” she replied. “He’s handsome, and he loves me madly—so I’m amused. That’s enough.”

Seeing she persisted longer than I expected—given her fickle nature—I decided to inquire further. Was this man someone her parents, especially her uncle—who controlled her fortune—would approve?

She admitted it was doubtful. “Isidore” had little money.

“Do you encourage him?” I asked.

“Furiously, sometimes,” she answered.

“Without knowing whether you’ll ever be allowed to marry him?”

“Oh, how dull you are! I don’t want to marry. I’m too young.”

“But if he loves you as much as you say, and nothing comes of it—he’ll suffer.”

“Of course he’ll break his heart. I’d be shocked if he didn’t.”

“I wonder if M. Isidore is a fool?” I said.

“He is, about me. But he’s clever otherwise—everyone says so. Mrs. Cholmondeley thinks he’ll rise through talent. All I know is, he sighs near me, and I can twist him round my finger.”

Wanting a clearer picture of this devoted M. Isidore—whose position seemed fragile—I asked her to describe him. But she couldn’t. She lacked words, or the skill to shape them into vivid images. She hadn’t truly observed him—nothing of his face, his expression, stayed with her. All she could say was: “He’s handsome, but more a gentleman than a boy.”

My patience often failed, my interest flagged—yet one thing held me: every hint, every detail she dropped, revealed, unconsciously, that M. Isidore treated her with care, dignity, restraint. I told her plainly that I believed him far too good for her—and that she was merely a vain flirt. She laughed, tossed her curls, and danced away as if I’d paid her a compliment.

Miss Ginevra’s studies were superficial. She took music, singing, dancing seriously—also embroidering fine cambric handkerchiefs she couldn’t afford to buy ready-made. History, geography, grammar, arithmetic? Left undone—or done by others. Much of her time was spent visiting. Madame Beck, aware her stay was limited and would not be extended regardless of progress, allowed her great freedom.

Mrs. Cholmondeley—her chaperone—a fashionable, lively woman—invited her frequently, and sometimes took her to evening parties. Ginevra approved this arrangement—except for one flaw: she had to dress well, yet lacked funds for varied attire. This problem consumed her thoughts. Her usually idle mind became fiercely active, driven by necessity and vanity.

She begged boldly of Mrs. Cholmondeley—not with shame, but cheerfully:
“My darling Mrs. C., I have nothing fit to wear for your party next week. You _must_ give me a book-muslin dress and a sky-blue sash. Do—there’s an angel! Will you?”

At first, Mrs. Cholmondeley gave in. But as requests multiplied, she resisted—like all of Ginevra’s friends. Soon, no more gifts came. Yet visits continued, and dresses were still supplied—along with gloves, bouquets, even jewelry. These, unlike her usual behavior—since she was not secretive—were hidden for a time.

Then, one evening, before a grand ball, she burst into my room, eager to show off her splendor.

She looked stunning—so young, so fresh, with a delicacy of skin and grace of form distinctly English, rare among continental women. Her dress was new, expensive, perfect. Every detail was flawless—costly finishes that elevated the whole effect.

I studied her from head to toe. She turned playfully, showing me every angle. Confident in her beauty, she was in high spirits—her small blue eyes sparkling.

She reached for a kiss, in her girlish way. But I stepped back.
“Steady,” I said. “Let us be steady. Let’s understand what this means.”

“Shall I do?” she asked.

“Do?” I repeated. “There are many ways to ‘do.’ And frankly, I don’t understand yours.”

“But how do I look?”

“You look well-dressed.”

She thought that praise too cold. She pointed to her jewels.
“Look at this _parure_—the brooch, earrings, bracelets. No one in the school has such a set—not even Madame.”

“I see them,” I said. (Pause.) “Did M. de Bassompierre give you these?”

“My uncle knows nothing about them.”

“Mrs. Cholmondeley?”

“Not a chance. She’s stingy. She gives me nothing now.”

I turned away.

“Now, old Crusty—old Diogenes,” she said, using her pet names for me when we clashed. “What’s wrong now?”

“Go away. I don’t enjoy looking at you or your trinkets.”

She hesitated.

“What now, Mother Wisdom? I haven’t gone into debt. Not for the jewels, nor the gloves, nor the bouquet. My dress isn’t paid for—but Uncle de Bassompierre will cover it. He never checks the items, just the total. And he’s rich. Who cares about a few guineas?”

“Will you leave? I want to shut the door… Ginevra, people may tell you you’re beautiful in that gown—but to me, you’ve never looked lovelier than in the plain gingham dress and straw bonnet I first saw you in.”

“Others don’t have your Puritan tastes,” she snapped. “And why do you preach to me?”

“Because I have little right—but you have even less to come strutting into my room like a jay in borrowed feathers. I respect neither your plumes, nor especially those peacock eyes you call a _parure_. Beautiful, yes—if you bought them with your own money. But not now.”

“On est là pour Mademoiselle Fanshawe!” announced the porter. And she skipped off.

The mystery of the _parure_ remained until a few days later, when she returned to confess.

“You needn’t be angry with me,” she began. “I’m not running Papa or Uncle deeply into debt. Everything is paid—except the recent dresses. The rest is settled.”

Ah, there was the clue—since Mrs. Cholmondeley hadn’t given them, and Ginevra’s own funds were slight.

“Ecoutez!” she whispered, drawing closer. “I’ll tell you everything. You’ll see it’s not only right, but clever. First, I _must_ go out. Papa said I should see the world. He told Mrs. Cholmondeley I had a bit of a schoolgirl air—‘bread-and-butter’ he called it—and wanted me to lose it before my debut in England. So I must go out. I must dress. Mrs. Cholmondeley is stingy. It would be unfair to burden Uncle with all my needs. That’s fair, isn’t it? That agrees with your sermons.”

“Well, then,” I said, “someone heard me complaining to Mrs. Cholmondeley about needing ornaments—someone who, instead of refusing, was delighted to offer a gift. You should have seen him—how shy, how nervous, how trembling with fear of rejection!”

“That’s enough, Miss Fanshawe. I suppose I’m to understand that M. Isidore is your benefactor—that he gave you this costly _parure_, the gloves, the bouquets?”

“You speak so unpleasantly,” she said. “But yes—I allow Isidore the pleasure of expressing his admiration by giving me a small token.”

“It’s the same thing. Now, Ginevra, I don’t understand these matters well—but I believe you’re doing something very wrong. Perhaps you think you’ll marry him? Have your parents agreed? Do you love him truly?”

“Mais pas du tout!” (She always switched to French when speaking something cruel or defiant.) “Je suis sa reine, mais il n’est pas mon roi.”

“Excuse me,” I said, “but I think this is mere coquetry. You’re not great—but you’re above taking advantage of a man’s generosity when you feel nothing for him. You love M. Isidore more than you admit.”

“No,” she said. “I danced with a young officer the other night whom I love a thousand times more. I often wonder why I feel so cold toward Isidore. Everyone says he’s handsome. Other women admire him. But somehow—he bores me. Let me think…”

I encouraged her.

“Yes,” I said. “Try to understand yourself. To me, it seems chaotic—like a rag-bag.”

“Like this,” she said suddenly: “he’s too romantic, too devoted. He expects more from me than I’m willing to give. He thinks I’m perfect—full of virtues I’ve never had, nor intend to have. And in his presence, I find myself trying to live up to his idea of me. It tires me to pretend to be sensible, to be good. He really believes I am sensible. But with you, dear crosspatch—you take me as I am, flawed, silly, selfish, frivolous. You know me, and that makes it easier.”

“This is all well,” I said, struggling to keep my tone grave, “but it doesn’t change the fact that you’re accepting gifts. Pack them up, Ginevra, and send them back.”

“No,” she said firmly.

“Then you’re deceiving him. By accepting his presents, you imply he’ll one day receive something in return—your love.”

“But he won’t,” she interrupted. “He has his reward now—the joy of seeing me wear them. That’s enough for him. He’s only bourgeois.”

That phrase—arrogant, meaningless—shook me from my momentary softness. I hardened again.

She went on:
“Besides, he doesn’t expect marriage. He knows I’m not serious. He’s content with adoration. And I’m content with admiration. It’s a fair exchange.”

Madame Beck was a woman of unshakable composure—gentle with the world, yet untouched by affection for any part of it. Her own children did not stir her from the quiet discipline of her nature. She cared deeply for their welfare, watched over their health and future with vigilant attention, but never once did she show the warmth that might have softened her sternness. I’ve seen her seated in the garden, watching Trinette walk the little ones along a distant path, her face calm, thoughtful, composed. She often pondered what she called “leur avenir”—their future—but when the youngest, a frail and charming child, spotted her and broke free from the nurse, toddling eagerly toward her with laughter and breathless excitement, Madame would simply extend one hand to stop the sudden rush. “Prends garde, mon enfant!” she’d say, without a trace of emotion. The child would stand near her for a moment, then be led back to Trinette—no smile, no embrace, no tender word.

Her treatment of the eldest daughter, Désirée, was equally revealing, though in a different way. This girl was wilful, sharp-tongued, and cruelly clever at provoking others. “Quelle peste que cette Désirée! Quel poison que cet enfant là!” she was called alike in kitchen and classroom. She delighted in mischief—sneaking into servants’ rooms, tearing caps, soiling shawls, smashing porcelain in the dining room or raiding the storeroom for preserves and sweet wine, always leaving behind clues that pointed to the staff. When Madame learned of these acts, her only response was: “Désirée a besoin d’une surveillance toute particulière.” And so she kept her close, watching her constantly—not because she sought to correct her, but because she lacked the courage to confront her openly. She never explained the harm in such behavior, nor warned of its consequences. Instead, she relied on silence and scrutiny.

But this strategy failed. Désirée turned her attentions from the servants to her mother. Anything belonging to Madame’s dressing table or work desk that she could reach, she stole and hid. Madame saw it all, yet pretended not to. She had no strength of spirit to face her daughter’s faults directly. When something valuable disappeared, she would pretend she thought Désirée had taken it playfully, and ask her to return it. But Désirée had long since learned to lie as easily as she stole. She would deny having touched the brooch, the ring, or the scissors. Then Madame would feign belief, quietly watch her every move until she discovered where the stolen items were hidden—a crevice in the garden wall, a corner of the attic, an outbuilding. Once found, Madame would send Désirée out with Trinette, and while she was gone, retrieve the things herself.

Désirée, true to her cunning parentage, showed no shame when confronted with her loss. She remained perfectly unruffled.

The second child, Fifine, was said to resemble her father, who had died young. Though she carried her mother’s strong frame, blue eyes, and rosy cheeks, her spirit came from elsewhere. She was honest, joyful, impulsive—warm-hearted and energetic, though prone to accidents. One day, she tumbled headlong down a flight of stone steps. Madame, hearing the crash (she heard everything), hurried from the dining room, picked her up, and said calmly, “Cet enfant a un os de cassé.”

At first we hoped it was not serious. But it was. One arm hung limp and lifeless. “Let Meess take her,” Madame said, “et qu’on aille tout de suite chercher un fiacre.” Without hesitation, she left the house, cool and composed, to find a surgeon. The family doctor wasn’t home, but that didn’t matter. She searched until she found someone suitable, brought him back with her.

By then, I had already cut off Fifine’s sleeve, undressed her, and laid her gently in bed. We all gathered around—Trinette, the cook, the portress, and myself—watching anxiously as the new doctor entered. I focused on calming Fifine, whose cries, loud and desperate, filled the small, overheated room. They grew louder still as the stranger approached her bed.

“Let alone!” she cried fiercely in broken English. “I will not you—I want Dr. Pillule!”

“And Dr. Pillule is my very good friend,” replied the man in fluent English, “but he is busy three leagues away. So now, when we’re a little calmer, we’ll begin. Soon your arm will be bandaged and mended.”

He called for a glass of *eau sucrée*, gave her a few spoonfuls—Fifine loved sweets, and anyone could win her favor through food—and promised more after the procedure. Then he began his work.

He needed help, and asked the cook, a strong, sturdy woman, to assist. But she, along with the portress and nurse, fled. I hesitated to touch the child’s injured limb—my hands trembled—but before I could act, Madame stepped forward. Her hand was steady, mine shook.

“Ca vaudra mieux,” the doctor said, turning from me to her.

He made the right choice. My stoicism would have been forced; hers, genuine. When he finished, he nodded. “Merci, Madame; très bien, fort bien!” He admired her composure. She, in turn, appreciated his praise.

And perhaps it was not just her strength that impressed him. As the evening deepened and a lamp was lit, I noticed that unless Madame Beck were less than a woman, she could not help being drawn to him. This young doctor—though young—had a presence unlike any other. He stood tall in the cramped chamber, towering over the Dutch-made women around him. His profile was sharp and noble, his eyes quick and bright, though they darted too swiftly from face to face. Yet there was a kindness in them, and in his mouth, a charm. His chin was full, well-formed, almost Grecian. And his smile—oh, his smile—was hard to define. It pleased, yes, but also stirred something deeper, something private and unsettling, like a mirror held up to one’s own hidden flaws.

Yet Fifine liked it. Despite the pain, she reached out her hand at the end, saying goodnight with a friendly wave. He patted her hand gently, and then he and Madame descended together, talking animatedly, she in high spirits, he listening with a mild, amused air—just a hint of playful mischief beneath the surface.

I noticed that though he spoke French well, his English was flawless. His complexion, eyes, and build were distinctly English. But more than that—when he passed me on his way out, turning briefly to speak to Madame, I caught sight of him clearly. A memory surged, sudden and complete. This was the same man I had spoken to at the bureau—the one who helped me with my trunk, who guided me through the dark, rain-soaked park. And now, as he walked down the long vestibule, I recognized his step—the same firm, even stride I had followed under the dripping trees.

It seemed likely that this visit would be his last. Dr. Pillule was expected home the next day, so there was no reason for the substitute to return. But fate had other plans. Dr. Pillule had been called to attend a wealthy, anxious old man in the ancient university town of Bouquin-Moisi. After prescribing travel and change of air, he was asked to accompany the patient on a journey lasting several weeks. Thus, the young doctor remained.

I saw him often. Madame refused to leave Fifine in Trinette’s care; she insisted I spend much time in the nursery. I believed he was skilled. Fifine recovered quickly under his care. But even as she healed, he stayed. Destiny and Madame Beck seemed aligned—both determined he should become familiar with every corner of the Rue Fossette.

No sooner had Fifine left his care than Désirée claimed to be ill. The girl had a gift for pretense. Captivated by the attention and indulgence of sickness, she decided illness suited her perfectly and took to her bed. She performed admirably, and Madame played her part with equal skill—though she saw through the deception completely, she treated it with solemn gravity and false concern.

What surprised me most was that Dr. John—so named by Fifine, and soon adopted by all of us—agreed to go along with the charade. At first, he looked uncertain, glanced between mother and daughter, paused as if weighing the truth. But then he smiled faintly, shrugged, and joined in the farce.

Désirée ate heartily, jumped about in bed, built tents from sheets, lounged among pillows, threw shoes at her nurse, mocked her sisters—overflowing with health and mischief. She only appeared weak when her mother and doctor arrived.

I knew Madame was glad to have Désirée out of the way, but I wondered why Dr. John continued. Every day, he came punctually, received the same warm welcome, the same exaggerated concern from Madame. He wrote harmless prescriptions and watched her with a sharp, amused eye. She met his glances without anger—she had too much sense for that.

Though flexible in manner, he was not subservient. He enjoyed his role at the pensionnat, lingered strangely around the Rue Fossette, yet carried himself with independence. Still, he often seemed lost in thought, distant.

It was not my place to question his demeanor, yet I could not help observing. He paid me little notice—only the kind of attention one gives to a chair or a carpet, something unremarkable. Often, while waiting for Madame, he would gaze out the window, smile to himself, listen, as if believing he were alone. And I, meanwhile, studied him—his face, his movements, the peculiar blend of interest, attachment, and mystery that bound him to this secluded household.

He never seemed aware that I observed him. Nor would he have known, had it not been for one afternoon, when I sat near him, studying the golden hue of his hair, his beard, his skin—the way light caught him, making him glow like a statue from legend. I compared him silently to Nebuchadnezzar’s golden image, shining under the sun.

Then, suddenly, a new idea struck me—sharp, vivid, undeniable. I don’t know how I looked at him. Surprise and certainty overwhelmed me. I forgot myself entirely—until I saw his reflection in the oval mirror beside the window, catching my movement.

Though naturally cheerful, he was sensitive to direct gazes. Startled, he turned and said, his tone polite but dry, edged with mild rebuke: “Mademoiselle does not spare me. I am not vain enough to believe it is my merits that draw her attention. It must be some defect. Dare I ask—what?”

I was stunned—yet not utterly undone. I had not stared from admiration or curiosity, but from a quiet, unspoken awareness. I could have defended myself, but chose not to. I remained silent. I rarely spoke to him. Let him think what he wished. I returned to my sewing, keeping my head bent low for the rest of his visit.

There is a strange comfort in being misunderstood—especially when truth cannot be known. In places where one is never truly seen, it is oddly satisfying to be ignored completely. Who among us has not, when mistaken for a thief, felt a flicker of amusement rather than outrage?

It was summer, and the heat pressed down like a weight upon the quiet streets of Villette. The air hung thick and still, as though the world itself held its breath. In this season of languor and feverishness, Georgette, the youngest of Madame Beck’s children, fell ill—her temperature rising swiftly, her small frame trembling with the first grip of sickness. Désirée, who had lately recovered from her own ailments, was sent off with Fifine to Bonne-Maman in the countryside, not out of affection, but as a precaution against contagion. The school now stood at the edge of concern. Medical care was no longer a luxury—it was necessary.

Madame Beck, ever practical and unyielding in her decisions, chose to ignore the return of Dr. Pillule, who had been absent for only a week. Instead, she summoned Dr. John, the English physician, to continue his visits. It was an unexpected move. I had thought, with some satisfaction, that such a decision would be reversed: surely, a woman of Madame Beck’s discernment would never allow a young man—especially one so charming and unguarded—to attend upon her pupils? Yet Madame Beck, prudent as she was, could also be boldly daring. She did not hesitate. She brought Dr. John into the very heart of the school, assigning him to care for Blanche de Melcy—proud, graceful, and full of pride—and Angélique, her friend, vain and quick with laughter.

I watched Dr. John closely. There was a certain warmth in his manner, a subtle glow beneath his composed exterior, as though he took genuine pleasure in being trusted. If discretion had allowed it, he might have justified his presence without reproach. But here, in a land where convents rose like silent sentinels and confessionals whispered secrets between stone walls, such a presence could not go unnoticed. The school buzzed with talk. The kitchen murmured. The town caught wind of it. Parents wrote letters, came in person, demanded explanations.

Had Madame Beck been weak, she would have fallen. A dozen rival schools stood ready to pounce, eager to exploit any misstep—any lapse of propriety—as a reason to discredit her. But Madame Beck was not weak. Though she might have been little more than a Jesuit in miniature, I found myself clapping the hands of my heart, and with them, crying aloud in silence: *Brava!* For she met every challenge with a grace that was both effortless and commanding. No one matched her in that peculiar charm—what I can only call the “roundness and frankness of a good woman”—a quality that won hearts where stern logic failed.

“Ce pauvre Docteur Jean!” she would say, chuckling softly, rubbing her plump white hands together as though warming herself by a fire of affection. “Ce cher jeune homme! The best creature in the world!” And then she would explain, with all the ease of truth, how she had come to employ him—for her own children, who adored him so completely they would scream themselves into fits at the thought of another doctor. “Where I trust for my own,” she’d add, “it is natural to extend that trust to others. Besides,” she’d conclude with a wave of her hand, “it’s only temporary. Blanche and Angélique have the migraine. Dr. John wrote a prescription. Voilà tout!”

The parents were silenced. Blanche and Angélique, ever eager to please, sang duets in praise of their physician. The other girls followed suit, declaring unanimously that when they were ill, they would want no one but Dr. John. Madame Beck laughed, and so did the parents. The Labassecouriens, it seemed, possessed a vast organ of philoprogenitiveness—the love of offspring—carried to extremes. In most homes, the will of the child ruled supreme. And so, instead of being condemned, Madame Beck was praised. People said she had acted with maternal indulgence. She emerged from the crisis not diminished, but strengthened. Liked more than ever.

Yet even now, I could not fully understand why she had taken such a risk—why she had chosen to defend Dr. John, to keep him close, when it might have cost her everything. What people said, I knew well enough: the entire household—pupils, teachers, servants alike—whispered that she was going to marry him. They had decided it, as if it were already written in fate. Age made no difference to them; it would be so. And indeed, appearances offered no strong argument against such a belief. Madame Beck seemed determined to keep him near. She had cast aside her former protégé, Dr. Pillule, without regret. She received Dr. John personally, always with cheerfulness, with a lightness that belied her years. She began to pay attention to her appearance—no longer dressing in morning dishabille or nightcaps and shawls. When he came early, she greeted him with braids neatly arranged, a silk dress fitted perfectly, brodequins laced with precision. Her whole attire was fresh as a flower, complete as a model.

But I do not believe her intention went beyond showing a handsome man that she was not entirely plain. And plain she was not. Though lacking in classical beauty or elegance of form, she pleased. Without youth’s bright graces, she still stirred warmth. One never tired of seeing her. She was never dull, never flat, never colorless. Her hair, unfaded, her eyes with their gentle blue light, her cheeks with their healthy bloom—all these things pleased not in flashes, but steadily, constantly.

Did she dream of taking Dr. John into her home, endowing him with her savings—said to amount to a modest fortune—and making him comfortable for life? Did Dr. John suspect such dreams? I saw him once emerging from her presence, a mischievous half-smile playing on his lips, a gleam of masculine vanity in his eyes—pleased, perhaps, with the idea of being admired, even courted.

He was not perfect. With all his good looks and kindness, he must have known he was not meant to succeed in matters of the heart if he encouraged hopes he did not intend to fulfill. But did he truly mean to fail? People said he had no money, that he lived entirely by his profession. And Madame Beck—though fourteen years his senior—was a woman who never seemed to age, never to fade. They were certainly on good terms. Perhaps he was not in love. But how many people are ever in love, or marry for love, in this world?

We waited for the outcome. For what he waited, I do not know. Nor for what he watched. But there was something about his manner—expectant, vigilant, absorbed—that never faded. It grew stronger, deeper. He had always been just beyond my understanding, and now he slipped farther away.

One morning, little Georgette was worse—feverish, restless, crying inconsolably. I suspected the medicine prescribed might disagree with her, and I hesitated to give it again. I waited anxiously for Dr. John’s arrival, eager to consult him. The bell rang. He entered. I heard his voice speaking to the portress. As usual, he ascended quickly, three steps at a time, appearing suddenly like a cheerful surprise.

Five minutes passed. Ten. Still no sign of him. Where could he be? Perhaps waiting below. But Georgette continued to cry, calling me by my familiar name: “Minnie, Minnie, me very poorly!” My heart ached. I descended to find him.

The corridor was empty. Where had he gone? Could he be with Madame in the dining room? Impossible—I had left her moments before, dressing in her own chamber. I listened.

Three pupils practiced in nearby rooms—the dining-room, the larger drawing-room, the smaller. Between them and the corridor lay only the portress’s cabinet, originally intended as a boudoir. Farther off, in the oratory, a class of over a dozen sang a *barcarolle*, their voices weaving through the air: “fraîchë,” “brisë,” “Venisë.” Amidst this symphony of sound, what could I hear?

A sudden, giddy laugh—a treble, almost girlish—came from the portress’s cabinet. Close by, the door stood half-open. Then a man’s voice, low and pleading: “For God’s sake!” A pause. Then, Dr. John emerged—his eyes bright, yet not with joy or triumph. His fair cheek was flushed. On his brow, a look of torment, of anxiety, yet beneath it, something tender, wounded.

The open door shielded me. Had I been in his path, I believe he would have walked past without seeing me. Some deep sorrow gripped him—not pride wounded, but affection hurt—deeply, cruelly hurt.

Who had done this? Who in that house held such power over him? Madame Beck, I believed, was in her chamber. The room he had just left belonged to the portress—Rosine Matou. A pretty, frivolous French girl—airy, fickle, vain, and mercenary. Surely, it was not *she* who had caused his pain?

But as I pondered, her voice broke through the door, clear and sharp, singing a light French tune. I glanced in—doubting my senses.

There she sat at the table, dressed in a smart pink *jaconas*, trimming a tiny blond cap. Nothing else moved in the room—except goldfish in a glass globe, flowers in pots, and a broad beam of July sun. A puzzle. But I must go upstairs.

Dr. John sat beside Georgette’s crib. Madame Beck stood before him. The child had been examined, comforted, and now slept quietly. As I entered, Madame was discussing the doctor’s health, noting changes in his appearance, warning him of overwork, urging rest and a change of air.

He listened, smiling, indifferent. “You’re *trop bonne*, Madame,” he said. “I feel perfectly well.”

She turned to me. “What do you think, Miss Lucie? Is he not paler? Thinner?”

I rarely spoke in Dr. John’s presence. He saw me as neutral, passive—something to be overlooked. But now, I answered—deliberately, clearly: “He looks ill now—but perhaps it’s only temporary. Dr. John may have been vexed or troubled.”

I did not see his face. I cannot say how he took it.

Georgette asked me, in broken English, for a glass of sweetened water. I replied in English. For the first time, I think, Dr. John noticed. Until now, he had assumed me foreign, addressing me as “Mademoiselle,” giving instructions in French. He seemed about to speak—then stopped himself.

Madame Beck resumed her advice. He shook his head, laughed, rose, and bid her good morning—with courtesy, yet with the careless air of one overwhelmed by too much attention.

When he was gone, Madame sank into the chair he had occupied. She rested her chin in her hand. All warmth vanished from her face. She looked stony, stern—almost bitter. She sighed. Once. Deeply.

The morning bell rang. She rose. Passing a dressing-table with a mirror, she glanced at her reflection. One single white hair streaked her nut-brown hair. She plucked it out, shuddering.

In the full daylight, her face, though still rosy, showed signs of aging. Youth’s contours were gone. Ah, Madame! Wise as you were, even you knew weakness. Never had I pitied you before. But now, as you turned away from the glass, my heart softened.

A calamity had struck. Disappointment, that old hag, stood before you with her grisly greeting—and your soul refused to embrace her.

And Rosine—my bewilderment knew no bounds. That day, I passed her cabinet five times, hoping to glimpse the secret of her charm. She was pretty, young, well-dressed. All admirable qualities—enough, perhaps, to explain a young man’s distress. Still, I found myself wishing—half-wishing—that Dr. John were my brother, or that he had a sister or mother who might gently reprove him. But I broke that wish—flung it away—realizing its folly. “Someone,” I thought, “might as well sermonize Madame about her young physician. And what good would that do?”

I believe Madame sermonized herself. She did not falter. She did not make a spectacle of herself. She had no tender feelings to crush, no great passions to betray. She had work—real, demanding work—to occupy her mind. And she had sense—true, genuine sense, rare in men and women alike. By these gifts, she carried herself with wisdom, with dignity. Brava! once more, Madame Beck. You faced a monster of desire—an Apollyon of longing—and you fought bravely. You conquered.

Behind the house on Rue Fossette lay a garden—large for a city, and to my memory now it seems serene and lovely. Time, like distance, softens certain places; and when all around is stone, blank walls, and sun-baked pavement, how precious becomes a single bush, how enchanting an enclosed patch of earth where green things grow and flowers bloom.

There was a whisper among the neighbors that Madame Beck’s home had once been a convent. Long before the city crept into this quarter, when fields and avenues stretched far and wide, and deep shade wrapped the land as it should embrace a holy house, something terrible had happened here—something that stirred fear and left behind a tale of ghosts. A legend lingered: of a nun in black and white, seen sometimes on certain nights, wandering through the gardens. Though the ghost must have faded centuries ago—houses now rose all around—certain relics remained: ancient fruit trees, giants of the orchard, still rooted in the soil like living monuments. At the base of one—a pear tree so old it seemed to have outlived time itself, its trunk mostly dead but a few branches still bearing spring blossoms and autumn fruit—you could scrape away the mossy earth and glimpse beneath the roots a slab of smooth, hard, black stone. It was said, though never proven, that this was the cover of a vault beneath, where a young girl had once been buried alive by a cruel monastic order for breaking her vow. Her spirit, they whispered, haunted the place long after her body turned to dust; her shadow, her dark robe and white veil, flickered through moonlight and mist, visible only to those with trembling eyes.

But beyond such romantic tales, the garden held real charm. On summer mornings, I rose early to walk alone, savoring the quiet. In the evenings, I lingered, waiting for the moon to rise or catching a breath of the cool night air, imagining the dew falling like a gentle kiss. The grass was thick and green, the gravel paths bright and clean. Sunlit nasturtiums clung to the roots of the ancient trees like bursts of flame. There was a large arbor shaded by an acacia, and a smaller, more hidden bower nestled in vines that wound along a high gray wall, their tendrils weaving a crown above, their clusters hanging in abundance where jasmine and ivy met and entwined.

No doubt, at midday, when Madame Beck’s school emptied and pupils and boarders scattered like birds, the garden became crowded and noisy—noisy enough to drown even the bells of St. Jean Baptiste. But at dusk, when the externes had gone home and the boarders sat quietly at their studies, it transformed into a sanctuary. Then it was peaceful, hushed, almost sacred. I often walked there in the evening, drawn by the stillness, the mellow air, the scent of flowers breathing softly under the fading light.

One such evening, I stayed later than usual. The twilight deepened, the air cooled, and the blooms, no longer touched by sun, seemed to exhale their fragrance in reply to the dew descending from the sky. A light glowed in the oratory window—Madame Beck’s household was gathered for evening prayer. As a Protestant, I sometimes chose not to attend. “Just a little longer,” solitude murmured, and the pale moon overhead whispered, “stay—everything is calm now. No one will miss you. You’ve been worn down by the day’s heat and noise. Savor these moments.”

The garden’s backside was bordered by the rear of a row of buildings—the boarding houses of the neighboring college. These were plain stone, bare except for a few attic windows high up, belonging to servants’ rooms, and one lower casement said to belong to a master. Yet despite this, one narrow alley ran parallel to the high wall on that side—and it was forbidden to the girls. Called *l’allée défendue*, any pupil who entered would face the strictest punishment allowed by Madame Beck’s otherwise mild rules. Teachers might pass freely, but the path was narrow, overgrown with thick shrubs, their branches forming a canopy that let only scattered shafts of sunlight through. Even during daylight, few ventured there; after dark, it was avoided entirely.

From the beginning, I found myself drawn to it. Its seclusion, its dimness, intrigued me. At first, fear of seeming odd kept me away. But slowly, as people grew accustomed to me and my quiet ways—my habits, my subtle differences, which were neither striking nor offensive, but simply part of who I was—I began to visit the path regularly. I tended some colorless flowers growing between the shrubs, cleared away last autumn’s leaves that choked a wooden seat at the end, and cleaned it with a pail of water and brush borrowed from Goton, the cook. Madame saw me and smiled—whether truly or not, I couldn’t say—but she seemed sincere. “Look,” she said, “how tidy Miss Lucy is! You like this path, then?” “Yes,” I replied, “it’s quiet and shady.” “That’s right,” she answered kindly, and added that since I wasn’t responsible for supervision, I could stay there as much as I liked. She even suggested I allow the girls to come and speak English with me.

On that night, I sat on the seat I’d restored, hidden from view, listening to what sounded like distant city noises. They weren’t distant at all—this school stood in the heart of the city. Five minutes brought you to the park, ten to palatial homes. Wide streets nearby sparkled with lights, filled with carriages heading to balls or opera. While our dormitory rang with curfew and silence, the world outside celebrated. But I felt none of that joy. My nature lacked such instincts. I had never seen a ball or opera, though I’d heard them described. Not with longing—to reach them, to shine in them—but merely with curiosity. I wanted only to see, not to participate.

Above me, a young crescent moon hung low in the sky. Through a gap in the branches, I caught sight of it, and beside it, stars I knew from childhood. That same golden curve, with its dark sphere, had once leaned against an old thorn atop a field in England. Now it leaned beside a spire in this foreign capital. Oh, my childhood! I had feelings—even if I lived quietly, spoke little, appeared cold. When I thought of the past, I *could* feel. But the present? Better to be stoic. And the future—mine, especially—better to be numb. So I guarded the pulse of my soul carefully, keeping it still.

I remembered how, once, a thunderstorm broke with fury. A hurricane shook the building. The Catholic girls panicked, praying aloud. I, however, was seized by the storm. It forced me awake, roused me violently. I dressed, slipped out the casement near my bed, and sat on the ledge, feet resting on the roof below. It was wet, wild, pitch-black. Inside, the dormitory trembled with prayers. I couldn’t return. The storm held me—its thunder roared like a hymn no human voice could sing. Clouds split open, lightning blinding. For twenty-four hours afterward, I ached for escape—for something to lift me from my life and carry me forward. That ache, I had to crush. Like Jael driving a nail through Sisera’s temples, I silenced it. Unlike Sisera, it didn’t die—it only stunned. And sometimes, it woke again, wrenching against the nail, making my head bleed, my nerves thrum.

But tonight, I was not restless. My inner turmoil slept. An angel-like presence hovered near—perhaps just the ideal, the dream—pouring balm on my temples, holding a mirror before my closed eyes, showing visions that repeated themselves in dreams. A soft glow from her wings and robe spread over me, over the tent, over the landscape. Jael, the stern woman, sat apart, softened slightly by her captive, yet thinking more of Heber’s return.

By which I mean: the cool night, the sweet dew, filled me with quiet hope—not for anything specific, but a general sense of peace, of ease. Such a mood, so rare and sweet, ought to have heralded good things. Alas, no good came.

Then, abruptly, the harsh reality intruded—ugly, crude, as it so often does. Amid the silence of the stone buildings, trees, and high wall, I heard a sound. A casement creaked. Before I could look up, a tree above me shook—as if struck. Something fell at my feet.

Nine o’clock chimed from St. Jean Baptiste’s clock. Day was fading, but it wasn’t dark. The crescent moon offered little light, but the lingering gold of sunset and the clear sky sustained the twilight. Even in my shadowed path, I could read small print if I approached an opening.

I saw at once what had fallen: a box—small, made of ivory, white and tinted. The lid opened easily. Inside, violets lay piled over a folded pink note, addressed: *Pour la robe grise*. I wore a dress of French grey. Good.

Was this a love letter? A *billet-doux*? I’d heard of such things, but never held one. Was this it?

Not likely. I didn’t believe it for a moment. I had no admirer, no suitor. The other teachers dreamed of lovers—one even believed in a future husband. The older girls knew of suitors; two or three were already betrothed. But I had never stepped into that realm. When others went to church or strolled the boulevards, they always claimed to meet someone of the opposite sex whose gaze told them they were desirable. I never experienced that. I walked, I prayed, I attended mass—but no one seemed to notice me.

Everyone else in Rue Fossette had received a glance from the young doctor’s blue eyes at some point. But not me. His eyes, I must admit, were guiltless, calm as the sky they resembled.

So while I listened to their stories, I wondered at their confidence, their joy—but never thought to look ahead myself.

This, then, was no love letter. I opened it calmly, convinced it wasn’t.

It read—translated:

“Angel of my dreams! A thousand thanks for keeping your promise. I scarcely dared hope you would. I thought you half-joking, and you seemed to think the plan too dangerous—the hour too late, the path too secluded, so often haunted by that dragon, the English teacher—*une véritable bégueule Britannique*, as you say—*espèce de monstre, brusque et rude comme un vieux caporal de grenadiers, et revêche comme une religieuse*” (the reader may forgive my modesty in allowing this flattering sketch of myself to remain in the original).

“You know,” it continued, “that little Gustave has been moved to a master’s room—that favored chamber whose lattice overlooks your prison-ground. I, the best uncle in the world, am allowed to visit him. How I trembled approaching the window, peering into your Eden—an Eden for me, though a desert for you! How I feared to see emptiness—or worse, the dragon! But when I saw, through gaps in the branches, the gleam of your straw hat and the flutter of your grey dress—your dress I’d recognize anywhere—my heart leapt. Why won’t you look up? Cruel, to deny me one glance of those dear eyes! One look would revive me!

I write this quickly, while the doctor examines Gustave. I’ve sealed it in this small casket, with a bouquet of the sweetest flowers—though less sweet than you, my Peri, my all-charming! Ever yours—you well know whom!”

“I wish I did know whom,” I muttered.

And indeed, I wished I knew who *she* was—the girl in the grey dress, the one addressed as “angel.” The note was meant for someone wearing grey and a straw hat. But both were common. The hat was ordinary, worn by many. The grey dress—Madame Beck herself wore one. Another teacher, three boarders—many wore the same shade. It was simply fashionable.

Still, I knew I had to go in. Lights moved in the dormitory—prayers were over, girls were going to bed. Soon, doors would lock, lights extinguish. The front door stood open, letting in the night’s coolness. From the porter’s lodge, a lamp shone, illuminating the long vestibule, the drawing-room doors, and the great street door.

Suddenly, a bell rang—quick, quiet, a cautious tinkle. Rosine darted from her lodge and opened the door. Someone entered, stayed a moment, then left. Rosine came to the garden gate, lamp in hand, looking around. “What a joke!” she laughed. “No one was there.”

“Let me pass,” a familiar voice pleaded. “Only five minutes.” A tall, commanding figure emerged from the house and strode into the garden.

A man in the garden at night—unthinkable. But he knew his privilege. Perhaps he trusted the darkness.

He wandered the paths, searching, stepping on flowers, breaking branches. Finally, he entered the forbidden walk.

There I met him—like a ghost, perhaps.

“Dr. John! It’s found.”

He didn’t ask who found it. With a glance, he saw I held it.

“Do not betray her,” he said, staring at me as if I were the dragon.

“If I were ever tempted to betray, I couldn’t—because I don’t know,” I replied. “Read the note. You’ll see how little it reveals.”

“Perhaps you’ve already read it,” I thought. But I couldn’t believe he wrote it—his style was too different. Besides, I foolishly imagined it would hurt him to call me such names.

His face flushed as he read. “This is too much,” he said. “Cruel. Humiliating.”

I thought it *was* cruel, seeing him so affected. Whether he was at fault or not, someone must be more to blame.

“What will you do?” he asked. “Tell Madame Beck? Cause a scandal?”

I thought I should tell. I said so. But added I didn’t think it would cause a stir—Madame was too discreet to make a fuss about such matters.

He stood silent, thinking. Too proud, too honorable, to beg me to keep silent when duty demanded disclosure.

I wanted to do right, but dreaded hurting him.

Then Rosine glanced out through the open door. I could see her clearly—her dress was grey, like mine.

That detail, combined with earlier events, gave me an idea: perhaps no student was involved. If so, I had no obligation to interfere.

“I’ll keep silent,” I said, “if you can assure me no pupil is implicated. Take the casket, the flowers, the note. I’ll forget it all.”

“Look there!” he whispered, grabbing the casket and pointing through the branches.

I looked.

There was Madame—wrapped in shawl, gown, slippers—descending the steps softly, creeping like a cat around the garden. In two minutes, she’d reach Dr. John.

Yet if she was a cat, he was a leopard—silent, swift, graceful. He watched her turn a corner, then crossed the garden in two noiseless bounds. She reappeared—he was gone.

Rosine helped him, closing the door between him and his pursuer.

I could have fled—but I chose to face Madame openly.

Though I often spent twilight in the garden, I’d never stayed so late. I knew she’d noticed my absence. She’d come to find me, to catch me unawares.

I expected scolding.

But she was kind. Not a word of reproach. No surprise. With her flawless tact—greater than anyone’s, I believe—she claimed she’d simply come out to enjoy “the evening breeze.”

“What a beautiful night!” she exclaimed, gazing at the stars—the moon now hidden behind the tower of Jean Baptiste. “How pleasant! How fresh the air!”

Instead of sending me in, she stayed, walking a few turns with me down the main path. When we returned, she leaned gently on my shoulder as we climbed the steps. At parting, she pressed her cheek to mine. “Good night, my dear friend. Sleep well.”

I smiled, lying awake later—smiling at Madame.

Her sweetness, her grace—so perfect, so practiced—were proof, for one who knew her, that suspicion stirred within her mind. From some hidden vantage—through parted leaves or an open window—she had likely seen something: a glimpse, a shadow, a footfall, a murmur. Though Dr. John had spoken softly, the hum of his voice seemed to echo across the grounds.

She hadn’t seen *what* happened—perhaps not yet. But a delicate, tangled plot lay before her, begging to be unraveled. And at its center, wrapped in cobwebs, was I—Miss Lucy—clumsily caught, like a foolish fly.

I found myself smiling—nay, laughing—once more at Madame within twenty-four hours of the scene described in the previous chapter. Villette’s climate, though not as damp as England’s, is no less changeable. The soft glow of the sunset gave way to a night of fierce wind, and the following day brought a dry, tempestuous storm—dark and clouded, yet without rain. Dust swirled across the streets, lifted from the boulevards like a veil of grit, dimming the light and filling the air with a restless haze.

Even had the weather been fair, I doubt I would have ventured into the garden again that evening. My quiet alley, once so dear, now felt exposed. The seclusion I’d cherished was gone, replaced by an uneasy awareness. That casement, through which billets had rained down, had turned the once-private nook into something vulgar. The flowers seemed to watch; the knots in the tree trunks listened. Some plants bore the marks of Dr. John’s hurried search—trampled and bent—and footprints marred the beds. But before dawn broke, while the wind still howled, I managed to erase every trace, careful not to let any eye catch sight of it.

That evening, I sat at my desk, German grammar open before me, as the students settled into their lessons. The other teachers picked up their needlework, and silence fell over the refectory—a smaller room than any classroom, reserved only for boarders, whose number did not exceed two dozen. Two lamps hung above the tables, lit at dusk, marking the beginning of “la lecture pieuse,” or the devotional reading. It was here, each night, that we gathered under a solemn hush, books set aside, faces grave, and minds prepared for what followed.

The book used was always the same—an ancient volume, its cover worn as if by centuries, its pages yellowed like autumn leaves. I longed to hold it, to turn its sacred pages, to read the title myself. But I could only listen, as a heretic might hear scripture read aloud, my mind reeling under the weight of absurdity. These were legends of saints—tales so exaggerated, so far-fetched, they made one laugh inwardly. Yet beneath the monkish fantasy lay something darker: priestcraft, moral tyranny, stories of noblewomen humbled, reduced to servitude under the false piety of confessors who wielded power like weapons.

One tale, repeated often—the story of Conrad and Elizabeth of Hungary—was especially harrowing. It spoke of forced renunciations, cruel penances, lives shattered in the name of faith. Such narratives felt like nightmares, dreams of oppression and suffering. I endured these readings for several nights, sitting quietly, pretending to be absorbed in my own work. Once, I snapped the points of my scissors, stabbing them deep into the worm-eaten table out of sheer tension. But eventually, the heat grew unbearable—my temples throbbed, my heart pounded, and sleep became fractured with unrest. I could stay no longer.

Prudence dictated escape. From that moment on, as soon as Rosine lit the lamps, I slipped away swiftly, silently—taking advantage of the brief flurry before silence descended. I vanished just as the students tucked their books away. There was no candlelight allowed outside the refectory; the teacher who left was cast into darkness, with only unlit halls, classrooms, or bedrooms to retreat to.

In winter, I walked the long classrooms, pacing quickly to keep warm. If the moon shone, I was grateful; if only stars glimmered, I adapted. In summer, when twilight lingered, I climbed to my chamber in the dormitory—its five large windows like doors to the sky. I opened my casement, leaned out, and gazed upon the city beyond the garden. Music drifted from the park or palace square, and I lived in my own silent world, thinking my own thoughts, free from intrusion.

This particular evening, I fled the refectory as usual, fleeing the Pope and his works. I ascended the stairs, approached the dormitory door—always kept shut—and pushed it open softly. The hinges, well-oiled, moved without a sound. Before I saw, I *felt* that someone was there. Not movement, not breath, but presence. The great room, usually empty, held life. All the white beds—called “lits d’ange”—were visible at a glance. They were all vacant. No sleeper rested.

Then came the soft scrape of a drawer being pulled open. I stepped sideways, and my vision cleared. I saw my own bed, my toilet, my locked work-box, and drawers beneath. And there stood Madame—dumpy, motherly, wrapped in a modest shawl, wearing a clean nightcap. She was searching my belongings with meticulous care.

The lid of the work-box was open. The top drawer was drawn out. One by one, she opened each drawer, lifting every item, unfolding garments, scanning papers, unlidding tiny boxes. Her movements were precise, unhurried, almost reverent. She worked like a true star—unhasting, yet never resting.

I watched, secretly amused. Had I been a man, I might have admired her. She was neat, thorough, efficient. Others’ clumsiness irritated me; hers pleased. I stood transfixed—but then I forced myself to move. If she turned, she might see me. Then there would be no hiding. We would confront each other—no masks, no pretenses. I would look into her eyes, and she into mine. We would know, instantly, that our roles could no longer continue. And we would part forever.

Why invite such a catastrophe? I was not angry. I had no desire to leave. This was the easiest employment I could hope for, and I respected Madame’s intelligence, even if I disagreed with her beliefs. Her system didn’t harm me. I was untouched by it. And since I had no lover, no hopes of love, my heart was safe—like a beggar’s purse, barren and thus immune to theft.

So I turned and fled, descending the stairs as silently as a spider slipping down the banister. When I reached the schoolroom, I laughed—quietly, but deeply. I knew now that she had seen Dr. John in the garden. I knew what she thought. The irony of her suspicion, so wildly misplaced, delighted me. But the laughter faded, replaced by a sudden anger, then bitterness. Like striking rock and watching water spring forth, my emotions surged.

Never had I felt such turmoil—laughter and pain, fire and grief, warring within me. I wept hot tears—not because of her mistrust, which meant nothing to me—but for deeper reasons. Complicated thoughts stirred, disrupting my peace. Yet by morning, the storm had passed. I was Lucy Snowe again.

Returning to my room, I found everything restored—drawers locked, clothes neatly folded. My dresses lay as I had left them. A small bunch of dried white violets—given anonymously once, preserved between the folds of my best dress—lay undisturbed. My black silk scarf, lace chemisette, collars—all untouched. Had even one article been creased, I might have resented it. But finding everything in order, I whispered, “Let bygones be bygones. I am unharmed. Why should I bear malice?”

Yet one mystery remained: how had Dr. John known the ivory box had fallen, if he hadn’t helped drop it? He arrived promptly to retrieve it. The question gnawed at me. Could I, if given the chance, ask him directly? While he was absent, I believed I had the courage.

Georgette, meanwhile, was recovering. Her doctor visits grew rare. He would have stopped entirely, had Madame not insisted on occasional calls until the child was fully well.

One evening, after I’d heard Georgette’s lisping prayer and tucked her into bed, Dr. John entered the nursery. He took the child’s hand and said, “Cette enfant a toujours un peu de fièvre.” Then, looking at me with a sharper glance than usual, “Le Docteur John l’a-t-il vue dernièrement? Non, n’est-ce pas?”

She knew better than anyone. “Well,” she continued, “I’m going out—to run some errands by carriage. I’ll call on Dr. John and send him to the child. I insist he sees her tonight. Her cheeks are flushed, her pulse quick. You will receive him—I shall be away.”

The child was merely warm from summer heat. Sending for a doctor was as unnecessary as calling a priest for extreme unction. Moreover, Madame rarely ran errands in the evening. This was the first time she’d chosen to leave during one of Dr. John’s visits. Clearly, this was deliberate. But I felt no anxiety.

“Ha! ha! Madame,” I thought, “your cleverness has misfired.”

She left dressed smartly—shawl of fine quality, a green hat that suited her fresh complexion. I wondered what she intended. Would she send Dr. John? Would he come?

Madame had told me not to let Georgette sleep until the doctor arrived. So I occupied myself with stories and gentle talk, soothing the child. I liked Georgette—she was sensitive, affectionate. Holding her in my arms was a comfort. Tonight, she insisted I rest my head on her crib pillow, even wrapping her tiny arms around my neck. The tenderness overwhelmed me. Tears pricked my eyes. Such pure emotion, so innocent, touched something deep within.

An hour passed. Georgette murmured, sleepy. “And you _shall_ sleep,” I decided, “despite maman and médecin, if they don’t arrive in ten minutes.”

Then—ringing! And footsteps, swift and startling, echoing up the stairs. Rosine ushered in Dr. John, staying behind, as was common among Villette’s servants. She wasn’t afraid of him—only of Madame’s presence. Smart, trim, and bold, she stood with hands in her apron pockets, eyeing him as if he were a portrait.

“Le marmot n’a rien, n’est-ce pas?” she asked, jerking her chin toward Georgette.

“Pas beaucoup,” replied the doctor, scribbling a harmless prescription.

“Eh bien!” she pressed, stepping close. “And the box—did you get it? Monsieur rushed off like a storm last night. I didn’t get to ask.”

“I found it,” he said simply.

“And who threw it?” she asked freely—words I longed to speak but lacked the courage.

“That may be my secret,” he replied, calm and unassuming.

“Mais enfin,” she persisted, “monsieur knew it was thrown—how did you know?”

“I was attending a young patient at the college nearby,” he explained. “I saw it fall from his window and came to retrieve it.”

Ah—so simple. The note had mentioned a physician examining “Gustave.”
