“Ah ça!” Rosine exclaimed. “So there’s nothing hidden—no mystery, no romance?”

“Not more than on my palm,” he replied, showing his hand.

“Quel dommage!” she sighed. “And I—was beginning to imagine things.”

“Vraiment! You’re out of luck,” he said coolly.

She pouted. He couldn’t help laughing at her expression. His smile softened his face, revealing a warmth that made him seem kinder. I saw his hand go to his pocket.

“How many times have you opened the door for me this month?” he asked.

“Monsieur should have counted,” she replied.

“As if I had anything better to do!” he teased—but I saw him slip a gold coin into her hand. She took it without hesitation and danced off to answer the bell, ringing every five minutes as servants fetched half-boarders.

Do not judge Rosine too harshly. She was not a bad person—only practical. She saw no shame in taking what she could, nor fear in speaking plainly to a gentleman.

From this exchange, I learned more than about the ivory box: the fault did not lie in Madame’s wardrobe, nor in Rosine’s apron. Those garments were as blameless as Georgette’s blue tunic. So who then was responsible? What was the truth behind it all?

Some clues emerged, but many remained dark. “However,” I thought, “it’s none of my business.” I turned from the face I’d been staring at—Dr. John’s—and looked out the window toward the garden.

He stood by Georgette’s bed, slowly pulling on his gloves, watching her as her eyes closed and her lips parted in sleep. I waited for him to leave, as he always did—with a quick bow and a barely audible “good-night.”

Just as he reached for his hat, my gaze caught the lattice window across the garden—the one already noted—cautiously opening. A hand appeared, holding a white handkerchief. It waved.

I don’t know if it was answered from somewhere unseen in our house. But moments later, a white object fluttered from the window—billet number two.

“There!” I breathed.

“Where?” Dr. John asked sharply, turning toward the window.

“They’ve done it again,” I said. “A handkerchief waved—and something fell.” I pointed.

“Go at once,” he ordered. “Pick it up and bring it here. Nobody will notice you. I would be seen.”

I went. After a brief search, I found a folded paper lodged in a shrub. I brought it back.

He tore it into pieces without reading.

“It’s not her fault,” he said, looking at me. “You must remember.”

“Whose fault?” I asked.

“Who is it?”

“You don’t know yet?”

“Not at all.”

“Have you no guess?”

“None.”

“If I knew you better,” he said, “I might trust you with a confidence—entrust you to guard a most innocent, excellent, yet inexperienced creature.”

“As a duenna?” I asked.

“Yes,” he said absently. “What snares surround her!”

For the first time, he studied my face—perhaps seeking kindness, perhaps looking for someone worthy of such a charge.

I felt no calling to chaperone angels. But recalling the scene at the bureau, I felt I owed him something. If I could help, I would.

“I’m willing,” I said, “to do what I can to protect anyone you care about.”

“I’m not interested personally,” he replied, with a modesty I admired. “I know the character of the person from the opposite house—who has twice intruded here. I’ve met the object of these attempts in society. She is exquisite, refined—her very nature should repel such impertinence. Yet she remains unaware, innocent. I wish to shield her—if I could. But I cannot approach her myself.”

“Then tell me how I can help,” I said. “Only tell me who she is.”

“But you _must_ know,” he said earnestly, voice low. “So spotless, so good, so beautiful—impossible that one house could hold two like her. I allude, of course—”

At that moment, the latch of Madame Beck’s chamber door clicked—suddenly, as if startled. Then came the suppressed burst of a sneeze.

These little accidents happen to the best.

Madame—excellent woman—had returned quietly, crept upstairs on tiptoe, and entered her room. Had she not sneezed, she might have heard everything. But the sneeze betrayed her.

She stepped forward, composed, alert, as if she had just arrived. No novice would have guessed she’d been listening at the keyhole for ten minutes. She pretended to sneeze again, declared herself “enrhumée,” and began recounting her errands with cheerful detail.

The prayer-bell rang. I left her with Dr. John.

When Georgette recovered, Madame sent her away to the countryside. I felt a pang of loss—she had been a small light in my quiet life, and her absence left me more alone than before. But I could not complain. I lived in a house full of energy, laughter, and noise; companionship was within reach, yet I chose silence. One by one, the teachers offered me friendship, each in their own way. I tested them all. The first was honest but narrow-minded, coarse in feeling, and utterly self-absorbed. The second—a Parisienne—was outwardly refined, but beneath the surface lay emptiness: no beliefs, no principles, no true affection. Her soul seemed stripped bare, like a shell without a creature inside. She craved gifts above all else, and the third teacher, though otherwise dull and unremarkable, shared that same hunger. This woman, however, had another trait: an almost animal greed. Money itself stirred her deeply—the sight of gold brought a strange green gleam to her eyes, as if she were drawn to it like a moth to flame. Once, as a sign of favor, she took me upstairs, opened a hidden door, and revealed a hoard of crude, heavy coins—about fifteen guineas’ worth in five-franc pieces. She cherished it like a bird guards its nest. These were her savings. She would speak of them with a kind of obsessive devotion, staring at them for hours, even though she was barely twenty-five.

The Parisienne, on the other hand, spent recklessly—though not necessarily in action, only in spirit. Her profligacy showed itself once, cautiously, like a snake peeking from under a leaf. I glimpsed it briefly, and found it unsettlingly new. If it had come forward boldly, I might have stood firm, observed it coldly from head to tail. But it retreated quickly when I reacted with sudden anger, hissing into hiding. From that moment, she hated me.

She was always in debt. Her salary vanished long before payday—not just on clothes, but on perfumes, cosmetics, sweets, and spices. What a cold, detached hedonist she was! I can still see her: thin face, sallow skin, sharp features, perfect teeth, lips like a thread, a broad chin, wide-open eyes that held no warmth—only a craving light that never gave thanks. She despised work and adored what she called pleasure—meaning idle, heartless, thoughtless distraction.

Madame Beck knew her well. Once she spoke of her to me, mixing insight with indifference and disdain. I asked why she kept such a woman in her employ. “Because it suits my interest,” she replied plainly. Then she pointed out something I’d already noticed: Mademoiselle St. Pierre had a rare talent for controlling unruly students. A silent, chilling authority surrounded her—no shouting, no force, yet she held them fast, like frost halting a rushing stream. She taught little, but enforced discipline perfectly. “I know she has no principles, perhaps no morals,” Madame admitted, “but her presence in class is always proper, even dignified. That’s all that matters. Neither the girls nor their parents look beyond that—and so, neither do I.”

This school was a lively, noisy, almost giddy place. Efforts were made to mask chains with flowers. A subtle current of Roman Catholicism ran through every custom. Physical indulgence was allowed—almost encouraged—as a balance to strict spiritual control. Minds were shaped in servitude, but to keep them from questioning, every chance for bodily enjoyment was seized. Here, as everywhere, the Church aimed to raise children strong in body, weak in soul—plump, rosy, cheerful, ignorant, passive, unquestioning. “Eat, drink, and live!” she says. “Care for your bodies; leave your souls to me. I hold their fate—I guide their path—I guarantee their end.” A bargain many Catholics accept gladly. Lucifer offers the same: “All this power and glory shall be yours—for it is mine, and I give it to whom I will. If you worship me, all shall be yours.”

At this time—when summer reached its fullest bloom—Madame Beck’s house became as festive as any school could be. All day, the folding doors and casement windows stood open. Sunlight settled into the air like a resident guest. Clouds drifted far off, beyond the sea, perhaps resting over misty islands like England—our beloved land of fog—but gone entirely from this sun-drenched continent. We lived more in the garden than indoors. Classes were held and meals eaten in the “grand berceau.” And there was a growing sense of celebration, nearly tipping into freedom. The autumn vacation was two months away, but before that came a grand event: Madame’s fête.

Organizing it fell mostly to Mademoiselle St. Pierre. Madame pretended ignorance, as if she were unaware of the festivities being prepared in her honor. Especially, she never suspected the secret subscription collected each year from the entire school to buy her a gift. Let us quietly omit mention of the brief, hushed meeting in her own room about this.

“What would you like this year?” asked her Parisian assistant.

“Oh, nothing. Leave it. Let the poor children keep their francs,” Madame said, looking gentle and modest.

St. Pierre would then thrust out her chin. She knew Madame well. She called her displays of kindness “grimaces”—nothing more. She never pretended to respect them.

“Quick!” she’d snap. “Name the item. Jewels or porcelain? Haberdashery or silver?”

“Well… two or three spoons, and the same number of forks, in silver.”

And so it was: a fine case containing 300 francs worth of silverware.

The fête’s program included: presentation of the silver, a garden meal, a play performed by pupils and teachers, followed by dancing and supper. To me, it seemed magnificent—especially since Zélie St. Pierre understood these things and managed them with skill.

The play was central—requiring a month of rehearsal. Casting demanded care, then lessons in voice, posture, and expression, followed by endless practice. For all this, St. Pierre wasn’t enough. Another talent was needed—someone with different strengths. That person was M. Paul Emanuel, professor of literature.

I never saw him during rehearsals, but often caught sight of him crossing the *carré*—the square hall between the house and school. I heard him in warm evenings, lecturing with open doors, and his name, along with stories about him, echoed through the halls. Miss Ginevra Fanshawe, our former acquaintance, who had been cast in a leading role, often filled my ears with tales of him during our talks. She found him hideously plain and claimed she nearly fainted at the sound of his footsteps or voice.

He was indeed a dark little man—sharp, severe. Even to me, he seemed harsh: black hair cropped close, a broad, pale brow, thin cheeks, flaring nostrils, piercing eyes, and a restless stride. Irritable, they said, especially when addressing the clumsy group under his direction. He would burst out in fury at their flat emotions, hollow expressions, weak delivery. “Listen!” he’d roar, and his voice cut through the building like a trumpet. When a timid voice—Ginevra’s, Mathilde’s, Blanche’s—mimicked him, a wave of scorn or rage would follow.

“You are nothing but dolls,” I heard him thunder. “You have no passions. You feel nothing? Your flesh is ice, your blood is frozen! I demand fire—life—soul!”

But his demands were futile. When he finally accepted that, he tore apart the tragedy they’d been learning and returned the next day with a short, comic piece. That, they embraced eagerly. Soon, he had it memorized and polished in their minds.

Mademoiselle St. Pierre always presided over M. Emanuel’s classes. I was told her poise, attention, tact, and grace impressed him deeply. She had the gift of pleasing people—briefly. But the charm faded fast, like dew under sun, like gossamer in wind.

The day before the fête was as festive as the fête itself. It was devoted to cleaning, rearranging, and decorating the three classrooms. Inside, there was constant bustle—no corner was quiet, no space untouched. So I fled to the garden. All day, I wandered or sat there alone, warmed by the sun, sheltered by trees, companioned only by my thoughts. I remember exchanging only two words with anyone that day. I didn’t feel lonely—I welcomed the peace. As a spectator, I passed through the rooms several times, watching how they transformed: a green room and dressing room were created, a tiny stage built with scenery, M. Paul Emanuel and Mademoiselle St. Pierre directing everything, while eager students—among them Ginevra—worked happily under their guidance.

The great day arrived. The sun rose hot and clear, and burned steadily until nightfall. Every door and window stood open, giving a sense of summer freedom—freedom, it seemed, was the theme of the day. Teachers and students descended for breakfast in dressing gowns and curlers, enjoying a morning of careless comfort, anticipating the evening’s elaborate dress. Like aldermen fasting before a feast, they relished their slovenliness.

Around nine, the “coiffeur” arrived. It was sacrilegious, some said, that he set up shop in the oratory—right beside the holy water, candle, and crucifix—where he performed his sacred art. Each girl was summoned in turn. Emerging from his hands, her head was smooth as a shell, marked by flawless white lines, braided in Greek-style plaits that shone like lacquer. I joined the line, and when I looked in the mirror afterward, I hardly recognized myself. The mass of woven brown hair amazed me—I feared it wasn’t all mine, and pulled hard to confirm it was. I now acknowledged the coiffeur as a master—someone who could make beauty from mediocrity.

Afterward, the dormitory became the scene of ablutions, dressing, and adornment—elaborate, intricate, prolonged. To me, it remains a mystery how so much time could be spent achieving so little. The process seemed complex, the result simple. A crisp white muslin dress, a blue sash (in the Virgin’s colors), white or straw-colored kid gloves—this was the uniform. Three hours went into putting it on. Yet though simple, the effect was impeccable—perfect in style, fit, freshness. Every head was styled with precision, and the overall look—stiff but elegant—suited the sturdy Labassecourien features, though too rigid for softer beauty. On the whole, it was admirable.

Seeing this sea of white and blue, I felt like a shadow against light. I dared not wear a sheer white dress. The weather and rooms were too hot for heavy fabrics, so I searched shops until I found a material like crape, in a purple-gray hue—the color of mist over a blooming moor. My seamstress made it as best she could, wisely noting it was “so sad—so dull,” so extra care was needed. She was right: I had no flower, no jewel, and no natural flush of color. We ignore such flaws in daily routine, but on bright occasions like this, they stand out.

Still, in this shadowy gown, I felt at ease. I wouldn’t have in anything brighter. Madame Beck also wore a subdued dress—nearly as quiet as mine—except for a bracelet and a large brooch glittering with gold and gems. We met on the stairs, and she nodded and smiled approval. Not because I looked well—something she likely didn’t care about—but because I was dressed “appropriately,” “decently.” Convenance and Decence were the gods she worshipped. She paused, placed her gloved hand—holding a perfumed, embroidered handkerchief—on my shoulder, and whispered a sneer about the other teachers (whom she had just praised). “Nothing so absurd,” she said, “as mature women dressing like fifteen-year-olds. As for St. Pierre—she looks like an old coquette pretending to be innocent.”

Dressed hours before anyone else, I enjoyed the peace of going not to the garden—where servants were setting tables and chairs—but to the empty, cool, clean classrooms. Their walls were freshly stained, floors scoured and still damp, pots of fresh flowers in corners, draperies newly hung.

I withdrew to the first classe—a smaller, neater room—and took a book from the glass-fronted cabinet, whose key I kept. I sat down to read. The glass door opened onto the large berceau; acacia branches brushed the panes, stretching toward a rosebush blooming by the opposite lintel. Bees hummed contentedly among the blossoms. I began reading. Just as the quiet hum, the dappled shade, and the warm solitude began to draw me into reverie, a sharp ring of the front door bell—sharper than any it had ever made—snapped me back.

The bell had rung all morning, as workers, servants, stylists, and seamstresses came and went. It would ring again in the afternoon, with about a hundred outside students arriving by carriage. And it would ring through the evening as parents and friends gathered for the play. So a loud ring was expected. But this one had a special edge—cutting through my dream, startling my book from my lap.

I bent to pick it up when—firm, fast, straight—through vestibule, corridor, courtyard, divisions, grand hall—came a step: quick, regular, determined. The closed door of the first classe—my refuge—offered no resistance. It burst open, and a paletôt and Greek bonnet filled the doorway. Two eyes first glanced at me, then locked on, searching.

“That’s her!” a voice declared. “I know her: the Englishwoman. Too bad. Any Englishwoman—however tongue-tied—will serve my purpose, or I’ll know why.”

Then, with stiff politeness (perhaps thinking I hadn’t grasped his earlier harsh muttering), and in the worst French imaginable: “Meess——, you must play. I am planted there.”

“What can I do for you, M. Paul Emanuel?” I asked. It was him—clearly agitated.

“You must play. I won’t allow you to shrink, frown, or pretend virtue. I read your mind the night you arrived. I see your talents: you can act. You must.”

“But how, M. Paul? What do you mean?”

“There is no time,” he continued, switching to French. “Let us forget excuses, hesitation, nonsense. You must take a part.”

“In the vaudeville?”

“In the vaudeville. You’ve said it.”

I gasped, horrified. What did he mean?

“Listen,” he said. “I’ll explain. Then answer—yes or no. Your answer will determine how I view you forever.”

A storm of irritation glowed in his cheeks, sharpened his gaze. Such a nature—irritated, sentimental, hesitant, stubborn—could turn violent quickly. Silence was the best response. I listened.

“The whole performance is collapsing,” he began. “Louise Vanderkelkov is ill—at least her ridiculous mother claims so. I believe she could perform if she wanted. It’s only willpower missing. She had a role—you know or don’t—it doesn’t matter. Without it, the play fails. There are only hours left to learn it. No girl here would listen, would accept. It’s not a likable role—her petty pride, that base quality women possess, revolts at it. Englishwomen are either the best or worst of their kind. God knows I hate them usually” (he muttered through clenched teeth). “I turn to an Englishwoman to save me. Will she say yes—or no?”

A thousand objections flooded my mind. The language, the time, the exposure… My instincts recoiled, my confidence faltered, my pride—this “vile quality”—trembled. “No, no, no!” said every instinct. But looking up at M. Paul, seeing in his furious, searching eyes a plea beneath the threat, my lips formed the word: “Yes.”

For a moment, his stern face softened with relief. Then, swiftly regaining composure, he said: “To work! Here is the script. Here is your role. Read.”

I read. He didn’t praise. At some lines, he frowned, stamped. He gave me a lesson. I tried hard to imitate. The role was unpleasant—a man’s, a foolish fop’s. I couldn’t put heart or soul into it. I hated it. The play was trivial—a comedy about two rivals vying for a flirtatious woman. One lover was the “Bear”—a good, brave, rough diamond. The other was a butterfly, a talker, a traitor. And I was to be the butterfly, the talker, the traitor.

I did my best—bad, I knew. It angered M. Paul. He seethed. I worked harder, trying to surpass my best. Perhaps he saw my effort. He grudgingly said, “It will do.” As voices began rising from the garden and white dresses fluttered among the trees, he added: “You must be alone to learn this. Come with me.”

Without time to think, I was swept along—like a whirlwind—upstairs, up two flights, then a third. This fiery little man seemed to know every corner. I was carried to a solitary, high attic, shut in, and locked. He took the key and vanished.

The attic was no haven. I doubt he knew how terrible it was, or he wouldn’t have imprisoned me so casually. In summer, it was as hot as Africa; in winter, colder than Greenland. Boxes and clutter filled it. Old dresses hung on the walls. Cobwebs covered the ceiling. Rats, beetles, cockroaches lived here—rumor even said the ghostly Nun of the garden had been seen here. A dimness covered one end, where an old russet curtain hid a row of winter cloaks—each pinned like a criminal to a gallows. From behind that curtain, the Nun was said to emerge. I didn’t believe it, nor was I afraid. But I saw a large, dark rat glide from the alcove. And my eyes landed on countless black beetles dotting the floor. These disturbed me more than I care to admit—along with the dust, clutter, and stifling heat. Had I not opened the skylight and propped it up, I would have suffocated. Beneath it, I placed a large chest, climbed a smaller box, wiped the dust, arranged my best dress carefully around me, and perched on this makeshift throne. Seated, I began to learn my part—while keeping watch on the beetles and cockroaches, which I feared more than the rats.

After Madame Beck’s festive gathering—three weeks of ease, a single day of wild merriment, and one more of utter weariness—came a time of quiet reckoning: two months of unrelenting study, the true heart of the school year. These final weeks were when real effort was demanded, when teachers and students alike poured their energy into preparing for the prize examinations. The burden fell heavily on all, but especially on those who taught subjects central to the curriculum. Professors sharpened their lessons, mistresses urged their pupils forward, and every student labored under the weight of expectation. A public display was expected—a demonstration of learning—and no means were too great to achieve it.

I paid little heed to how others prepared; my own duties absorbed me entirely. My task was among the most demanding: teaching English to ninety students who saw the language as both mysterious and difficult, and training their tongues in sounds they found nearly impossible—the soft hushes and sharp hisses that marked speech in the British Isles. It was a challenge not only of intellect but of patience.

The examination day arrived—so solemn, so charged with consequence. We dressed with care, avoiding anything frivolous or light; modest, composed garments were worn. To me, the day felt like a trial laid solely upon my shoulders. Unlike the other teachers, I was expected to preside over the testing of my subject. M. Paul, the headmaster, took charge of all other disciplines, refusing help from any colleague. He ruled the school with an iron hand, brooking no rivals. Even Madame Beck, who longed to examine in geography—her favorite subject—was forced to yield to his authority. He stood alone on the examiner’s platform, save for one exception: English. He could not manage it himself. Reluctantly, he left it to me—though not without a flash of envy in his eyes.

He had a deep disdain for vanity in others, though he seemed to revel in his own. He loved being seen, admired, acknowledged—but detested the same in anyone else. When he could not suppress someone’s prominence, he seethed like a storm bottled up inside.

On the evening before the exam, I walked in the garden with the other teachers and boarders. M. Emanuel joined me in the forbidden alley. His cigar glowed between his fingers, his dark coat hung loosely, his Greek cap’s tassel shadowing one temple. His whiskers curled like a cat’s in anger, and his blue eyes held a flicker of irritation.

“So,” he said abruptly, stopping before me, “you will sit like a queen tomorrow—beside me? I can see you already savoring the joy of power. Ah, little ambitious one, I see your pride shining!”

But I was not proud. I did not care for praise or recognition. If the audience had been filled with friends, perhaps I might have felt differently. But they were strangers. Their approval meant nothing to me. For him, such moments burned with warmth and meaning. He craved them. I did not. Yet I found something compelling in his jealousy—it stirred him, made him vivid. It lit strange fires in his dull face, sparked shadows in his violet-blue eyes (he once told me his black hair and blue eyes were “one of his beauties”). There was honesty in his rage—no pretense, no mask.

I did not deny his claim. I simply asked where the English exam would fall—early or late?

“I hesitate,” he replied. “Should it come at the start, when few are present and your ambition won’t be flattered by a crowd—or at the end, when everyone is tired and attention is spent?”

“You are harsh, Monsieur,” I said, feigning sorrow.

“One must be harsh with you. You are one of those who must be kept down. I know you. Others pass you by and think you a mere shadow. But I looked at your face once—and that was enough.”

“Do you truly understand me?”

He did not answer directly. Instead, he continued, “Were you not thrilled when you succeeded in that vaudeville? I watched you. Your face blazed with desire to win. Fire, not just light—je me tiens pour averti.”

“The feeling I had then,” I said, “was not triumph. I despised the role you gave me. I had no connection with the audience below. They are decent people, I suppose—but do I know them? Are they mine? Can I care if I stand before them again tomorrow? Will this examination mean anything to me beyond duty? It is merely a task—one I wish done.”

“Shall I take it from you?”

“With all my heart—if you do not fear failure.”

“But I would fail. I know only three English phrases: ‘de sonn,’ ‘de mone,’ ‘de stare’—is that right? Perhaps we should abandon the exam altogether.”

“If Madame agrees, I agree.”

“Heartily?”

“Very heartily.”

He smoked in silence, then turned suddenly.

“Give me your hand,” he said. The bitterness vanished from his face, replaced by something softer—kindness, even generosity.

“Let us not be rivals,” he added. “Let us be friends. The exam shall happen, and I’ll choose the best time. Instead of hindering you—as I nearly did ten minutes ago—I will help. I have my cruel moods. I’ve always had them since childhood. But you are alone, a stranger, making your way. It may be good for you to be known. Let us be friends. Do you agree?”

“From the bottom of my heart, Monsieur. I welcome a friend more than any triumph.”

“Poor thing,” he murmured, turning away.

The examination passed smoothly. M. Paul honored his word and made my part as easy as possible. Then came the prize-giving, the closing of term, and the departure of the students. And so began the long vacation.

That vacation! Could I ever forget it? I doubt it. Madame Beck left the first day to join her children by the sea. The other teachers returned to family or friends. Every professor fled the city—some to Paris, some to Boue-Marine. M. Paul set off for Rome. The house emptied completely—save for me, a servant, and one deformed, mentally impaired girl, a sort of crétin, whom her stepmother refused to take back.

My heart nearly broke. Longings pulled at me, stretching every nerve. How slow the September days felt! How silent, how empty the house seemed! The garden, once vibrant, now lay grey and still, dusted with the residue of summer. At the start of those eight weeks, I scarcely believed I could endure them. My spirits had been sinking for some time; now, without work to sustain me, they sank faster. Even hope was gone. The future offered no comfort, no promise. Existence itself often felt meaningless—a surrender to despair. Life, when viewed with full awareness, appeared a barren desert: endless sands, no greenery, no water, no shelter. The dreams youth clings to—love, purpose, joy—I knew not, nor dared to know. When they knocked at my heart, I barred them out. Sometimes, rejected, they brought tears—but I could not let them in. I feared the sin of presumption too deeply.

Religious reader, you may preach to me about this. Moralists will scold. Philosophers will frown. Stoics will disapprove. Cynics will sneer. Epicureans will laugh. Well, let them. I accept all. Perhaps they are right. But perhaps, in my place, you too would have been wrong. That first month was long, dark, heavy.

The crétin did not seem to suffer. I did what I could to feed and warm her. She wanted only food, sun, or fire when sunlight failed. Her mind slept, content in its emptiness. Her brain, eyes, ears, heart—none woke to pain or labor. Lethargy was her paradise.

For three weeks, the weather remained hot and dry. Then storms broke in the fourth and fifth weeks. I don’t know why the shift in air crushed me more than calm skies ever had—but it did. In that vast, hollow house, my nerves could barely hold. I prayed constantly for strength, for peace. The conviction seized me—Fate was my enemy, unyielding, unchangeable. I did not blame God’s mercy or justice. I accepted that suffering was part of His design. And I trembled knowing I was one of those chosen to bear it.

It was a relief when the crétin’s aunt came and took her away. The girl had been a burden—she couldn’t go outside, couldn’t be left alone. Her mind, like her body, was twisted. A vague impulse toward harm lived within her. She rarely spoke, sat for hours, twisting her face into grotesque expressions. Being with her felt like sharing space with a wild animal. Personal care required the courage of a nurse. My resolve faltered often, sometimes failing utterly. These duties were hard—but my inner pain was worse. Tending her made me lose appetite, sent me weak to the fountain. Yet she never touched my heart, never brought tears.

Once freed, I began walking. At first, I stayed near Rue Fossette. Gradually, I ventured beyond the gates, wandered along roads, through fields, past cemeteries, farmhouses, lanes, woods—nowhere in particular. A restless force pushed me forward. Loneliness gnawed at me like hunger. I walked entire days—through noon heat, afternoon drought, dusk, and moonrise.

Alone, I imagined others. Madame Beck, happy at the seaside with her children and friends. Zélie St. Pierre, in Paris. Ginevra Fanshawe, traveling southward with relatives. Ginevra seemed the happiest. She moved through golden landscapes, beneath autumn suns warming harvests and vineyards. Moons rose above her like crystal crowns.

But it meant nothing. I felt those suns, saw those moons—but wished only to be buried beneath earth, hidden from their glow. I could not live in their light, could not love them. Yet Ginevra had something I lacked—something powerful, gentle, enduring. True Love followed her. She was never truly alone. Was she unaware? Impossible. I pictured her quietly grateful, loving secretly, waiting to confess fully. I saw her beloved half-aware of her affection, comforted by it. Between them, a thread of understanding, invisible yet strong, carried prayers across miles. Ginevra became, in my mind, a kind of heroine.

One day, realizing my thoughts were slipping, I whispered: “I believe my nerves are breaking. My mind has suffered too much. A sickness is growing. What shall I do? How can I stay well?”

There was no way to stay well. Soon, after a night of unbearable sorrow, I fell ill. I took to bed. The Indian summer ended, and equinoctial storms began. For nine dark, wet days—hours rushing past in noise and chaos—I lay in a fever of nerves and blood. Sleep abandoned me. At night, I would rise, search for her, beg her return. Only wind and rain answered. Sleep never came.

But once—once—she came. Not in kindness. In anger. Impatient with my pleas, she brought a dream of vengeance. By the clock of St. Jean Baptiste, it lasted less than fifteen minutes—but it tore me apart. It felt like a visitation from eternity. Between midnight and one, a cup was pressed to my lips—black, strong, strange—drawn not from a well, but from a boundless, seething sea. Suffering mixed in mortal measure tastes nothing like this. After drinking, I woke, thinking it over. The end had come. Trembling, I rose on my knees. Horrors gripped me—indescribable agony, mental torment. The worst was this: I dreamed the dead I loved, who had loved me, met me elsewhere—strangers now. My soul was pierced by despair. No reason remained to live. Yet Death challenged me with a cold, proud voice. When I tried to pray, only these words came: “From my youth up Thy terrors have I suffered with a troubled mind.”

It was true.

The next morning, Goton brought tea and urged me to call a doctor. I refused. I thought no doctor could heal me.

One evening—clear-headed, not delirious—I rose, dressed myself, weak and trembling. The dormitory was unbearable. The white beds turned into specters—each crown a skull, sun-bleached and ancient. Dead dreams of older worlds stared from wide-open eye sockets. That night, I knew Fate was stone, Hope a false idol—blind, cold, unfeeling. I felt the trial God had given me was reaching its peak. I must act—my hands, though feeble, must turn it.

It rained still, but less fiercely. Twilight fell. From the lattice, I saw clouds trailing low like mourning banners. It seemed Heaven grieved for all earthly pain. The weight of my dream lessened. The unbearable thought—that I would no longer be loved—began to yield to hope. If I could leave this house—this tomb-like roof—and walk to a quiet hill far in the fields, perhaps clarity would return.

Wrapped in a cloak, I stepped out. Bells rang from a church nearby. They called me in. I entered. Any sacred ritual, any sign of worship, any chance to speak to God was as vital to me as bread to the starving. I knelt with others on the stone floor. The church was old, dim, purpled by stained glass light.

Few remained after the service. Half left. Those who stayed confessed. I did not move. Doors closed. Silence settled. One by one, penitents approached the confessional. I watched. Whispers passed. Comfort returned. A pale woman near me said softly, “Go now. I am not ready.”

Mechanically, I rose and went. I knew what I was doing. My mind had raced ahead. This step could not make me worse. It might soothe.

The priest inside never looked at me. He simply leaned his ear to my lips. He was likely kind, but this was routine. Habit had dulled him. I hesitated. I didn’t know the confession formula. So instead of beginning, I said: “Mon père, je suis Protestante.”

He turned sharply. He was not a native priest—his features showed he was French. Though aged, he had feeling, intelligence. He asked gently why, being Protestant, I came to him.

“I am dying for a word of comfort,” I said. “I’ve lived alone for weeks. I’ve been ill. A weight presses on my mind. I cannot carry it anymore.”

“Is it a sin?” he asked, startled.

I reassured him. Then, as best I could, I told him the outline of my pain.

He looked thoughtful, puzzled. “You surprise me,” he said. “I’ve never had a case like yours. Confession follows a pattern. Yours breaks it. I’m not prepared.”

Of course I hadn’t expected him to be. But simply speaking—to a human, yet sacred ear—had already eased me.

“Must I leave, father?” I asked.

“My daughter,” he said kindly, “go for now. But your words have struck me. Confession often becomes mechanical. You have poured your heart out—rarely done. I will ponder this. Were you of our faith, I would know what to say. Minds like yours find peace only in retreat, prayer, sacrifice. The world offers no solace for such souls. Holy men tell such penitents to climb upward through penance, self-denial, difficult deeds. Tears become their bread and drink. Their reward comes later. I believe these pains are messages from God—calling you back to the true Church. You were made for it. Protestantism is too dry, too cold for you. The more I reflect, the clearer it seems—your case is extraordinary. I will not forget you. Go now—but return.”

I rose, thanked him, and began to leave. He called me back.

“You must not come here,” he said. “This church is too cold. Come to my house. I live…” He gave me his address. “Be there tomorrow at ten.”

I bowed. I pulled down my veil, wrapped my cloak tighter, and slipped away into the night.

I cannot say where my soul wandered during that strange night when I lost consciousness. Whatever visions it beheld, or wherever it journeyed in its trance, it carried no word back to memory—no whisper, no clue. It remained a silent mystery, sealed from imagination as if by an unbreakable vow. Perhaps it soared upward, catching sight of the eternal home I longed for, hoping at last to be released from this weary body, believing the bond between spirit and flesh had finally dissolved. But then—perhaps—an angel appeared, warning me away from heaven’s threshold. With gentle yet firm hands, it guided me weeping back down, binding me once more, trembling and reluctant, to this frail frame—cold, wasted, and now more than ever a burden.

I returned to myself with pain, with resistance, with a moan and a shiver that ran through every limb. The reunion of spirit and body was not a meeting, but a struggle—a wrenching, painful reattachment. When sight returned, it came red, as though swimming in blood; hearing rushed back like thunder; awareness awakened in terror. I sat up, stunned, wondering what world I had woken into, among what unfamiliar beings. At first, nothing made sense: a wall was not a wall, a lamp not a lamp. I might have seen a ghost as clearly as any ordinary object—everything seemed spectral, unreal.

But slowly, faculties settled. The machinery of life resumed its rhythm. Still, I knew not where I was. Only gradually did I realize I had been moved from the spot where I fell. No longer on the portico steps, I was now indoors—sheltered from the storm by walls, windows, and ceiling. I had been carried somewhere—into a house—but which one?

My thoughts turned instinctively to the pensionnat in the Rue Fossette. Half-dreaming, I tried to place myself—was I in the great dormitory? One of the small ones? Yet nothing matched. There were no empty white beds, no long line of tall windows. “Surely,” I thought, “they haven’t brought me to Madame Beck’s own room?” Then my gaze fell upon an easy-chair covered in blue damask. Slowly, other pieces emerged—cushioned seats to match, a wood fire glowing brightly on a clear hearth, a carpet patterned with bright blue arabesques over a soft fawn ground. Pale walls bore a delicate garland of azure forget-me-nots entwined with golden leaves and tendrils. Between two windows hung a gilded mirror, flanked by curtains of blue damask.

In the mirror, I saw myself—not lying in bed, but stretched upon a sofa. I looked pale, spectral. My eyes seemed larger, darker, hollowed out by fatigue; my hair stood out stark against my ashen face. From the furnishings, the layout of doors and windows, it was clear this was a room unknown to me—unknown, even, in name or location.

Yet more puzzling still was the way my mind refused to settle. As I stared at the blue chair, it began to feel familiar. So did a scroll-shaped couch, a round centre-table draped with a blue cloth bordered in autumn-colored foliage. And above all, two little footstools with embroidered covers, and a small ebony-framed chair whose seat and back were stitched with vivid flowers on a dark background.

Struck by these details, I searched further. And there they were—old friends, scattered throughout the room. "Auld lang syne," the past whispered from every corner. On the mantel, two oval miniatures—faces I knew by heart: the pearls around high powdered heads, the velvet ruffs framing white throats, the full muslin kerchiefs, the lace ruffles on sleeves. On the shelf beside them, two china vases, remnants of a tiny tea-service, smooth as enamel, thin as eggshell, and a white alabaster group under glass—each flaw, each crack, I could have named like a seer.

And then—the hand screens. Drawings in pencil, finished like engravings. I gasped. These were mine—my own hands had traced those lines, stroke by stroke, years ago, when I was a girl, using a clumsy, finicky pencil now reduced to bones.

Where was I? Not just in space, but in time? For all these things belonged to another age, another country. Ten years had passed since I last saw them. Since I was fourteen, I had never set eyes on them again. I breathed aloud, “Where am I?”

Then a figure stirred—unnoticed until now. A woman, plain and unremarkable, dressed in a simple bonne’s cap and print dress. She spoke neither French nor English, her dialect incomprehensible to me. But she bathed my temples with cool, scented water, raised the cushion beneath my head, gestured for silence, and returned to her post at the foot of the sofa.

She knitted quietly. Her eyes averted, I studied her. How had she come here? What did she do among the relics of my childhood? Why did these scenes—these days—return to haunt me now?

Too weak to pursue the mystery, I dismissed it as a dream, a fever-fit. But I knew it wasn’t sleep. I believed myself sane. I wished the light were dimmer, so I wouldn’t see the pictures, the ornaments, the screens, the worked chair so clearly. But they were real—every detail exactly as I remembered—just as they had been in the drawing-room of my godmother’s house at Bretton. Only the proportions were different. That room had changed shape, but not its soul.

I thought of Bedreddin Hassan, carried in sleep from Cairo to Damascus. Had some unseen power lifted me from the church steps, borne me across land and sea, and laid me gently before the hearth of Old England? But no—this hearth was cold. Its fire burned no longer. The household gods had long since left.

The bonne rose again, seeing my eyes open. She put down her knitting, busied herself at a small stand, poured water, measured drops from a phial. She approached with a glass. What dark draught was this? Some elixir of the sages? Some magic potion?

It was too late to ask. I drank it passively, instantly. A tide of calm washed over my mind—soft, soothing, warmer than balm. The ache of weakness faded. My limbs grew heavy, but without desire to move. That was no loss. The bonne placed a screen between me and the lamp. I saw her rise—but not return. In that gap, I slipped into sleep.

When I woke, everything had changed. Daylight filled the room—not warm summer sun, but the dull, gray light of a raw autumn morning. I felt certain now: I was in the pensionnat. The rain beat against the windowpane. Wind howled through trees beyond—proof of a garden. The chill, the emptiness, the whiteness—it confirmed it. I say *whiteness*—for dimity curtains framed my view, drawn before a French bed.

I lifted them. Expecting the long, whitewashed chamber, I blinked instead at a small cabinet—walls of sea-green, a single high lattice shaded with muslin festoons. Instead of five wide windows, one alone. Instead of twenty painted stands with basins and ewers, a toilette-table dressed like a lady for a ball—white robe over pink skirt, polished mirror crowned, a pin-cushion frilled with lace. A low green-and-white chintz armchair, a washstand with marble top and pale-green ware—these furnished the tiny room.

Reader—I was alarmed. Why? You may wonder. What could frighten one in such a neat, even pretty chamber? This: those objects could not be real. They must be ghosts. Or—if that were too wild—then I must be ill, delirious. And even then, this was the most extraordinary delusion ever visited upon a mind.

Yet I knew them. The green chintz of the chair, the carved black frame of the mirror, the milky green of the china, the cracked marble top—each detail I recognized, as I had recognized the rosewood and porcelain of the drawing-room.

Bretton! Bretton! Ten years ago flashed in that mirror. Why did Bretton and my fourteenth year return to me now? Why only the furniture, not the rooms, not the place? That pincushion—crimson satin, gold beads, thread-lace—was mine. I had made it. I rose, took it in my hand. There, in gold, the cipher “L. L. B.”—Louisa Lucy Bretton. My godmother’s initials.

“Am I in England? Am I at Bretton?” I murmured. Hastily, I pulled up the blind. I expected St. Ann’s Street, the towers of the minster. Or perhaps a street in Villette. Instead, I saw through a frame of leafy branches—a grassy meadow, a lawn terrace, forest trees rising from lower ground. Tall beeches, groaning under October’s gale. Yellow leaves drifted in heaps, swept by the west wind. Beyond, flat land, hidden by the trees. Secluded. Strange. Unknown.

I lay down again. My bed stood in a small alcove. Turning my face to the wall, I hoped to shut out the confusion. But no—on the green space between the looped-up curtains, a broad gilded frame held a portrait. Watercolor, sketch-like, yet well done. A boy’s face—fresh, alive, animated. Sixteen, fair-skinned, rosy-cheeked, long hair with a golden sheen, piercing eyes, a playful mouth, a gay smile. A pleasant face—especially for those who loved him. Parents, sisters. A romantic schoolgirl might have fallen for it in its frame.

Those eyes—would they one day flash with love? I couldn’t tell. But his lips hinted at caprice, at fleeting regard, even as they smiled.

Quietly, I whispered to myself:

“Ah—this picture used to hang in the breakfast-room, too high, as I thought. I remember climbing a music-stool to unhook it, holding it in my hand, staring into those hazel eyes, their lashes like a pencilled laugh. I liked to study the blush of his cheek, the curve of his mouth.” Even now, I doubted fancy could improve on that chin, that lip. And yet—why did beauty so charm and so wound?

Once, I brought little Missy Home, lifted her in my arms. “Do you like it, Polly?” I asked. She said nothing, but her eyes darkened, trembled. “Put me down,” she whispered. So I did. “The child feels it too,” I thought.

Now, recalling all this, I added: “He had faults. But never a finer nature—liberal, kind, sensitive.” And aloud, I said: “Graham!”

“Graham!” echoed a voice at my bedside. “Do you want Graham?”

I turned. The mystery deepened. To see the portrait was strange enough. But to turn and find the living man—no, not the man, but his mother—tall, well-dressed, in widow’s silk, her braided hair beneath a modest cap—was astonishing. Hers was a good face—stronger now, perhaps, less soft, but full of character. She was unchanged—only sterner, more robust. But it was Mrs. Bretton. My godmother.

I kept silent. Inside, I trembled. My pulse fluttered. My cheeks grew cold.

“Madam,” I said, “where am I?”

“In a safe asylum,” she replied. “Well protected. Rest easy until you’re stronger—you look ill.”

“I’m so confused,” I said. “I don’t know if I can trust my senses. But you speak English?”

“I should think you could hear that,” she answered. “I’d find it hard to talk French for long.”

“You’re not from England?”

“I’ve just arrived. Have you been long in this country? You seem to know my son.”

“Do I? Perhaps. Your son—the portrait?”

“That’s his youth. You called his name.”

“Graham Bretton?”

She nodded.

“I’m speaking to Mrs. Bretton, formerly of Bretton, ——shire?”

“Quite right. And you—your pupils tell me you teach English in a foreign school here. My son recognized you.”

“How was I found? By whom?”

“My son will tell you later. But now you’re too weak. Try to eat something, then sleep.”

Despite everything—exhaustion, shock, exposure—the fever seemed to ease. For nine days I’d eaten nothing solid, suffered constant thirst. Now, when breakfast was offered, I felt hunger. I tasted the tea eagerly, ate the dry toast she gave me. It was little, but enough. Later, the bonne brought broth and a biscuit.

As evening fell, the wind still howled, the rain poured. I grew tired of the bed. The room, though pleasant, felt cramped. I longed for warmth, for firelight. And I thought of her son. When would I see him? Not till I left this room.

At last, the bonne came to make my bed. She prepared to wrap me in a blanket and carry me to the chintz chair. But I refused. I dressed myself.

Just as I sat down to rest, Mrs. Bretton returned.

“Dressed!” she exclaimed, smiling—her familiar smile, not soft, but pleasant. “You’re better then? Stronger?”

She spoke as she always had—as if I were still the child she once watched over. That same quiet authority, born not of wealth or rank, but of strength and natural care—like a tree sheltering a herb. I spoke without hesitation.

“May I go downstairs, madam? I’m cold and restless.”

“If you’re strong enough,” she said. “Come then. Here’s an arm.” She offered it. I took it. We descended a carpeted stair to a landing. A tall door stood open, leading into the blue-damask room.

How warm and welcoming it was—the amber glow of the lamp, the crimson flush of the fire. Tea waited—English tea. The silver urn, the massive pot, the thin porcelain cups with purple and gold—familiar to me. The seed-cake, baked in its peculiar mould, sat on the table. Graham liked it. It was placed before his plate, with silver knife and fork beside it. He was expected. He might even be here already.

“Sit down,” she said, as I faltered near the hearth. She seated me on the sofa. But I moved behind it, saying the fire was too hot. In the shade, I found a seat more to my liking.

Mrs. Bretton never made a fuss. She brewed tea, picked up the newspaper. I watched her—her movements young, alert. Though over fifty, she seemed untouched by age. Portly, yes—but quick. Serene, yet sometimes impetuous. Health and temperament kept her vibrant.

As she read, I sensed she listened—for her son. She never confessed worry, but the storm raged on. If Graham were out in that wind… I knew her heart would follow him.

“Ten minutes late,” she said, checking her watch. Then, a glance toward the door—her brow cleared. Soon, even my ear caught the clang of a gate, footsteps on gravel, the doorbell.

He was here. His mother filled the teapot, drew closer the blue chair—her own seat, but one he claimed without shame.

He came up the stairs—damp, wind-tossed, and strode straight in.

“Is it you, Graham?” she asked, hiding a smile.

“Who else?” he replied, claiming the chair with irreverent ease.

“Don’t you deserve cold tea for being late?”

“The urn sings cheerfully. I’ll get no deserts.”

“Wheel yourself to the table, lazy boy. Only my chair suits you. If you had any sense of propriety, you’d always leave it for the Old Lady.”

“So I should. But the dear Old Lady insists on leaving it for me. How is your patient, mamma?”

“Will she come forward and speak for herself?” Mrs. Bretton asked, turning to me.

I stepped forward. Graham rose courteously to greet me. He stood tall, a figure worthy of his mother’s pride.

“So you’re down,” he said. “You must be much better. I didn’t expect to meet you here—or thus. I was alarmed last night. If I hadn’t been called to a dying patient, I wouldn’t have left you. But my mother is something of a doctor, and Martha is an excellent nurse. I saw it was fainting—nothing dangerous. But I’ll learn what caused it soon. For now—do you really feel better?”

“Much better,” I said calmly. “Much better, thank you, Dr. John.”

Sometimes, the struggle against one’s deepest nature seems futile—like a storm battling the sea, with no end in sight. Yet even such efforts leave their mark. They nudge actions toward reason, even when feeling resists; they shape lives into something more balanced, quieter, more composed on the surface. And it is only the surface that most eyes see. What lies beneath—the hidden currents of sorrow, longing, and quiet prayer—belongs not to human judgment but to God. Man, weak as you are, cannot fathom your soul’s depths. But He can. Bring your secrets to Him. Show Him the pain You carry, the wounds You’ve endured, the questions You cannot answer. Kneel before Him, and ask for light in darkness, strength in weakness, patience in suffering. For though the waiting may be long, though the healing may come in ways you never dreamed, it will come. The waters will stir. The messenger will descend—not in the form you hoped, perhaps, nor at the moment you prayed for—but come He will. And those who wait by the pool, weeping through years of silence, will finally see the dawn.

Heaven’s time moves slowly. To mortal eyes, the flight of angels seems endless. Their journeys may span ages. One cycle of departure and return might embrace generations. Dust rises, breathes briefly, suffers, and returns to dust—forgotten again and again—while divine messengers circle far above. To many broken hearts, the first and only visitor is Azrael, the angel of death. But hope remains.

The next morning, I tried to rise. As I dressed, sipping cold water from the carafe on my washstand to steady my trembling hands, Mrs. Bretton entered. “Here is an absurdity!” she declared, her voice sharp and brisk. But she didn’t linger on words. In two minutes, with her usual decisive energy—once so familiar in how she managed her son, whom he often resisted—she had me back in bed. “There you stay till afternoon,” she said firmly. “My boy left strict orders, and I assure you, my son is master. You’ll have breakfast soon.”

And soon she brought it herself—her own strong hands carrying the tray, refusing to leave it to servants. She sat beside me while I ate. Not everyone, even among our closest friends, brings comfort in sickness. Some make us feel watched, exposed, burdened. But Mrs. Bretton was different. Her presence was like sunlight in a dim room. Her black eyes sparkled, her cheeks glowed with health, her hands moved with purpose. Her confidence, her clear mind, her quiet strength—it all lifted me. She was like a breath of clean air. Her son called her “the old lady,” yet she carried the vigor of youth. I marveled at how twenty-five years still seemed to live in her.

“I’d bring my work here,” she said, taking the empty teacup from me, “and sit with you all day—if only John Graham hadn’t forbidden it. ‘Now, mamma,’ he said, ‘don’t wear out your goddaughter with gossip.’ He insisted I stay in my own quarters and spare you my company. He says, Lucy, he thinks you had a nervous fever—judging by your look. Is that true?”

I hesitated. “I’m not sure what I had,” I replied. “But I suffered deeply—especially within.” I did not wish to speak further. The details belonged to a part of my life I could never share with her. To open up would be to invite her into a world she could not understand—a world of shadows and solitude. The difference between us could be likened to a stately ship sailing calm seas, crewed and guided, and a lifeboat resting dry in a dark shed—only launched when storms rage and danger calls. My godmother had never been cast upon such a sea. Her crew wouldn’t believe it. So the half-drowned sailor keeps his story to himself.

She left, and I lay back, content. It was kind of Graham to remember me before leaving. The day passed quietly, but the thought of evening made it bearable. I felt weak, and rest was welcome. After the morning’s restless energy—the invisible tasks, the sense of duty—afternoon settled like a soft blanket. The house grew still. The maid’s steps faded. And I drifted into a dreamy state, not unpleasant.

My small room seemed transformed—a cave beneath the sea. The colors were pale: white and green, like foam and deep water. The cornice bore shell-like ornaments; dolphins curved in the ceiling angles. Even the red satin pincushion looked like coral. The dark glass might have held a mermaid’s reflection. When I closed my eyes, I heard the wind subside—like a swell retreating from a rock. Far off, the tide withdrew from a distant shore. A world above, high and bright, sent down only murmurs and lullabies.

Evening came. Martha brought a light. With her help, I dressed quickly. Stronger now than in the morning, I walked unaided to the blue saloon.

Dr. John had finished his rounds earlier than usual. His figure stood in the window recess opposite the door, reading a newspaper by the fading light. The fire burned brightly, but the lamp remained unlit. Tea had not yet arrived. Mrs. Bretton, after a full day outdoors, lay half-reclined in her chair, asleep.

He saw me enter and stepped forward, moving carefully so as not to wake her. His voice was low, gentle—always smooth, never harsh. “This is a quiet little château,” he said, inviting me to sit near the casement. “Have you noticed it on your walks? From the road, it’s not visible. Just beyond the Porte de Crécy, you turn down a lane that becomes an avenue—through meadow and trees—to this very door. It’s not modern, but built in the old style of the Basse-Ville. More manoir than château. They call it ‘La Terrasse’ because its front rises from a broad grassy walk, with steps leading down to the avenue. See there—the moon is rising. She looks well through the trees.”

Where doesn’t the moon look well? Every scene, every landscape, is hallowed by her light. Now, she rose above a nearby bank—rosy at first, then flaming, then clearing to gold. In moments, she floated pure and bright into the calm sky.

Did the moonlight soften Dr. Bretton? Did it touch him with romance? I think it did. Though he was not given to sentiment, he sighed softly—quietly, to himself. No need to guess why. I knew it was beauty that stirred him—and Ginevra.

That knowledge pressed upon me. Perhaps it was my duty to name her. He was ready—I saw it in his face. A wealth of thoughts, questions, emotions waited behind his restraint. To spare him the awkwardness of beginning, I could simply speak her name. Love would then flow freely.

I had almost found the right words: “You know Miss Fanshawe has gone on tour with the Cholmondeleys…” But before I could speak, he shifted the subject.

“The first thing this morning,” he said, turning from the moon and sitting down, “I went to Rue Fossette and told the cuisinière you were safe. Do you know she hadn’t realized you were missing? She thought you were still in the dormitory. How carefully you must have been tended!”

“Oh, that’s easy to imagine,” I replied. “Goton could do nothing but bring me tisane and bread. I refused them so often that she gave up coming daily. Only once a day at noon to make my bed. But I believe she’s kind-hearted—would have cooked me mutton chops if I’d eaten them.”

“What did Madame Beck mean by leaving you alone?”

“She couldn’t foresee I’d fall ill.”

“Your nerves took a heavy toll?”

“I’m not sure what my nervous system is, but I felt utterly drained.”

“That makes treatment difficult,” he said. “Medicine can’t give good spirits. Hypochondria stands at the threshold—medicine sees the torture chamber but can do little. You need cheerful company. You should be with people. Exercise, too.”

I nodded. His advice sounded sensible, safe, conventional.

“Miss Snowe,” he continued, shifting gears, “may I ask—what is your religion? Are you Catholic?”

I looked up, surprised. “Catholic? No! Why would you think that?”

“The way you were handed over to me last night made me wonder.”

“Handed over? I don’t recall. I still don’t know how I came to be here.”

“Well,” he began, “under strange circumstances. Yesterday, I was attending a critical case—a rare illness, uncertain treatment. I saw a similar case in Paris, but that won’t interest you. Finally, the patient’s worst symptoms eased, and I left. My shortest route took me through the Basse-Ville. It was a wild, wet night. As I rode past an old church belonging to the Béguines, I saw a lamp burning over the entrance. A priest stood there, lifting someone in his arms. The light revealed his face—I recognized him. A man I’ve met at many sickbeds, rich and poor alike. A good man, better than most. Our eyes met. He called me to stop. He was carrying a woman—fainting or dying.”

“‘This is one of your countrywomen,’ he said. ‘Save her, if she’s not dead.’”

I was examined—unconscious, bloodless, nearly cold. “Who is she?” I asked.

He told a curious tale. That evening, you had gone to confession. Your appearance—exhausted, suffering—combined with what you’d said—”

“Things I said? I wonder what they were!”

“Awful crimes, no doubt,” he said, “but he couldn’t tell me. The seal of confession silenced him. Still, your confessions didn’t make him your enemy. On the contrary—he was so moved, so sorry you were out alone on such a night, that he felt it a Christian duty to follow you, unseen, until you reached home. Perhaps, unconsciously, he also wanted to learn where you lived—did you tell him?”

“No,” I said. “I avoided any hint. And as for my confession… Dr. John, you’ll think me mad, but I couldn’t help it. It was my ‘nervous system,’ as you say. I couldn’t express it—but my days and nights were unbearable. A terrible loneliness gnawed at me. A force inside me needed release—like the blood flowing through the heart, which, if blocked, seeks another way out. I needed companionship, friendship, counsel. I found none in my rooms. So I went to church. I didn’t confess sins—I had none. My life hasn’t been dramatic enough for that. All I poured out was despair.”

“Lucy,” he said gently, “you should travel—for six months. Your calm nature is becoming excitable. Confound Madame Beck! Does that little widow have no compassion, to leave her best teacher to suffer alone?”

“It wasn’t her fault,” I said. “No one’s fault. I won’t blame anyone.”

“Then who is to blame?”

“Me,” I said. “And Fate.”

“You must take better care,” he smiled, perhaps at my grammar.

“Change of air—change of scene,” he added. “Now, about our earlier question—Père Silas, despite his skill—still knows nothing. You didn’t return to Rue Fossette. Your wanderings—there must have been fever—”

“No,” I corrected. “The fever came later. Don’t pretend I was delirious. I was perfectly aware.”

“Good. Then you were as collected as I am now. You wandered in the opposite direction. Near the Béguinage, caught in the storm and darkness, you fainted. The priest found you. I followed. Together, we got a fiacre and brought you here. Père Silas, though old, insisted on carrying you upstairs himself. He would have stayed until you revived. I had to leave—my other patient was dying. Extreme unction couldn’t wait. We left you in Martha’s care. Now—will you convert?”

“No,” I said, smiling. “And don’t let Père Silas know where I live. He’ll try to convert me. But thank him for me. If I ever grow wealthy, I’ll send money for his charities. Look—your mother wakes. Ring for tea.”

He did. Mrs. Bretton sat up, startled, indignant at having slept. “I didn’t sleep!” she protested. “Only a momentary doze!”

“Hushaby, mamma,” he teased. “Sleep again. You look like innocence itself.”

“My slumbers? What nonsense! I never sleep by day.”

“Exactly—a seraph’s lapse, a fairy’s dream. Mamma, you always remind me of Titania.”

“That’s because you’re so like Bottom.”

“Miss Snowe—have you ever heard anything like mamma’s wit? She’s sprightly for her size and age.”

“Keep your compliments to yourself,” she snapped. “And don’t ignore your own size. It’s increasing. Lucy, does he look like an incipient John Bull? He used to be slender as an eel. Now, I see a heavy dragoon—beef-eater tendencies. Graham, take notice! If you grow fat, I disown you.”

“As if you could disown yourself! I’m indispensable to the old lady’s happiness. Without my six feet of iniquity, she’d pine in green melancholy. It keeps her lively—it maintains the ferment of her spirit.”

They stood on either side of the fireplace, arguing playfully. Their words weren’t tender, but their eyes spoke volumes. The greatest treasure of Mrs. Bretton’s life lived in her son’s heart. And he—though bound by love for her—had another passion, newer, stronger, claiming his soul. Ginevra. Ginevra. Did Mrs. Bretton know? Would she approve?

I couldn’t tell. But I suspected—if she saw Ginevra’s games, her coldness and coyness, her cruel teasing and sudden kindnesses—if she knew how she had hurt Graham, how she had humiliated him, preferred others over him—then she would call her foolish, or wicked, or both. I thought so too.

That second evening passed more sweetly than the first. We talked easily, without returning to old sorrows. Friendship deepened. I felt more at ease, more at home. That night, instead of crying myself to sleep, I slipped into dreams along a path lined with peaceful thoughts.

In the early days of my stay at the Terrace, Dr. John Graham never sat near me, nor did he come close when pacing the room. He moved with a quiet gravity, his expression thoughtful, but I felt certain he was thinking of Miss Fanshawe—of her name, her face, her presence. I waited, listening, ready to respond with tenderness and understanding whenever he might speak of her. My patience was like a soldier on guard, ever alert; my sympathy, a well-stocked fountain, prepared to flow freely at the slightest sign of need.

Then, after a brief inner hesitation—one I could see in his eyes, and which I respected—he finally brought up the subject. It came gently, almost anonymously. “I hear your friend is traveling for her vacation?” he said.

“Friend, indeed!” I thought, though I kept it to myself. To contradict him would have been unkind. He had to believe what he wished; so I nodded and let the word stand. But still, curious, I asked, “Whom do you mean?”

He had taken a seat beside my worktable. Now, he picked up a spool of thread and began unwinding it carelessly, as if distracted. “Ginevra—Miss Fanshawe,” he said. “She’s gone with the Cholmondeleys on a tour through southern France.”

“She has,” I replied.

“Do you two correspond?”

It surprised me that he should assume such intimacy between us. “You’ll be astonished,” I said, “to learn that I’ve never once thought to ask for that privilege.”

“You’ve seen letters from her?”

“Yes—several addressed to her uncle.”

“They must be full of wit and charm,” he said. “She has such sparkle, so little pretense in her soul.”

“She writes quite plainly enough when she writes to M. de Bassompierre,” I replied. “Anyone can read them.” (Indeed, Ginevra’s letters to her wealthy cousin were often nothing more than formal requests for money.)

“And her handwriting?” he asked. “Must be delicate, elegant, feminine?”

It was—so I told him.

“I truly believe everything she does is done well,” he said.

I hesitated. He pressed further: “And you, who know her—can you name any flaw in her?”

“She does many things very well,” I answered.

(Flirtation among them, I added silently.)

“When do you think she’ll return to town?” he asked soon after.

“Pardon me, Dr. John,” I said, “but I must clarify. You honor me by assuming a closeness with Miss Fanshawe that I don’t possess. I’ve never known her plans or secrets. Her true friends are elsewhere—among the Cholmondeleys, for example.”

He looked startled—as if I’d been wounded by jealousy, just as he was. “Please forgive her,” he said. “Judge her kindly. The dazzle of society may mislead her, but she’ll soon see these people are empty. She’ll return to you, stronger in trust, deeper in affection. I know the Cholmondeleys—they’re shallow, showy, selfish. But Ginevra values you more than a hundred such.”

“You are kind,” I said simply.

A sharp retort burned on my lips—I wanted to deny that I was her confidante, that I was hurt by her absence—but I held back. I allowed myself to be seen as the discarded, pining companion of the beloved Miss Fanshawe. But reader, it was not easy. It was painful.

Yet he went on: “Still, while I comfort you, I cannot comfort myself. I cannot hope she will do me justice. De Hamal is worthless, yet I fear he pleases her. A terrible illusion!”

That was too much. My patience snapped—without warning. Illness and fatigue had weakened me, made me brittle. “Dr. Bretton,” I burst out, “there is no delusion like your own. In every way but one, you are honest, clear-headed, sound-minded. But on this one point—you are enslaved. I say plainly: where Miss Fanshawe is concerned, you deserve no respect—and I give you none.”

I rose and left the room, my heart pounding.

The scene had happened in the morning. By evening, I had to face him again. And then I saw I had done harm.

Dr. John was not made of ordinary flesh. His nature was broad and strong, yet delicately wrought—finer, far finer, than most men’s. His sensitivity was hidden, unless something sharp touched it and revealed its depth. He didn’t wear his feelings openly; he wasn’t quick to perceive others’ emotions, but he *felt* deeply. He was generous, kind—always ready to help, always willing to listen. But expect subtlety, intuition, and you’d be disappointed.

That night, when he entered the room and the lamp lit his face, I saw him clearly—his whole being laid bare. To be called “slave” and stripped of respect—how must he feel? He didn’t argue against it. Instead, he quietly considered whether it might be true. He searched for answers in that accusation, wondering if it explained his failure, his unrest. He seemed distant, perhaps cold, to both me and his mother. Yet there was no bitterness, no malice. Only sorrow—a man’s quiet dignity even in pain.

When I hurried to place his chair and hand him his tea with trembling hands, he said, “Thank you, Lucy,” in a tone as warm and pleasant as ever.

For my part, I knew only one course: I must make amends—or I could not sleep. I couldn’t bear the tension. Convent silence, solitary study—anything seemed better than living in conflict with him.

As for Ginevra—she could fly on silver wings to the highest stars, to wherever her lover’s imagination placed her. I would no longer dispute the sky she occupied.

I tried long and hard to catch his gaze. Once, twice, our eyes met—then he looked away. There was nothing to say, so I remained silent.

After tea, he sat quietly, reading. I wished I could go sit beside him—but feared he’d react with anger. I longed to speak, but dared not whisper.

His mother left the room. Then, overwhelmed by regret, I murmured, “Dr. Bretton.”

He looked up. His eyes were not cold. His mouth was not cruel. He was open, willing to hear. His spirit was too gentle to sour in one moment.

“Dr. Bretton,” I said, “forgive my hasty words. Please, please forgive me.”

He smiled.

“Perhaps I deserved them, Lucy. If you don’t respect me, it’s because I’m not worthy. I’m an awkward fool—perhaps I fail in some way. Where I wish to please, I seem not to.”

“You can’t be sure of that,” I said. “Even if you do, is it your fault—or another’s blindness? But now, let me take back what I said in anger. In every way, I deeply respect you. If you think too little of yourself and too much of others—that is not weakness. That is nobility.”

“Can I think too much of Ginevra?”

“I believe you may,” I said. “You believe you cannot. Let us agree to differ. Just forgive me—that is all I ask.”

“Do you think I hold ill-will for one warm word?”

“I see you don’t—and can’t. But say it: ‘Lucy, I forgive you.’ Say it—so I can lay down my pain.”

“Put aside your pain,” he said, “as I put mine. You wounded me a little, Lucy. But now the pain is gone. I don’t just forgive—I’m grateful. As if you were a true friend.”

“I *am* your friend,” I said. “You are right.”

So ended our quarrel.

Reader, if later in this story you find my view of Dr. John changing, please forgive the shift. I write as I felt then—my perceptions as they appeared at the time. And he showed his depth by being kinder to me afterward than before.

Yes, the misunderstanding altered our relationship—though not as I feared. Something invisible, thin as frost, had always separated us—a fragile barrier of reserve. Those few angry words, however, melted it. From that day forward, he no longer stood on ceremony with me. He spoke freely, honestly, about himself, about what mattered most. And I found myself eager to listen.

Of course, I heard much about Ginevra.

“Ginevra!” he would say, his voice soft with admiration. “So fair, so good—her sweetness, her innocence!” And though I knew the truth—the plain, practical facts—I could not help feeling a glow around her image, reflected from his devotion.

Still, I confess—he often spoke nonsense. But I learned to be patient. I had been taught a lesson: crossing him, grieving him, displeasing him—each caused me deep pain.

In a strange new way, I became selfish. I gave myself entirely to indulging his moods, bending to his will.

And yet—when he stubbornly doubted his chances with Ginevra, I found myself believing more strongly than ever that she was only teasing him, playing games to keep him longing. At heart, I believed she cherished every glance, every word he offered.

Sometimes, despite my resolve, he tested me. In the midst of my quiet endurance, he struck a spark from my remaining firmness—and fire flashed.

One day, trying to calm him, I said, “In my mind, I’m certain Miss Fanshawe *must* eventually accept you.”

“Certain?” he asked. “Easy to say—but what grounds do you have?”

“The best.”

“Now, Lucy, tell me—what?”

“You know them as well as I. And knowing them, Dr. John, I’m amazed you don’t trust her fidelity. To doubt now is almost to insult.”

“Now you’re speaking fast,” he said, “and breathing short. Go on—faster, shorter—until you explain fully. I must have it.”

“You shall,” I said. “You’re generous—lavish, even. You’d give Père Silas alms, light his tapers, enrich his shrine. Ginevra, Dr. John—”

“Hush,” he said.

“No,” I insisted. “I will not. I *will* go on. Ginevra has received gifts from your hands more times than I can count. You’ve sought the rarest flowers, devised the most delicate presents—gifts so fine, only a woman could imagine them. And she owns a set of ornaments—purchased with generosity verging on extravagance.”

His face flushed—modesty, though he’d never shown it himself.

“Nonsense,” he said, cutting a silk thread with my scissors. “I gave them to please myself. I felt honored she accepted.”

“She did more than honor you,” I said. “She pledged her word—to repay you. And if she cannot pay in love, she owes you gold.”

“But you don’t understand her,” he said. “She’s too disinterested to care for gifts. Too simple to know their worth.”

I laughed. I knew how she valued each jewel. Money, schemes, scarcity—these had shaped her thoughts for years.

He continued. “Whenever I gave her something—no excitement, no joy. Just quiet acceptance. She’d leave a bouquet beside her, forget it. Or if I fastened a bracelet on her arm—however lovely it was—she wouldn’t look at it. Never dazzled.”

“So she returned it?”

“No. Too kind for that. She’d pretend to forget, keep it quietly, without thought.”

“In that case,” I said, “how can you build hope on such acceptance?”

“If I gave her everything,” he said, “and she took it—still, I’d hesitate. Her nature is beyond material sway.”

“Love is blind,” I began.

But then—his eye flickered. A blue, subtle ray—like a memory, like a secret—passed sideways. It reminded me of old days, of his portrait. For a moment, I wondered: was his belief in her naïveté genuine—or was it a mask?

Was he, perhaps, seeing her more clearly than he admitted?

It was only a glance—maybe imagined. But it silenced me. And so, the conversation ended.

My stay at La Terrasse extended beyond the end of the vacation, thanks to Mrs. Bretton’s thoughtful care. Her son had declared one day that “Lucy is not yet strong enough to return to that dreadful pensionnat,” and without delay, she drove to the Rue Fossette, met with the directress, and secured my continued rest under the pretense that full recovery required both quiet and change. But this kindness brought an unexpected visit—Madame Beck herself came calling.

One bright afternoon, she arrived in a fiacre, drawn all the way to the château. I suppose she wanted to see for herself what sort of place Dr. John inhabited. The grounds were lovely, the house neat and inviting—more than she expected, clearly. She praised everything: the blue salon was “une pièce magnifique”; she congratulated me warmly on having found such admirable friends—“tellement dignes, aimables, et respectables.” She paid me a compliment too, though it was delivered with her usual flourish. When Dr. John entered, she rushed toward him with uncontainable energy, launching into a torrent of French—brimming with praise for his home, his mother (“la digne châtelaine”), and his own appearance, which, indeed, looked robust and well-kept. He listened with a good-natured smile, amused but patient, as she poured out her admiration. That day, Madame Beck was radiant—like a catherine-wheel spinning with light and cheer. But when I followed her to the carriage, just before she climbed in, I saw a startling shift: the sparkle vanished. She sat rigid, grave, almost severe. A strange little woman.

I teased Dr. John afterward about her devotion to him. He laughed heartily—his eyes lit up as he mimicked her dramatic speeches. He had a keen sense of humor and made the most delightful companion—when he wasn’t thinking of Miss Fanshawe.

It’s said that sitting in gentle sunlight helps the weak regain strength. When little Georgette Beck recovered from illness, I would carry her in my arms and walk slowly through the garden beneath a wall heavy with ripening grapes. The sun warmed her frail body just as it ripened the fruit.

There are people whose presence brings comfort like sunlight does to the body. Dr. Bretton and his mother were among them. They didn’t seek to impress; they simply gave joy naturally—without effort or show. Every day during my stay, some small plan emerged that brought pleasure and meaning. Though Dr. John’s schedule was full, he always managed to join us on our outings. I never understood how he balanced so much—so many duties, yet always time left for us. I often saw him busy, but never overwhelmed, never flustered or irritable. His work flowed with ease, done with quiet strength and cheerful energy. Under his guidance, I saw more of Villette—its streets, its outskirts, its people—in two weeks than I had in eight months before.

He took me to places I’d never heard of, shared insights with genuine interest. Talking with him was never a chore—nor was listening. He avoided generalities and dry facts. Instead, he spoke with attention to detail, observing character with depth, not surface. This gave his words weight. And because he drew from his own experience—not from books or borrowed phrases—his thoughts felt fresh, alive, rare.

In those days, I began to see another side of him—one that bloomed like dawn. While his mother had deep kindness, Dr. John possessed something greater—a wider generosity. When we visited the Basse-Ville, the poorer part of town, I realized his visits weren’t only medical—they were acts of compassion. He moved among the destitute not as a duty, but as a man who simply couldn’t ignore suffering. The poor loved him; hospital patients greeted him with warmth, even reverence.

But let me pause. I must not drift into blind praise. I know well that Dr. John was not perfect—no more than I am. Human weakness touched every part of him.
