There was no moment when he didn’t reveal something less than divine. A god could not have borne his occasional cruelty, his vanity, or his fleeting indulgence in the present. He did not waste himself on excess, but he fed his self-love with quiet pride—taking delight in attention, in being admired, without regard for cost or consequence.

The reader may notice a contradiction: the public Dr. Bretton—the modest, tireless physician—and the private man—aware of his worth, enjoying praise, sometimes recklessly seeking it. Both are true.

It was hard to serve him quietly. You thought you’d made something small for him unnoticed—only for him to smile and say he’d watched every step. He liked knowing he was cared for, and he let it show in his eyes, in his voice.

And yet, he had a stubborn way of repaying kindness. If his mother worked for him, he returned it with laughter, teasing affection, and liveliness. If I helped him, he planned some quiet pleasure in return.

His knowledge of Villette amazed me—deep, thorough, intimate. Not just the streets, but hidden halls, museums, sacred spaces—he seemed to hold the key to every door. I knew nothing of science, but art stirred me. I loved visiting galleries, especially alone. In company, I felt drained—forced to pretend interest, to keep up conversation while half my mind shut down. No well-bred child or educated adult ever failed to shame me in such moments. But Dr. Bretton understood. He’d take me early, before crowds came, leave me there for hours, then return when his own duties were done. Alone, I could breathe. I didn’t always admire—I questioned, studied, judged. At first, there was conflict: my will demanded I praise what was supposed to be great, while my instinct refused. The harder I tried, the more I resisted. Eventually, I gave up. Why force myself? I settled into stillness, finding peace before ninety-nine out of a hundred paintings.

To me, a truly original painting was as rare as a great book. Standing before famous works, I dared to think: “This isn’t nature. Nature’s light never looked like this—never so murky, so unnatural.” The sky was indigo, but not sky-blue; the trees were thick, shapeless blobs. Some fat women painted with care struck me as anything but goddesses. Many Flemish scenes—exquisite in detail—were better suited to fashion plates than art. Yet here and there, truth broke through: a storm on a mountain, a southern sunlit day, a face that revealed genius, a portrait that caught a soul. These fragments I treasured—like friends.

One morning, nearly alone in a gallery, I found myself before a massive painting, placed in prime light, protected by a rope, with a cushioned bench before it—for those who wished to worship it long enough to need a seat. It seemed to rule the room.

It depicted a woman—larger than life, I thought. If weighed, she’d surely tip the scales between fourteen and sixteen stone. She was immensely fed—meat, bread, vegetables, liquids—must have gone into her frame. She lay half-reclined on a couch. But why? Daylight shone around her. She looked healthy, strong enough to work twice over. She had no excuse for lounging. She should have stood—or at least sat upright. And her clothes? Out of twenty-seven yards of fabric, she’d made something utterly inadequate. Then, the mess around her—pots, vases, goblets, flowers tangled together, curtains piled haphazardly—was unbearable. Checking the catalogue, I learned the name: “Cleopatra.”

I sat down—why not? The bench was there. I found the painting mostly absurd, though some details—roses, gold cups, jewels—were finely done. But overall, it was grandiose nonsense. As I studied it, the room filled. I didn’t mind. Soon I grew bored and turned to nearby still lifes—wildflowers, mossy nests, eggs gleaming like pearls beneath green water—all hanging humbly beneath that monstrous canvas.

Then a tap on my shoulder. I turned. A frowning face leaned close.

“Que faites-vous ici?” a voice asked.

“Mais, Monsieur, je m'amuse.”

“You amuse yourself? And how, pray? First, please rise. Take my arm—we’ll go to the other side.”

I obeyed. M. Paul Emanuel had returned from Rome, now a seasoned traveler, and no less strict than before.

“Let me escort you to your party,” he said, leading me across the room.

“I have no party.”

“You’re not alone?”

“Yes, Monsieur.”

“Did you come alone?”

“No. Dr. Bretton brought me.”

“Dr. Bretton and his mother, of course?”

“No—just Dr. Bretton.”

“And he told you to look at *that* picture?”

“Not at all. I discovered it myself.”

M. Paul’s hair was cut short—like raven feathers. I began to guess his intent and enjoyed keeping calm, watching him grow agitated.

“Astonishing English boldness!” he exclaimed. “Such strange women, these English!”

“What’s wrong, Monsieur?”

“Wrong? How dare you, a young lady, sit calmly like a boy, staring at *that* painting?”

“It’s ugly, but why shouldn’t I look?”

“Bon! bon! Let’s speak no more of it. But you must not be here alone.”

“If I have no society—no *party*, as you call it—what difference does it make? No one interferes with me.”

“Taisez-vous, et asseyez-vous là—là!” He set a chair firmly in a dull corner, before four particularly dreary frames.

“Mais, Monsieur?”

“Mais, Mademoiselle, sit and do not move—entendez-vous?—until someone comes for you or I give permission.”

“Quel triste coin!” I said. “Et quelles laids tableaux!”

Indeed, they were. Four paintings titled “La vie d’une femme.” Painted flat, pale, lifeless. The first: a “Jeune Fille” leaving church, missal in hand, dress prim, eyes down, mouth tight—a caricature of prudish hypocrisy. The second: a “Mariée,” kneeling, hands pressed together, eyes wide, showing the whites—annoyingly devout. The third: a “Jeune Mère,” gazing sadly at a swollen, unhealthy baby. The fourth: a “Veuve,” black-clad, holding a child, both studying a fancy tombstone. All were cold, ghostly, stiff. What women to live with—insincere, joyless, empty beings. As bad as the idle Cleopatra in her absurd splendor.

I couldn’t focus long on them. Slowly, I turned back to the gallery.

A crowd gathered around the Cleopatra—half of them ladies. Later, M. Paul explained: these were “des dames,” allowed to view what no “demoiselle” should glance at. I told him plainly I disagreed. He responded with his usual authority—demanding silence and accusing me of rashness and ignorance. Never had I seen a man so domineering in a professor’s role. Yet I noticed he stared at the painting himself with ease, lingering long. Occasionally, he glanced my way—checking if I obeyed.

After a while, he approached again.

“Had you been ill?” he asked. “I heard you were.”

“Yes—but I’m well now.”

“Where did you spend the vacation?”

“Mainly at the Rue Fossette—partly with Madame Bretton.”

“Heard you were left alone there—is that true?”

“Not entirely. Marie Broc was with me.”

He shrugged. His face shifted—anger, pity, disdain, sorrow—flickered across it. Marie Broc was known to him. Each lesson in the lowest class brought a battle within him: irritation at her appearance, her manners, her unpredictability—yet also pity for her suffering. His nature pulled him in opposite directions. He rarely yielded to anger, but when he did, it was terrifying. His passions were fierce, his attachments and aversions equally intense. Holding them back didn’t lessen their power—it only made them more visible. Such men often inspired fear, but that fear was misplaced. Nothing unsettled him more than dread in others. Nothing calmed him like trust, tempered with gentleness. But understanding that required insight—and few understood him.

“How did you manage with Marie Broc?” he asked after a pause.

“Monsieur, I did my best—but it was terrible to be alone with her.”

“So you lack courage. Perhaps charity too. You don’t have the qualities of a Sister of Mercy.”

[He was religious in his way—the self-sacrificing ideals of Catholicism deeply moved him.]

“I don’t know. I cared for her as well as I could. But when her aunt came to take her away, I was relieved.”

“Ah! You’re selfish. Women have nursed entire hospitals of such cases. Could you not have done the same?”

“Could Monsieur do it himself?”

“Women worthy of the name surpass our flawed, indulgent sex in such virtues.”

“I washed her, kept her clean, fed her, tried to entertain her. But she made faces instead of speaking.”

“You think you’ve done great things?”

“No—only what I could.”

“Then your powers are limited. Tending one idiot made you sick.”

“Not from that, Monsieur. I had a nervous fever—my mind was ill.”

“Vraiment! Vous valez peu de chose. You’re not heroic. Your courage fails in solitude—it only gives you the nerve to stare coldly at Cleopatra.”

I could have shown anger. But I hadn’t been angry with him before, and I didn’t feel like starting now.

“Cleopatra,” I repeated quietly. “Monsieur has been looking at Cleopatra too. What does he think of her?”

“Cela ne vaut rien,” he replied. “A magnificent woman—imperial height, Juno’s form—but one I’d never want as wife, daughter, or sister. So you will not look at her again.”

“But I’ve been looking—while you’ve spoken. I can see her clearly from here.”

“Turn to the wall and study your four pictures of a woman’s life.”

“Excuse me, M. Paul—they’re too hideous. If you admire them, I’ll leave you to them.”

“Mademoiselle,” he said, forcing a grimace that might have been a smile—though it was more like a twitch. “You Protestants astonish me. You Englishwomen walk through red-hot ploughshares without burning. I believe if some of you were thrown into Nebuchadnezzar’s furnace, you’d emerge untouched by fire.”

“Will Monsieur move an inch to the side?”

“How? What are you looking at now? Are you recognizing someone in that group of young men?”

“I think so—yes. There’s someone I know.”

One morning, Mrs. Bretton entered my room with brisk precision and asked me to open my drawers and show her my dresses. I did so without protest or hesitation.

“That will do,” she said after a brief inspection. “You must have a new one.”

She left, then returned shortly with a dressmaker, who measured me carefully. “I mean,” she declared firmly, “to follow my own taste and have things done my way—this time at least.”

Two days later, the dress arrived—a soft pink gown.

“That’s not for me,” I said quickly, already feeling a surge of resistance. The thought of wearing it was like donning the robes of some distant, exotic queen.

“We shall see whether it is for you or not,” my godmother replied, unshaken by my objection. Then, with that quiet authority that never allowed room for argument, she added: “Mark my words—you’ll wear it this evening.”

I believed I wouldn’t. I believed no force in the world could make me wear such a thing. A pink dress? It was foreign to me, as if it had come from another life entirely. I had never worn anything like it—and I wasn’t about to begin now.

Still, my godmother decreed that I would accompany her and Dr. Graham to a concert that very night. It was to be held in the grand hall of the principal musical society, a prestigious event featuring the most accomplished students from the Conservatoire. Afterward, there would be a lottery for charity, and—most remarkable of all—the King, Queen, and Prince of Labassecour were expected to attend. Graham had sent tickets and insisted on formal attire as a mark of respect to royalty. He also reminded us to be ready by seven o’clock.

At six, I was led upstairs. Without any struggle, I found myself guided by forces beyond my control—quietly overruled, gently but completely shaped by someone else’s will. The pink dress went on, softened with delicate black lace. I was pronounced *en grande tenue* and invited to look in the mirror. I did, hesitantly, trembling slightly. Then I turned away just as quickly. Seven o’clock struck. Dr. Bretton arrived. My godmother and I descended.

She wore a dignified brown velvet gown. As I walked beside her, I envied her solemn elegance—how gracefully those dark folds fell! In the drawing-room doorway stood Graham.

“I hope he won’t think I’ve been trying to draw attention,” I whispered to myself, anxious and ashamed.

“Here, Lucy,” he said, handing me a bouquet. “Some flowers.” He glanced at my dress—not critically, not admiringly—but with a kind smile and a nod that instantly eased my fears. His approval, simple as it was, settled something deep within me. The dress itself was modest—no flounces, no frills—only the color startled me. But since Graham saw nothing absurd in it, I soon began to accept it too.

I suppose those who attend concerts regularly cannot truly understand the excitement felt by someone for whom such an occasion is rare. Though I didn’t expect great joy from the music—I knew little of what to anticipate—I found pleasure in the journey there. The snug warmth of the closed carriage on a crisp, clear night; the companionship of two people so cheerful and generous; the stars flickering through the trees along the avenue; then the sudden openness of the sky as we passed into the city, the lights glowing at the gates, the guards standing watch, and the mock inspection that made us laugh—all of it stirred me deeply. Much of the charm, I suspect, came from the atmosphere of warmth and friendship around me. Dr. John and his mother were both in high spirits, teasing each other playfully all the way, treating me with the same ease and kindness they might have shown a family member.

We traveled through the finest streets of Villette—brightly lit, more alive now than at midday. How radiant the shops looked! How full and joyful the flow of life along the wide pavements! As I watched, the memory of the Rue Fossette surfaced—the walled garden, the schoolhouse, the vast, silent classrooms where, at this very hour, I used to wander alone, gazing at the stars through tall windows, listening to the distant voice reading the "lecture pieuse" in the refectory. Soon enough, I would return to that solitude. And the shadow of that future quietly dimmed the brilliance of the present moment.

By then, we’d joined a stream of carriages heading toward the same destination. Ahead loomed a magnificent building, blazing with light. I had only a vague idea of what awaited inside—no public theater or concert hall had ever welcomed me before.

We stepped under a portico crowded with people, bustling and chattering. I barely registered the details until I found myself ascending a broad, carpeted staircase—deep crimson underfoot—leading to massive doors, also draped in red, standing closed and solemn.

I hardly noticed how the doors opened—Dr. John handled that part. But they did open, revealing a hall so vast and dazzling that my breath caught. High ceilings arched above, painted in shimmering gold, broken by white cornices and garlands of alabaster and gilded leaves. Every surface seemed touched by art—walls curved like waves, columns rose proudly, and everywhere, crimson reigned: carpets, draperies, cushions. From the dome hung a chandelier so immense and brilliant it seemed not made by human hands, but by eastern genii. I imagined it might hold the magic lamp, and waited for a giant hand to appear, guarding its treasures. It was simply a chandelier, reader—but to me, it was a vision from another world.

We moved forward, unaware of our path—until suddenly, we met another group coming from the opposite side. For a fleeting instant, I saw them clearly: a woman in dark velvet, a man whose bearing and face struck me as noble and striking, and a third figure in pink with black lace.

I took them in—each one—believing them strangers. But almost immediately, I realized I was looking into a large mirror set between two pillars. The reflection showed our own party. For the first—and perhaps only—time in my life, I saw myself as others might see me. There was no need to linger on the result. It brought a pang of regret, a discordant note in my heart. Not flattering, certainly—but not devastating either. I should count myself lucky it wasn’t worse.

At last, we were seated in places offering a sweeping view of the hall. It was packed, filled with a distinguished crowd. The women weren’t necessarily beautiful, but their dresses were flawless. Even those who seemed awkward in private somehow carried themselves with grace when dressed for the occasion. Foreigners, especially, seem to know how to transform themselves—how to carry their heads, bend their arms, shape their expressions for display. The *grande toilette* brings out a reserve of poise, a practiced elegance, always worn with pride.

Among them were women of a particular beauty—one I had never seen in England. Their figures were solid, firm, sculptural—like marble caryatids or Phidian goddesses. They had no sharp angles, no frailness. Their faces were regular, round, calm—reminiscent of Dutch paintings, with low-country classic features. Their expression was profound stillness, a peace untouched by passion. A polar snowfield might be the only match for their depth of serenity. These women needed no jewelry; their hair, braided tightly, offered enough contrast to their smooth skin. Their dresses were plain, even severe—because their forms required no adornment.

Once, I had known one such woman intimately. She carried a quiet strength of self-love so powerful it was almost overwhelming. She could not care for anyone else—her devotion was entirely inward. Her veins ran cool, her blood sluggish; her arteries nearly blocked by placid lymph.

Such a woman sat directly before us—majestic, cold, blonde, and perfect as a white column topped with gold. She was a focus for every eye, aware of it yet unmoved by it. Impervious to admiration, she stood like a statue.

Noticing Dr. John’s gaze fixed upon her, I leaned close and whispered, “For heaven’s sake, guard your heart. You needn’t fall in love with *her*. I warn you—she might let you die at her feet and not care.”

“Very well,” he replied. “But how do you know the sight of her indifference might not stir me all the more? Desperation can be a powerful stimulant. Still,” he shrugged, “you know nothing of these matters. I’ll speak to my mother. Mamma, I’m in danger.”

“As if that interests me!” Mrs. Bretton retorted.

“Oh, the cruelty of my fate!” he lamented. “No man has ever had a less sentimental mother than mine. She never thinks such a disaster could happen to her—having a daughter-in-law.”

“If I don’t care, it’s not because I haven’t had it threatened for ten years. ‘Mamma, I’m going to marry soon!’ you shouted before you’d even grown out of jackets.”

“But one day, it will happen. Suddenly, when you think you’re safest, I’ll go forth like Jacob or Esau and take a wife—perhaps one of these daughters of the land.”

“At your peril, John Graham. That’s all.”

“My mother wants me to remain a bachelor forever. What a jealous old lady! But now—look at that splendid creature in pale blue satin, with hair like silk, shimmering like her dress. Wouldn’t you feel proud, mamma, if I brought her home and introduced her as Mrs. Bretton, junior?”

“You’ll bring no goddess to La Terrasse. That little château won’t hold two mistresses—especially if the second is as tall, broad, and round as that wooden doll in wax and kid and satin.”

“She’d fit your blue chair perfectly!”

“Fit my chair? I defy the foreign usurper! Let it be a sorrowful seat for her. But hush, John Graham! Hold your tongue and use your eyes.”

While they bantered, the hall—already full—continued to fill. Party after party arrived, until the semicircle before the stage became a dense sea of heads rising from floor to ceiling. The stage, really a large temporary platform, was now crowded. Around two grand pianos, a flock of young girls—pupils of the Conservatoire—had gathered silently. I had watched them assemble while Graham and his mother debated the belle in blue. I followed with interest as they were arranged and positioned. Two men oversaw them—both familiar to me.

One, a bearded man with long hair, was M. Josef Emanuel, a renowned pianist and the foremost music teacher in Villette. He taught twice weekly at Madame Beck’s pensionnat to privileged pupils. The other was M. Paul—his half-brother.

M. Paul amused me. He seemed utterly at home before such a grand audience, commanding and controlling nearly a hundred young ladies with effortless authority. He was earnest, energetic, absolute. Yet—what business did he have here? He couldn’t distinguish one note from another. I knew his presence stemmed not from musical talent, but from a love of spectacle and power—an innocent, almost childlike desire for dominance. It soon became clear that even his brother, M. Josef, was under his influence. Never had I seen such a tiny hawk of a man. As the real musicians appeared—famous singers and performers—M. Paul faded into the background. He couldn’t bear being overshadowed; where he couldn’t shine, he retreated.

Now, everything was ready—except one section of the hall, reserved for royalty. It was covered in crimson, furnished with cushioned benches flanking two regal chairs beneath a canopy.

A signal was given. The doors opened. The audience rose. The orchestra erupted. And to a chorus of welcome, the King, Queen, and Court of Labassecour entered.

Until now, I had never seen a king or queen in person. So I strained every sense to absorb these living symbols of European royalty. Anyone seeing royalty for the first time feels a strange mix of awe and disappointment—expecting a throne, a crown, a scepter. Instead, I saw a middle-aged man in uniform and a young woman beside him. Half cheated, half relieved.

The King was fifty, slightly bent, with grey hair. No face in the hall resembled his. I knew nothing of his character or habits. At first, the lines etched across his brow, around his eyes, near his mouth, puzzled me—like hieroglyphs carved in iron. But soon, I sensed their meaning. There sat a man in silent suffering—a nervous, melancholy soul. Those eyes had seen a ghost often—the ghost of Hypochondria. Perhaps she stood before him now, on the stage, amid the glittering crowd. Hypochondria rises in throngs, dark as doom, pale as sickness, nearly as strong as death. She whispers: “Not so happy,” and freezes the blood, dims the light.

Some might say it was the weight of the crown that bowed his head. Others, early loss. Both may have played a role—but they were poisoned by the deeper enemy: constitutional melancholy. The Queen, his wife, understood. A gentle, thoughtful woman, she bore a quiet shadow of grief upon her face. Not beautiful, not like the marble-featured women described earlier. Her form was slender, her features distinguished but tied to royal lineage—too familiar to delight fully. Yet her eyes—those were her own. They sparkled with pity, kindness, sweet sympathy. She moved not as sovereign, but as a loving woman. Her son, the Prince of Labassecour, leaned on her knee. Throughout the evening, I saw her watching the King, sensing his withdrawal, trying to draw him back with stories of the boy. She would listen, then repeat them softly to her husband. He would start, listen, smile—then sink back into silence. A poignant scene. Yet no one else seemed to notice. To the aristocracy and bourgeoisie alike, it was invisible.

With the royal couple came their court—foreign ambassadors, elite expatriates. They took the crimson benches. The ladies sat; most men stood. Their dark coats formed a somber backdrop to the splendor ahead. The splendor itself had layers: matrons in velvet and jewels in the middle, young girls in white, pale rose, or soft blue in the front—pure, simple, ethereal. These were the blossoms of Villette’s nobility. No gems, no elaborate hair, no rich fabrics—just youth, grace, and innocence. Some of them I recognized—two former pupils of Madame Beck’s: Mesdemoiselles Mathilde and Angélique. They’d struggled in class, unable to master even *The Vicar of Wakefield*. One had eaten more bread and butter than any human ought—once even stealing slices she couldn’t finish. Truths, yes—wholesome truths.

Another girl caught my eye—Ginevra Fanshawe. She sat beside the daughter of an English peer. Delicate, lively, unlike the stiff foreign beauties. Her hair wasn’t braided tight like a shell—it flowed naturally, curled and free. She chattered happily, content with herself and her place. I didn’t look at Dr. Bretton, but I knew he saw her. He grew quiet, answered his mother briefly, sighed often. Why sigh? Because he liked challenges—he loved pursuit under difficulty. Here was the ultimate challenge. His beloved shone from a height beyond reach. He couldn’t approach her, couldn’t be sure she’d even glance his way.

I watched to see if she would. Our seats were near the royal benches—she must see us. Soon, her eyes landed on Dr. John. Then she raised a glass to examine Mrs. Bretton. A minute later, she laughed and whispered to her neighbor. When the music began, her attention drifted to the stage.

As for the concert itself—there’s little worth recording. My impressions were those of a novice. The Conservatoire girls, frightened, played tremulously on the grand pianos. M. Josef stood beside them, but lacked the force of his brother, who could have compelled courage through sheer intimidation. M. Paul would have placed them between two fires—fear of the audience and fear of himself—and made the latter far greater. M. Josef couldn’t do that.

Then came a tall, sulky woman in white satin. She sang. Her voice amazed me—not for beauty, but for trickery. Like a conjuror, she made it leap and twist. But a simple Scotch melody played by a street musician once moved me far more.

Next, a gentleman stepped forward, bowing deeply toward the royal couple, touching his heart with gloved hand. He delivered a passionate lament against a “fausse Isabelle.” I thought he sought the Queen’s sympathy, but she listened with polite courtesy, not true emotion. His anguish was painful. I was glad when he finished.

The best part came afterward—rousing choruses. Delegates from provincial choirs—thick, hearty Labassecouriens—gave their voices with gusto. Their rough, honest singing satisfied the ear with raw power.

And so the evening passed—splendid, strange, unforgettable.

Three days more, and I must return to the _pensionnat_. I counted each moment as if time were marked on a clock—each second a tiny loss, each minute a step toward an end I longed to reach yet dreaded. I wished the hours would slow, but they slipped away like water through fingers. Before I knew it, they were gone, even as I still clung to them.

“Lucy won’t leave us today,” Mrs. Bretton said gently at breakfast, “we can easily ask for another delay.”

“I wouldn’t ask for one, even if I could,” I replied. “I want this farewell over with. I want to be back in the Rue Fossette again. I must go now—this morning—my trunk is packed and secured.”

Yet my departure was not entirely in my hands. Graham had promised to accompany me, but he was occupied all day, returning only at dusk. Then came a brief exchange of words. Mrs. Bretton and her son urged me to stay one night longer. I wanted to cry—I was so restless, so eager to leave. It felt like the criminal on the scaffold yearning for the axe to fall: I just wanted the pain to end. How deeply I felt it, they could never know. My emotions were beyond their understanding.

It was dark when Dr. John helped me from the carriage at Madame Beck’s door. The lamp above glowed softly; rain drizzled down, as it had all day. The lamplight shimmered on the wet pavement. This was the same kind of night that had brought me here not a year ago—the very same scene. I remembered the shape of each paving stone, once noted idly while my heart pounded with anxiety, waiting for the door to open. I stood then alone, a supplicant, unknown and afraid. On that night, too, I had met him—Dr. John—who now stood beside me. Had I ever told him of that first meeting? No. And I never felt the need to. It was a quiet memory, kept safely within me, where it belonged.

Graham rang the bell. The door opened instantly—this was the hour when half-boarders left, so Rosine was alert.

“Don’t come in,” I said, but he stepped into the well-lit vestibule anyway. I didn’t want him to see how close I was to tears. His nature was too tender—he would feel compelled to soothe, to heal, even when nothing could truly be mended.

“Hold fast, Lucy,” he said. “Think of my mother and me as true friends. We’ll never forget you.”

“And I will never forget you, Dr. John,” I whispered.

My trunk was carried inside. We shook hands. He turned to leave—but hesitated. He hadn’t done enough, hadn’t said enough, to satisfy his generous heart.

“Lucy,” he called after me, “will you feel terribly alone here?”

“At first, yes.”

“Well, my mother will visit soon. And I’ll do something—I’ll write. Just whatever cheerful nonsense comes to mind. Will you let me?”

“Good, gallant heart,” I thought to myself. But I smiled and shook my head. “No need. Don’t burden yourself. You write to *me*—you’ll have no time.”

“Oh, I’ll find time. Goodbye!”

He was gone. The heavy door slammed shut. The axe had fallen. The pain was real.

I gave myself no time to think or mourn—swallowing my tears as though they were wine—I moved directly to Madame Beck’s sitting room to make the formal visit expected of me. She received me with practiced warmth, even showing a hint of affection, though brief. Ten minutes later, I was dismissed. From there, I went to the refectory, where pupils and teachers gathered for evening study. I was welcomed again—not with hollow formality, I believed, but with genuine kindness. After that, I was free to return to the dormitory.

“And will Graham really write?” I asked, sinking onto the edge of my bed, weary.

Reason crept up to me through the dim twilight of the long chamber, whispering quietly: “He may write once. His kindness might inspire him. But it cannot last—it must not repeat itself. To build your hopes on such a promise is folly. To mistake a fleeting puddle holding one sip for a spring that feeds the seasons—that is madness.”

I bowed my head. I sat in silence for an hour. Reason continued, placing a cold hand on my shoulder, touching my ear with lips as blue as winter.

“If,” she murmured, “if he should write—what then? Do you imagine pleasure in replying? Fool! I warn you. Keep your answer short. Expect no joy—no comfort—no expansion of feeling. Let no faculty bloom. Allow no warm exchange. Nurture no bond of friendship…”

“But I’ve spoken to Graham,” I pleaded. “You didn’t object.”

“No,” she said. “I needed not. Conversation is good discipline. You speak imperfectly. While you talk, you remember your place—you are reminded of hardship, poverty, sacrifice. Your words carry the weight of it.”

“But,” I insisted, “when speech fails, when presence is weak, isn’t it right to use writing—to express what faltering lips cannot?”

Reason answered only, “At your peril you hold to that belief. At your peril you let it guide any word you write.”

“But if I feel—may I never express it?”

“Never!” she declared.

I groaned beneath her harshness. Never—never—oh, cruel word! This hag, this Reason, refused me light, laughter, hope. She demanded only crushing, submission, despair. According to her, life was merely labor for bread, endurance of pain, and constant mourning. Reason might be right—but no wonder we sometimes rebel, flee from her rod, and give ourselves one truant hour to Imagination—her gentle, bright rival, our sweet comfort, our divine hope. We must break bounds, despite the vengeance that follows. Reason is as vengeful as a demon. For me, she was always a venomous stepmother. I obeyed her not out of love, but fear. Long ago, I would have died under her sting—her emptiness, her coldness, her endless blows—had it not been for that kinder power who holds my secret allegiance.

Often Reason cast me out into winter darkness, tossing me a bone dogs had abandoned. She swore her stores held nothing more. Yet when I looked up, I saw a face among stars—a radiant one, brighter than all—sending down a ray of sympathy. A spirit softer than human reason descended, bringing air like eternal summer, flowers that never fade, trees whose fruit is life, breezes from a world without sun. She fed my hunger with strange, sweet food gathered by angels at dawn, eased my unbearable fears, calmed my weariness, revived my paralyzed hope. Divine, compassionate, sustaining force! When I kneel before anything less than God, it shall be at thy white wings—whether on mountain or plain. Temples have been built to the Sun, altars to the Moon. Oh, greater glory! To thee no hands raise stones, no lips utter prayers—yet hearts across ages worship thee faithfully. Thou hast no walls, no dome—thy temple is space, thy rites the harmony of worlds!

Sovereign, complete! Thy strength lies in martyrs, thy triumph in heroes. Unquestioned deity—thou art beyond decay.

This heavenly daughter remembered me tonight. She saw me weep and came with comfort: “Sleep,” she said. “Sleep sweetly—I will gild your dreams.”

She kept her word, watching me through the night. But at dawn, Reason took her place. I woke with a start—the rain beat against the windows, the wind moaned. The night-lamp flickered on its black stand in the middle of the dormitory. Day had broken. I pitied those whose minds are stunned by sorrow instead of stirred. This morning, the pang of waking pulled me from bed as if gripped by a giant hand. How swiftly I dressed in the chill of the early dawn! How deeply I drank the icy water from my carafe—my usual tonic, like others who seek solace in strong drink when shaken by grief.

Soon the bell rang its _réveillée_ for the whole school. Dressed, I descended alone to the refectory, where the stove burned and the air was warm. Elsewhere in the house, it was cold—the biting severity of a continental winter. Though November had barely begun, a northern wind had already brought frost across Europe. I had disliked the stoves when I first arrived, but now I found comfort in them—as English folk do by a fireside.

Seated near the stove, I fell into deep thought about life, chance, fate. My mind, calmer and stronger than the night before, set strict rules: no looking back at past joys, no indulgence in nostalgia. Only patient movement through the present wilderness, trust in faith, attention to the guiding cloud and pillar—both awe-inspiring and illuminating. No idolatry of emotion, no longing for a distant paradise that may only be seen from a dying height.

Gradually, a mingling of strength and sorrow wound itself around my heart, steadying my pulse, preparing me for the day. I lifted my head.

As I mentioned, I sat near the stove, built into the wall beneath the refectory and the carré, warming both rooms. Beside it was a window opening into the carré. As I raised my eyes, a cap-tassel, a brow, two eyes filled the pane. Those eyes fixed on mine. They were watching me. I hadn’t known I was crying—until now I felt the tears on my cheek.

What a strange house, where no corner is safe, where no tear escapes notice, no thought remains hidden. And this new intruder—this outdoor, male spy—what business brought him here at such an hour? What right did he have to look at me so? No other professor would dare cross the carré before class. M. Emanuel cared nothing for hours or propriety. He needed a reference book from the first-class library. He came to find it—and passed the refectory. He wore eyes everywhere—he saw me through the window. Now he opened the door and stood before me.

“Mademoiselle, vous êtes triste.”

“Monsieur, j’en ai bien le droit.”

“You are sad and rebellious,” he continued. “I see two tears on your cheek—hot as sparks, salty as sea crystals. As I speak, you watch me oddly. Shall I tell you what you remind me of?”

“Monsieur,” I said, “I will soon be called to prayers. My time for conversation is brief—please excuse——”

“I excuse everything,” he interrupted. “My mood is so gentle—neither rebuff nor insult could disturb it. You remind me of a young wild creature, newly caught, untamed, facing the first approach of the tamer—with fire and fear in its eyes.”

Unjust intrusion—rude, reckless, especially from a teacher. He sought to provoke a response. I’d seen him rouse passion before. In me, he would find no satisfaction. I remained silent.

“You look,” he said, “like one who would take a draught of sweet poison and reject wholesome bitters.”

“Indeed, I’ve never liked bitters. Nor believe them wholesome. And whatever is sweet—poison or food—cannot deny its own delight. Better to die quickly with pleasure than live long without charm.”

“Yet,” he replied, “you should take your bitter dose daily—if I had the power to give it. And as for the beloved poison—I would shatter the cup that holds it.”

I sharply turned my head away—partly because his presence disturbed me, partly to avoid questions. Fear that answering might betray me.

“Come,” he said more softly, “tell me the truth. You grieve at parting from friends—is it not so?”

The softness was no more welcome than the curiosity. I stayed silent. He entered the room, sat on the bench two yards away, and persisted, patiently, in trying to draw me out—efforts doomed to fail, for I could not speak. Finally, I begged to be left alone. As I spoke, my voice trembled, my head sank onto my arms and the table. I wept quietly, but fiercely. He stayed a while. I did not lift my eyes or speak until the closing door and his retreating steps told me he was gone. These tears brought relief.

I had time to wash my face before breakfast. I suppose I appeared as composed as anyone else—though not quite as cheerful as the young woman who took the seat opposite, twinkling with amusement, and stretched a white hand across the table to shake mine. Miss Fanshawe, returned from travel and flirtation, had grown plump, her cheeks round as apples. I had last seen her in evening dress. But now, in her school gown—a careless peignoir of dark blue, faintly plaided with black—she seemed even more charming. The contrast made her skin glow, her bloom freshen, her golden hair shine.

“I’m glad you’re back, Timon,” she said. Timon was one of her many names for me. “You don’t know how often I’ve missed you in this dreary place.”

“Oh, have you? Then surely you have work for me—stockings to mend, perhaps.” I never believed Ginevra acted without motive.

“Crabbed and crusty as ever!” she laughed. “I expected nothing less. It wouldn’t be you otherwise. But now, come, grandmother—do you still love coffee and hate pistolets? Are you ready to trade?”

“Take your own way.”

Her way was to make things convenient for me. She didn’t care for the weak, sour coffee served at school. But she loved the morning pistolets—fresh-baked rolls, delicious, and given in generous portions. Since I didn’t need mine, I always gave half to her. She, in turn, sometimes shared her coffee. This morning, I welcomed the drink. I wasn’t hungry—but I was parched. I don6't know why I chose to share with her rather than another. Or why, when we shared a drink on walks, I always let her have the larger portion—be it beer, wine, or milk. So it was, and she knew it. Thus, though we quarreled daily, we never drifted apart.

After breakfast, I retreated to the first classe, where I read—or more often, thought—alone until the nine-o’clock bell signaled the arrival of externes and demi-pensionnaires, and the start of the day’s bustle, which lasted until five P.M., unrelenting.

I had just seated myself when a tap came at the door.

“Pardon, Mademoiselle,” said a pensionnaire, entering softly. She took a book from her desk and withdrew on tiptoe, muttering as she passed, “Que mademoiselle est appliquée!”

Appliquée indeed! The tools of study lay before me, but I did nothing. Nothing had been done, and nothing would be. The world gives credit for virtues we lack. Madame Beck herself considered me a serious scholar, often warning me not to study too much—lest “the blood go to my head.” Everyone in the Rue Fossette believed “Meess Lucie” was learned—except M. Emanuel, who, by some means of his own, had glimpsed my true abilities and enjoyed chuckling in my ear over their modest limits. For my part, I cared little. I loved thinking my own thoughts. I enjoyed reading a few books—those where the writer’s soul was visible in style or sentiment. I lost interest in characterless works, however clever. I understood my mind had limits—thankful for what it had, content with its scope, never craving more.

The polite pupil had scarcely left when another burst in—unannounced, uninvited. I would have known him even blind. My reserved manner had gradually shaped the behavior of those around me. Rarely now did I suffer rudeness. At first, Germans would slap my shoulder, challenge me to races. Labassecourien girls would grab my arm, drag me to the playground, insist I join games. But these had stopped—without my needing to forbid them. Now, only one person dared familiarities—and since she was English, I could endure it. Ginevra Fanshawe often caught me crossing the carré, spun me into a forced waltz, and delighted in my disarray. She was the one who now broke into “my scholarly solitude.” She carried a large music book under her arm.

“Go practice,” I said at once. “Off to the little salon!”

“Not till I’ve talked to you, chère amie. I know where you’ve been on vacation, and how you’ve been courting beauty and enjoying life like any other belle. I saw you at the concert the other night—dressed, actually, like everyone else. Who is your tailor?”

“Tittle-tattle begins prettily! My tailor? Nonsense. Go away, Ginevra. I really don’t want company.”

“But when I want yours so much, ange farouche, does your slight matter? Dieu merci! We know how to handle our gifted countrywoman—the learned ‘ourse Britannique.’ And so, Ourson, you know Isidore?”

“I know John Bretton.”

“Oh, hush!” (She put her fingers in her ears.) “You crack my eardrums with your Englishisms. But—how is our dear John? Tell me. Poor man, he must be devastated. What did he say about my behavior the other night? Wasn’t I cruel?”

“Do you think I noticed you?”

“It was a wonderful evening. Oh, that divine de Hamal! Watching the others sulk and fade in the distance—then the old lady—my future mother-in-law! But I fear Lady Sara and I were a bit rude in teasing her.”

“Lady Sara never teased her. And as for you—don’t worry. Mrs. Bretton will survive your jest.”

“She may—old ladies are tough. But poor John! Tell me what he said. I saw he was shattered.”

“He said you looked as if you were already Madame de Hamal.”

“Did he?” she cried, delighted. “He noticed that? How charming! I thought he’d be furious with jealousy.”

“Ginevra, have you truly finished with Dr. Bretton? Do you want him to give you up?”

“Oh! You know he _can’t_—but wasn’t he furious?”

“Quite,” I agreed. “As mad as a March hare.”

“Well, and how _ever_ did you get him home?”

“How _ever_, indeed! Have you no pity for his poor mother and me? Imagine us holding him down in the carriage, him raging between us, nearly driving us all mad. Even the coachman got confused—we lost our way.”

“You don’t say so? You’re teasing me. Now, Lucy Snowe—”

“I assure you it’s true—and true that Dr. Bretton refused to stay in the carriage. He broke free and insisted on riding outside.”

“And afterwards?”

“Afterwards—when he finally reached home—the scene was unbearable.”

“Oh, but describe it—you know it’s fun!”

“Fun for _you_, Miss Fanshawe? But”—with solemn gravity—“you know the proverb: ‘What is sport to one may be death to another.’”

“Go on, darling Timon.”

“Conscientiously, I cannot—unless you swear you have a heart.”

“I have—one so vast, you don’t know!”

“Good. Then you’ll understand Dr. Graham Bretton refusing his supper—the chicken, the sweetbread untouched. Then—well, it’s pointless to dwell on the details. Suffice it to say, his mother had never struggled so hard to keep his sheets tucked in during his worst childhood fits.”

“He wouldn’t lie still?”

“He wouldn’t lie still. The sheets could be tucked—but keeping them tucked in was the battle.”

“And what did he say?”

When silence had fallen over the house—after dinner was done, after the clamor of the evening hour had faded, and darkness had settled in like a soft blanket—I slipped away to the kitchen. I asked for a candle, just for half an hour, for a very special purpose. Goton, my kind friend, smiled and said, “Of course, chou-chou—two candles if you want them.” With that small flame in hand, I crept upward, quiet as a shadow, toward the dormitory.

But there, tucked beneath her nightcap’s lace trim, lay Mistress Ginevra Fanshawe—her figure rumpled, her breath shallow. She seemed asleep, but I knew better. Her eyelids flickered, betraying a watchful stillness. She’d be awake soon enough, and then—oh, the chatter! The endless talk that would flood in at the worst possible moment. I couldn’t stay. I longed for solitude—just one quiet moment—to read the letter I’d waited so long to open.

So I turned away. I went to the classrooms, hoping for peace there. But no—candles lit, benches stacked, dust swirling through the air, coffee grounds scattered across the floor like dark rain. The rooms were being cleaned, and all was chaos. Frustrated, I did not give up. I would find somewhere—anywhere—where I could be alone.

I took a key from its hiding place and climbed three flights of stairs. At last, I reached a narrow, silent landing, opened a warped, worm-eaten door, and stepped into the garret—a cold, black, forgotten space high above the world. No one would come here. Not even Madame Beck. I shut the door behind me, placed the candle on a dusty chest, wrapped myself in a shawl against the chill, and pulled out the letter.

My hands trembled—not with fear, but with longing. I broke the seal slowly, as if afraid to wake something too precious. *Would it be long? Short? Kind? Cool?* I brushed my fingers across my eyes, clearing the silver haze of emotion that clung to my vision like a gentle shower.

It was long. And kind. So kind—more than I had dared hope. To my guarded heart, which had braced itself for disappointment or worse, it felt like mercy. To my hungry soul, starved for warmth and meaning, it felt like salvation. I had feared so much, wished so little—yet here was abundance. This quiet joy, this simple pleasure of reading words meant for me, carried a sweetness I had never known before. In that frosty attic, lit only by a guttering candle, I was happier than queens in gilded halls. It was brief, yes—but while it lasted, it was real. A bubble, yes—but a honey-sweet one.

Dr. John had written with such warmth. He spoke of our walks, our talks, the places we’d seen together—the way sunlight fell on the water, the laughter shared under trees. He wrote as though he, too, remembered them fondly—not just for my sake, but because they brought him joy. That thought pierced me: he had poured his heart out not merely to please me, but because he wanted to. And that—though it might never happen again—was enough for now. For this moment, nothing marred the peace. It felt as though a seraph had paused beside me, rested a wing upon my beating heart, and breathed healing into my spirit.

Dr. John, you wounded me later. But forgive me—I forgive you—for the sake of that one moment of grace.

Then—something stirred. A sound. A whisper of movement. Was it the wind? Or footsteps?

I froze. My candle flickered. From the far corner, near the pile of cloaks where the malefactors’ garments hung like ghosts, I heard it again—a soft glide across the floor.

I turned. The room stretched long and dark. And there—in the center—stood a figure. Black skirt, straight and narrow. White veil wrapped tightly around the head. The face hidden. But the eyes—glinting, fixed, cold.

A nun.

I gasped. My blood turned to ice. If she had moved closer, I would have fainted. Instead, she retreated. I bolted for the door.

How I descended the stairs, I do not know. Instinct guided me. I avoided the refectory and rushed to Madame Beck’s sitting room. I burst in, breathless, trembling.

“There is something in the garret!” I cried. “I saw it. Go—look for yourselves!”

“Everyone,” I insisted, though only four were present—Madame Beck, her mother, her brother M. Victor Kint, and another man speaking quietly to the elder woman. They rose, startled. I pointed toward the stairs, pleading for them to follow. Men were there—hope, strength. I needed them.

But then—my letter. Left on the chest. Alone. Without it, I could not breathe.

I ran back, calling them after me. When I reached the garret, the candle was gone—snuffed out. Darkness swallowed everything. But someone had brought a lamp. Light cut through the gloom.

The candle stood extinguished. But the letter—where was it?

“Oh! They’ve taken it!” I sobbed, searching wildly, clawing at the floor. “My letter! My letter!”

“What letter, Lucy?” a voice asked—familiar, close.

I looked up.

And there he was—Dr. Bretton himself. John Graham. He had been called that evening to attend Madame Kint. He had been in the room when I entered.

“Was it *your* letter?” he asked gently.

“Yours,” I whispered. “You wrote it. I came here to read it—alone. I saved it all day. I didn’t open it until now. I barely glanced at it. I can’t bear to lose it.”

He held my hand—warm, steady—led me down to the salon, where fire burned. We sat before the stove.

“You don’t need to cry,” he said softly. “What does it matter? Come—let’s go. They’re sending for the police. We don’t need to stay.”

But I could not stop. “I can’t help it,” I murmured. “It was everything.”

He watched me, calm, kind. Then, quietly—“Here. Your letter.”

I stared.

He had found it on the floor. Hidden in his pocket.

A trick? A test? A kindness?

Tears welled again—not from grief, but from relief. I couldn less. I could not speak.

“Are you satisfied now?” he asked.

“Yes,” I whispered. “Happy.”

“Good. Now—how do you feel? You’re still trembling.”

“I’m calm,” I said. “No longer afraid.”

“Then tell me what you saw. You said ‘something.’ But what?”

“I won’t say,” I replied. “Unless someone else sees it too. Otherwise, I’ll be accused of dreaming.”

He leaned forward. “Tell me,” he urged. “As your doctor. I see more in your eyes than you think. Your cheek is pale. Your hand won’t still. Speak.”

“You’ll laugh,” I warned.

“If you don’t tell me, you’ll get no more letters.”

I laughed weakly. “You’re laughing now.”

“I may laugh again—only to help you. Lucy, I care for you. I want to understand.”

His smile vanished. His expression softened. He became serious, almost tender.

So I told him—everything. About the legend of the house, the stories I’d once told him during a ride through Bois l’Etang. About the figure in black and white, standing still, watching.

He listened, thoughtful. Then, as others passed downstairs, he sighed.

“They’ll talk about thieves,” he said. “Let them. But you—keep your secret. Don’t speak of the nun.”

“I don’t know if it was real,” I confessed. “Or if it came from my mind.”

“It was a spectral illusion,” he said gently. “A product of prolonged mental strain. Your nerves are unsettled.”

“Can it happen again?” I asked, chilled.

“Possibly. But happiness is the cure. A cheerful mind, the preventive.”

“Cultivate happiness?” I echoed bitterly. “Is that how you do it?”

He smiled. “I’m naturally cheerful. Adversity touched us once—my mother and I—but we laughed at it. It passed.”

“And you never feel sorrow?”

“Only once—about Ginevra Fanshawe.”

“She made you suffer?”

“Pooh! Nonsense. I’m better now.”

And he was. His eyes sparkled with health, his posture strong, his voice light.

“Why can’t you be like me?” he asked. “Buoyant, fearless? Try to snap your fingers. Just once.”

I hesitated.

“If Miss Fanshawe came now—would you flinch?”

“I’d let her try to move me. Only by true love—passionate, real love—would I yield. I’d demand forgiveness at no lesser price.”

“Once, her smile was worth gold to you.”

“Transformed,” he said. “I am free now.”

He stood tall, radiant, unshackled.

“Miss Fanshawe led me through a phase,” he continued. “Now I ask for passion in return. Love for love. And plenty of it.”

“Ah, Doctor,” I murmured, “you once loved under difficulty—charmed by pride and indifference.”

He laughed. “My nature changes. One mood mocks the next.”

He pulled on his gloves. “Will the nun come again tonight?”

“I don’t think so.”

“Tell her—Dr. John sends his compliments. Ask her to wait for a visit.”

“She had a white cloth over her face,” I said. “But her eyes—glittered.”

“Cold and fixed,” I added.

“Confusion to her ghostly dress!” he said, laughing. “But those eyes—bright, soft.”

“No,” I said. “Cold. Unfeeling.”

“Then she shall not haunt you,” he declared. “Give her a firm shake of the hand if she returns. Will she stand that?”

I smiled. I believed she wouldn’t.

And had there been anything in the garret?

They searched. Found nothing. The cloaks were undisturbed. The broken pane in the skylight? Common. Hail had fallen days before.

Madame Beck questioned me closely. I described only a shadowy figure in black. I never said “nun.” I knew she’d dismiss it as fantasy.

She praised my discretion. Told me not to speak of it to anyone.

So the tale ended.

But I—left alone in my thoughts—wondered still.

Was it real?

Was it dream?

Or was it born of my own troubled heart?

And if it was, could it return?

To wonder sadly? No—something new stirred within me, pushing sorrow aside for a time. Imagine a hidden valley, cradled deep in the forest’s quiet, cloaked in mist and shadow: its grass damp, its plants pale and dew-laden. Then, one day, a storm or an axe opens a break in the ancient oaks. The wind rushes in; sunlight spills down. That cold, dim hollow transforms into a cup of radiant light, filled with summer’s golden glow and the blue brilliance of a sky it had never known before.

A new belief took root in me—the belief that happiness was possible.

It had been three weeks since the incident in the garret, and now I held five letters from him—each sealed with the same clear wax, written in the same steady hand, brimming with warmth and comfort. At the time, they felt like life itself poured into words—like nectar drawn from a sacred spring. Years later, when I reread them, they seemed gentle, pleasant, shaped by someone content and at peace. In the last two, there were lines that flickered between playfulness and tenderness—“by _feeling_ touched, but not subdued.” Time, dear reader, had softened their edge, mellowed them into something mild and sweet. But when I first read them, fresh from his hand, they burned like wine from heaven—something only Hebe might serve, and even gods would savor.

Does the reader remember what I said earlier? Might they wonder how I replied to these letters—whether I answered with reason’s strict caution, or with the full surge of emotion?

Truthfully, I chose both. I served two masters. I bowed to Reason in one chamber, then turned my heart toward another altar. I wrote two replies—one for myself, one for Graham.

First, I cast Reason out. Feeling and I locked the door behind her, barred it tight, and sat down together. We spread paper, dipped our pen, and poured out our hearts without restraint. We wrote of love that clung close, of gratitude that ran deep—of a bond so strong it wanted to take on every pain in his life, to shield him from every storm, to carry away every lightning strike. Once, let me say this clearly—I scorn any whisper of “warmer feelings.” Women do not entertain such things when, from the very start, they’ve never doubted that to fall into them would be sheer folly. Love is never launched unless hope has already risen above its troubled waters. So when I wrote, I confessed a devotion that longed to absorb all sorrow, to protect him with fierce care. It was honest, raw, and real.

But just as I finished, the bolts gave way. Reason burst back in—vigorous, sharp, unyielding. She snatched the pages, read them, sneered, erased, tore them up, rewrote, folded, sealed, and sent off a short, firm letter of only one page. And she was right.

I did not live only on letters. I was visited, cared for. Every week, I was taken to La Terrasse. Dr. Bretton made sure I knew why: “To keep the nun away,” he said. “I mean to stop her from claiming you. I dislike her deeply—mostly because of that cold face-cloth and those grey eyes. As soon as I heard about them, I was repelled. I want to see who’s cleverer—her or me. I wish she’d come again while I’m here—but she never does.” In short, he saw me as a patient, and treated me with both medical skill and natural kindness—a course of warm, attentive care.

One evening in early December, I walked alone in the carré. It was six o’clock. The classroom doors were shut, but inside, students roared in playful chaos. The carré was dark, save for a red glow beneath the stove. Frost covered the glass doors and windows, and here and there, starlight pierced the white veil, glittering like scattered diamonds. It was a clear night, though moonless. That I dared walk alone in such darkness showed my nerves were healing. I thought of the nun, but no longer feared her—not even when the staircase rose behind me, leading upward through blackness to the haunted attic. Still, my heart fluttered when suddenly I heard breathing and rustling. Turning, I saw a deeper shadow in the steps—a shape moving downward. It paused near the classroom door, then glided past me. At that moment, the distant doorbell rang.

Sounds stir feelings. This figure was too low and round to be my gaunt nun—it was only Madame Beck on duty.

“Mademoiselle Lucy!” Rosine burst in, lamp in hand, from the corridor. “They’re waiting for you in the salon.”

Madame saw me, I saw Madame, Rosine saw both—yet no one recognized the other. I hurried to the salon. There I found whom I expected—Dr. Bretton—in evening dress.

“The carriage is at the door,” he said. “My mother sent it to take you to the theatre. She was going herself, but someone arrived, so she said, ‘Take Lucy instead.’ Will you go?”

“Now? I’m not dressed,” I said, looking down at my plain merino gown.

“You have half an hour. I meant to tell you sooner, but I decided only at five when I heard there’d be a true performance—by a great actress.”

He named her—and the name sent a shiver through me. Once, it could stir whole continents. Now, it rests in silence. She is gone—buried in night and memory. But then, her star blazed at its height, bright and blazing.

“I’ll go—I’ll be ready in ten minutes,” I vowed, and darted off, not once doubting what perhaps crosses your mind now: that going anywhere with Graham without Mrs. Bretton might be improper. I couldn’t have imagined such a thought—let alone voiced it—without feeling shame that would burn through me. Besides, my godmother, knowing her son and knowing me, would never have worried over such a thing. She’d as soon chaperone a brother and sister.

No grand attire was needed. My dull crape would do. I searched the great oak wardrobe in the dormitory, where forty dresses hung. But changes had been made—some garments moved to the garret, including my crape. I must fetch it. I took the key and climbed upstairs, fearless, almost careless. I unlocked the door and stepped in.

The reader may believe it or not—but as I entered, the garret was not entirely dark. From one corner, a solemn light glowed—like a star, yet broader. It lit the deep alcove and part of the tarnished scarlet curtain. Then, silently, it vanished—along with the curtain and alcove. That end of the room became pitch black. I didn’t investigate. I had no time, no will. I grabbed my dress—hanging near the door—and fled, locking the door with trembling hands, rushing down to the dormitory.

But I shook too much to dress myself. My fingers trembled. I called Rosine and offered her a bribe. She liked bribes, so she helped—plaiting my hair neatly, straightening my lace collar, tying my ribbon precisely. She worked like the tidy Phillis she could be when she wished. After handing me gloves and handkerchief, she took the candle and guided me downstairs. Then I realized—I’d forgotten my shawl. She ran back to get it, and I stood with Dr. John in the vestibule, waiting.

“What’s this, Lucy?” he asked, looking down at me closely. “You’re excited again. Ha! Is it the nun?”

I denied it sharply. I resented being suspected of another vision. He remained doubtful.

“She was here,” he insisted. “Her presence leaves a mark—on the eyes, on the expression. You can’t mistake it.”

“She wasn’t,” I repeated—because I truly believed she hadn’t been.

“The signs are there,” he said. “That pale look, that ‘raised’ gaze the Scots call it.”

I finally told him what I’d actually seen. Of course, he dismissed it as mere illusion—nervous disorder. I didn’t believe him, but I didn’t argue. Doctors are so certain, so fixed in their materialist views.

Rosine returned with the shawl. I was bundled into the carriage.

The theatre was packed—every seat filled. Royalty and nobility were there. Palaces and hotels emptied their guests into the hushed tiers. I felt deeply honored to be among them. I longed to see the woman whose fame had stirred such expectation. Would she live up to it? With intense curiosity, with stern focus, I waited.

She appeared at nine that December night—rising above the horizon. She could still shine with a pale, noble grandeur, but her star was nearing its end. Close up, she was a ruin—hollow, half-destroyed—an orb dying, half lava, half flame.

I’d heard her called “plain”—expected angularity, harshness, pallor. What I saw was the ghost of a queen—Vashti, once radiant as daylight, now faded like twilight, consumed like wax in fire.

At first, I thought she was merely a woman—though an extraordinary one—moving with grace and power before the crowd. Then I realized my mistake. Something neither human nor divine dwelled in her. In each eye sat a devil.

These forces drove her through the tragedy, sustaining her frail body. As the drama intensified, they shook her wildly—passions of the pit tearing her apart. They wrote HELL across her proud brow. They tuned her voice to torment. They twisted her regal face into a demonic mask. She stood as Hate, Murder, Madness incarnate.

It was astonishing—a revelation.

It was horrifying—immoral.

Swordsmen bleeding on sand, bulls goring horses—these were gentler sights than Vashti torn by seven devils. Devils screaming, rending their prison, refusing exorcism.

Suffering had struck her. She stood before the audience neither yielding nor enduring—nor even resenting it. She was locked in battle, rigid in defiance. She wore no costume, but draped in pale, sculptural folds. A background of crimson set her apart—white as alabaster, silver, death itself.

Where was the artist of Cleopatra? Let him come and study this. Let him seek the flesh, the muscle, the blood he adored. Let materialists come and look.

I said she does not resent grief. No—such weakness would lie. To her, pain becomes a thing to fight, to tear apart. Scarce a substance herself, she grapples with abstractions. Before calamity, she is a tigress—ripping her sorrows, shredding them in fury. Pain brings no wisdom—tears no harvest. Death, sickness—she sees them as rebels. Wicked, perhaps, but also strong. Her strength has mastered Beauty, subdued Grace, bound them at her side—captives fair and docile. Even in frenzy, her movements are imperial, regal. Her loose hair, flying in rage or revel, remains angelic, glowing under a halo. Fallen, rebellious, banished—she remembers the heavens she defied. Heaven’s light follows her exile, revealing the desolation beyond.

Place Cleopatra—or any soft creature—before her. Watch her slice through the mass like Saladin’s scimitar through a cushion. Let Rubens rise from the grave, bring his army of plump women—Moses’ rod could part the sea, drown them all in a wave.

Vashti was not good, I was told—and I saw she was not. Though a spirit, she came from Tophet. Yet if such unholy force can rise from below, might not equal holiness descend from above?

What did Dr. Graham think?

For long stretches, I forgot to watch him. The genius of the performance pulled me from my usual self—like a sunflower turning from the south to a fierce, red, comet-like light. I’d seen acting before—but never anything like this. Never anything that silenced desire, stunned hope, surpassed imagination. Instead of teasing what might be done, it revealed what *was*—a river swollen with winter thunder, carrying the soul like a leaf down its steel-edged current.

Miss Fanshawe, ever wise, said Dr. Bretton was serious, impassioned, too grave, too sensitive. I never saw him that way. His nature was not contemplative, nor sentimental. He was impressionable—as water is—but not easily shaped. The breeze moved him, the sun warmed him—fire could not brand him, metal could not carve him.

Dr. John could think—well. But he was more action than thought. He could feel—vividly—but not with passion. Bright, soft influences pleased him—like rose and silver, pearl and purple coloring summer clouds. But storms, wildness, danger, flame—he had no sympathy for them. When I looked at him again, I found amusement and understanding. He watched Vashti not with awe, nor reverence, nor fear—but with keen curiosity. Her agony didn’t move him. Her cry—worse than a shriek—barely stirred him. Her fury revolted him slightly, but not with horror. Cool Englishman! He watched the Pythian madness of the night as calmly as cliffs overlook the Channel tide.

Seeing his face, I longed to know his thoughts. Finally, I asked.

“How do you like Vashti?” I said.

“Hm-m-m,” he murmured—soft, but full of meaning. Then a strange smile played on his lips—critical, almost indifferent. Perhaps for minds like his, empathy was numb.

In a few crisp words, he gave his judgment—not as an artist, but as a man. It was a verdict of condemnation.

That night was already marked in my life—not with white, but with a deep-red cross. But I had not finished with it. More memories were to be inscribed in indelible ink.

Near midnight, as the tragedy reached its climax, the house held its breath. Even Graham bit his lip, furrowed his brow, sat motionless. The entire theatre was silent. All eyes focused on one point: the white figure slumped on a chair, trembling in her final struggle—fighting her worst enemy, the one she could not defeat. Only her gasps, her moans, her defiant breaths broke the silence. An overwhelming will convulsed a failing body, forcing it to resist death inch by inch, selling every drop of blood, fighting every faculty—determined to see, to hear, to breathe, to live, even after death whispered, “Thus far, and no farther.”

Then—behind the scenes—a rustle, a stir. Feet ran. Voices spoke. What was it? the house demanded.

A flame. A smell of smoke.

“Fire!” echoed through the gallery. “Fire!” screamed, repeated, roared. Panic erupted—rushing, crushing, blind, selfish, cruel chaos.

And Dr. John? Reader, I see him still—calm, courageous, serene.

“Lucy will stay seated,” he said, glancing down at me with the same quiet strength I’d seen beside his mother’s hearth. Yes, I would have stayed—even under falling rocks. But instinct kept me still. I wouldn’t move to burden him, defy his will, or distract him.

We were in the stalls. For moments, we were trapped in terrible pressure.

“How terrified the women are!” he said. “But if men weren’t nearly as afraid, order might hold. This is shameful. I see fifty selfish beasts. If I were near, I’d knock down every one. Some women are braver than some men. Look—Good God!”

As he spoke, a young girl clinging quietly to a gentleman before us was violently pushed from his arms and thrown underfoot. She vanished in seconds. Graham lunged forward. He and the older man—strong, though grey-haired—pushed back the crowd. Her head and long hair fell over his shoulder. She seemed unconscious.

“Trust her to me,” said Dr. John. “I’m a doctor.”

“If you have no lady with you, be it so,” the man replied. “Hold her—I’ll make a path. We must get her air.”

“I have a lady,” Graham said. “But she won suffice.”

He summoned me with his eyes. We were separated. Determined to reach him, I wormed through the living wall, crawling where I couldn’t climb or step.

“Hold onto me—don’t let go,” he said. I obeyed.

Our guide was strong and skilled. He cut through the throng like a wedge. With effort, he broke through the suffocating mass—and brought us to the freezing night.

“You’re English!” he said, turning to Dr. Bretton.

“English. And I speak to a countryman?” Graham replied.

“Right. Wait two minutes while I find my carriage.”

“Papa, I’m not hurt,” a girlish voice said. “Am I with papa?”

“You’re with a friend. Your father is near.”

“Tell him I’m not hurt—just my shoulder. Oh, my shoulder! They trod here.”

“Dislocation, perhaps,” muttered the doctor. “Let’s hope nothing worse. Lucy, help me.”

I assisted as he adjusted her position, wrapped her gently. She stifled a moan, lying quietly in his arms.

“She’s very light,” Graham said. “Like a child.” He leaned close. “Is she a child, Lucy? Did you notice her age?”

“I am not a child,” she said firmly. “I am seventeen.”

“Tell papa to come—I’m anxious.”

The carriage arrived. Her father took her from Graham. But during the transfer, she winced, moaned.

“My darling!” the father said tenderly. Then to Graham: “You said you’re a doctor?”

“I am—Dr. Bretton, of La Terrasse.”

“Good. Will you step into my carriage?”

“My own is here. I’ll find it and follow.”

“Then please come with us.” He named his address: “Hôtel Crécy, Rue Crécy.”

We followed. The carriage sped. I and Graham sat in silence. It felt like an adventure.

After some delay finding our own vehicle, we reached the hotel about ten minutes later. It was a foreign-style hotel—a collection of homes, not an inn—a tall, stately building with a vaulted arch leading into a square courtyard.

We alighted, climbed a wide public staircase, and stopped at Numéro 2 on the second floor. The first floor housed a “prince Russe,” Graham informed me. Ringing the bell at a massive door, we were admitted to elegant apartments. Announced by a servant in livery, we entered a drawing-room with a roaring English fire and foreign mirrors gleaming on the walls. Near the hearth stood a small group: a slight figure in a deep armchair, women attending, the iron-grey gentleman watching anxiously.

Those who live apart from the world—shut away in schools, cloisters, or quiet houses behind high walls—are often forgotten by those outside, as if they had vanished into silence. Friends who once wrote letters, sent messages, or visited regularly may suddenly stop entirely. There is no warning, no explanation—only a long, unbroken stillness. The post arrives without your name; the door remains untouched; the familiar voice grows silent.

And yet, there are always reasons—though the one left behind cannot see them. While they sit still, their loved ones move through life’s whirlwind: births, deaths, travels, affairs that fill days with noise and haste. To the isolated soul, time stretches endlessly—each hour feels heavy, each minute dragging like a burden. But to others, that same stretch of days may be filled with urgency, change, and decisions too many to count.

The wise person learns to endure this silence. They hold their thoughts close, keep their heart quiet, and accept that sometimes life demands stillness—like a dormouse curling into its nest, shutting out the world until spring returns. They say to themselves: *It is right. It must be so.* And perhaps, one day, the frost will melt, the sun will warm the earth again, and joy will come back—not because it was earned, but because it was allowed.

That is how I lived for seven weeks after the evening at the theatre. No word came. Not a letter, not a visit, not even a message. My days were empty as blank pages.

Midway through that silence, I began to wonder—had something happened? Had my friends fallen ill? Was there grief at La Terrasse? The mind, when alone too long, begins to ache. Doubts grow thick and dark, pressing in from every side. Sleep becomes an enemy; dreams turn cruel, showing disasters, abandonment, loss. At night, I tossed and started, haunted by visions of emptiness. I was thin, pale, worn down—not by sickness, but by longing.

By the seventh week, I accepted the truth: this silence was not personal. It was fate. Circumstance. A part of my life’s pattern. I did not blame myself—I knew better than that—but I could not help feeling the weight of it. The road ahead felt rough, and I longed for light.

I tried to fill the void. I began a delicate lace project, studied German with fierce concentration, read dry, dense books from the library—anything to keep my hands busy, my mind occupied. But all these efforts felt like chewing on iron or drinking saltwater. They gave no comfort.

My worst moment came at post-time. I dreaded it. I knew the exact hour, though I tried to forget it. I would wait, tense and trembling, listening for the ring of the bell. When it came—and nothing followed—the disappointment was like a blow. I felt as though I were trapped in a cage, waiting for food that never arrived.

In truth, I suffered deeply. Fear, loneliness, despair—they crept in like shadows. Hope grew frail. Sometimes, despair came so close I could feel its breath—cold, heavy, choking. I used to feel it sink into my chest, making every movement difficult, every thought slow. That letter—the one I waited for—was all I had left to believe in. Without it, life tasted bitter.

When hunger became unbearable, I turned to my only treasure: the five letters I kept locked in a case. Oh, how bright those months seemed now! Those letters were stars in my sky. I would read them at night, lighting a small wax taper, stealing up to my room after hours. I’d open the box and read them over and over, as if each word could feed me. But soon, the words lost their meaning. The gold faded. I pined, growing thinner, weaker—yet not truly ill.

One evening, late, I sat reading, feeling the power to understand slipping away. The letters had become hollow. Then—footsteps. Quick, light steps on the stairs. I recognized them instantly: Ginevra Fanshawe.

She entered, dressed in bright silk, her shawl slipping from her shoulders, her curls damp and loose. She looked tired, annoyed.

“It was a stupid evening,” she said. “Stupid people.”

“Mrs. Cholmondeley?” I asked.

“No. I didn’t go there.”

“Then where?”

“My uncle de Bassompierre has arrived.”

“Your uncle de Bassompierre? Are you glad?”

“Not at all. He’s hateful.”

“Because he’s foreign?”

“He’s English enough. His mother was French—de Bassompierre. She died, left him estates, a title, and the name. Now he’s a grand man.”

“And you hate him for that?”

“Don’t I know what Mama says? He wasn’t really my uncle—he married Mama’s sister. She hates him. Says he killed Aunt Ginevra with coldness. He looks like a bear.”

She sighed. “Such a dreadful evening. I’ll never go to his hotel again. Imagine walking into a room alone, and this fifty-year-old man coming forward, talking for a few minutes, then turning his back and leaving. How rude! I suppose his conscience troubled him. Everyone says I look just like Aunt Ginevra. Mama says it’s ridiculous.”

“Were you the only guest?”

“The only one—except Missy, my cousin. A spoiled little thing.”

“M. de Bassompierre has a daughter?”

“Yes, yes. Don’t ask so many questions. I’m exhausted.”

She yawned and threw herself onto my bed. “Oh, and guess what? Mademoiselle was nearly crushed at the theatre a few weeks ago.”

“I heard.”

“She lives in a big hotel on the Rue Crécy?”

“Exactly. How do you know?”

“I’ve been there.”

“You have? Really? You’re everywhere lately. Did Mother Bretton take you?”

“Dr. and Mrs. Bretton were there. They have access to the apartments. ‘My son John’ attended her during the accident—accident? Bah! She probably deserved it for acting so high and mighty. Now there’s a friendship forming. I heard talk of ‘auld lang syne.’ How boring!”

“All?” I asked.

“All? Well, I forgot to mention the old woman and her boy.”

“Dr. and Mrs. Bretton were there tonight?”

“Yes, yes. And Missy played hostess. What a vain little doll!”

Ginevra was sulking. Her pride had been wounded. She had expected attention, flattery, admiration—but none came. She lay there, fuming, defeated.

“Is Miss de Bassompierre well now?” I asked.

“As well as anyone. But she plays sick to get attention. Watch her lying on a couch while ‘my son John’ forbids excitement—ugh! It was nauseating.”

“It wouldn’t have been so bad if you were in her place.”

“I hate ‘my son John!’”

“Who is ‘my son John’? Dr. Bretton doesn’t call him that.”

“Then she should. He’s a clumsy, bear-like John.”

“That’s not true. And since I’ve run out of patience, I demand you leave this room.”

“Passionate thing! Your face is red as a poppy. Why are you always so angry about Jean?”

I was furious—but not worth arguing with such a fragile creature. I blew out the candle, locked my bureau, and left her there.

The next day was Thursday—a half-holiday. Breakfast was done. I returned to the first classe. The post-hour approached. I sat, waiting—like someone expecting a ghost. A letter was unlikely. But still, I couldn’t forget it might come.

It was a cold January day. The east wind blew sharp and biting. I had grown sensitive to weather—how the north and east winds deepened pain, made sorrow heavier. The south could soothe; the west, sometimes cheer—unless it brought storm clouds, which drained all energy.

I remember leaving the classe, running bareheaded to the bottom of the garden, hiding among the leafless bushes. I hoped the postman’s ring would come while I was out of earshot. I muffled my head in my apron, blocked my ears—afraid of the sound, afraid of the silence that would follow.

At last, I returned. The classe was empty. On my black desk, I saw a white object—a flat, white thing. The post had come. Rosine had been here. Like an angel, she had left a sign.

It was a letter. A real letter. From the only person I had ever known to write to me. My heart leapt.

I stepped closer, bending to read the handwriting. My hand trembled. I expected a firm, masculine script. Instead, I saw a delicate, feminine scrawl. For a moment, I thought: *This is cruel.*

But I recovered. Life goes on, even when it hurts. Our senses remain—even when joy is gone.

I opened the letter. By then, I recognized the writing. It was dated “La Terrasse.” It read:

> *Dear Lucy,*
> *I’ve been wondering what you’ve been doing lately. Not that I think you’d have any trouble explaining yourself—you’re always busy, always happy, just like us at La Terrasse. Graham is busier than ever—so sought after, so engaged, I tell him he’ll grow conceited. As a good mother, I try to keep him humble—no praise from me, as you know. But still, he’s a fine boy. His mother’s heart dances when she sees him. After a full day of dealing with tempers, caprices, and suffering—sometimes even causing it—he comes home in such a kind, pleasant mood that I feel as if my day dawns when others’ nights fall.*
> *He needs correction, of course, and I give it. But he’s so resilient—no matter how I try to make him sulk, he turns around with jokes. You know him well. I’m just an old simpleton, writing about him.*
