> *As for me, I’ve had my old Bretton agent visiting. We’ve been buried in business. I want to restore some of what his father left him. He laughs at my worries, saying I should see how well he can provide for both of us. He teases me about wanting sky-blue turbans, diamonds, livery servants—an hotel. Talking of turbans, I wish you’d been with us the other night. He was tired. After tea, he fell asleep in my chair. I took the opportunity to play a trick: I brought out the sky-blue turban and carefully placed it on his head. It suited him perfectly—Eastern, but fair-haired. I wrapped my cashmere around him, and there he was—a young bey, a pacha, the most splendid sight.*
> *It was fun—but only half-enjoyed, since I was alone. You should have seen it.*
> *He woke up. The mirror told him what he looked like. Now I live in fear of revenge.*
> *But to the point: I know Thursday is a half-holiday. Be ready by five. I’ll send the carriage to pick you up. Come—there may be someone you know. Goodbye, my dear, wise, grave little goddaughter.*
> *Very truly yours,*
> *Louisa Bretton.*

A letter like that changes everything. I might still have been sad—but I was calmer. My friends were safe. Graham was well. His mother was cheerful. Their love for me remained unchanged.

Yet, how strange it was to compare their seven weeks with mine. And how wise it is for people in unusual positions to keep silent about their pain. The world understands hunger, starvation—physical suffering. But who understands the agony of being shut away? Who can imagine going mad in solitude?

They see the prisoner released—mad, broken, gone. But how his mind unraveled, how his nerves burned then froze—this is too complex for most. Speak of it? It would be like standing in a market square and speaking in the tongue of Nebuchadnezzar, the king who dreamed and raved.

Let such understanding remain rare. Let the world continue to believe only physical pain deserves pity. For now, let it be so.

Later that day, the cold deepened. A storm rose from the north, heavy with snow. I feared no carriage would come. But trust Mrs. Bretton—she would not be denied.

At six, I was lifted from the carriage and carried over the snow-covered steps of La Terrasse.

I ran through the vestibule, up the stairs, into the drawing-room. There stood Mrs. Bretton—like a summer day in winter. Even if I’d been twice as cold, her kiss and embrace would have warmed me.

After years of bare rooms and cold benches, the blue saloon dazzled me. The fire blazed crimson and bright, like Christmas itself.

She held my hand, chatted, scolded me for being thinner. Then she said the snow had tangled my hair and sent me upstairs to fix it.

In my sea-green room, I found another fire, candles lit—two tall waxlights flanking the mirror. Between them, a figure stood—small, pale, dressed in white, touched with scarlet. A winter spirit.

For a moment, I thought of Graham and his illusions. But then, the figure turned.

A large eye, framed by long lashes, met mine. The lashes were dark, softening the gaze.

“Oh,” she whispered, “you’ve come.”

She smiled slowly, deeply.

I knew her. Only once had I seen that face—fine, delicate, unforgettable.

“Miss de Bassompierre,” I said.

“No,” she replied. “Not Miss de Bassompierre—for you.”

I waited.

“You’ve changed,” she said, stepping closer. “But you’re still you. I remember your face, your hair, your expression…”

I moved toward the fire. She stood opposite, studying me. Her eyes grew softer, deeper, then dimmed.

“It makes me almost cry to remember,” she said. “But don’t think I’m sentimental. I’m glad. Truly glad.”

I was confused, speechless.

“I think we met only that night,” I stammered, “when you were hurt…”

She smiled. “You’ve forgotten. I’ve sat on your knee, been lifted in your arms, shared your pillow. You don’t remember the night I came crying—like a naughty child—to your bed, and you took me in? You don’t recall how you comforted me when I was frightened?”

Then I understood.

“And you are little Polly?”

“I am Paulina Mary Home de Bassompierre.”

The evening at La Terrasse was quiet, even subdued, despite the warmth of the fire and the cheerfulness of my godmother’s nature. She made an effort to entertain us, but true joy did not stir until the distant sound of hooves broke through the howling wind outside—signals that someone had returned from the storm. How often, I thought, do women sit safely by the hearth while their hearts wander into the cold dark, braving blizzards and icy roads, waiting for a loved one to come home. That night, both father and son arrived—the Count de Bassompierre riding alongside Dr. Bretton.

We could not tell who first heard the horses; the weather was so fierce we all rushed down to meet them. But as they entered, both were covered in snow—like two figures carved from winter itself. Mrs. Bretton immediately ordered them to the kitchen, forbidding them to step on her carpeted stairs until they had shed their frosty cloaks. Into the kitchen we followed, drawn by the scene: it was a large, old Dutch-style room, warm and inviting with its beams and hearth. The little Countess darted around her equally snowy father, clapping her hands. “Papa, papa!” she cried. “You look like a giant Polar bear!”

He shook himself, sending a shower of snow flying, and she fled back laughing, only to return moments later eager to help him remove his arctic disguise. When he finally freed himself from his coat, he playfully threatened to bury her under it like an avalanche.

“Then come,” she said, bending forward as if to invite the fall. And when he raised the coat above her head, she leapt aside like a young chamois, quick and light.

Her movements were soft, supple, graceful—like a kitten. Her laughter rang clear and bright, like silver bells. As she took her father’s cold hands in hers and rubbed them gently, standing on tiptoe to kiss his lips, there seemed to glow about her a halo of pure affection. The dignified seigneur looked down upon her with the kind of love men reserve for what they hold most dear.

“Mrs. Bretton,” he said, “what am I to do with this daughter—or daughterling—of mine? She doesn’t grow in wisdom or stature. Doesn’t she seem just as much a child as she was ten years ago?”

“She cannot be more of a child than your own son here,” replied Mrs. Bretton, who was arguing with Graham over his clothes—she insisted on a change he resisted. He leaned against the dresser, laughing, keeping her at arm’s length.

“Come now, mamma,” he said, “let’s compromise. Let’s have a Christmas wassail cup, and toast Old England right here by the fire.”

So while the Count warmed himself by the flames and Paulina danced freely across the spacious kitchen—happy in her freedom—Mrs. Bretton directed Martha to prepare the wassail bowl. She poured the steaming drink into a Bretton flagon, serving it round with a small silver cup I recognized as Graham’s christening cup.

“Here’s to Auld Lang Syne!” the Count declared, raising his glass high. Then, turning to Mrs. Bretton:

“We twa ha’ paidlet i’ the burn
Fra morning sun till dine,
But seas between us braid ha’ roared
Sin’ auld lang syne.

“And surely ye’ll be your pint-stoup,
And surely I’ll be mine;
And we’ll taste a cup o’ kindness yet
For auld lang syne.”

“Scotch! Scotch!” Paulina called out. “Papa is speaking Scotch—and he is partly Scotch. We are Home and de Bassompierre, Caledonian and Gallic.”

“And is that a Scotch reel you’re dancing, you Highland fairy?” her father asked. “Mrs. Bretton, there will soon be a green ring growing in the middle of your kitchen. I wouldn’t answer for her being quite cannie—she’s a strange little mortal.”

“Tell Lucy to dance with me, papa,” she said. “There’s Lucy Snowe.”

Mr. Home—still very much the plain Mr. Home beneath the title of Count—held out his hand to me kindly. “I remember you well,” he said. “Even if my memory weren’t trustworthy, your name has been on my daughter’s lips so often, and I’ve heard so many stories about you, that you feel like an old friend.”

Everyone had tasted the wassail except Paulina, whose dancing had not once been interrupted. But she wasn’t to be left out.

“Let me taste,” she said to Graham, reaching toward the cup he was placing beyond her reach on the dresser.

Mrs. Bretton and Mr. Home were deep in conversation now. Dr. John had watched Paulina’s dance closely—he liked it. Not only were her movements soft and beautiful, pleasing to his eye, but her ease in her father’s house pleased him too—it made him feel at ease. Once again, she seemed a child to him—almost his playmate. I wondered how he would speak to her. His first words revealed that the memories of “little Polly” had returned, stirred by her joyful spirit.

“Your ladyship wishes for the tankard?”

“I think I said so. I think I intimated as much.”

“Couldn’t consent to a step of the kind on any account. Sorry for it, but couldn’t do it.”

“Why? I’m quite well now. It won’t break my collarbone again or dislocate my shoulder. Is it wine?”

“No. Nor dew.”

“I don’t want dew—I don’t like dew. What is it?”

“Ale—strong ale—old October. Brewed, perhaps, when I was born.”

“It must be curious. Is it good?”

“Excessively good.”

He took it down, drank a second dose of this potent brew, smiled mischievously, then placed it carefully back on the shelf.

“I should like a little,” Paulina said, looking up. “I’ve never had ‘old October.’ Is it sweet?”

“Perilously sweet,” Graham answered.

She gazed at him with the expression of a child longing for something forbidden. At last, he relented, took the cup down, and let her sip from his hand. His eyes—always alive with emotion—shone with quiet delight. He held the cup just so, allowing only a single drop at a time to touch her rosy lips.

“A little more—a little more,” she said, tapping his wrist lightly with her finger. “It smells of spice and sugar, but I can’t taste it. Your wrist is so stiff, and you’re so stingy.”

He gave in, whispering seriously, “Don’t tell my mother or Lucy. They wouldn’t approve.”

“Nor do I,” she said, suddenly changing tone—just as if the drink had lifted a spell. “It’s anything but sweet. It’s bitter and hot, and takes away my breath. Your old October was only desirable because it was forbidden. Thank you, no more.”

With a slight, graceful bow—careless yet elegant—she glided away and rejoined her father.

I believe she spoke the truth: the girl of seventeen still carried within her the heart of a seven-year-old.

Graham watched her go, puzzled, uncertain. He kept his eyes on her throughout the evening, but she seemed unaware of his gaze.

As we moved upstairs to the drawing-room for tea, she took her father’s arm—her natural place was beside him. Her attention was wholly his. He and Mrs. Bretton led the conversation, and Paulina was their most attentive listener, urging them to repeat tales and adventures.

“And where were you then, papa? What did you say? Tell Mrs. Bretton what happened that time.” So she drew him out.

She no longer danced or laughed wildly—the infantine sparkle had faded. She was calm, thoughtful, obedient. It was lovely to see her bid goodnight. Her manner toward Graham was touched with dignity. In her quiet smile and gentle bow, the Countess showed herself. Graham responded with solemnity. I saw he struggled to reconcile the dancing sprite with the refined young woman.

The next morning, gathered round the breakfast table, shivering after our cold ablutions, Mrs. Bretton declared no one should leave the house unless absolutely necessary.

Indeed, leaving seemed impossible. Snow drifted against the lower windows, and outside, sky and air were chaotic, wind and snow locked in furious battle. No new snow fell, but the old was torn up, whirled into spirals, cast into strange shapes.

Paulina supported her mother’s decree.

“Papa shall not go out,” she said, seating herself beside his chair. “I’ll take care of him. You won’t go into town, will you, papa?”

“Ay, and No,” he replied. “If you and Mrs. Bretton are *very* good to me, Polly—kind and attentive—if you pet me nicely and make much of me, I might wait an hour after breakfast to see if this razor-edged wind calms. But you give me nothing—no breakfast—you let me starve.”

“Quick, Mrs. Bretton,” Paulina urged, “pour the coffee—while I attend to the Count de Bassompierre in other ways. Since he became a count, he’s needed so much care.”

She cut and prepared a roll.

“There, papa, are your ‘pistolets’ charged,” she said. “And here’s marmalade—just like the kind we had at Bretton, which you said was as good as if it had been made in Scotland—”

“And which your little ladyship used to beg for my boy,” Mrs. Bretton interjected. “Do you remember? How you’d come to my elbow, whisper, ‘Please, ma’am, something good for Graham—a little marmalade, or honey?’”

“No, mamma,” Dr. John broke in, laughing but blushing. “It surely wasn’t so. I couldn’t have cared for such things.”

“Did he or didn’t he, Paulina?”

“He liked them,” she insisted.

“Never blush for it, John,” Mr. Home said warmly. “I like them myself. And Polly showed good sense in caring for a friend’s comfort. I taught her that—though I never let her forget it. Polly, offer me a slice of tongue.”

“There, papa,” she said. “But remember—you’re only waited on with such care on condition that you’re persuadable and stay at La Terrasse today.”

“Mrs. Bretton,” the Count said, “I want to send my daughter to school. Do you know of any good one?”

“There’s Miss Snowe’s—Madame Beck’s.”

“Miss Snowe teaches?”

“I am a teacher,” I said, glad of the chance to clarify. For some time I’d felt awkward—Mrs. Bretton and Graham knew my situation, but the Count and Paulina did not. They might alter their kindness once they learned my station. I spoke plainly, though a wave of unexpected thoughts rose in me, making me sigh without meaning to.

Mr. Home didn’t raise his eyes for nearly two minutes. Perhaps he hadn’t heard. Or perhaps, as a Scotsman—pride being proverbial—he felt silence was proper after such a confession. Yet though he was simple in appearance and habits, I had always sensed a depth in him. Was his pride real or false? I leave that question open. But for me, he was always a gentleman—true and kind.

By nature, he was reflective and sensitive. A quiet melancholy shadowed his thoughts and feelings—not merely a tint, but a deeper cloud shaped by sorrow and loss. He didn’t know much about me, nor fully understand what I was. Often, his assumptions amused me. But he saw my life lay on the shaded side of the hill. He believed I tried to walk straight, honestly. If he could have helped, he would have. Unable to, he still wished me well. When he looked at me, his eyes were kind. When he spoke, his voice was gentle.

“Yours,” he said, “is a difficult calling. I wish you health and strength to succeed in it.”

His daughter reacted differently. Her eyes widened—astonished, almost alarmed.

“Are you a teacher?” she exclaimed. Then, pausing, “Well, I never knew what you were. I never thought to ask. To me, you’ve always been Lucy Snowe.”

“And what am I now?” I asked, unable to resist.

“Yourself, of course. But do you really teach here, in Villette?”

“I do.”

“And do you like it?”

“Not always.”

“And why do you keep doing it?”

Her father glanced at her, perhaps ready to stop her—but only said, “Proceed, Polly, proceed with your catechism. Prove yourself the little wiseacre you are. If Miss Snowe blushed, I’d have to silence you. But she only smiles—so press her harder. Well, Miss Snowe, why do you continue?”

“Chiefly,” I said, “for the money.”

“Not then from pure philanthropy? Polly and I were clinging to that idea as the kindest explanation for your oddness.”

“No, sir. Rather for the roof over my head, and the peace of mind knowing I work for myself—so I don’t burden anyone.”

“Papa,” Paulina said softly, “I pity Lucy.”

“Take that pity,” Mr. Home replied, “and put it back where it came from—into the heart that sent it. If my Polly ever learns by experience how fragile this world’s comforts are, I hope she’ll act as Lucy does: work for herself, so she burdens neither friend nor family.”

“Yes, papa,” she said quietly. “But poor Lucy—I thought she was rich, with wealthy friends.”

“You thought like a child,” he said. “I never did. When I studied Lucy’s manner, I saw she guarded herself, acted, rather than was served. This path, I believe, has given her wisdom. If she lives long enough, she may bless Providence for it. Now—about this school: would Madame Beck accept my Polly, do you think, Miss Lucy?”

I said it would be easy to try. Madame Beck liked English pupils. “If you, sir,” I added, “take Miss de Bassompierre in your carriage this afternoon, I’m sure Rosine will answer your ring quickly, and Madame will put on her best gloves to receive you.”

“In that case,” Mr. Home said, “there’s no need to delay. Mrs. Hurst can send her things later. Polly can settle into her horn-book by nightfall. And you, Miss Lucy, I trust you’ll keep an eye on her and let me know how she fares. Do you approve, Countess?”

The Countess hesitated. “I thought… I thought I had finished my education…”

“That only proves we can be wrong. I believe otherwise—especially after hearing your insights this morning. Ah, my little girl, you have much to learn. Papa should have taught you more. Come—try Madame Beck. The weather seems to calm, and I’ve finished my breakfast.”

“But, papa!”

“Well?”

“I see an obstacle.”

“I don’t.”

“It’s enormous—impossible to overcome. As big as you in your greatcoat and the snowdrift atop it.”

“And, like that drift, capable of melting?”

“No! It’s too solid—too real. It’s you, papa. Miss Lucy, warn Madame Beck not to accept me—because in the end, she’d have to take papa too. He’s so teasing, I’ll tell tales about him. Listen all: Five years ago, when I was twelve, he decided I was spoiled, unfit for the world. I cried—but M. de Bassompierre was firm. I went to school. What happened? He came too—every other day. Madame Aigredoux complained, but it did no good. Eventually, both of us were expelled. Tell Madame Beck this—fair warning.”

Mrs. Bretton turned to Mr. Home. “What do you say to that?”

He offered no defense. Judgment was given—Paulina triumphed.

Yet she had other moods. After breakfast, when the elders withdrew—likely to discuss business—Paulina, Dr. Bretton, and I were left alone. The child vanished. With us, her peers, she rose to the level of a young lady. Her face changed—its dimpled openness softened into something more serious, her features sharper, less mobile.

Graham noticed it too. He stood near the window, watching the snow. Then he approached the hearth and began to talk—not with his usual ease. He chose words carefully, hesitantly, poorly. He spoke vaguely of Villette—its people, buildings, sights.

She responded with maturity—intelligent, poised. Though traces of little Polly lingered—quick glances, animated gestures—there was a polish, a grace that smoothed them into elegance. A man less observant than Graham might not have seen the remnants of childhood.

Yet Graham observed everything. Every flicker, every hesitation, every lisp—he caught it. Sometimes, when speaking fast, she still lisped. But she flushed and corrected herself carefully, with a charm that was almost as amusing as the error.

Each time, Graham smiled.

Gradually, the tension eased. Their conversation grew warmer. Paulina’s cheeks and lips softened into a familiar, dimpling smile. She lisped once—and forgot to correct herself.

And Dr. John changed. Not gayer—no jests, no levity—but his presence seemed more comfortable, his speech smoother, more confident. Ten years had passed since they last met, but the bond remained. Some natures deepen with time, not diminish. For them, silence bred closeness, closeness led to unity.

But Graham must go. His profession demanded it. He left the room—but returned before leaving the house. I know he came back—not for the paper or card in his desk, but to confirm, one last time, that the image of Paulina he carried was true. That he hadn’t mistaken her under artificial light.

No—he found the impression stronger. He took away a final glance—shy, tender, innocent—as beautiful as a fawn lifting its head from ferns, or a lamb rising from its bed.

Left alone, Paulina and I sat in silence. We each took up our work. Her old wooden box was replaced by one of mosaic, adorned with golden tools. Her fingers, once tiny and trembling, were now swift and skilled. Yet the same delicate mannerisms remained—the quick turn to fix a strand of hair, the brush of silk to remove imaginary dust.

That morning, I craved silence. The storm’s fury had left me hushed, awed. The white, bloodless passion of January still raged—though spent, it was not spent. Had Ginevra Fanshawe been there, she would not have let me rest. She’d have questioned me endlessly, filled the room with noise and speculation.

But Paulina only glanced at me—quiet, piercing. Her lips parted, as if to speak—but she saw my silence and respected it.

From that moment, my days held no lack of change. I ventured out often, with Madame Beck’s full approval—her regard for me had grown steadily since the beginning, and now, seeing me welcomed at noble houses and grand estates, her respect deepened into something like admiration. Yet she never overstepped herself in praise; she was too sharp, too balanced to let emotion cloud her judgment. Her pride was carefully measured, her interests pursued with cool precision. She did not flatter or fawn, but she made it clear—without words—that she valued the company I kept, not because they were wealthy or powerful, but because they were decent, thoughtful people whose presence elevated rather than diminished her establishment.

Still, she never spoke kindly of me or my friends. Only once, when she sat beneath the sun in the garden, a cup of coffee beside her and the Gazette in hand, looking calm and satisfied, did she offer a remark as I asked leave to go out for the evening.

“Oui, oui, ma bonne amie,” she said, smiling slightly. “I give you permission freely and gladly. Your work here has always been admirable—full of care and discretion. You have earned the right to enjoy yourself. Go whenever you wish. And your choice of acquaintances—I approve. It is wise, proper, worthy.”

She closed her lips and returned to her paper.

It was then I noticed something strange: the sealed packet of five letters, hidden in my drawer, had vanished. At first, panic seized me. But within seconds, I steadied myself.

“Patience,” I whispered. “Say nothing. Wait quietly. They’ll return.”

And they did. The next day, neatly folded and tied again, they lay exactly where they’d been. They had simply been borrowed—examined, perhaps—and then returned. I knew who had taken them.

Madame Beck. I could only suppose so.

What must she have thought of those letters? What did she make of Dr. John Bretton’s writing—the clarity, the warmth, the quiet strength woven through each line? Did she recognize the wit beneath the modest tone, the intelligence behind the simple phrasing? How did she view the gentle kindness scattered through the pages—not lavished, but placed like rare stones in a quiet stream? Oh, Madame Beck—what did these words mean to you?

I believe she liked them. Once, after she had taken the letters from me (and what a word to use—*borrowed*—in speaking of such a woman!), I caught her watching me with a steady, thoughtful gaze. Not harsh, not cruel—but curious, as if she were trying to understand something beyond her reach.

It was during the brief break between classes, when the girls ran out into the courtyard. I remained in the first classe with her. When our eyes met, she spoke without hesitation.

“There is,” she said, “something very remarkable in the English character.”

“How?” I asked.

She laughed softly, repeating the word in English: “How?”

“I cannot say *how*. But the English—they think differently. In friendship, in love, in everything. And yet,” she added, rising and moving briskly away like the compact little horse she resembled, “they do not need to be watched.”

Then I murmured to myself, “Then may you, Madame, leave my letters undisturbed from now on.”

But a sudden wave of sorrow crashed over me. I remembered—never again would such letters come to me. The last one had been read. That river I had drunk from, whose waters had sustained me, was turning its course elsewhere. It left my small shelter dry and barren, flowing far off into lands I could not follow. The change was just, natural—even necessary. But still, I grieved. I had loved that stream. I had almost revered it. And now it was gone, like a vision fading in the desert heat.

Though I tried to remain composed, I could not help weeping. Tears fell fast onto my hands, my desk—silent, heavy, and brief.

Yet soon I reminded myself: “The hope I mourned brought pain, and caused me pain. It lived long enough. Its death should be welcome.”

And so I tried to accept it. Long suffering had taught me patience. In time, I closed the eyes of my grief, covered its face, and laid it to rest with quiet dignity.

But the letters—those letters—must be hidden. People who have lost something precious always guard their mementos closely. To see them every day would be unbearable—a constant wound reopened.

On a quiet holiday afternoon, I went to retrieve them, intending to decide their final fate. But this time, I felt a pang of anger. The packet was there, yes—but the ribbon was undone and retied. Other signs told me someone had opened my drawer.

This was too much. Madame Beck was discreet, intelligent, and cautious. If she had read them, I could bear it—though it pained me. But the idea that she might have shared them with others… that she might have shown them to someone else… it filled me with horror.

And I suspected whom. Her cousin, M. Paul Emanuel, had visited her the night before. She often consulted him, discussing matters she kept from everyone else. That morning, in class, he had looked at me with an odd glance—one I hadn’t understood then, but now recognized clearly: cold, angry, unreadable. He had seen something in those letters—something he disliked.

He would not judge me fairly. I had always found him suspicious, severe. The thought that he might have read them—letters meant only for friendship—shook me to the core.

Where could I hide them? Where in this house could anything truly be safe? Could a lock protect them? A padlock seal them from prying eyes?

Not in the attic. Too damp. Rats gnawed through wood, mice built nests among old things. My letters—still dear, even now—might be eaten by vermin, their ink blurred by moisture. No. The attic would not do.

I sat in the dormitory window seat, thinking. It was a frosty afternoon, the winter sun low and pale. Beyond the garden, the “allée défendue” stretched quiet and still. The old pear-tree—the nun’s tree—rose bare and gaunt against the sky.

A sudden idea struck me—one of those strange thoughts that come unbidden to lonely minds. I put on my bonnet, cloak, and furs, and walked into the city.

I wandered through the ancient quarter, drawn instinctively toward shadowed streets and forgotten corners. Finally, I came upon an old shop—like a treasure chest of forgotten things. I needed a metal box that could be sealed, or a thick glass jar that could be stopped tightly. Amongst dusty heaps, I found a large glass bottle. I bought it.

Back in my room, I rolled up the letters, wrapped them in oiled silk, bound them with twine, and placed them inside the jar. Then I took it to the old Jewish broker, who sealed it tightly, stopping the neck with wax and making it air-tight. As he worked, he glanced at me with wary eyes—white lashes like frost. I think he believed I was planning something dark.

There was no joy in this act—only a strange, solemn satisfaction. The mood was like that which led me to the confessional. It was a burial.

I returned to the pensionnat just as dusk fell. By seven, the moon rose. At half-past seven, when all was quiet—pupils studying, teachers busy, Madame Beck with her family, Rosine gone from the hall—I wrapped myself in a shawl, took the sealed jar, and slipped out through the first-classe door.

I passed through the berceau, then entered the “allée défenduhe.” The old pear-tree stood at the far end, near my favorite seat. Methusaleh, they called it—ancient, sturdy, with a deep hollow near its roots. I knew of it, hidden beneath ivy and vines.

I cleared the growth, found the hole—large enough for the jar—and pushed it deep inside. From a shed at the garden’s edge, I fetched a slate and some mortar. I covered the hollow with the stone, secured it with cement, filled the space with black earth, and replaced the ivy.

Then I leaned against the tree, resting, as any mourner might linger beside a fresh grave.

The night air was still, veiled in a soft mist that turned the moonlight into a hazy glow. Something in that mist—perhaps electricity—touched me strangely. I felt as I had once in England, under the northern lights, when the sky shimmered with banners and swift messengers. I did not feel happy—but strong. Fortified.

If life is war, it seemed my lot to fight alone. Now I considered how to leave this place—where supplies ran thin, where hope had dried up. Perhaps another battle must be fought. If so, I was ready. I had nothing to lose. God might grant me gain.

But what path lay open? What plan could I follow?

I was still pondering when the moon, dim until now, grew brighter. A beam of light cut through the haze, and a shadow became distinct. I looked closer—whiter and darker it grew. Shape formed suddenly. I stood three yards from a tall figure: a woman in black robes, her face covered by a white veil.

Five minutes passed. I did not run. I did not scream. She stood silent. I spoke.

“Who are you? Why do you come to me?”

She did not answer. Her face was hidden—no features visible—but her eyes stared at me.

I felt not brave, but desperate. And desperation can sometimes stand in for courage. I stepped forward. I reached out to touch her. She moved back. I followed. She retreated swiftly. A thicket of evergreens blocked the way. I passed through—then saw nothing.

I waited. “If you have a message,” I said, “return and deliver it.”

Nothing came. Nothing returned.

No Dr. John to turn to now. No one to whisper, “I have seen the nun again.”

Paulina Mary began to visit me often in the Rue Crécy. In the old days, though she had never declared affection for me, my presence had become a kind of habit to her—unnoticed, yet necessary. I used to notice that if I withdrew to my room, she would soon come running after me, open the door, peep in, and say with her firm little voice: “Come down. Why do you sit alone? You must come into the parlour.”

Now she urged me again: “Leave the Rue Fossette. Come live with us. Papa would pay you far more than Madame Beck.”

Mr. Home himself offered me three times my current salary to serve as companion to his daughter. I refused. I think I would have refused even if I were poorer, more uncertain. I was not made for that role. I could teach. I could instruct. But being a governess or a companion felt unnatural—like wearing clothes that did not fit.

Rather than be a governess in a great house, I would willingly take a maid’s job—buy gloves, clean stoves, sweep stairs—in peace and independence. Rather than be a companion, I would sew shirts and starve.

I was not a bright shadow, nor a reflection of beauty. Not like Miss de Bassompierre. I was often quiet, subdued—by nature, not by force. But that quietness had to be chosen, not imposed. It suited me at my desk, among my pupils, in the garden seat that was mine. My qualities were fixed, not flexible. I could not be a foil, a decoration, a part of someone else’s grand design.

Madame Beck and I, though different, understood each other. I was not her companion, nor her children’s governess. She gave me freedom. She tied me to nothing—not even to her own interests. Once, when she returned from a fortnight’s absence due to a relative’s illness, anxious about the school, she found everything well-ordered. She rewarded each teacher—except me. At midnight, she came to my bedside.

“I have no gift for you,” she said. “But I will make fidelity profitable for the St. Pierre. If I try to make it profitable for you, misunderstanding may follow—separation. But one thing I *can* do: leave you free. That is what I will do.”

She kept her word. Every restraint she had ever placed on me, she removed quietly, one by one. So I found pleasure in obeying her rules—not out of fear, but out of respect. I gave double effort to the pupils entrusted to me, not because I was forced, but because I chose to.

As for Paulina Mary, I visited her gladly, though I would not live with her. Soon, I realized her need for me would fade. M. de Bassompierre seemed blind to this truth—unaware of the changes taking place in his daughter. He still saw her as a child, though she was growing into a woman.

I often wondered whether he would approve of her future. He was deeply engaged in science—intense, focused, sometimes stubborn in his pursuits—but trusting in everyday matters. He spoke of what “Polly” would do when she was grown, as if she were still a girl. And she, standing beside him, would sometimes smile, take his head in her hands, and kiss his grey hair. At other times, she would pout, toss her curls. But she never said, “Papa, I *am* grown up.”

She had different ways with different people. With her father, she was childlike—affectionate, playful. With me, serious and thoughtful. With Mrs. Bretton, respectful but reserved. With Graham, shy—very shy. Sometimes she tried to be cold, avoided him. His footsteps startled her. His entrance silenced her. When he spoke, her answers faltered. When he left, she remained flustered.

Even her father noticed.

“My little Polly,” he said once, “you live too quietly. If you grow up this way, you won’t fit into society. You’ve made Dr. Bretton a stranger. Why?”

“_Rather_, papa,” she replied gently, “I liked him as a child.”

“And now?”

“Nothing. Yes, I like him a little—but we are grown strange to each other.”

“Then rub it off, Polly. Talk when he is here. Don’t fear him.”

“He does not talk much. Is he afraid of me, papa?”

“Oh, certainly! What man wouldn’t be afraid of such a quiet little lady?”

“Then tell him not to mind my silence. Say it’s my way, and I mean no harm.”

“Your way? You little chatterbox! It’s not your way—it’s your whim!”

“Well, I’ll improve, papa.”

And the next day, she tried. I saw her attempt to speak easily with Dr. John. His face softened, warmed. He answered her gently, as if afraid to disturb a fragile thing. There was something delicate, almost magical, in her effort—timid yet sincere.

When he left, she approached her father.

“Did I keep my word, papa? Did I behave better?”

“My Polly behaved like a queen. If this continues, I shall be proud. Soon we’ll see her receiving guests with grace. Miss Lucy and I must polish ourselves—lest we be outshone. Still, there’s a little flutter, a tendency to stutter—like when you were six.”

“No, papa!” she protested. “That’s not true.”

“I appeal to Miss Lucy. Didn’t she say ‘yeth’ when asked about the palace of the Prince of Bois l’Etang?”

“Papa, you’re teasing! I can pronounce every letter perfectly. But tell me—why are you so eager for me to be civil to Dr. Bretton? Do you like him?”

“To be sure. For old sake, yes. He’s a good son to his mother, kind-hearted, clever in his work. Yes, the callant is well enough.”

“_Callant_! Ah, Scotchman! Papa, is it Edinburgh or Aberdeen accent?”

“Both, my pet. And likely Glasgow too. That’s why I speak French so well—a good Scots tongue makes for good French.”

“_The_ French! Again, Scotch! You’re incorrigible.”

“Well, Polly, you must persuade Miss Snowe to teach both of us—to make you steady and womanly, and me refined and classical.”

The way M. de Bassompierre spoke of me—of “Miss Snowe”—often gave me pause. How different people saw me! Madame Beck thought me learned and cool. Miss Fanshawe, sharp and cynical. Mr. Home, a model of propriety—perhaps too strict, too cautious—but perfect in her role. Professor Paul Emanuel, however, saw me as fiery, reckless, bold. I smiled at all of them. If anyone truly knew me, it was Paulina Mary.

Since I would not be her paid companion, though our talks were warm and pleasant, she suggested we study together—German, which we both found difficult. We agreed to take lessons from the same teacher in the Rue Crécy. This gave us regular hours to meet. M. de Bassompierre approved—delighted that Madame Minerva Gravity would spend her leisure with his beloved daughter.

And so, in the quiet of the garden, beneath the watchful eye of the old pear-tree, I buried my letters—my hopes, my past—and stepped forward into the unknown.

The next day unfolded with more activity and liveliness than I had anticipated—though perhaps only my own expectations had been so modest. It was the birthday of one of the young princes of Labassecour, the eldest, I believe, the Duc de Dindonneau, and in his honor, a holiday had been declared at the schools, especially at the prestigious Athénée—the town’s leading college. The students there had prepared a loyal address to present, and for this occasion, they were gathered in the grand public hall where annual examinations and prize-givings took place. After the presentation, a professor would deliver an oration—a *discours*—to mark the event.

Several of M. de Bassompierre’s scholarly friends, many of whom were connected with the Athénée, were expected to attend, along with the respected municipal officials of Villette, including M. le Chevalier Staas, the burgomaster, as well as parents and relatives of the students. M. de Bassompierre had been invited by his peers to join them, and naturally, his daughter Paulina would be included. She sent a brief note to Ginevra and me, inviting us to come early so we could meet her there.

As Miss Fanshawe and I dressed in our dormitory on the Rue Fossette, she suddenly burst into laughter, pausing mid-gesture as she adjusted her clothes and staring at me.

“What is it now?” I asked, curious.

“It just seems so strange,” she replied, her usual blend of honesty and insolence flashing in her eyes, “that you and I should now be on such equal footing—visiting the same circles, sharing the same connections.”

“Indeed,” I said, “I never held much regard for the sort of company you used to frequent. Mrs. Cholmondeley and her set would never have suited me at all.”

“And who *are* you, Miss Snowe?” she asked, her tone open and unguarded, so candid that I couldn’t help but smile.

“You once called yourself a nursery governess,” I reminded her. “When you first arrived here, you actually looked after the children in this house—I’ve seen you carry little Georgette like a nursemaid. Few governesses would stoop so low. And now Madame Beck treats you with more respect than she does the Parisienne St. Pierre—and your proud cousin, no less, calls you her closest friend!”

“Amazing,” I agreed, amused by her playful confusion. “Who am I, then? Perhaps some hidden figure in disguise. Too bad I don’t look the part.”

“I’m surprised you’re not flattered,” she pressed on. “You take it all so calmly. If you truly are the nobody I once thought you were, you must be remarkably composed.”

“The nobody you once thought me?” I repeated, feeling a flush rise to my cheeks—but I refused to let anger take hold. What did it matter how a schoolgirl casually labeled someone as *nobody* or *somebody*? I simply said, “I’ve merely been treated with courtesy. And what is so remarkable about that? Why should kindness throw a person into disarray?”

“One can’t help being struck by certain things,” she insisted.

“By wonders of your own making,” I countered. “Are you ready now?”

“Yes. Let me take your arm.”

“I’d prefer not. We’ll walk side by side.”

She always leaned heavily on me when she took my arm, and since I was neither a gentleman nor her lover, I found it uncomfortable.

“There again!” she exclaimed, as we crossed a large square and entered the quiet, pleasant park on our way to the Rue Crécy. “I meant it as a compliment—to show approval of your dress, your appearance. I wanted to say I’m not ashamed to be seen walking with you.”

“Ah,” I said, “so you meant to declare that if Mrs. Cholmondeley were gazing from her window with her lapdog, or Colonel de Hamal brushing his teeth on a balcony, and happened to catch sight of us, you wouldn’t blush for your companion?”

“Yes,” she answered, her frankness—her greatest strength—giving even her falsehoods a kind of honest clarity. It was, in short, the only thing keeping her character from fading into nothingness.

I left any reply to my expression—my lower lip twitching before words could form. My face spoke scorn, not reverence; the look I gave her carried no trace of deference.

“Scornful, sneering creature!” she snapped as we walked. “No one has ever been such a Turk to me as you!”

“You bring it upon yourself,” I said. “Leave me alone. Be sensible. I will.”

“As if one could leave you alone when you’re so odd, so mysterious!”

“The mystery is entirely in your imagination—nothing more than idle thoughts. Please keep them out of my sight.”

“But are you really *anyone*?” she persisted, pushing her hand under my arm despite my resistance. Her arm pressed tightly against mine, as if trying to force itself into place.

“Yes,” I said. “I’m a rising woman: once a companion to an elderly lady, then a nursery governess, now a teacher.”

“Do tell me who you are,” she urged, clinging to the idea of a secret identity with absurd determination. She squeezed my arm, coaxed, pleaded—until I had to stop in the park and laugh. Throughout the walk, she spun endless variations on this theme, proving by her stubborn belief—or disbelief—that she couldn’t imagine how anyone without wealth, title, or name could maintain dignity. As for me, I found peace in knowing I was known where it mattered. The rest weighed lightly on me. Lineage, social rank, intellectual depth—all occupied the same small corner in my mind: third-class lodgers, given only a tiny sitting room and back bedroom. Even if the grand rooms stood empty, I never admitted it to them, believing modest accommodations better suited their station. The world judged differently—and I suppose it’s right to do so. Yet I still believe I’m not wholly wrong in my view.

There are those whose moral standing crumbles with lowered status, whose self-respect vanishes with lost connections. Are they not justified in valuing position and association as shields against degradation? If a man fears he would despise himself if it were known his ancestors were humble, poor, laborers—not aristocrats, not rich men—would it be fair to condemn him for hiding such truths, trembling at the risk of exposure? The longer we live, the wider our understanding grows. We grow less eager to judge others’ choices, less quick to doubt the wisdom of the world. Wherever defenses are layered—whether guarding virtue or reputation—know that they are needed.

We reached the Hôtel Crécy. Paulina was waiting, and Mrs. Bretton was with her. Under their escort and that of M. de Bassompierre, we were soon led into the assembly hall and seated in good seats, near enough to see the Tribune clearly. Before us, the students of the Athénée were arranged in order. The town’s officials and burgomaster occupied places of honor. The young princes and their tutors sat prominently. The hall was filled with the city’s nobility and leading citizens.

At first, I hadn’t cared who would deliver the speech. I expected some learned man to rise and offer a formal address—half lecture to the students, half flattery to the royals.

The Tribune was empty when we entered. But within ten minutes, it filled abruptly. In a single instant, a head, chest, and arms emerged above the crimson desk. I recognized that head instantly—its shape, color, bearing, expression. The dark, tight skull, the broad pale brow, the blue fire in the eyes—details so familiar, so woven into memory with countless whimsical associations, that their sudden reappearance stirred a flicker of laughter in me. I confess, I laughed until I felt warm—then I lowered my head, pulled my veil down, and let my handkerchief conceal my mirth.

I think I was glad to see M. Paul. It was pleasant, even, to see him there—fierce and frank, dark and candid, testy and fearless—as though he still ruled his classroom. His presence was a surprise. Though I knew he taught Belles-Lettres at the college, I hadn’t expected to see him. With *him* in the Tribune, I knew we wouldn’t suffer through empty formalities or insincere flattery. But what followed—what poured forth swiftly, passionately, continuously—I was utterly unprepared for.

He addressed the princes, nobles, magistrates, and townspeople with the same ease, the same sharp, fiery earnestness, as he used to speak to the three divisions of the Rue Fossette. He spoke to the students not as boys, but as future citizens, as budding patriots. The political upheavals that would later shake Europe had not yet been foreseen, and M. Emanuel’s spirit seemed fresh and new to me. Who would have thought the flat, dull soil of Labassecour could produce such strong convictions, such deep national feeling?

I need not detail his opinions here. But I may say I believed him not only sincere but right. Despite his passion, he was grounded and rational. He crushed Utopian dreams beneath his heel, dismissed wild fantasies with contempt. But when he faced tyranny—ah, then! A light sparked in his eye, one worth seeing. When he spoke of injustice, his voice rang clear and bold, like a trumpet echoing through twilight from the park.

His audience didn’t fully grasp his fire, but some of the students caught it. As he finished, they rose and gave him a long, loud, thunderous cheer. For all his temper, he was their favorite professor.

As we left the hall, he stood at the entrance. He saw me, recognized me, and raised his hat. He offered his hand as we passed and asked, “Qu’en dites-vous?”—a question perfectly characteristic, reminding me even in his moment of triumph of his restless curiosity, his lack of what I considered necessary self-control—among his flaws. He shouldn’t have cared what I thought, or what anyone thought. But he did care. And he was too true to nature to hide it, too impulsive to suppress it. Well—if I faulted his eagerness, I admired his honesty. I wanted to praise him. I had plenty of praise in my heart. But alas—no words came. Who finds words at the perfect moment? I stammered something awkward, and was relieved when others rushed up with enthusiastic congratulations, filling the silence I could not.

A gentleman introduced him to M. de Bassompierre. The Count, equally impressed, invited him to dine with his friends—many of whom were also acquainted with M. Emanuel. He declined dinner, for he was always somewhat reserved around the wealthy. There was a quiet strength in him—a sturdy independence—not flashy, but evident as one came to know him. He promised, however, to visit later with his friend, M. A——, a French Academician.

At dinner, both Ginevra and Paulina looked beautiful—each in her own way. Ginevra had the advantage in physical charm, perhaps, but Paulina shone in subtler, deeper ways: in the brightness and eloquence of her eyes, in her graceful demeanor, in the gentle variety of her expressions. Ginevra’s deep crimson dress complemented her light curls and rosy complexion. Paulina’s attire—simple yet flawless, white and sheer—highlighted the delicate warmth of her skin, the soft animation of her face, the tender depth of her eyes, the rich flow of her dark hair—darker than her Saxon cousin’s, as were her brows, lashes, irises, and mobile pupils. Nature had sketched these features lightly and carelessly in Miss Fanshawe, but in Miss de Bassompierre, they had been refined into exquisite detail.

Paulina was awed by the scholars, yet not silenced. She spoke softly, modestly, with effort—but with such sweetness, such fine intelligence, that her father often paused mid-sentence to listen, his gaze fixed on her with proud delight. It was M. Z——, a courteous and learned Frenchman, who drew her into conversation. I was enchanted by her French—it was flawless: correct structure, true idioms, pure accent. Ginevra, who had lived half her life abroad, could never match it—not because she lacked words, but because she lacked precision and purity. On this point, M. de Bassompierre was pleased; he was a strict critic of language.

Another observer was present—Dr. Bretton, who had arrived late due to professional duties. At the moment he sat down, both ladies were quietly examined by him. That watchful glance returned several times. His arrival stirred Miss Fanshawe, who had seemed listless. She became bright, cheerful, chatted—though rarely on topic, or rather, on topics below the occasion’s dignity. Her light, disconnected chatter might have pleased Graham once—perhaps it still did. Or perhaps it was only fancy that suggested while his eyes were satisfied and his ears fed, his taste, his keen intellect, remained unfulfilled. Certainly, though his attention was taxed, he responded courteously. He showed no irritation, no coldness. Ginevra sat beside him, and during dinner, he focused almost entirely on her. She seemed content and left the table in high spirits.

Yet no sooner had we entered the drawing-room than she sank into silence. She threw herself onto a couch and declared both the speech and the dinner dull affairs, asking her cousin how she could endure such a group of prosaic “gros-bonnets” as her father gathered around him. The moment the men began to move, she stopped speaking, sprang up, and rushed to the piano, playing with spirited energy.

Dr. Bretton, one of the first to enter, took his place beside her. I thought he would not stay long—he had a seat near the hearth that seemed to call to him. But he only glanced at it. While he watched, others moved toward Paulina. Her grace and mind captivated the thoughtful Frenchmen. Her delicate beauty, gentle manner, and natural tact pleased their sensibilities. They gathered around her—not to discuss science, which would have silenced her—but to talk literature, art, life—subjects on which it soon became clear she had read and reflected deeply.

I listened. And I knew Graham heard too—his hearing as keen as his vision. He absorbed every word. I felt the conversation suited him perfectly—pleased him nearly to pain.

In Paulina, there was more strength—of feeling, of character—than most suspected—even Graham himself. More than she would ever reveal to those who didn’t seek to see it. To speak plainly, reader, no true beauty, no real grace, no genuine refinement exists without strength as firm, as complete, as reliable. As you might expect good fruit from a rootless tree, so you might expect lasting charm from a weak soul. For a time, beauty may bloom around weakness—but it cannot withstand a storm. It fades, even under sunlit skies.

Graham would have recoiled if anyone hinted at the strength beneath Paulina’s delicacy. But I, who had known her as a child, sensed the solid roots anchoring her graces to the earth of reality.

While Dr. Bretton listened and waited for a break in the circle, his gaze swept the room. By chance, it landed on me—seated quietly near my godmother and M. de Bassompierre, who were engaged in what Mr. Home called “a two-handed crack,” or what the Count would call a tête-à-tête. Graham smiled, crossed the room, asked how I was, remarked that I looked pale.

I smiled in return—my own thought. It had been nearly three months since Dr. John had spoken to me—three months he didn’t even notice had passed. He sat down, fell silent. He preferred to observe rather than speak. Ginevra and Paulina were opposite him—now he could study both forms, both faces.

New guests had arrived since dinner—ladies and gentlemen alike. Among the men, I had already glimpsed, in passing, a stern, dark, professorial figure hovering in an inner salon, visible only in silhouette. M. Emanuel knew many of the men present, but I believe he was unfamiliar with most of the women—except me. As he looked toward the hearth, he saw me and instinctively moved forward. But seeing Dr. Bretton nearby, he hesitated, then stepped back. If that had been all, no quarrel would have arisen. But he didn’t just stop—he frowned, pouted his lip, and made such an ugly face that I turned away.

M. Joseph Emanuel had arrived, along with his austere brother. At that very moment, he took over from Ginevra at the piano. What a masterful touch replaced her childish jingle! How grand and grateful the instrument sounded under the hands of a true artist!

“Lucy,” Dr. Bretton began, breaking the silence with a smile as Ginevra passed him, glancing sideways, “Miss Fanshawe is certainly a fine girl.”

Of course I agreed.

“Is there,” he continued, “another in the room as lovely?”

“I think there is none as handsome.”

“I agree, Lucy. You and I often agree in opinion, in taste, I think—at least in judgment.”

“Do we?” I said, uncertain.

“I believe if you had been a boy, Lucy—my mother’s godson instead of her goddaughter—we might have been close friends. Our opinions would have blended.”

He wore a teasing air—a light, half-caressing, half-ironic gleam in his eye. Ah, Graham! I’ve spent many solitary moments wondering how you truly saw Lucy Snowe. Was your estimation always kind? Had I possessed wealth and station, would your regard for me have been the same? And yet—I do not mean to accuse. No—you sometimes saddened or troubled me. But my temperament is easily disturbed—like a shadow under a cloud. Perhaps before the scales of justice, I stand more at fault than you.

Still, I struggled to suppress the ache in my heart as I realized that while Graham could give others his deepest, most serious attention, he offered me only light mockery—only jests. So I asked calmly, “On what points do we agree so closely?”

“We both possess a sharp observant faculty. You may not credit me with it, but I do.”

“But you spoke of taste. We may see the same things and judge them differently.”

“Let us test it. Of course, you admire Miss Fanshawe’s merits. Now, what do you think of others? Your mother, for instance—or the gentlemen yonder, Messieurs A—— and Z———or, say, that pale little lady, Miss de Bassompierre?”

“You know what I think of your mother. I haven’t thought of Messieurs A—— and Z——.”

“And the other?”

“I think she is, as you say, a pale little lady—pale, certainly, now, from fatigue and excitement.”

“You don’t remember her as a child?”

“I wonder sometimes whether you do.”

“I had forgotten her. But it’s notable that memories—people, events, even looks or words—can reappear under certain conditions, certain shifts in mind.”

“That’s possible.”

“Yet,” he went on, “the return is incomplete—dream-like, airy. It needs confirmation. A witness is required. Were you not a guest at Bretton ten years ago, when Mr. Home brought his little daughter—whom we called ‘little Polly’—to stay with my mother?”

“I was there the night she arrived and the morning she left.”

“A peculiar child, wasn’t she? I wonder how I treated her. Was I fond of children then? Was I kind—great, reckless, schoolboy that I was? But you don’t remember me, of course?”

“You’ve seen your portrait at La Terrasse. It resembles you. In manner, you were almost the same yesterday as today.”

“But, Lucy, how is that? Such an oracle makes me curious. What am I today? What was I ten years ago?”

“Gracious to whatever pleased you—unkind or cruel to nothing.”

“There you are wrong. I think I was almost a brute to *you*, for example.”

“A brute? No, Graham. I would never have endured brutality.”

“This,” he said, “I do remember: quiet Lucy Snowe tasted nothing of my grace.”

“As little of my cruelty.”

“Oh, had I been Nero himself, I could not have tormented a being as harmless as a shadow.”

I smiled—but I also stifled a groan. Oh, I wished he would leave me alone—stop referring to me. These labels—these attributes—I pushed them away. His “quiet Lucy Snowe,” his “inoffensive shadow”—I returned them, not with scorn, but with deep weariness. Their weight was leaden; let him not burden me with it. Happily, he soon changed the subject.

M. Paul Emanuel had a sharp sensitivity to interruptions during his lessons—any disturbance, no matter how small, was met with a fury that made the very air tremble. To enter his classroom under such circumstances was considered by both teachers and students alike as perilous as stepping into a storm unprepared. Even Madame Beck, who usually moved with calm authority, would rush through the room like a startled bird, her skirt pulled tight, skirting the edge of the raised platform as if avoiding jagged rocks. As for Rosine, the gatekeeper, whose duty it was every half-hour to fetch pupils from their studies for music lessons in the oratory or saloon, she often became so flustered on her second or third attempt that she could barely speak—her face pale, her breath caught in her throat, all from the terrifying glare behind M. Paul’s spectacles.

One morning, I sat in the carré, stitching on an embroidery begun by one of the girls but left unfinished. My fingers worked steadily, while my ears listened to the rising cadence of a voice in the neighboring classroom—harsh, insistent, growing more agitated by the moment. A solid wall separated us, and just beyond me stood a glass door leading to the courtyard—a quick escape route, should the storm break this way. But instead of fear, I found myself amused by the mounting tension.

Poor Rosine was not spared. That morning alone, she had already crossed the forbidden threshold four times. Now, for the fifth time, she faced the daunting task of retrieving a pupil directly from under M. Paul’s watchful eye.

“Mon Dieu! Mon Dieu!” she gasped. “What shall I do? Monsieur will kill me—I’m certain of it! He’s furious!”

With trembling courage, she opened the door.

“Mademoiselle La Malle au piano!” she called out.

Before she could retreat or fully close the door, a voice thundered:

“From this moment—this class is forbidden. The first person who opens this door or enters this room will be hanged—be it Madame Beck herself!”

Ten minutes later, Rosine’s soft slippers scuffed again along the corridor.

“Mademoiselle,” she whispered, “I wouldn longer go back in there for five francs. Monsieur’s glasses—they’re truly dreadful. And here’s a messenger with a message from the Athénée. I told Madame Beck I couldn’t deliver it, and she said you must take it.”

“Me?” I replied. “That’s rather unfair! It’s not my job. Come on, Rosine—be brave. Take it yourself.”

“I? Impossible! I’ve crossed him five times today. Madame must hire a gendarme. Ouf! I can’t bear it anymore!”

“Bah! You’re just afraid. What does the message say?”

“Exactly the kind he hates most—an urgent summons to go immediately to the Athénée. There’s an official visitor—an inspector—someone important—and Monsieur _must_ go. You know how he feels about ‘must.’”

Yes, I knew well. The little man detested constraints, despised obligation. Anything forced upon him provoked rebellion. Still, I accepted the task—not without dread, but with a mix of fear and curiosity. I opened the door, stepped inside, and shut it quickly behind me, my hand unsteady. To move slowly, to rattle a latch, or leave a door ajar—these were sins worse than the act itself.

There I stood. He sat at his desk, bent over his work. His mood was clearly foul—perhaps at its worst. He’d been teaching arithmetic, a subject he disliked, and which always soured his temper. Not a student dared breathe too loudly when he mentioned numbers. He didn’t lift his head at my entrance. To look up at a breach of his rule was an effort he couldn’t bring himself to make—thankfully, because it gave me time to walk down the long room. I preferred facing his anger close up rather than feeling it from afar.

At the front of the class, I paused. Naturally, I wasn’t worthy of immediate attention. He continued his lesson. But silence wouldn’t suffice. He had to hear me. He had to answer.

Too short to see over the elevated desk, I peeked around its edge, hoping only for a better view of his face. When I entered, it had struck me as oddly resembling a sallow, dark tiger. Twice I glimpsed him—unnoticed. The third time, my eye barely cleared the desk’s shadow when it was seized—pierced—by his spectacles. Rosine was right: those lenses held a cold, unyielding terror, deeper than the fire in his own eyes.

Now, proximity worked in my favor. His nearsightedness rendered the glasses useless for spotting someone right before him. So he removed them. Suddenly, we stood on equal ground.

I was glad I wasn’t truly afraid—because standing there, I felt nothing but calm. When he demanded rope and gallows for the sentence he’d just declared, I offered him a needle full of embroidery thread with such quiet grace that it must have softened some of his rage. I didn’t show off the gesture. I simply passed the thread around the desk and tied it neatly to the back of his chair, ready for hanging.

“What do you want?” he growled, his voice low and grating, teeth clenched, as if swearing silently that no force on earth would draw a smile from him.

I began without hesitation: “Monsieur,” I said, “I ask for the impossible, for unheard-of things.” Then, deciding honesty was best, I delivered the message in a swift, quiet voice, exaggerating its urgency.

He refused to listen. “I won’t go. I won’t leave this class, not even if every official in Villette summoned me. I won’t move an inch for king, cabinet, or chamber.”

I knew he *had* to go. Duty and reason both demanded it. So I waited in silence, as if he hadn’t spoken. He asked what else I wanted.

“Only your reply to give the messenger.”

He waved a dismissive hand.

Then I reached toward the bonnet-grec resting on the windowsill. He followed my movement with narrowed eyes—no doubt amazed and pitying my boldness.

“Ah!” he muttered. “If it came to that—if Miss Lucy touched my bonnet-grec—she might as well wear it herself, become a boy for the night, and kindly go to the Athénée in my place.”

With respect, I placed the bonnet on his desk. Its tassel seemed to nod at me ominously.

“I’ll write a note of apology—that will suffice,” he insisted, still resisting.

Knowing it wouldn’t, I gently pushed the bonnet toward his hand. It slid across the polished surface, carrying with it the delicate steel-framed spectacles. And then—fearfully—the glasses fell to the platform below.

I’d seen them fall before—always unharmed. This time, however, they shattered. Each lens became a broken star.

Dismay gripped me—dismay and regret. I knew how much those glasses meant. M. Paul’s vision was rare, hard to correct; these were perfect for him. I’d heard him call them his treasures. As I picked them up, cracked and useless, my hands shook. I feared what I’d done—but I was even more sorry than afraid.

For a few moments, I couldn’t meet his gaze. He spoke first.

“There!” he said. “I am now widowed of my spectacles! I suppose Mademoiselle Lucy will admit the rope and gallows are fully earned. You tremble at your fate. Ah, traitress! traitress! You mean to leave me blind and helpless in your hands!”

I lifted my eyes. His face wasn’t angry. It wasn’t even sad. Instead, it bloomed with the same gentle smile I’d seen that evening at the Hotel Crécy—warm, alive, forgiving. He wasn’t furious. He wasn’t even hurt. In fact, he showed mercy where others would have punished. This disaster, which I thought had ruined my chance to persuade him, became my greatest ally. Difficult to manage when unharmed, he became tenderly pliant once I stood before him as a guilty, remorseful offender.

Still teasing me—calling me “a strong woman,” “a terrible Englishwoman,” “a little destroyer”—he admitted he could not refuse one who had shown such dangerous power. It was like the great Emperor smashing a vase to strike awe. Finally, he crowned himself with the bonnet-grec, took the broken spectacles from my hand with a warm, forgiving clasp, bowed, and left for the Athénée—cheerful, spirited, and in high spirits.

Yet despite all this kindness, I found myself quarreling with M. Paul again by nightfall. And though I wished it otherwise, I couldn’t help it.

It was his custom—pleasant and welcome—to arrive unexpectedly in the evenings, bursting into our quiet study hour. Without warning, he’d impose his presence, commandeer our activities, send books aside, pull out workbags, and with a single volume or handful of pamphlets, replace the dull religious readings with something grand—some passionate tragedy read with fire and soul. Or he’d illuminate our dim world with tales of Parisian wit, reading passages from enchanting novels or witty feuilletons, always censoring anything unsuitable for young girls. Where deletion left gaps, he would improvise—filling in dialogue, description—often creating something stronger than the original.

That evening, we sat in silence, the pupils studying, the teachers working. I was embroidering—something light, yet meaningful. It was meant as a gift, and the occasion was near, so I worked quickly, focused.

We heard the sharp bell ring—the familiar sound. Then his footsteps, swift and sure. “Voilà Monsieur!” everyone murmured at once. The double doors flew open as they always did for him, and he stood among us.

Two long tables, each lit by a central lamp, flanked by benches. Teachers sat beneath the lamps, students arranged by age and diligence—serious ones near the light, the idle ones toward the edges. M. Paul’s habit was to offer a chair to Zélie St. Pierre, the senior mistress, then take her seat, using the bright light needed by his nearsightedness.

As usual, Zélie rose with a wide, toothy smile—one that stretched from ear to ear but never warmed the eyes. But M. Paul ignored her. Perhaps he saw her, perhaps not—he was capricious, like women are said to be. Or maybe his new spectacles excused his oversight. Whatever the reason, he passed her by, came to the other side of the table, and before I could rise, whispered, “Don’t move,” and settled between me and Miss Fanshawe—my ever-present neighbor, whose elbow always pressed into mine, no matter how many times I said, “Ginevra, I wish you were at Jericho.”

Easy enough to say “Don’t move,” but how could I? I had to clear space for him, ask the girls to shift so I could retreat. Ginevra could cling to me, “keeping warm” on winter nights, poking and fidgeting until I pinned a needle to my belt for protection—but M. Paul was not to endure such treatment. So I swept away my materials, made room for his book, and withdrew—leaving just a yard of bench, a reasonable distance, I thought.

But M. Paul was never reasonable. Like flint and tinder, he sparked instantly.

“You don’t want me for a neighbor,” he growled. “You act like a noblewoman. You treat me like a pariah.” He scowled. “Very well—I’ll fix this.” And he began.

“Rise, all of you, Mesdemoiselles!” he commanded.

They rose. He sent them all to the other table. Then he placed me at one end of the long bench, carefully bringing my basket, silk, scissors, everything I needed. He seated himself at the opposite end.

The absurdity of it stunned us all. Not a soul dared laugh. To giggle would have been fatal. I took it calmly. I sat isolated, cut off from conversation, and returned to my work, quiet and content.

“Is this far enough?” he demanded.

“You are the judge, Monsieur.”

“You well know it isn’t. You created this vast emptiness. I did nothing.”

And with that, he began to read.

He chose a French translation of what he called “a drama by Williams Shackspire—the false god,” he added, “of those foolish pagans, the English.” How differently he might have described him, had his temper been calmer, I need not say.

The translation was poor, and I couldn’t hide my disdain for its clumsy phrases. Not that I spoke—only looked. But his spectacles caught every glance. He noticed them all. Soon, he discarded the lenses entirely, letting his eyes burn freely. And at the northern pole of the room, where he’d exiled himself, he grew hotter than any southern sun.

When the reading ended, we waited—uncertain whether he’d leave in silence or vent his anger. Suppression wasn’t in his nature, but what had I done? I hadn’t spoken. Could I be blamed for moving my eyes?

Supper arrived—bread, milk diluted with tepid water. Out of respect, the rolls and glasses remained untouched.

“Take your supper, ladies,” he said, busy writing notes in the margin of his “Williams Shackspire.”

They took theirs. I took mine—roll and glass—but stayed seated, continuing my work as I ate, calm and composed, almost serene. It felt as if his restless, fiery presence drew all agitation from me like a magnet, leaving only peace.

He rose. Would he leave without another word? He turned toward the door.

No—he turned back. Perhaps to retrieve his pencil case, left on the table.

He took it, snapped the pencil against the wood, broke the point, re-sharpened it, pocketed it, and walked straight to me.

The others chattered around the other table—loudly, as usual. They never lowered their voices at meals.

M. Paul stood behind me. He asked what I was making.

“A watchguard,” I said.

“For whom?”

“For a gentleman—one of my friends.”

He leaned down and, in a whisper—true to form—his voice hissed into my ear.

Of all the women he knew, he said, I was the one who could make herself most unpleasant. With me, friendship was impossible. I had an “unbearable character,” perverse beyond belief. How I managed it, he didn’t know. But whatever goodwill anyone brought to me—crac!—it turned to discord, kindness to hostility. He, M. Paul, meant me no harm. He claimed the right to be a neutral acquaintance, free of malice. Yet how I treated him! With biting sarcasm, rebellious energy, unjust fury!

I couldn’t help widening my eyes slightly. “Sarcasm? Energy? Fury? I didn’t know…”

“Shh! Right now! There! There I go—alive as gunpowder!” He was sorry—for my sake. This impetuosity, this passion—generous, perhaps, but excessive—would ruin me one day. It was a pity. I wasn’t, he believed, wholly without good qualities. If only I’d listen, be calmer, less flighty, less vain, less obsessed with appearances, less captivated by men praised for their height, “doll-like colors,” “moderately good noses,” and immense stupidity—I might yet become useful, even admirable. But as it was…

His voice faltered.

I wanted to look up, to reach out, to soothe him. But I feared I’d either laugh or cry—such a strange blend of tenderness and absurdity.

I thought he was finished. But no. He sat down to continue.

While he, M. Paul, spoke of such painful truths, he would dare my anger for my own good. He’d mention a change in my dress. He confessed that when he first saw me—or rather, caught glimpses of me—he found my appearance satisfactory. The gravity, the plain simplicity, inspired hope. But what cursed influence had led me to add flowers to my bonnet, wear “embroidered collars,” and even appear in a _scarlet gown_? He might guess, but he wouldn’t say.

I interrupted—this time with indignation and horror.

“Scarlet, Monsieur Paul? It wasn’t scarlet! It was pink—pale pink—softened with black lace!”

“Pink or scarlet, yellow or crimson, green or blue—it makes no difference. All are flashy, reckless colors. And that lace? That’s just another ‘trinket.’” He sighed over my decline. “I cannot, I’m sorry to say, be precise on this matter. I lack the names of these trinkets—I might make small errors, inviting your mockery and provoking your sudden, passionate temper. But generally speaking—and I know I’m right—your costume has lately taken on ‘worldly airs,’ which wound me deeply.”

What “worldly airs” he saw in my plain winter merino and white collar, I couldn’t imagine. When I asked, he said it was too concerned with effect. And then—“Do you not have a ribbon bow at your neck?”

“And if you disapprove of a ribbon bow for a lady, Monsieur, would you also reject something like this for a gentleman?”—holding up my bright silk-and-gold chainlet.

His only answer was a groan—over my levity, I suppose.

After a few silent minutes, watching me stitch more diligently than ever, he asked: “Will what I’ve just said make you hate me completely?”

And I, after a pause, answered quietly:
“Perhaps not. But it certainly makes me understand you better.”

I rose before dawn, my body still heavy with sleep but my mind already awake and focused. Kneeling on the cold dormitory floor beside the central stand, I finished the final stitches of the guard I had been weaving throughout the night. The dim glow of the last watch of the night-lamp barely lit the room, but it was enough for me to see the delicate strands of silk and beads take shape under my fingers. Every thread had been used—every bead carefully selected. I had doubled the length, knowing from experience that richness and precision mattered when crafting something meant for a man whose taste demanded both subtlety and strength. To finish it, I needed only a clasp. Fortunately, I possessed one: the fastening of my sole necklace. I detached it with care, reattached it to the end of the chain, then wound the completed piece tightly into a small box I’d purchased especially for this purpose—a shimmering shell case of “nacarat” hue, adorned with a tiny crown of blue stones that sparkled like frozen stars. Inside the lid, using the tip of my scissors, I etched three initials: P. C. D. E. A quiet promise, hidden in plain sight.

The reader may recall Madame Beck’s annual fête—the grand celebration marked by gifts from the school. Each year, a collective offering was made in her honor, a tradition reserved solely for her and, in a quieter form, for her cousin and mentor, M. Emanuel. His recognition was not arranged or forced; it arose naturally from the respect his students held for him. Though he disdained jewels and fine things—clearly stating he would accept neither silver nor gold—he valued sincerity over cost. A single flower, a drawing, offered without pretense, pleased him more than any glittering ring or ornate box. He was not wise by worldly standards, yet there was something tender in his nature, a deep empathy for truth—even when it was painful. He could forgive misfortune if it was confessed honestly, but if he found deceit, if he sensed a lie beneath the surface, he became merciless. With a gaze that pierced through illusion, he could expose the hidden flaws of the soul—the twisted thoughts, the secret wounds, the false masks men and women wore. And when he did, he did so with brutal clarity, as if justice demanded nothing less. I often wept for those he unmasked. I resented him too. Yet I knew he believed himself righteous.

M. Paul’s fête fell on the first of March, a Thursday. The day dawned bright and clear, the sun warm upon the windows. It was also the morning of mass, and the afternoon brought a half-holiday—time to walk, shop, visit. These combined joys inspired a rare freshness among the girls. Clean collars appeared, the usual dull woolen uniforms replaced with lighter garments. Even Mademoiselle Zélie St. Pierre, usually so cautious with money, wore a silk dress—unheard of in Labassecour, where such extravagance was frowned upon. She even summoned a hairdresser to style her hair, and whispers spread that she had dabbed her hands and handkerchief with a new perfume. Poor Zélie—she often lamented her life of confinement, her debts, her lack of freedom. She dreamed of marriage, of a husband who would pay her bills, clothe her, and allow her, at last, to “goûter un peu les plaisirs.” Rumors said she was enamored of M. Emanuel. And indeed, he watched her often—intently, silently. I’ve seen him fix his gaze on her for minutes while the class wrote in silence, his eyes sharp and unreadable. She flinched under his attention—half-pleased, half-afraid—and he seemed to know every tremor of her spirit. He saw beyond words, beyond smiles, into the depths where shame and longing hid. He could be cruel, yes—but never unjust. Not when the truth was laid bare.

After breakfast and mass, the bell rang. The classroom filled with girls dressed neatly, each holding a bouquet of spring flowers—tender blossoms, fragrant and fresh. Only I sat with empty hands. I liked flowers growing wild, rooted in soil. Once cut, they seemed hollow, fleeting. Their likeness to life saddened me. I never gave them to those I loved, nor did I wish to receive them. Zélie noticed my absence. Her eyes swept over me, searching for some hidden bloom, some discreet token. But there was none. She smiled, satisfied.

“How wise of you, Miss Lucie,” she said, “to save your money. I foolishly spent two francs on these hot-house flowers.”

She displayed her bouquet proudly.

Then—footsteps. _His_ footsteps. They came as always, brisk and precise, but today they carried a different weight. We felt it—an unspoken warmth, a hint of something softer beneath the usual rigidity. When he entered, the room brightened. He looked… changed. Gone was the dark paletôt, its folds hiding his figure. Instead, he wore a clean coat and a silk waistcoat, neat and refined. The defiant bonnet-grec was absent; he walked bare-headed, a Christian hat tucked in his gloved hand. He looked better than ever—his blue eyes clear, his dark face glowing with quiet kindness. One might forget his nose was broad, his cheek thin, his brow severe. He stood before us not as a tyrant, but as a man capable of grace.

He placed his hat and gloves on the desk. “Bon jour, mes amies,” he said—not with cheer, nor with the oily tone of a priest, but with a voice that belonged only to him, one that spoke when his heart moved his lips. That heart, though irritable, was not stone. Beneath its surface lay a tenderness that drew him toward children, toward girls, toward women—though he fought it, he could not deny the bond.

“We all wish you a good day, Monsieur,” Zélie began, stepping forward. “We offer our congratulations on your fête.”

She laid her bouquet before him. Then one after another, the girls passed by, leaving their nosegays. Soon, the desk was crowned with a towering pyramid of flowers—so lush, so vibrant, it nearly swallowed him whole. Silence followed. We waited.

Five minutes passed. Ten. Still no word.

Then, from behind the floral mountain, a voice emerged—low, deep, almost growling.

“Est-ce là tout?”

Zélie glanced around.

“Have all given their bouquets?” she asked.

Yes. All had offered theirs. The senior mistress confirmed it.

“Est-ce là tout?” he repeated, lower now, heavier.

Zélie stepped forward again, smiling sweetly. “Monsieur, I have the honor to inform you that, with one exception, every girl has presented her bouquet. Miss Lucie, being a foreigner, may not have known our custom—or perhaps she found it too frivolous to observe.”

“Famous!” I muttered under my breath. “You’re quite the speaker, Zélie.”

From the estrade, a hand rose—gesturing, silent, dismissing words.

Then, slowly, he emerged. Standing before the map of the world on the wall, he stared straight ahead, and once more, in tones that seemed almost tragic:

“Est-ce là tout?”
