I could have corrected it. I had the little shell box in my hand. I had planned to give it. But first, the absurdity of his performance amused me. Then Zélie’s intervention sparked defiance. I had no desire to defend myself against her insinuations. And besides—M. Paul had taken my refusal so seriously. He deserved to be annoyed. So I kept my box, my composure, and sat like stone.

“It is well,” he said at last. A shadow crossed his face—anger, sorrow, resolve. He swallowed hard, then launched into his customary speech.

I don’t remember what he said. I didn’t listen. The effort to suppress his emotion, the abrupt shift from pain to control, left me oddly unsettled. The humor faded.

Near the end of his talk, a distraction broke the tension. I dropped my thimble. In bending to pick it up, I struck my head on the corner of the desk. The noise startled him. He abandoned his forced calm, cast aside dignity, and erupted—this time, not in French, but in English.

Somehow, during his speech, he had crossed the Channel and landed on British soil. And there he stood, scathing.

A quick glance swept the room—sharp, cynical—settling on me. Then he turned his fury on “les Anglaises.”

I had never heard English women treated so harshly. He spared nothing—their minds, morals, manners, appearance. He mocked their height, their necks, their arms, their clothes, their education, their skepticism, their pride, their hypocrisy. He ground his teeth, as if he longed to say worse. He was bitter, venomous, ugly.

“Little wicked creature,” I thought. “Am I to fear offending you? No. You shall be indifferent to me—as the shabbiest bouquet in your pile.”

But I couldn’t hold it. At first, I endured it. Fifteen minutes passed, and I remained still. But then he attacked our greatest names, our heroes, our history—spreading lies, defiling symbols, dragging the Union Jack through mud. Zélie grinned, the whole class laughed. They hated England secretly. And then—I struck my desk sharply, opened my mouth, and shouted:

“Vive l’Angleterre, l’Histoire et les Héros! A bas la France, la Fiction et les Faquins!”

Silence. Shock. I suppose they thought I’d gone mad. The Professor pulled out his handkerchief, pressed it to his lips, and smiled—darkly, triumphantly. He had won. He had made me angry. Instantly, he softened. He resumed his poetic tone, praised the flowers, compared the girls to blossoms, complimented Zélie’s bouquet, and announced a spring outing—breakfast in the country, for “those he could call friends.”

“Donc je n’y serai pas,” I said, without thinking.

“Soit,” he replied. He gathered the flowers, strode out of the room, and I, gathering my things, climbed the stairs. I don’t know if he was angry—but I was.

Yet within an hour, sitting on the edge of my bed, replaying his look, his words, I began to smile. A pang of regret stirred—had I not offered the box? I had meant to please him. Fate had other plans.

Later, remembering that desks were not sacred, and fearing someone might open mine—especially since it bore the initials P. C. D. E.—I descended to the classroom.

It was empty. The day pupils had gone home, the boarders were walking, the teachers shopping. The rooms were silent. The grande salle, with its globe, chandeliers, and closed piano, rested in peace. I paused at the first classe door—it was slightly ajar. Unusual. Usually locked. I hesitated, then heard a sound—a step, a chair shifting, the creak of a drawer opening.

“Madame Beck is inspecting,” I thought. But when I peered inside, it wasn’t her. It was a man—M. Emanuel—sitting in my chair, his hand rummaging through my desk, his face buried in my papers. He wore his ordinary paletôt, the bonnet-grec discarded on the floor.

I knew he visited my desk. Often. He left signs—exercises corrected overnight, books mysteriously appearing, pamphlets tucked into my basket. All smelled of cigars. I’d tried to air them once. He caught me, took the book from my hands, and would have thrown it into the stove. I snatched it back. After that, I stopped protesting.

But I’d never seen him. Until now.

And here he was—smoking, his pale blue breath curling from his lips as he leaned over my desk. I stepped quietly forward, standing behind him, leaning over his shoulder.

My heart ached. After this morning’s hostility, after my silence, after his hurt—he had brought me two handsome volumes, chosen with care. Now, he stirred my papers gently, rearranging them, not destroying. I watched him, unconscious, doing me good. My anger melted. I did not dislike him.

He turned. He heard me breathe. His nerves were sharp, but he didn’t flinch.

“I thought you were in town,” he said, regaining his composure. “It’s fortunate you’re not. Do you think I care about being caught? Not I. I come here often.”

“I know,” I said.

“You find books. But you don’t read them—they’ve passed under this,” he gestured to his cigar.

“They have. But I do read them.”

“Without pleasure?”

“Monsieur must not be contradicted.”

“Do you like them? Are they acceptable?”

“You’ve seen me reading them a hundred times. I have few diversions. I value what you provide.”

“I mean well. If you see that, and enjoy my efforts—why can we not be friends?”

“A fatalist would say—because we cannot.”

“This morning,” he said, “I woke cheerful. I came happy. You spoiled my day.”

“No, Monsieur. Only a few hours. Unintentionally.”

“Unintentionally? No. It was my fête. Everyone wished me joy. The little girls gave violets, whispered blessings. You—nothing. Not a leaf, not a glance. Was that unintentional?”

“I meant no harm.”

“Then you truly did not know our custom? You would have spent a few centimes on a flower, had you known it was expected?”

“I knew. I was prepared. Yet I spent nothing.”

He studied me. Then, slowly, he nodded. The moment hung between us—quiet, charged. And I knew, in that silence, that something had shifted.

The reader should not rush to conclusions—should not assume, with too generous a heart, that from the day of our first encounter, M. Paul transformed into a man gentle and easy to live with, no longer capable of casting shadows of danger or discomfort around him. No. He remained, at his core, a small man governed by moods that defied reason. When overwrought—which was often—he became sharply irritable, his temper flaring like tinder in a storm. And beneath his veins ran a dark current, thick with the poison of jealousy—not merely the tender kind born of love, but a colder, sharper breed rooted in pride and intellect. I used to watch him as he sat, brow furrowed, lip curled, scrutinizing my exercises—always finding fault, though there were fewer than he wished. He liked me to stumble, to make mistakes; a cluster of errors pleased him as much as a handful of nuts might delight a child. I once thought, sitting quietly beside him, that he bore a strange resemblance to Napoleon Bonaparte. I still think so.

In his utter lack of magnanimity, he mirrored the Emperor. M. Paul would have quarreled with twenty learned women without hesitation, carried on petty wars with entire circles of scholars, never caring for dignity or decorum. He would have banished fifty Madame de Staëls had they crossed him. I remember one fierce episode involving Madame Panache—a woman temporarily employed by Madame Beck to teach history. She was intelligent—truly, she knew her subject well—and possessed the rare art of making what she knew shine. Words flowed easily from her, confidence filled her voice. Her appearance was striking; many would have called her “a fine woman.” Yet something about her boldness, her fullness of presence, grated against M. Paul’s delicate, exacting sensibilities. The sound of her voice echoing through the hall would send him into agitation; her long stride down the corridor would prompt him to snatch up his papers and flee.

One day, with deliberate malice, he intruded upon her class. In seconds, he grasped her method—different from his own. Without ceremony or courtesy, he pointed out what he called her errors. Whether he expected submission, I do not know. But he met resistance—sharp, unyielding, and laced with rebuke for his unwarranted interference. Instead of withdrawing gracefully, he threw down the gauntlet. Madame Panache, fierce as any warrior queen, picked it up instantly. She snapped her fingers in his face, launched into a torrent of words. M. Emanuel was eloquent—but Madame Panache was relentless.

A bitter rivalry followed. Rather than laugh at her pride, M. Paul detested her with genuine fury. He pursued her with grim determination, refusing sleep, food, even pleasure, until she was expelled from the school. He won the battle, but I cannot say the victory adorned him with grace. Once, I ventured to suggest this. To my surprise, he admitted I might be right. Yet he insisted that when confronted with people like her—coarse, self-satisfied, proud—he lost control. A deep, active aversion drove him to war, to eradication.

Three months later, hearing she had fallen on hard times and faced real want, he forgot his hatred. With the same energy he had shown in destruction, he now worked tirelessly to secure her another post. When she came to thank him, to mend their rift, her familiar voice—slightly loud, her manner—slightly forward—overwhelmed him. Within ten minutes, he rose abruptly and bowed her out, himself nearly bowing with the effort, consumed by nervous irritation.

In his hunger for power, his need to dominate, M. Paul resembled Bonaparte. He was not always a man to be obeyed. Sometimes, it was necessary to resist—to stand firm, to meet his gaze and tell him plainly that his demands exceeded reason, that his rule approached tyranny.

He watched the emergence of talent under his charge with suspicion, almost hostility. When a new gift began to stir within a student, he scowled. He held back his hand—perhaps saying, “Come on if you can,” but offering no aid. Only after the struggle was over, after life had been drawn into breath, after the heart beat and the eyes sparkled with awareness, did he consider recognition. But even then, he refused to nurture. “Prove yourself true before I cherish you,” he declared. And how difficult he made that proof! Thorns, stones, obstacles—every step was paved with hardship.

He observed trials without emotion, demanding they be endured. He followed the marks left behind—sometimes stained with blood—watching closely, coldly, like a stern guard over a suffering pilgrim. When at last he allowed rest, he opened the eyelids with ruthless fingers, peering into the brain, into the heart, searching for vanity, pride, falsehood—any flaw hidden in the deepest corners of being.

If, finally, he permitted sleep, it was only for a moment. Then he would wake the student suddenly, assign new tasks, send them on exhausting errands when they were weary, test their temper, their mind, their strength. Only when every trial had been faced, when the most corrosive acid had failed to tarnish the metal, did he acknowledge its worth—and then, in silence, stamped it with his approval.

I am not blind to these flaws. Until the end of the last chapter, M. Paul had not been my teacher. He had not given me lessons. But shortly after, overhearing me admit ignorance in arithmetic—one subject where even a charity-school boy would blush—he took me under his wing. He examined me, found me woefully lacking, gave me books, set me tasks.

At first, he did so with pleasure—even triumph. He condescended to say I was “bonne et pas trop faible”—well-intentioned, not entirely without ability—but, he added, “as yet in a state of wretchedly imperfect mental development.” This was true: the beginning of every effort has always brought me a peculiar weakness. Even forming simple acquaintances, I could never prove myself quick-witted. Each new chapter in life began with a heavy, discouraging passage.

While this lasted, M. Paul was kind, patient, gentle. He saw my pain, felt my shame. His eyes grew moist when mine clouded with tears. Burdened as he was, he stole moments of rest to help me. But when the darkness began to lift, when my faculties stirred, when I began to work beyond what he assigned—when I doubled, trebled, quadrupled his tasks to please him—his kindness turned harsh. The light in his eyes dimmed, changed from warmth to fire. He grew restless, resistant, controlling. The more I did, the less he seemed satisfied.

Sarcastic remarks—so sharp they stunned me—began to fill the air. Then came veiled insults about “the pride of intellect.” I was warned, vaguely, of some doom if I ever stepped beyond the bounds meant for my sex, if I dared crave knowledge deemed unfeminine.

Alas, I had no such desire. What I loved, I sought to fulfill. But the grand hunger for science—the divine thirst for discovery—I knew only in fleeting flashes. Yet when M. Paul mocked me, I wanted those feelings more deeply. His injustice sparked ambition—it fueled my spirit, gave wings to my dreams.

At first, his sneer hurt. But soon it burned in my veins, setting my pulse racing. Whatever gifts I had—feminine or otherwise—they were God’s giving. I resolved to be ashamed of none.

The conflict was fierce. It seemed I had lost his affection. He treated me strangely. In his worst moments, he accused me of feigning incompetence when I claimed to be “faible.” Other times, he turned on me suddenly, accusing me of plagiarism—of extracting wisdom from books I had never heard of, books I would surely fall asleep reading.

Once, when he leveled such an accusation, I rose against him. I gathered his books from my desk, filled my apron, and dumped them at his feet. “Take them away, M. Paul,” I said. “Teach me no more. I never asked to be made learned, and you have forced me to see that learning is not happiness.”

I returned to my desk, laid my head on my arms, and refused to speak to him for two days.

He wounded me. His affection had been sweet, precious—something new and rare. Now, it seemed gone. I cared nothing for his lessons.

Yet the books stayed. He restored them carefully. He came as usual to teach. He made peace—too quickly, perhaps. I should have held firm. But when he looked kind, offered his hand, I could not recall the harshness. Reconciliation is always sweet.

One morning, a message arrived from my godmother, inviting me to attend a lecture in the public rooms. Dr. John delivered it himself, speaking to Rosine, who, seeing M. Emanuel passing to the first class, boldly stepped before my desk, hands in pockets, and repeated the invitation aloud—saucily, defiantly—ending with: “Qu’il est vraiment beau, Mademoiselle, ce jeune docteur! Quels yeux—quel regard! Tenez! J’en ai le cœur tout ému!”

When she left, M. Paul demanded why I allowed “cette fille effrontée, cette créature sans pudeur” to address me so.

I had no answer. Rosine spoke as she pleased—her sense of reverence was underdeveloped. And what she said was true: Graham _was_ handsome. He had fine eyes, a compelling glance. I couldn’t help but agree. “Elle ne dit que la vérité,” I said.

“Ah! vous trouvez?” he asked.

“Mais, sans doute.”

Our lesson that day was grueling. We were relieved when it ended. The students rushed out, trembling, exultant. I too prepared to leave—until a command stopped me.

“I want fresh air,” I muttered. “The stove is too hot.”

He said nothing. Just silence. And there he sat—between my desk and the stove, a creature seemingly immune to heat—proceeding to confront me with… a Greek quotation.

M. Paul harbored a deep suspicion that I knew both Greek and Latin. Like monkeys said to possess speech but hide it for fear of misuse, I was believed to conceal a wealth of knowledge—secretly, criminally. It was whispered that I’d enjoyed a classical education, feasted on Hymettus honey, hoarded golden truths in memory, silently feeding my mind.

He tried every trick to expose my secret—placing Latin and Greek texts in my path, watching me like jailors watched Joan of Arc. He quoted passages, rolling out their beauty with musical precision—his voice rich, expressive, commanding. And all the while, he fixed me with a piercing, often malicious eye.

He expected grand reactions. None came. I didn’t understand. So I couldn’t be charmed—or annoyed.

Frustrated, angry, he clung to his belief. My responses were stone. My face, a mask.

He could not accept the truth—that I was simply ordinary. People must believe in illusions. If none are offered, they invent them.

At times, I wished his suspicions were true. There were moments when I would have given anything to possess the knowledge he attributed to me. I longed to shock him, to shatter his doubts with a sudden burst of brilliance. I yearned to stand before him, blazing with learning, and confound his assumptions—his “lunettes”—with a dazzling revelation.

Oh, why had no one taught me while I was young? Why had no one made me clever? That way, I could have crushed the mocking spirit in Paul Carl David Emanuel with one cruel, triumphant act.

But no such feat was possible. Today, as always, his quotations fell flat. He shifted ground.

“Women of intellect,” he said—now he was at home.

To him, such a woman was a freak of nature, a mistake, useless in creation—neither wife nor worker. Beauty fulfilled the first role. He believed, truly, that gentle, passive mediocrity was the only comfort for a man’s thoughts. As for work—only male minds could produce meaningful results—hein?

This “hein?” was meant to provoke contradiction. But I replied: “Cela ne me regarde pas: je ne m’en soucie pas.” Then: “May I go, Monsieur? They’ve rung for the second déjeuner.”

“What of that? You’re not hungry?”

“I was,” I said. “I’ve had nothing since breakfast at seven. I’ll have nothing till dinner at five if I miss this bell.”

“Well, I’m in the same plight. Share with me.”

And he broke his brioche in two, handing me half.

His bark was worse than his bite. But the real attack was yet to come.

As we ate, I couldn’t help expressing my wish that I really did know what he accused me of.

“Do you sincerely feel yourself an ignoramus?” he asked, softening.

Had I answered yes—meekly, fully—I believe he would have reached out, and we would have been friends. But I said: “Not exactly. I am ignorant of the knowledge you claim I have. But I _sometimes_, not _always_, feel a knowledge of my own.”

“What do you mean?” he asked sharply.

Unable to explain, I changed the subject.

He finished his half. Seeing I hadn’t eaten enough—and smelling apples from the refectory—I asked if he too noticed the fragrance.

He admitted he did.

“If you let me out by the garden door, I’ll fetch you a plateful,” I said. “They’re excellent—Goton stews them with spice, sugar, and vin blanc. May I go?”

“Petite gourmande!” he smiled. “I haven’t forgotten how much you liked the pâté à la crème I gave you. And you know very well, fetching apples for me is really getting them for yourself. Go—but return quickly.”

And so, on parole, I was released.

My plan: go swiftly, return with speed, place the plate at the door, and vanish—leaving consequences for later.

But his keen instinct anticipated me. He met me at the threshold, pulled me back in, and seated me immediately.

Taking the plate from my hands, he divided the fruit and ordered me to eat my share.

I complied—reluctantly. Irritated by my hesitation, he unleashed a masked, dangerous assault.

All he had said before, I could dismiss as noise—empty threat. Not this.

It was the same unreasonable demand he had made before: that on the next examination day, I—foreigner, untrained—take my place on the first form, improvise a French composition on any subject dictated by any spectator, without grammar or lexicon.

I knew the outcome. I, whose mind refused impromptu invention; who in public was a cipher; whose creativity only stirred in quiet hours, in solitude, in morning silence or evening peace—how could I be expected to summon inspiration on a platform, between Mathilde and Coralie, under Madame Beck’s gaze, for the amusement of a bourgeois from Labassecour?

We battled over this more than once—loud, fierce, tangled in argument and refusal.

On this day, I was reprimanded harshly.

“The obstinacy of your whole sex,” he said, “is concentrated in you. You have an ‘orgueil de diable.’”

I feared failure? What did it matter? Who was I that I shouldn’t fail? It would do me good.

He wanted to see me humiliated—I knew he did.

Then he paused.

“Would you speak now? Be tractable?”

“Never. Not in this matter. Law itself could not compel me. I’d pay a fine, endure imprisonment, rather than write for show, perched on a platform.”

“Could softer motives move you? Would you yield for friendship’s sake?”

“Not a whit. No form of friendship has the right to demand such a thing. True friendship would never harass me thus.”

Then, with a sneer—M. Paul could sneer with supreme artistry—curling his lip, flaring his nostrils, narrowing his eyes—he said: “Under certain persuasions, from certain quarters, je vous vois d’ici—eagerly accepting the sacrifice, passionately preparing for the effort.”

“Making a fool of myself, a warning, an example, before a hundred and fifty parents of Villette.”

I lost patience.

“I want to be free—to get out into the air! I’m almost feverish.”

“Chut!” he said. “This is just an excuse to run away. _He_ is not hot, with the stove behind him. How can you suffer, safely shielded by his person?”

“I don’t understand your constitution. I know nothing of salamanders. I’m a phlegmatic islander. Sitting in an oven doesn’t agree with me. May I at least go to the well? The apples made me thirsty?”

“If that’s all,” he said, “I’ll fetch the water.”

He went.

And with the door only latched behind me, I did not hesitate.

Before he returned, his half-worried prey had escaped.

Spring was advancing, and the air had turned unexpectedly warm, stirring a quiet unrest in my bones. This sudden shift in temperature brought with it a weakening of strength—something I felt deeply, as though my body were surrendering to the season’s softness. Even minor exertion left me drained; sleepless nights followed by sluggish days became my companions. On a Sunday afternoon, after walking half a league to the Protestant church, I returned weary and spent. Seeking refuge in my solitary retreat—the first classe—I sank into a chair, resting my arms and head upon the desk, which now served as both cradle and pillow.

For a while, I listened to the gentle hum of bees drifting through the berceau, their song like a lullaby carried on the breeze. Through the glass door, I watched Madame Beck and a lively gathering of friends strolling along the central path beneath orchard trees heavy with blossoms. The branches wore a bloom so pure and golden it reminded me of mountain snow kissed by sunrise. Among them moved a young girl—Mademoiselle Sauveur—who caught my attention more than any other. I had seen her before at Madame Beck’s, and heard whispers that she was M. Emanuel’s goddaughter, tied by old bonds of friendship between her family and the Professor. Though Paul was not among the guests today, I remembered seeing her near him once—her arm linked through his, leaning close with the ease of a child trusted by a kind guardian. When she did so, a strange sensation passed through me—an uneasy foreboding, something like a presentiment—but I refused to dwell on it.

My gaze lingered on Mademoiselle Sauveur, tracing the gleam of her bright silk dress as it danced through the tender green leaves. But soon my eyes grew heavy. The warmth, the drone of insects, the stillness—it all lulled me. And then, quietly, I slipped into sleep.

Two hours passed unnoticed. When I awoke, the sun had vanished behind the towering houses, casting long shadows across the garden. The room was dim, the bees had gone home, the flowers closed their petals, and the party had dispersed. Every path lay empty.

Yet I stirred without discomfort—no chill crept into my limbs, no numbness tingled in my cheek or arms. That surprised me. For where my head and arms had rested on the hard wooden desk, there now lay a thick shawl, neatly folded. Another one wrapped gently around me, shielding me from the evening air. Both had been taken from the corridor where such things hung. Who had done this? Which of the teachers—or pupils—had shown such care?

None but St. Pierre held ill will toward me. But who possessed the quiet grace, the thoughtful hand, to act so tenderly? Whose step was so light, whose touch so soft, that I wouldn’t have known or felt her presence? Ginevra Fanshawe, brilliant and bold, would never have hesitated to pull me from my seat if she’d noticed me sleeping. So I concluded: *It must have been Madame Beck.* She had entered unseen, found me slumbering, and thought to spare me from catching cold. She saw me as a useful instrument, well-tended and efficient—no reason to damage what served her purpose.

And now, I thought, I’ll take a walk. The evening air is fresh, not too cold.

So I opened the glass door and stepped into the berceau. I walked to my own alley—had it been darker, I might not have ventured there. Not yet had I forgotten the odd vision I’d once experienced there, months ago. But now, a faint ray of sunset still touched the grey crown of Jean Baptiste, and the birds had not yet retired to their nests among the ivy-draped walls.

I paced slowly, lost in thoughts much like those I’d harbored when I buried my glass jar—how I might rise in life, move closer to independence. This idea, though not often pursued lately, had never fully left me. Whenever a certain glance turned away, or a face darkened with unkindness, I returned to this dream. Little by little, I had shaped half a plan.

“Living costs little,” I told myself, “in this sensible town of Villette, where people care less for appearances and pride than they do in England. Here, no one blushes to be plain or thrifty. A modest house, wisely chosen, needn’t cost much. Once I’ve saved a thousand francs, I’ll rent a small tenement—a large room for teaching, two or three smaller ones for students. I’ll furnish it simply: benches, desks, a blackboard, an estrade with a chair and table, a sponge, chalk. Begin with day-pupils. Work upward. Madame Beck started no higher—she speaks of it often—and now she owns this garden, these buildings, a secure future, and a flourishing school that will provide for her children.”

“Courage, Lucy Snowe! With self-denial now, and steady effort later, you can claim your place. Don’t complain that this goal seems selfish, narrow, lacking grandeur. Be content to labor for independence until you’ve earned the right to look beyond. But after that—what then? Is there nothing more? No true home? Nothing dearer than yourself? Nothing to love so deeply that it draws out the best in you, makes you willing to lay down your own burdens and live for others? Must your life remain only a crescent—never full?”

I looked around. I saw countless others in similar circumstances. Men and women alike lived under conditions of denial, of sacrifice. Why should I expect to be among the few spared? Yet I believed in hope—even in the darkest lots. I believed this life was not all—neither beginning nor end. I believed, even as I trembled. I trusted, even as I wept.

So I settled that account. It is right, sometimes, to face our lives honestly, to balance the books without deceit. He who lies to himself about joy and calls sorrow happiness is a poor fraud. Write anguish as anguish, despair as despair—in bold letters, with a firm hand. You’ll pay your debt better that way. Try to pass off pain as privilege—see if the great creditor accepts the counterfeit. Offer water when blood is asked—will he take it? Not a whole pale sea for one red drop.

Then I paused before Methusaleh—the ancient tree, patriarch of the garden—and leaned my forehead against its gnarled bark. My foot touched the stone sealing the small tomb at its roots. I recalled the feelings buried there—the memory of Dr. John, the warmth of my affection for him, the faith I placed in his goodness, the delight I took in his grace. What had become of that strange, one-sided bond—half truth, half illusion? Was it dead? I did not know. But it was buried.

Sometimes, I dreamed the earth stirred beneath it—golden hair pressing through cracks in the coffin lid. Had I been too hasty? I used to ask myself, especially after brief encounters with Dr. John. He still smiled kindly, his hand warm, his voice gentle when he spoke my name. I never liked “Lucy” more than when he said it. But I came to understand that his kindness was not meant for me alone. It was part of his nature—like honey from a fruit, perfume from a flower. The nectarine does not love the bee it feeds. The sweetbriar does not pine for the air that carries its scent.

“Good-night, Dr. John,” I whispered. “You are good, you are beautiful—but you are not mine. Good-night, and God bless you.”

The words left my lips—and then I heard an echo, close at hand.

“Good-night, Mademoiselle—or rather, good-evening. The sun has barely set. I hope you slept well?”

I started, but only for a moment. I knew that voice.

“Slept, Monsieur? When? Where?” I asked.

“You may well ask. It seems you turn day into night, and choose a desk for a pillow—rather hard lodging.”

“It was softened for me, Monsieur,” I replied. “That unseen, gift-bringing thing which haunts my desk remembered me. No matter how I fell asleep—I awoke pillowed and covered.”

“Did the shawls keep you warm?”

“Very warm. Do you ask thanks for them?”

“No. You looked pale in your sleep. Are you homesick?”

“To be homesick, one must have a home. I have none.”

“Then you have greater need of a careful friend. I scarcely know anyone, Miss Lucy, who needs one more than you. Your very faults demand it—you need watching, regulating, keeping down.”

This notion of “keeping down” clung to M. Paul like a habit. Even total submission could not free him from it. But what did it matter? I listened, untroubled by over-submission. His work would be undone if I gave him nothing to manage.

“You need watching,” he continued, “and I am glad I see it. I watch you—and others—often, closely, more than you suspect. See that window with the light?” He pointed to a lattice in one of the college boarding-houses. “That is a room I’ve hired—on paper, a study. In truth, a post of observation. There I sit for hours. My book is this garden—its contents, human nature—especially female nature. I know you all by heart. Ah, I know you well—St. Pierre, the Parisienne, *cette maîtresse-femme*, my cousin Beck herself.”

“It is not right, Monsieur,” I said.

“Comment? Not right? By whose creed? Does Calvin or Luther condemn it? What concern is that to me? I am no Protestant. My father—rich though I’ve known poverty, once starving in a garret in Rome, living on a meal a day—was a devout Catholic. He gave me a priest and a Jesuit for tutors. I retain their lessons. And what discoveries, *grand Dieu!* have they led me to!”

“Discoveries made by stealth seem dishonourable,” I replied.

“Puritaine!” he laughed. “Yet see how my Jesuit training serves me. You know St. Pierre?”

“Partially.”

He laughed again. “You say ‘partially’—but *I* know her *thoroughly*. She played the amiable before me, offered me velvet paws, flattered, fawned. I am accessible to flattery—though against reason. She was young, or seemed so. Like all her countrywomen, she dressed well, carried herself with cool ease, spared me embarrassment—”

“Monsieur, that must have been unnecessary. I never saw you embarrassed.”

“Mademoiselle, you know little of me. I can be as embarrassed as a petite pensionnaire. There is modesty in me—diffidence—”

“Monsieur, I never saw it.”

“Mademoiselle, it is there. You ought to have seen it.”

“I have observed you in public—on platforms, before crowned heads—and you were as composed as ever.”

“Neither titles nor crowns stir my modesty. Publicity is my element. I breathe freely in it—yet here, at this moment, sentiment has come alive. However, I disdain to be overcome by it. If I were a marrying man—which I am not—and needed to ask a woman if she could see me as a husband, then I would prove myself modest, as I claim.”

I believed him. And in believing, I honored him with a sincerity that ached in my heart.

“As for St. Pierre,” he went on, regaining composure, “she once intended to be Madame Emanuel. I don’t know where I might have been led, but for that little lattice with the light. Ah, magic lattice! What miracles of discovery have you wrought! Yes—I have seen her rancor, her vanities, her levities—not only here, but elsewhere. I have witnessed what shields me from her arts. I am safe from poor Zélie.”

“And my pupils,” he said after a pause, “those fair young girls—so mild, so meek—I have seen the most reserved romp like boys, the demurest snatch grapes from the wall, shake pears from the trees. When you arrived, I watched you early—marked your preference for this alley, your taste for solitude. I observed you long before we spoke. Do you remember when I silently approached you, offering a knot of white violets—when we were strangers?”

“I remember. I dried them, kept them. I still have them.”

“It pleased me that you accepted them calmly, without prudery—that feeling I dread to awaken, and detest when revealed in eye or gesture. To return: not only did *I* watch you—but another guardian angel hovered near. Night after night, my cousin Beck descended those steps, gliding unseen, following your movements when you didn’t know.”

“But, Monsieur,” I said, “you couldn’t see from that distance what happened here at night?”

“By moonlight, possibly—with a glass. But the garden itself is open to me. At the bottom, in the shed, there’s a door leading to a court connected to the college. I possess the key. I come and go as I please. This afternoon, I entered through it and found you asleep. Tonight, I’ve used the same entrance again.”

I could not help saying, “If you were a wicked, designing man, how terrible this would be!”

He paid no heed. He lit his cigar, leaned against a tree, and watched me with that cool, amused look he wore when at ease. I decided it was time to speak my mind—since he often lectured me for hours, why not let me lecture him once?

“The knowledge you gain is bought too dearly, Monsieur. This stealth degrades your dignity.”

“My dignity!” he laughed. “When have I ever cared for it? It is *you*, Miss Lucy, who are ‘digne.’ How often have I taken pleasure in trampling on what you call my dignity—tearing it apart, scattering it to the wind, in those wild moments you witness with such hauteur, which you think resemble a third-rate London actor’s ravings.”

“Monsieur,” I said, “every glance from that lattice is a wrong done to the best part of your nature. To study hearts thus is to feast secretly, sacrilegiously, on Eve’s apples. I wish you were a Protestant.”

He ignored the wish, smoked on.

After a silence filled with quiet thought, he said suddenly, “I have seen other things.”

“What other things?”

He removed the cigar from his lips and tossed the stub into the shrubs. For a moment, it glowed in the darkness.

“Look at it,” he said. “Doesn’t that spark look like an eye watching us?”

He walked down the path, then returned.

“I have seen, Miss Lucy, things I cannot explain. They have kept me awake all night, searching for answers. I have not found them yet.”

His tone was strange. My blood ran cold. He saw me shiver.

“Afraid? Of my words—or that red jealous eye just fading?”

“I am cold. The night grows late and dark. The air has changed. It is time to go in.”

“It is past eight. But you shall go in soon. Answer me only this question.” He paused.

The garden was growing darker. Clouds gathered. Rain began to patter through the leaves.

I hoped he would notice—but he seemed absorbed.

“Mademoiselle,” he said, “do Protestants believe in the supernatural?”

“There is variation among Protestants, as among all sects,” I answered. “Why do you ask?”

“Why do you shrink? Speak so faintly? Are you superstitious?”

“I am constitutionally nervous. I dislike discussing such matters. Especially because—”

“You believe?”

“No. But I have experienced impressions—”

“Since you came here?”

“Yes. Not many months ago.”

“Here—in this house?”

“Yes.”

“Bon! I am glad. I knew it—before you told me. I felt a connection between us. You are patient; I am choleric. You are quiet and pale; I am tanned and fiery. You are a strict Protestant; I am a sort of lay Jesuit. But we are alike. There is affinity. Do you see it when you look in the mirror? Your forehead—your eyes—your voice—do they not resemble mine? I perceive it. I believe you were born under my star. Yes—under my star! Tremble, for when mortals share such a fate, their destinies intertwine. Knots form. Threads break. Damage remains.”

“These ‘impressions,’ you say, with English caution. I, too, have had them.”

“Tell me,” I urged.

“I desire no better. You know the legend of this house?”

“I do. Hundreds of years ago, a nun was buried alive beneath this very tree—where we now stand.”

“And that her ghost walks here.”

“Monsieur—what if it still walks?”

“Something walks. A shape visits this house by night—unlike any seen by day. I have seen it—more than once. Its robes—conventual—strange to behold. A nun!”

“I, too,” I whispered, “have seen her.”

“I expected that. Whether flesh or spirit, her business is with you—as much as with me. Well—I mean to uncover the truth. It has baffled me so far. But I intend to follow it to the end. I mean—”

He stopped. Suddenly, we both lifted our heads—simultaneously—looking toward the high tree shading the great berceau, its boughs brushing the roof of the first classe.

A sound—strange, inexplicable—came from that direction. As if the tree itself had moved, its weight of foliage crashing against the trunk. Yet no wind stirred. The feathery shrubs stood still. But the great tree convulsed. For minutes, a rending, heaving motion swept through the wood and leafage.

In the darkness, I thought I saw something solid emerge from the shadows—something heavier than mere shadow.

At last, the struggle ceased.

What had been born of these throes?

We watched, transfixed.

Suddenly, a bell rang in the house—the prayer-bell.

Instantly, from the berceau, swept into our alley—out of the gloom—an apparition, all black and white.

With a swift, angry rush—close, close past our faces—went the NUN herself!

Never had I seen her so clearly. Tall, fierce in posture. As she passed, the wind rose, sobbing. Rain poured down, cold and wild. The entire night seemed to tremble in her wake.

Where, then, was Paulina Mary? And how had my connection with the elegant Hôtel Crécy fared in her absence? For a time, it had been severed—M. and Miss de Bassompierre had journeyed abroad, dividing their weeks between the cities and countryside of France. Fate brought news of their return just days after they arrived. I was strolling one soft afternoon along a quiet boulevard, moving slowly under the gentle April sun, lost in thoughts that were not unpleasing, when I saw ahead a group of riders paused as if they had met by chance, exchanging greetings on the broad, smooth path lined with linden trees. On one side stood a middle-aged gentleman and a young woman; on the other—a young man of striking appearance. The lady’s bearing was graceful, her attire refined, her whole presence delicate and dignified. Yet, as I drew closer, I recognized them all: Count Home de Bassompierre, his daughter Paulina, and Dr. Graham Bretton.

How animated Graham looked! His expression held warmth, sincerity, and a quiet joy—yet there was also a restraint, a depth that spoke of deeper feeling beneath. This was the moment, this alignment of circumstance, that could captivate and hold a man like Dr. John. He admired Paulina not merely for her beauty or charm, but for the fullness of her setting—the grace of her upbringing, the polish of her world, the weight of her family's name. Had he seen her alone, unattended, dressed plainly, perhaps working in some modest role, he might have found her pleasing, even lovely—but not so moved. But here she was, framed by refinement, protected by rank, adorned by fashion and fortune. These were the conditions he required before surrendering his heart. And now, proud yet trembling, he bowed to her as though to a queen.

As for Paulina, her eyes held a soft, almost unconscious smile—not one of triumph, but of tender feeling. They parted. He rode past me swiftly, barely touching the ground, oblivious to everything around him. He looked radiant—his spirit stirred, his purpose awakened.

“Papa, there is Lucy!” rang out a cheerful voice. “Lucy, dear Lucy—do come here!”

I hurried toward her. She lifted her veil and leaned from her saddle to kiss me.

“I was planning to visit you tomorrow,” she said. “But now, tomorrow you’ll come to see me.” She named the hour, and I promised.

The next evening, I found myself in her private room—just the two of us. It had been some time since I’d last seen her, since the occasion when her worth had been weighed against Ginevra Fanshawe’s and clearly proven superior. Now she had much to tell me about her travels.

She was quick and lively in conversation, vivid in her descriptions—yet her speech remained natural, unhurried, her voice clear and gentle. She never seemed to rush or overstep. I listened intently, though eventually, she seemed to grow restless, needing a shift in subject. Her tale ended abruptly.

Then, in a voice both hesitant and pleading, she turned to me.

“Lucy—”

“Yes, I am here.”

“Is your cousin Ginevra still at Madame Beck’s?”

“She is still there. You must be eager to see her.”

“No—not really.”

“You want to invite her for another evening?”

“No… I suppose she still talks about marriage?”

“Not to anyone you care about.”

“But surely she still thinks of Dr. Bretton? She hasn’t changed her mind, has she? It was so settled two months ago.”

“Well, you know—it doesn’t matter. You saw how things stood between them.”

“There was a little misunderstanding that night, certainly. Does she seem unhappy?”

“Not at all. Let’s change the subject. Have you heard anything from Graham during your absence?”

“Papa received letters from him—about business, I believe. He took charge of an affair while we were away. Dr. Bretton seems to respect papa and enjoys helping him.”

“Yes. You met him yesterday on the boulevard—you could judge from his look whether his friends need worry about his health?”

“Papa thought the same. I couldn’t help smiling. He’s not especially observant—he often sees beyond what’s before him. But as Dr. Bretton rode off, he murmured, ‘Really, it does a man good to see the spirit and energy of that boy.’ He called him a boy—I think he nearly believes it, just as he thinks of me as a child. He wasn’t speaking to me, just muttering to himself.”

“Lucy…” Her voice dropped again, soft and searching. At once, she rose from her chair and sat beside me, on the stool at my feet.

I liked her. Not a sentiment I’ve often voiced about others in this story—but I will say it now. Close acquaintance revealed only gentleness, intelligence, and truth in Paulina. My regard for her ran deep. Perhaps admiration more superficial would have shown itself more boldly—but mine was quiet, steady.

“What do you wish to ask me?” I said. “Be brave—speak plainly.”

But courage did not light her eyes. When they met mine, they fell. There was no flush on her cheeks—no fleeting blush, but a deeper warmth rising within, coloring her skin, warming her breath.

“Lucy,” she whispered, “I truly want to know what you think of Dr. Bretton. Will you give me your honest opinion—of his character, his nature?”

“His character stands high—and rightly so.”

“And his disposition? Tell me about that,” she pressed. “You know him well.”

“I know him fairly well.”

“You’ve seen him at home—with his mother. Speak of him as a son.”

“He is a kind and devoted son—his mother’s comfort, her hope, her pride.”

She clasped my hand gently, stroking it with each word.

“And in other ways—is he good?”

“Dr. Bretton is compassionate—kind-hearted toward all humanity. He would show mercy even to the lowest savage or the worst criminal.”

“I heard some of papa’s friends say the same. They said many poor patients at the hospitals, who fear cold-hearted surgeons, welcome him.”

“They are right. I’ve seen it myself. Once, he took me through a hospital—I saw how they greeted him. Your father’s friends speak truly.”

A soft gratitude lit her eyes as she raised them briefly. She had more to say—but hesitated, as if waiting for the right moment, the right darkness.

Twilight was falling. The fire in her parlor glowed with dusky warmth. I sensed she wished the room dimmer, the hour later.

“How peaceful and private we feel here,” I remarked, to ease her.

“We do. Yes—it’s a quiet evening. I won’t be summoned down for tea. Papa is dining out.”

Still holding my hand, she played with my fingers unconsciously—wearing her own rings upon them, winding strands of her hair around them, pressing my palm to her warm cheek. Then, clearing her voice—soft and liquid as a bird’s song—she began:

“You must think it strange that I talk so much about Dr. Bret remember, but—”

“Not at all strange. Quite natural. You care for him.”

“And if I did,” she said quickly, “is that reason enough to speak? Do you think me weak—like my cousin Ginevra?”

“If I thought you even slightly like Madame Ginevra, I wouldn’t sit here waiting for your words. I’d rise, walk about, and deliver a lecture instead. Go on.”

“I intend to go on,” she retorted. “What else do you suppose I mean to do?” And she looked at me—little Polly of Bretton—petulant, sensitive.

“If,” she said firmly, “if I loved Dr. John so deeply I could die for it, that alone wouldn’t justify me speaking—wouldn’t let me utter a word. I’d remain silent—as silent as the grave—as silent as you, Lucy Snowe. You know it. And you know you’d despise me if I failed to control myself, if I whined about a feeling that was all on my side.”

“It is true,” I replied, “that I hold little regard for women who boast of their feelings—or mourn them loudly. But as for you, Paulina—speak. I want to hear every word you wish to say. Say all that brings you relief or joy. That is all I ask.”

“Do you care for me, Lucy?”

“Yes, I do, Paulina.”

“And I love you. Even as a small, troublesome girl, I felt content being near you. It was delightful to pour out my mischief and whims upon you. Now you are precious to me—I like to talk with you, trust you. So listen.”

She settled back, resting gently against my arm—light, unburdening, unlike the heavy, selfish weight of Mistress Fanshawe.

“A few minutes ago, you asked if we’d heard from Graham during our trip. I said there were two letters for papa—business matters. That was true. But I didn’t tell you everything.”

“You evaded.”

“I shuffled, I lied a little. But now I’ll tell the truth. It’s growing dark—perfect for talking. Papa often lets me open the mail and sort it. One morning, about three weeks ago, I found, among a dozen letters for M. de Bassompierre, a note addressed to *Miss* de Bassompierre. I spotted it instantly—the handwriting was familiar, yet striking. I was about to say, ‘Papa, another letter from Dr. Bretton,’ but the ‘Miss’ froze me into silence.

I had never received a letter from a gentleman before. Should I have shown it to papa? Let him read it first? I couldn’t bear the thought. I know papa’s view of me—he forgets my age, treats me like a schoolgirl. He doesn’t realize others see me as grown, tall, mature. So, caught between shame and excitement, I gave papa his twelve letters—his herd—and kept my one, my single lamb.

It lay in my lap during breakfast, staring up at me with a meaning I couldn’t understand. I felt myself split—still a child to him, but no longer one to myself.

After breakfast, I carried it upstairs, locked the door, and studied its outside. It took minutes to overcome the address, to break the seal. One doesn’t storm such a fortress at once—one sits before it, as besiegers do.

Graham’s handwriting is like himself, Lucy—clear, firm, rounded. No messy wax splash—just a full, solid drop, a clean, pleasant impression. No harsh angles—only smooth, soothing script. Like his face—like the chisel of his features. Do you know his autograph?”

“I’ve seen it. Continue.”

“The seal was too beautiful to break. So I cut it carefully with scissors. On the verge of reading, I hesitated again. It was too soon to drink that draught—the sparkle in the cup was so bright. I wanted to watch it a moment longer.

Then I remembered—I hadn’t said my prayers that morning. I’d rushed to join papa for breakfast, fearing to keep him waiting. Some might say I should have served God first. But I don’t believe heaven would envy me my duty to papa. I believe I’m superstitious.

Now, a voice seemed to whisper: another feeling is at stake—pray before you read. Deny yourself a moment, remember your duty.

I’ve had such impulses all my life. I put the letter down and prayed—adding, at the end, a fervent plea that whatever happened, I might never cause papa sorrow, nor neglect him in caring for others.

The very thought pierced my heart—I wept.

Yet still, Lucy, I knew that one day papa would have to learn the truth—be guided, persuaded, taught to accept it.”

She paused. Then softly: “I read the letter. Life is said to be full of disappointment. I was not disappointed. Before I read, and as I read, my heart did not just beat—it trembled, fast, like an animal drinking at a well. And the well was full—gloriously clear. It rose up of its own accord. I saw the sun through its flow—no mote, no moss, no insect—only pure, golden water, thrice-refined.”

She continued: “Life is said to bring pain to some. I’ve read biographies where the traveler passed from suffering to suffering, where Hope flew ahead, never staying long enough to be grasped. I’ve read of those who sowed in tears, whose harvest perished by blight or swept away by wind—and died in winter, empty-handed.”

“Was it their fault?” I asked.

“Not always. Some were good, striving people. I am not striving, nor actively good. Yet God has nurtured me—sun, moisture, protection—by my dear father. And now—now—another comes. Graham loves me.”

We both fell silent for a moment.

“Does your father know?” I asked quietly.

“Graham spoke of papa with great respect—but said he dared not approach him yet. He must first prove himself. He added that he needed some understanding of my own feelings before taking any step elsewhere.”

“And you replied?”

“I answered briefly—but not coldly. I feared my reply might be too warm—Graham is so exacting in taste. I wrote it three times—trimming, softening the words each time. At last, having shaped it until it seemed like a piece of ice touched with just a hint of fruit or sugar, I sealed and sent it.”

“Excellent, Paulina. Your instinct is perfect. You understand Dr. Bretton.”

“But how shall I handle papa? That is still my pain.”

“Don’t manage. Wait. Only refrain from writing again until your father knows and gives his consent.”

“Will he ever give it?”

“Time will tell. Wait.”

“Dr. Bretton wrote again—deeply grateful for my calm reply. But I anticipated your advice. I told him that while my feelings remained unchanged, I could not write again without my father’s knowledge.”

“You acted as you should. He will respect you for it. It will deepen his pride in you, his love—if it can grow further. Paulina, that gentle frost surrounding such pure flame—that is a rare gift of nature.”

“I feel Graham’s disposition,” she said. “I sense that no delicacy is too fine for his treatment.”

“You understand him perfectly. And even if he were one who expected greater boldness—you would still act with truth, openness, tenderness, toward your father.”

“Lucy, I trust I always will. Oh, it will hurt to wake papa from his dream—to tell him I am no longer a child!”

“Do not hurry. Leave it to Time and your kind fate. I’ve noticed her care for you—doubt not she will order the moment wisely. Yes—I’ve reflected on your life, as you have. We cannot foresee the future—but the past has been favorable.

As a child, I feared for you. Nothing alive was ever more sensitive than your nature. Under harshness or neglect, neither your body nor soul would have matured as they have. Pain, fear, struggle—these would have marred your features, frayed your nerves, stolen your health, your cheer, your grace. But Providence watched over you—not only for your sake, but for Graham’s. His star, too, was fortunate. To unfold fully, he needed a companion like you. And here you are—ready. You must be joined. I knew it the first day I saw you together at La Terrasse.

In all that concerns you both, I see promise, harmony, plan. I do not think the sunny youth of either will lead to stormy old age. I believe it is meant that you live in peace, be happy—not as angels, but as few mortals are. Some lives are blessed thus—it is God’s will. A trace of Eden remains. Others take a different path. Their journey meets fitful winds, sudden storms, early winter nights. But none of this happens without divine sanction. And I know somewhere, in His vast works, lies the justice of that fate—and the mercy that proves it.”

On the first of May, we—twenty boarders and four teachers—were summoned to rise at five in the morning, dressed and ready by six, under the command of M. le Professeur Emanuel, who would lead us on an excursion into the countryside. It was the day he had promised to take us for breakfast beyond the city walls. Though I had not originally been included in the invitation—indeed, I had been left out deliberately—I now raised the matter gently, only to be met with a sharp tug on my ear. I did not dare press further. “Je vous conseille de vous faire prier,” M. Emanuel said, his tone imperious, as if warning me against any more questions. One such reprimand was enough. I decided to go.

The morning dawned clear and still, like summer’s breath. Birds sang in the garden, and a soft dew-mist hung over the grass, promising warmth. We all agreed it would be hot, and with joy we shed our heavy coats, donning lighter clothes suited to the season. The fashion of the day was simple yet refined: clean cotton prints, light straw hats, carefully trimmed by French hands—modest, unassuming, yet perfectly suited to each wearer. No one wore faded silks or second-hand finery. At six, the bell rang cheerfully, and we descended the stairs in a steady stream, through the carré, down the corridor, into the vestibule.

There stood our professor, transformed. Gone was his stern paletôt and severe bonnet-grec; in their place, a young-looking belted blouse and a cheerful straw hat. He greeted us all with warm good-morrow, and most of us returned his smile with gratitude. We were lined up and set off.

The streets were quiet, the boulevards peaceful as open fields. I believe we were all happy as we walked along. M. Emanuel had a gift for stirring joy when he chose, just as he could awaken fear when he wished. He did not lead or follow, but moved among us, speaking to each in turn—more freely to his favorites, but never entirely ignoring those he disliked.

I had my own reason for wishing to remain unnoticed. Paired with Ginevra Fanshawe, I carried her slight but ever-present arm across mine—a burden both sweet and taxing. She remained in excellent health, and I confess it was no small task to bear the weight of her beauty. More than once that day, I wished there had been less of it. Still, I used her presence as a shield, shifting my position whenever I heard M. Paul approaching, placing her between us like a barrier.

My motive? The pink print dress I wore. Under ordinary circumstances, it might have seemed harmless. But under M. Paul’s gaze, it felt like walking through a field with a red border while a bull grazed nearby.

For a time, my strategy worked—along with careful adjustments to my black silk scarf. But soon he noticed that no matter which side he approached from, Miss Fanshawe remained beside me. Their acquaintance had never been smooth. His temper flared at her English accent; she found him rough, intrusive, repulsive. They clashed in every way.

After about the sixth attempt to shift positions, he suddenly leaned forward, fixed his eyes on me, and demanded sharply, “Qu’est-ce que c’est? Vous me jouez des tours?”

Before I could answer, he understood. I tried to hide behind the fringe of my scarf, but it was too late.

“A-h-h! c’est la robe rose!” he exclaimed—his voice carrying the sudden fury of a startled beast.

“It’s only cotton,” I said quickly. “Cheaper, and washes better than any other colour.”

“Et Mademoiselle Lucy est coquette comme dix Parisiennes,” he replied. “A-t-on jamais vu une Anglaise pareille? Regardez plutôt son chapeau, et ses gants, et ses brodequins!”

These things were no different from what the others wore—perhaps even plainer. But once he had seized upon this point, I braced myself for a lecture. Yet it never came. Instead, he offered only a single flash of amusement—a fleeting, teasing smile—and then said, “Courage! À vrai dire, je ne suis pas fâché. Peut-être même suis-je content qu’on s’est fait si belle pour ma petite fête.”

“But my dress is not beautiful, Monsieur—only clean.”

“J’aime la propreté,” he said.

And so, the storm passed. The sun of good humour prevailed. Clouds scudded away before its glow.

We reached the country, where they called it “les bois et les petits sentiers.” A month later, these woods and paths would be dusty and bare. Now, in May’s greenness and morning hush, they looked delightful. We arrived at a well, encircled by lime trees arranged in neat order, like a scene from Labassecour’s gardens. Here we paused. We sat on the grassy slope around the well, M. Emanuel taking his place among us, allowing us to gather close. Some approached eagerly—mostly children. Others kept their distance. And some, whose affection had softened even their fear, stayed farthest of all.

He began to tell a story.

He had a rare gift for narration—simple yet powerful, direct yet rich, spoken in a voice that charmed children and captivated scholars alike. There were moments of beauty in that tale—glimpses of feeling, strokes of description that settled deep into my mind and never faded. He painted a twilight scene—so vivid, so real, I can still see it clearly, as though drawn by a master hand.

I had no talent for improvisation. That very lack made me marvel all the more at someone who possessed it fully. M. Emanuel was not the kind to write books—but I had heard him speak with such effortless abundance, such mental richness, that his words felt like gold dust scattered on the wind. His mind was my library. Whenever he opened it to me, I entered a realm of bliss.

Though I was intellectually limited, unable to endure long reading, his thoughts were medicine to my spirit. His ideas cleared my vision, strengthened my inner sight. I often thought how wonderful it would be for someone who loved him deeply to collect and preserve these treasures—these fleeting gems he tossed so carelessly into the air.

When the story ended, he walked toward the knoll where Ginevra and I sat apart. In his usual manner—he never waited for volunteers—he asked, “Were you interested?”

“Yes,” I answered, as always, quietly.

“Was it good?”

“Very good.”

“Yet I could not write that down,” he said.

“Why not, Monsieur?”

“I hate mechanical labour. I hate to sit still. But I could dictate it to an amanuensis—if I found the right one. Would Mademoiselle Lucy write for me, if I asked?”

“Monsieur would be too quick. You’d urge me, and grow angry if my pen couldn’t keep pace.”

“Try one day. Let us see the monster I become under such conditions. But now, no dictation. I have another use for you. See that farmhouse yonder?”

“Surrounded by trees? Yes.”

“There we’ll breakfast. While the farmer’s wife prepares café au lait in a caldron, you and five others will butter half a hundred rolls.”

We formed back into line and marched to the farm. On seeing us, the farmer surrendered without resistance. Knives, plates, and fresh butter were provided. Six of us—chosen by M. Emanuel—set to work under his direction, spreading butter on a large basket of rolls that the baker had delivered in anticipation.

Coffee and chocolate were already hot. Cream and fresh eggs were added. M. Emanuel, generous as ever, wanted to order ham and jam, but some of us—perhaps presuming on our influence—objected, calling it reckless waste.

He railed at us, calling us “des ménagères avares.” But we let him speak, managing the meal our own way.

He stood by the hearth, watching with a pleased expression. He was happiest when others were joyful. He thrived on movement, laughter, abundance. When we asked where he would sit, he smiled and said, “You know well I am your slave, and you my tyrants. I dare not choose a chair without your permission.” So we placed him in the farmer’s great chair at the head of the table.

It was easy to love him, despite his storms. At his worst, it was only his nerves that flared—not his heart. Soothe him, understand him, comfort him, and he became gentle as a lamb. Harmless to all but the foolish, the stubborn, or the indifferent.

Always mindful of his faith, he asked the youngest girl to say a prayer before we ate. He crossed himself with the same devotion as a woman. I had never seen him pray before. He did it simply, with childlike sincerity. Watching him, I smiled. He caught my gaze and reached out his hand. “Donnez-moi la main! I see we worship the same God, in the same spirit, though by different rites.”

Most of his fellow professors were freethinkers, skeptics, even atheists—men whose lives bore scrutiny poorly. M. Emanuel was different. He was more like a knight of old—religious in his way, upright, pure. Innocent children and lovely youth were safe near him. He had strong passions, deep feelings, but his honor and piety held them in check. He was a man of strength tempered by grace.

Breakfast was joyful—not empty chatter, but genuine merriment. M. Paul led it, shaped it, elevated it. His lively nature flourished, unburdened by men or constraints. Surrounded only by women and children, he was free to be himself—and it was a pleasant self.

After the meal, we were free to play in the meadows. A few stayed to help the farmer’s wife put away her dishes. M. Paul called me aside, asking me to come sit with him beneath a tree, where he could watch the others frolic across the pasture. He would read aloud while he smoked his cigar.

He sat on a rustic bench. I sat at the base of the tree. I read from a pocket edition of Corneille—something I didn’t particularly enjoy, but he liked. He listened with a calm sweetness that contrasted sharply with his usual impetuous nature. Deep peace filled his blue eyes, smoothed his broad brow.

I, too, was happy—happy in the bright day, happier in his presence, happiest in his kindness.

After a while, he asked if I wouldn’t rather join my companions.

“No,” I said. “I’m content where I am.”

Then he asked, “If I were your brother, would you always stay with me?”

“I believe I would,” I said—and I meant it.

Again, he asked, “If I were to leave Villette, travel far away, would you be sorry?”

I dropped Corneille. I could not answer.

“Petite sœur,” he said softly, “how long could you remember me if we were parted?”

“That, Monsieur, I cannot say. Because I do not know how long I shall remember anything earthly.”

“If I went beyond the seas for two, three, five years—would you welcome me back?”

“Monsieur, how could I live in the meantime?”

“Pourtant j’ai été pour vous bien dur, bien exigeant.”

I hid my face in the book. Tears covered the pages.

Why did he talk like this? I asked.

He said he would not talk so again, and tried to cheer me with gentle words. But the tenderness he showed me for the rest of the day touched something deep within me. It was too soft. Too sorrowful. I would have preferred his usual abruptness, his whimsy, his anger.

When noon arrived—the day glowing like June—our shepherd gathered his flock and began the journey home. We had walked nearly a league. The children were tired. Most spirits flagged at the thought of walking back over hard, dusty roads.

But this had been anticipated. Just beyond the farm, we met two large vehicles—特意 hired for school parties. With careful arrangement, we all fit inside. Within an hour, M. Paul brought us safely back to Rue Fossette.

It had been a pleasant day. Almost perfect. Only briefly marred by a shadow of melancholy.

That evening, as sunset painted the sky, I saw M. Emanuel emerge from the front door with Madame Beck. They walked slowly down the central path, talking earnestly. He looked grave, restless. She appeared amazed, pleading, dissuading. I wondered what they discussed. When Madame Beck returned to the house as darkness fell, leaving M. Paul lingering in the garden, I thought to myself: *He called me ‘petite sœur’ today. If he were truly my brother, how I would like to go to him now, ask what troubles him. Look at him—leaning against that tree, arms crossed, brow bent. He needs comfort. Madame does not console—she only warns. What now?*

Suddenly, he broke from stillness. He strode swiftly, purposefully, down the garden. The carré doors were still open. I assumed he was going to water the orange trees, as he sometimes did. But instead, he turned sharply and headed straight for the berceau and the first-classe glass door.

I was in the first classe—watching him from there. But I could not wait. He moved so fast, so strangely. Fear took hold. Without thinking, I fled—my pulse racing, my breath short—until I reached the oratory, now empty.

I stood there, listening, heart pounding, gripped by a nameless dread. I heard him pass through the classrooms, slamming doors impatiently. I heard him enter the refectory, where the “lecture pieuse” was in session. I heard him demand, “Où est Mademoiselle Lucie?”

Just as I gathered courage to go down—just as I prepared to do what I most desired—to meet him—the voice of St. Pierre answered smoothly, falsely: “Elle est au lit.”

And he passed on, stamping with frustration, into the corridor.

There, Madame Beck met him, stopped him, scolded him, escorted him to the street door—and dismissed him.

As the door closed behind him, a sudden shock struck me like a blow. From the beginning, I knew he sought me. I had wanted him too. So why had I run?

What had driven me away? What had pulled me beyond his reach?

He had something to tell me. He was coming to tell me. My ears strained to hear it—and I had made it impossible.

Longing to listen, to comfort—while believing it hopeless—yet when the moment came, I fled as if from death itself.

Well, my madness had its price. Instead of comfort, certainty, the satisfaction I might have won—if only I had faced my fear for two minutes—I received silence, doubt, and bitter suspense.

I carried my punishment to bed, and spent the night counting it.

On a Thursday afternoon, Madame Beck summoned me to her side, asking if I had any pressing duties that would prevent me from running errands in town for her. With nothing occupying my time, I offered myself willingly. She handed me a list of silks, wools, and embroidery threads needed for the students’ projects, and soon I was ready—dressed appropriately for the brooding sky above, heavy with clouds and heat—when she called me back into the dining room.

“Forgive me, Miss Lucy,” she said, as though struck by a sudden thought, “I’ve just remembered one more task—if you’re not too burdened already?”

Of course, I assured her I was far from overtaxed. She hurried into the small salon and returned with a lovely basket brimming with rare greenhouse fruits—rosy, perfect, and luscious, nestled among dark green waxen leaves and pale yellow star-shaped blossoms of some exotic plant.

“There,” she said, “it’s light enough not to mar your fine appearance, unlike a servant’s chore. Please deliver this basket to Madame Walravens, along with my congratulations on her celebration. She lives in the old town, Number 3, Rue des Mages. The walk may be long, but you have the entire afternoon. Don’t rush—should you miss dinner, I’ll have a portion saved, or Goton, who holds you in affection, will prepare something special for you. You won detail, my dear Miss Lucy. And oh—please!” (calling me back once more), “be sure to see Madame Walravens herself and hand her the basket directly. She is quite particular about such things. Goodbye! Until we meet again!”

At last, I stepped out. The shopping took longer than expected—choosing and matching threads and fabrics being an endlessly tedious task—but eventually, every item was secured. The patterns for slippers, bell-ropes, and baskets were selected; tassels and slides for purses chosen; all the little details settled. Only the fruit and message remained.

I welcomed the idea of a long walk through the ancient, shadowed Basse-Ville. And I liked it even more as the evening sky deepened into a mass of black-blue metal, glowing at the edges, slowly turning crimson like molten iron.

Storms frighten me—not because of the thunder or lightning, but because they demand effort, movement, strength. But rain? That I can endure. A steady downpour asks only surrender—letting clothes and body grow wet, yielding quietly to nature’s will. In return, it cleanses the city, clears the streets, stills the world like a spell. It turns Villette into a forgotten desert city, Tadmor. Let the rains fall, let the floods rise—just so long as I first deliver this basket of fruit.

A distant clock, its voice unknown now that Jean Baptiste’s tower was too far away, chimed the quarter past five when I reached the address Madame Beck had given. It wasn’t really a street at all, more like a quiet square. Grass grew between the wide grey stones, the houses stood large and ancient, their backs hidden by trees suggesting gardens behind. Time seemed to linger here—business had fled. Once, wealthy men lived here; once, grandeur dwelled within these walls. The church rising above, with its dark, crumbling towers, was once the opulent shrine of the Magi. But wealth had flown, leaving behind empty nests—perhaps to shelter poverty, perhaps to stand cold and abandoned through endless winters.

Crossing the silent square, where drops as large as five-franc coins fell slowly onto the pavement, I saw no sign of life—except for an elderly priest, bent and leaning on a staff, passing by. He looked like a figure carved from age itself.

He had come from the very house I was meant to visit. When I paused before the door he had just closed, ringing the bell, he turned and stared. His gaze lingered—perhaps he found me incongruous: young, well-dressed, bearing summer fruit in such a place. Had a bright-faced maid opened the door, I might have felt out of place too. But when an old woman appeared—wearing a faded peasant dress, a cap both ugly and costly, lace flaps hanging long, cloth petticoat and jacket, and wooden clogs shaped like tiny boats—I felt the scene made sense. It was fitting, even comforting.

Her face, however, was anything but soothing. I’d rarely seen such a sour expression. She barely answered my question about Madame Walravens, and I feared she’d snatch the basket from my hands—until the old priest limped forward, stopped her, and listened carefully to my message.

His hearing seemed poor, making it hard to make him understand I must speak to Madame Walravens personally and give her the basket myself. After some patience, he grasped the point and, speaking to the old woman in the native tongue of Labassecour, persuaded her to let me enter. He then escorted me upstairs and left me in a sort of drawing-room.

The room was large, with a magnificent old ceiling and stained-glass windows like those in a chapel. But it was empty, and under the gathering storm, it looked ominous. A smaller room opened off it, its single window shuttered tight. In the dimness, I could barely make out the furniture. My attention was drawn to a painting on the wall.

Then—strangely—the image began to shift. It trembled, sank, rolled inward until it vanished entirely, revealing an arched opening leading into a stone passage and a winding staircase below. Cold, bare stone—no carpet, no paint. From the stairs came a soft tapping, like a stick on stone. Then a shadow crept down, followed by a form.

Was it real? This figure emerging from the arch, dimming the light?

It drew near. I saw it clearly. I understood where I was. No wonder this square was called the Quarter of the Magi—no wonder the three towers above had been blessed by ancient sages of lost arts. Magic ruled here. A spell had opened a fairy realm—the shrinking portrait, the secret passage, the stone stair—all part of a tale. And standing at the center was Cunegonde, the sorceress. Malevola, the evil fairy. What did she look like?

She might have been three feet tall, yet shapeless. Her thin hands rested atop each other, gripping a golden knob on a slender ivory staff. Her face was enormous, set not on her shoulders but directly before her chest—no neck at all. I could swear a hundred years lived in her features, and more in her eyes—those cold, hostile eyes, framed by thick grey brows and livid lids. How sternly they regarded me, filled with dull disdain!

She wore a blue brocade gown, dyed the color of gentian flowers, patterned with satin leaves. Over it, a rich shawl, so large it swept the floor with its multicolored fringe. But her jewels stole the eye: long, clear earrings that sparkled with a light no imitation could mimic; rings on her bony fingers, thick gold bands set with purple, green, and blood-red stones. Hunchbacked, dwarf-like, and frail, she was dressed like a barbarian queen.

“Que me voulez-vous?” she rasped, her voice more masculine than feminine—indeed, a silver beard sprouted from her chin.

I delivered the basket and the message.

“Is that all?” she demanded.

“That is all,” I replied.

“Truly, it was worth the trouble,” she said. “Return to Madame Beck and tell her I can buy fruit when I choose. As for her congratulations—je m’en moque!” And with that, she turned her back.

Just as she did, thunder cracked, and lightning blazed across the room—illuminating salon and inner chamber alike. The magic story unfolded with the fury of the elements. The traveler, lured into the enchanted castle, heard the storm awaken outside.

What was I to think of Madame Beck? She kept strange company. She sent gifts to a mysterious shrine—and the recipient seemed neither kind nor noble. There went Sidonia, trembling like disease incarnate, tapping her staff on the mosaic floor, muttering curses as she vanished.

Rain poured down. The sky, red moments before, now glowed deathly pale beneath its blackness, as if afraid. Despite my earlier confidence in facing storms, I hesitated to step back into this downpour. Lightning flashed fiercely, thunder rumbled close—this tempest had gathered directly over Villette, bursting at the zenith. Forked bolts pierced vertical torrents; red zigzags crossed white streaks of rain. All erupted from a sky swollen and black.

Leaving Madame Walravens’ cold, unwelcoming salon, I descended the staircase. A seat waited on the landing—I sat there, waiting. Someone glided along the gallery above. It was the old priest.

“Mademoiselle should not sit here,” he said gently. “Our benefactor would be displeased if he knew a stranger was treated thus in this house.”

He urged me so earnestly to return to the salon that I could not refuse without rudeness. The smaller room proved better furnished, warmer, more inviting. He led me in. Partially lifting the blind, he revealed what looked more like a chapel than a private chamber—a solemn, reverent space, more suited to relics than comfort.

The priest sat beside me, as if keeping company. But instead of speaking, he opened a book, fixed his eyes upon the page, and whispered softly—what sounded like a prayer or litany. A yellow glow from the storm-lit sky gilded his bald head; his figure remained in deep purple shadow. Still as a statue, he seemed to forget me altogether—only raising his eyes when thunder cracked or lightning flared, not in fear, but in quiet awe. I, too, was awestruck—but free from terror, my thoughts flowed freely.

Truthfully, I began to feel a resemblance between this man and Père Silas, the confessor before whom I had knelt in the Béguinage church. The likeness was faint—I’d seen him only in dim light, in profile—but still, I sensed it. I thought I even recognized his voice.

As I watched, he caught my gaze, lifting his eyes briefly. I turned to study the room—its own half-mystic charm.

Beside a cross of aged ivory, carved with intricate detail and tilted over a dark-red prie-dieu adorned with a rich missal and ebony rosary—hung the picture whose outline had drawn me earlier. Now, clearer, it was no Madonna. It was a portrait of a woman in nun’s garb. Her face, though not beautiful, was gentle—pale, young, shadowed by sorrow or illness. Not intellectual, not striking—yet sweet in a way that spoke of weakness, passivity, resignation. Yet I could not tear my eyes from it.

The priest, who seemed deaf and frail at first, clearly retained his faculties. Without looking up, without turning his head, he noticed where my gaze fell. In slow, clear tones, he murmured four words:

“She was much beloved.

“She gave herself to God.

“She died young.

“She is still remembered, still wept.”

“By that old lady, Madame Walravens?” I asked, thinking I’d uncovered the source of her bitter temper—the grief of loss.

He shook his head, half-smiling.

“No, no,” he said. “A noblewoman’s love for her grandchildren may be deep, her sorrow keen—but only the betrothed lover, denied by Fate, Faith, and Death, mourns as Justine Marie is mourned.”

I sensed he wished to be questioned, so I asked who had loved her, and who still mourned her. He told me a small romantic tale, spoken with quiet emotion, against the fading storm. I admit it could have been more moving had it avoided French sentimentalism and over-dramatization. But the priest was plainly French-born, bred in Rome—a true son of the Church. When he did lift his eyes, they glanced at me with sharpness and depth—one would not expect after seventy years. Yet I believed him good.

The hero of his tale was a former pupil—now his benefactor—who had loved Justine Marie, daughter of wealthy parents, when his prospects allowed him hope. But his father, once a banker, failed, died, leaving debts and ruin. The son was forbidden to pursue Marie—especially by the hunchbacked, monstrous grandmother, Madame Walravens, whose temper, twisted by deformity, became demonic. The gentle Marie had neither the courage to betray her lover nor the strength to remain faithful. She abandoned him, refused another suitor with greater wealth, withdrew to a convent, and died in her noviciate.

Yet the heart that loved her never healed. The truth of his devotion showed in how he later cared for her family.

Years after Justine’s death, disaster struck her household. Her father, a jeweler by trade but active on the stock exchange, fell into financial ruin and disgrace. He died of grief and shame. Left penniless, his widow and mother faced starvation—until their once-rejected suitor, hearing of their plight, arrived with extraordinary devotion.
