He took on their prideful scorn with pure charity—housing them, caring for them, supporting them with tenderness no son could surpass. The mother, a decent woman, died blessing him. The grandmother, cruel and godless, lived on, sustained entirely by this self-sacrificing man. He treated her—with whom he’d suffered lifelong pain and solitude—as a mother. He brought her to this house, “and,” the priest added, tears rising in his eyes, “here, too, he shelters me, his old tutor, and Agnes, a servant from his father’s household. Three-quarters of his income goes to our upkeep and other charities—he keeps only one-fourth for himself, bread and modest lodging. By this choice, he has made marriage impossible. He has given himself to God and to his angel-bride, as fully as I, a priest.”

He wiped his tears, then raised his eyes to mine—for a fleeting moment. I caught the glance, and in it, a meaning struck me.

These Romanists are astonishing creatures. One you know no more than a distant emperor or lost king knows you—and yet, sees you wholly. He plans your meeting, your path, your words, as though fate were merely a cover for design. Madame Beck’s sudden errand, my innocent journey to the Place of the Magi, the priest’s chance descent, his intervention, my invitation into the room, the portrait, the story—each incident seemed separate, random. Like loose beads. But threaded by that quick, cunning glance, they formed a chain, like the rosary on the prie-dieu. Where was the link? I sensed connection, but couldn’t trace it.

Perhaps my silence seemed odd. He broke it gently: “Mademoiselle, I trust your journey home isn’t too long through these flooded streets?”

“More than half a league.”

“You live——?”

“In the Rue Fossette.”

“Not” (with sudden warmth), “not at Madame Beck’s pensionnat?”

“The same.”

“Donc” (clapping his hands), “donc, you must know my noble student, my Paul?”

“Monsieur Paul Emanuel, Professor of Literature?”

“He and none other.”

A pause. The thread of connection suddenly snapped into focus. I felt it yield.

“Were you speaking of M. Paul?” I asked. “Was he your pupil—and the benefactor of Madame Walravens?”

“Yes, and of Agnes, the old servant. And more—(with emphasis)—he was and _is_ the true, constant, eternal lover of that saint in heaven—Justine Marie.”

“And who, father, are _you?_” I asked, though I already expected the answer.

“I am Père Silas,” he said, “that unworthy son of Holy Church whom you once honored with a noble and touching confidence—revealing to me the core of your heart, the inner shrine of your mind, which I, in truth, desired to guide toward the only true faith. I have never ceased to watch you, never lost interest. Trained by Rome, shaped by her teachings, inoculated with her truths, inspired by her zeal—I see what you might become. And I envy Heresy her prize.”

This startled me. I felt it too—shaped, trained, molded. “Not so,” I thought—but I held my peace.

“I suppose M. Paul does not live here?” I asked, shifting to safer ground.

“No. He comes only occasionally—to worship his beloved saint, to confess to me, to pay respects to the woman he calls his mother. He lives in two small rooms, without a servant. Yet he refuses Madame Walravens selling those jewels you see her wear—objects she clings to with childish pride, as relics of her son’s wealth.”

“How often,” I murmured, “has this man, M. Emanuel, seemed petty in small things—yet how great in the larger ones!”

I didn’t count confession or saint-worship among his greatness.

“How long since that lady died?” I asked, looking at Justine Marie.

“Twenty years. She was older than M. Emanuel. He was very young then—he is not much past forty.”

“Does he still weep her?”

“His heart will weep her always. The essence of Emanuel’s nature is—constancy.”

He said it with firm emphasis.

Then the sun broke through, pale and watery. Rain still fell, but the storm had passed. The heated sky had split, pouring out its lightning. Delaying further would leave no daylight for my return. I rose, thanked the priest for his hospitality and his story. He responded kindly with “pax vobiscum”—a blessing I welcomed, for it seemed sincere. But less so the cryptic phrase that followed.

“Daughter,” he said, “you _shall_ be what you _shall_ be.”

An oracle that made me shrug as soon as I stepped outside. Few of us know our destiny. For all that had happened, I still hoped to live and die a sober Protestant. There was something hollow within “Holy Church,” and something showy around it—that tempted me little.

I walked on, lost in thought. Whatever Romanism may be, there are good Romanists. This man, Emanuel, seemed among the best—touched by superstition, influenced by priestly power, yet remarkable for his faith, devotion, sacrifice, and boundless charity. The question remained: how did Rome treat such qualities? Did she cherish them for God’s sake—or exploit them for her own gain?

“Forget the professors,” Madame Beck had said. A wise woman, no doubt—but this was a misstep. That night, she should have left me calm—unstirred, untouched by emotion, unbound to anyone or anything. Instead, she planted in my mind the image of a man I was meant to forget.

Forget him? Ah! They thought they were clever, these wise ones. They showed me how noble he was—how deeply, how purely he loved. They painted him as a hero, pure and steadfast. But what did I know before that day? Only that he could be jealous, quick to suspicion; that his tenderness came like a fleeting warmth, his mercy like morning dew that vanished under the sun’s heat. That was all I had seen—fragments, shadows. And then Père Silas and Modeste Maria Beck—working together, I felt certain—opened the hidden chamber of his heart. They revealed a love so strong, born of youth and southern fire, that it had defied death itself. It had scorned the grave, held fast to the soul, and watched beside a tomb for twenty years.

This was not idle sentiment. He had proven his devotion not with words, but with deeds—by giving up vengeance, taking up sacrifice, devoting his life to those once dear to another woman. His loyalty was written in silence and service.

As for Justine Marie—I knew her well enough without seeing her. She was the kind of girl who lived in Madame Beck’s school: quiet, pale, slow-moving, harmless—neither remarkable nor wicked. If she wore wings, I knew whose imagination had given them to her. If light shone on her brow, I knew whose eyes had conjured that halo.

So why should I fear her? Why let the ghost of a dead nun stand between us? And what of his charities, draining his wealth? What of his vow of celibacy?

Madame Beck, Père Silas—you should not have raised these questions. They were the deepest mystery, the greatest obstacle, and yet the most powerful spark I had ever known. For days, nights, hours—I dreamed of them, woke to them, lived with them. There was no answer in the world—except where one small, dark man stood, sat, walked, lectured, wrapped in a threadbare paletôt, ink-stained and worn, beneath a bandit’s cap.

After my visit to the Rue des Mages, I _wanted_ to see him again. Now that I knew, I felt as if his face might reveal something new—something deeper than before. I longed to read in his expression the mark of that ancient devotion, that half-knightly, half-saintly courage the priest had spoken of. He had become my Christian hero—and I wanted to see him as such.

And fate did not delay. The next day, my new thoughts were tested. I was granted an audience with my “hero”—an audience neither grand nor poetic, but sharp and vivid in its own way.

At three o’clock, peace reigned in the first classe. Madame Beck herself presided, calm and orderly, teaching with clarity and grace. I sat quietly at my desk, drawing—a careful copy of a detailed engraving. I took pride in such work, in making the lines match perfectly, though it was little more than needlework in ink. Yet I found joy in it, and could produce delicate imitations of steel engravings—useless, perhaps, but beautiful to me.

Then—chaos.

A wild burst of motion shattered the stillness. My drawing, pencils, paper—all crumpled into a heap, swept from sight. I seemed flung from my chair like a dried nutmeg shaken from a spice box. The chair and desk, seized by a rushing figure in a paletôt, were carried far across the room. In seconds, I was standing in the center of the grand salle—a vast space rarely used, reserved for dancing and singing.

I regained my wits slowly. Before me stood two men—gentlemen, I supposed—one dark, one fair, both with thick mustaches, whiskers, and imperial beards. One wore a braided surtout, stiff and military; the other looked more like a student or artist, careless and aloof. M. Emanuel stood apart, his face dark with anger, hand outstretched like a speaker addressing a crowd.

“Mademoiselle,” he said, “your duty is to prove to these gentlemen that I am not a liar. Answer their questions as best you can. Write on whatever theme they choose. To them, I am a fraud—an impostor who writes essays and signs them with my pupils’ names. You will disprove this.”

Heaven above! This was the trial I’d feared, now crashing down upon me like thunder. These two—Boissec and Rochemorte—were professors from the college, dandies and pedants, cynics and mockers. It seemed M. Paul had shown them something I’d written—something he’d never praised, never mentioned. An essay, unremarkable in truth, only notable compared to the usual work of foreign girls. In England, it would have passed unnoticed. But these men doubted its origin. Now, I was to defend it, endure their scrutiny.

The scene that followed was unforgettable.

They began with classics. I was silent. Then French history. Mérovée? Pharamond? I barely knew the names. They moved through various subjects—philosophy, science, literature. Each time, only a shake of my head: “Je n’en sais rien.”

After a pause heavy with mockery, they turned to general knowledge—topics I knew well, ones I’d pondered often. M. Emanuel, watching silently, stirred slightly. Perhaps, he thought, I would show some wit now.

He was wrong.

Answers surged in my mind, clear and fast—but words failed me. I couldn’t speak. Or perhaps I wouldn’t. My nerves were frayed, my spirit crossed. I heard one of them whisper to the other: “Est-elle donc idiote?”

“Yes,” I thought, “an idiot—forever one, for men like you.”

But I suffered. I saw the sweat gather on M. Paul’s brow, his eyes speaking sorrow and frustration. He refused to believe I lacked intelligence—he thought I *could* respond, if only I *would*.

At last, to spare him, I stammered:

“Gentlemen, please let me go. You’ll gain nothing from me. As you say—I am an idiot.”

I wished I could have spoken calmly, or kept silent. But my tongue betrayed me. Seeing the judges turn to M. Emanuel with triumphant looks, hearing my voice tremble, I broke into choking sobs. It was not grief—it was rage. Had I been a man, I would have challenged them there. But it *was* emotion, and I would rather have been beaten than let it show.

Fools! Could they not see the clumsy hand of a novice in that essay they called a forgery? The subject was classical. I’d never heard it before. I had no foundation. But I read, researched, built a skeleton from dry facts, clothed it with language, tried to give it breath. It was hard, anxious work—until I was satisfied with the structure. I cared deeply about truth. I avoided error, not because I knew much, but because I refused to lie. Knowledge wasn’t stored in my head—it had to be gathered, fresh each time, like herbs from the wild. Messieurs Boissec and Rochemorte missed this. They mistook my effort for scholarship.

Still, they would not let me leave. I must write before them.

With trembling hand, I dipped my pen. My eyes blurred, tears threatening. One of them began to apologize—softly, with false delicacy.

“We act for truth’s sake. We do not wish to wound you.”

Scorn gave me strength. I replied simply:

“Dictate, Monsieur.”

Rochemorte chose the theme: “Human Justice.”

Human Justice? A cold abstraction, offering no inspiration. And there stood M. Emanuel—sad as Saul, stern as Joab—while his accusers smiled in triumph.

I looked at them. I was gathering courage to tell them I would write nothing more—that their theme insulted me, their presence mocked me—that to question his honor was to attack truth itself. I meant to say it all—when suddenly, a memory struck.

Those faces—buried in hair, moustache, beard—were the same ones I’d seen, lit by gaslight behind a portico, on the night I arrived in Villette. The very men who had chased me, breathless, through the city streets, driven me beyond endurance.

“Pious mentors!” I thought. “Pure guides for youth! If ‘Human Justice’ were truly just, you two would not hold your posts—or enjoy your reputations.”

An idea seized me. I began to write.

Human Justice appeared before me—not as a noble ideal, but as a red-faced, rude hag, arms akimbo. I saw her in her house—a den of disorder. Servants called for orders she ignored. Beggars waited outside, starving. Children crawled around her feet, screaming for help. She cared for none. She sat warm by the fire, smoked her pipe, sipped her syrup, and enjoyed her solitude. When a cry pierced her ears too sharply—she grabbed the poker or hearth-brush. If the sufferer was weak, she silenced him. If strong, she threatened, then reached into her pouch and tossed sugar-plums.

That was my sketch—hurried, biting, placed before them. M. Emanuel read over my shoulder. Without a word, I curtsied and left.

Later that day, M. Paul and I met again. At first, the encounter was tense. The examination had left a sting. A short, sharp exchange ended with me called “a little mocker, heartless,” and with him walking away.

But I did not want him gone entirely. Only to feel that such passion could not be indulged without consequence. Soon after, I saw him gardening in the berceau. He approached the glass door. I drew near. We spoke of flowers.

By degrees, he laid down his spade. Conversation resumed, grew easier. Then he touched on a painful point.

Half-apologetic, half-regretful, he admitted the excesses of that day—the volatility of his moods. “Yet,” he said, “some allowance must be made. But I cannot expect it from you, Miss Lucy. You know nothing of me—my past, my position, my sacrifices, my sorrows.”

His history. I seized the word.

“No, Monsieur,” I replied. “You are right. I know nothing of your history, your duties, your trials, your affections. Nothing. You are a stranger to me.”

“Hein?” he murmured, raising his brows.

“You see me only in class—strict, demanding, impatient. In town, you are active, bold, quick to lead, slow to persuade. A man without ties, without family—no attachments, no duties. To us, you are a machine operator, moving us here and there without regard for our feelings. You seek recreation in public, under chandeliers. This school, that college—your workshops, where you manufacture pupils. I don’t even know where you live. It’s natural to assume you have no home.”

“I am judged,” he said. “Your opinion is exactly what I expected. To you, I am neither man nor Christian. You see me void of affection, faith, principle. Well, Mademoiselle—such is our reward in this life.”

“You are a philosopher, Monsieur—a cynical one,” I said, glancing at his paletôt, which he brushed with a sudden movement. “Contemptuous of human weakness, above comfort, independent of pleasure.”

“And you, Mademoiselle?” he countered. “You are neat, cozy, and terribly insensitive.”

“But, Monsieur,” I pressed, “you must live somewhere. Tell me—where? And what servants do you keep?”

With a fierce thrust of his lower lip, he snapped:

“Je vis dans un trou! I live in a hole—a cave you wouldn’t dare enter. Once, I spoke of my ‘study’ in the college—know now that this ‘study’ is my entire home. My bedroom, my sitting room. As for servants…” (mimicking my tone) “…they number ten.”

He spread his fingers before my eyes.

“I polish my boots,” he continued, “brush my paletôt.”

“No, Monsieur,” I interrupted. “It’s obvious—you never do.”

“Je fais mon lit et mon ménage. I eat at a restaurant. My supper takes care of itself. I pass days laborious and loveless, nights long and lonely. I am fierce, bearded, monkish. Nothing living loves me—except a few old hearts worn like mine, and some poor souls, broken in body and spirit, whom this world rejects, but who inherit the kingdom of heaven.”

“Ah, Monsieur—but I know!”

“What do you know? Much, I suspect. Yet not me, Lucy.”

“I know you have a pleasant old house in the Basse-Ville—why don’t you go live there?”

“Hein?” he muttered.

“I liked it,” I said. “The steps leading to the door, the grey stones, the trees behind—real trees, tall and ancient. The boudoir-oratoire—make that your study. It’s quiet, solemn.”

He studied me closely. A faint smile touched his lips.

“Where did you learn this? Who told you?”

“Nobody. Did I dream it, Monsieur?”

“Can I enter your dreams? Can I guess a woman’s thoughts—awake or asleep?”

“If I dreamed it, I saw people too—a priest, old and bent; a servant, old and picturesque; and a lady—splendid, strange. Her head barely reached my elbow. Her gown was bright as lapis-lazuli, her shawl worth a thousand francs. She wore jewels so brilliant I’ve never seen anything sparkle so. But her body seemed broken, bent double. She looked older than human years—worn by sorrow. Morose, almost cruel. Yet someone cared for her—someone forgave her sins, hoping to be forgiven. Three old souls—mistress, chaplain, servant—all sheltered under one roof.”

He covered his face with his hand, but his mouth twitched with something like amusement.

“You’ve entered my secrets,” he said. “How?”

So I told him—the storm, the commission, the waiting, Père Silas’s story.

“As I waited,” I said, “he told me a tale.”

“A story? Père Silas is no novelist.”

“Shall I tell you?”

“Yes. Begin at the beginning. Let me hear Miss Lucy’s French—her best or worst. Give me a good handful of errors and a dose of English accent.”

“It’s not a long tale,” I said. “‘The Priest’s Pupil.’”

“Bah!” he said, his cheek flushing. “The old father chose the worst subject—his weakest point. But what of the pupil?”

“Oh, many things.”

“What things? Be specific.”

“The pupil’s youth, his manhood—his greed, ingratitude, implacability, fickleness. Such a bad pupil—thankless, cold, unchivalrous, unforgiving.”

“Et puis?” he asked, lighting a cigar.

“Et puis—he suffered calamities we didn’t pity, bore them with a spirit we didn’t admire, endured wrongs without sympathy. Finally, he took the unchristian revenge of piling coals of fire on his enemy’s head.”

“You haven’t told me all.”

“Nearly. I’ve given you the chapters.”

“You forgot the part about his lack of affection—his cold, monkish heart.”

“Yes. Père Silas said his vocation was nearly that of a priest—his life considered consecrated.”

“By what bonds?”

“By the ties of the past and the charities of the present.”

“So you know the whole story?”

“I’ve told you everything I heard.”

Silence fell. Then he said, “Now, Mademoiselle Lucy, look at me. With the truth you always keep, answer me one question. Raise your eyes. Look into mine. No hesitation. Trust me—I am a man worthy of trust.”

I raised my eyes.

“Now that you know me—my past, my burdens—knowing my faults—can we still be friends?”

“If Monsieur wants a friend in me, I shall be glad to have one in him.”

“But a close friend—intimate, real—kindred in all but blood. Will you be the sister of a poor, burdened, imprisoned man?”

I could not speak. But I think I answered. He took my hand. And in that touch, I found comfort—solid, sure, like rock.

“When I speak of friendship,” he repeated, “I mean true friendship.” I could scarcely believe such earnest words had reached my ear. The look in his eyes—kind, anxious—felt real. If he truly desired my trust, and truly offered his—then life offered nothing greater. In that moment, I was rich, strong, happy. To confirm it, I asked:

“Are you serious? Do you really need me? Can you care for me as a sister?”

“Surely, surely,” he said. “A lonely man like me, without a sister, must be glad to find one in a woman’s heart.”

“And may I rely on your regard? May I speak to you when I wish?”

“My little sister must test me,” he said. “I make no promises. She must tease and try until she shapes her brother to her will. After all, he is not unyielding in the right hands.”

As he spoke, his voice softened, his eyes warmed. A joy I had never known filled me. I envied no lover, no bride, no wife. I had this—this voluntary, self-offered friend. If he proved true—and he looked true—what more could I desire? But if it all faded, as once before had done—?

Still, for now, I believed.

Beyond the quiet influence of Fifine Beck’s mother, another force held sway over M. Paul and me—something unseen yet powerful, watching closely from behind a veil of silence: Rome, through its sacred confessional, kept a vigilant eye upon her son. That chamber, where I had once knelt in prayer, now became a silent witness to M. Emanuel’s monthly visits—a place where secrets were confessed, hearts laid bare, and vows tempered by faith.

Why was I so glad to call him friend? Had he not long been one already? Had he not shown time and again his quiet affection, his gentle regard?

Yes—he had. Yet still, I cherished hearing it spoken aloud, earnestly, as if he truly believed me worthy of such trust. I liked the humility in his voice, the tenderness with which he spoke of me, the way he seemed to seek permission before daring to hope. He had called me “sister.” That name felt right. I would accept it gladly, so long as it meant nothing more than a bond of soul and spirit. And since he had sworn celibacy, there seemed no risk that this bond might be tested by marriage.

But that night, as I lay awake, I turned over every word we’d shared. I longed for morning to come, for the bell to ring, for the lesson to begin. Once dressed, I found even prayers and breakfast dragging, each minute stretching into an eternity until finally, at last, the hour arrived when I would see him again.

I wanted to understand our new friendship—not just as words, but as action. How would he behave? Would he treat me like a brother? And could I, in return, meet him as a sister—with courage, with warmth, with openness?

He came.

Yet life is built on irony: expectation never matches reality. All day, he said not a word to me. His lesson was quieter than usual—softer, heavier with thought. He was kind to the pupils, fatherly even, but toward me, cold. As he left the classroom, I waited for a smile, a glance, something—anything. I received only a quick, shy nod.

This distance, I told myself, must be temporary. A mistake. A misunderstanding. Patience. It would pass.

But it did not. Days passed. The gap widened. I swallowed my surprise, my hurt, my longing. I remained silent.

Had I not asked, when he offered fraternity, “Dare I rely on you?” And had he not, knowing himself well, withheld all promise? True, he had urged me to test him—to tease, to try. But how could I? That power belonged to others. Not to me. I lacked both instinct and strength. Left alone, I stayed still. Pushed back, I retreated. Forgotten, I said nothing. No hint escaped my lips, no look crossed my eyes. I began to wonder if I had miscalculated—perhaps somewhere along the way, I had misunderstood.

Then came the evening of our customary lesson. Once a week, he gave me private instruction—reviewing past work, preparing for what lay ahead. These sessions often took place wherever the school allowed—sometimes in the large second division, especially when the day pupils had gone and only a few boarders lingered near the surveillante’s platform.

That night, as the clock struck the familiar hour, I gathered my books, pen, ink, and made my way to the division.

The room was empty, bathed in cool shadow. But beyond the open doors, the courtyard glowed with light and life. The sun, low in the west, cast a golden blush across walls and dresses, fusing colors into one radiant warmth. Girls sat quietly, studying or writing; among them stood M. Emanuel, speaking cheerfully to a teacher. His dark coat, his black hair, caught the crimson glow. When he turned, his face lit up with a smile that answered the sun’s kiss.

I took my seat at a desk.

Nearby, orange trees and blooming plants basked in the fading light. They had soaked up the sun all day and now thirsted for water. M. Emanuel loved gardening. I had always thought tending plants soothed him—gave him peace. Now he moved among them, fetching water from the well with his own hands, watering the orange trees, the geraniums, the vibrant cacti. A cigar rested between his lips—his small luxury, his daily comfort. Blue smoke curled gently around the flowers, mingling with the evening air.

He spoke no more to the students or teachers. Instead, he cooed to a tiny spaniel, soft and silky, who adored him above all others. She trotted beside him, eyes full of devotion, tail wagging like a metronome. Whenever he dropped his hat or handkerchief in play, she crouched beside it, guarding it like a queen’s banner.

There were many plants, and his task took time. The great clock ticked on. Another hour passed. The sunset faded. Day slipped away. My lesson would be short tonight—but perhaps not too short.

Yet still, he lingered. There were roses to care for, favorite blooms to water. Little Sylvie followed him down the paths, barking with joy. I put aside some books—I wouldn’t need them all. I sat. I waited. And slowly, twilight crept in.

Sylvie reappeared, bounding forward, announcing his return. The watering pot was set aside. Relief flooded me. He washed his hands in a stone bowl. There was no time for lessons now—the prayer bell would soon sound. But we would meet. He would speak. I could read his heart, if only he would let me.

After washing, he paused, adjusting his cuffs. He looked up at the young moon, pale against the opal sky, glowing faintly on the windows of Jean Baptiste. Sylvie watched, restless. She whined, jumped—trying to break the stillness.

“Petite exigeante,” he said softly. “You cannot be forgotten, not for a moment.”

He lifted her into his arms, strolled across the courtyard, passing within yards of the window where I sat. He walked slowly, cradling her, whispering tender names. At the front steps, he turned once more. He gazed at the moon, at the cathedral, at the rooftops fading into mist. He breathed in the sweet dusk, admired the garden’s bloom. Then suddenly, he looked around.

A sharp glance cut across the façade of the classrooms, sweeping the row of windows. I think he bowed. If he did, I didn’t have time to respond. In a moment, he was gone. The moonlit threshold stood empty, silent.

I gathered my things, returned unused books to their shelf. The prayer bell rang. I obeyed.

The next day brought no relief. He was occupied with college duties. I taught, endured the hours, watched evening fall. Alone, I prepared for the weight of solitude. Was it better to stay with others, or sit by myself? I chose the latter. No one here could offer comfort. Only under the lid of my desk could I find refuge—nestled between pages, warmed by pencil points, touched by ink.

With heavy heart, I opened the desk. One by one, familiar books were pulled out and returned—useless. They offered no solace.

Then I saw it: a pamphlet in lilac cover. I hadn’t seen it before. I had rearranged my desk earlier that afternoon. This book must have been placed there recently—during dinner.

I opened it.

It was neither story nor poem, essay nor history. It didn’t sing or narrate. It preached. It persuaded.

And though small, it held a spell. I listened willingly. It spoke of Roman Catholicism, of conversion—not through fear, but through comfort. It offered tenderness, indulgence, peace. The Church, it claimed, did not threaten. She guided. She loved. _She_ persecute? Never.

This was not for the hardened, nor for the strong. It was milk for babes—a mother’s voice, soft and sweet. Its aim was not reason, but emotion. It sought to win hearts through love, not logic. Like St. Vincent de Paul gathering orphans, it spoke with gentle grace.

One argument stood out: the Catholic could pray for loved ones in purgatory—freeing them from suffering. The writer said nothing of those who believed in immediate salvation. But I thought of that doctrine—and found it more comforting.

Still, I smiled. The little book amused me. It was sentimental, shallow—yet something about it lifted my spirits. I laughed at the idea of a wolf-cub wrapped in sheep’s wool, bleating like a lamb. It reminded me of Methodist tracts I’d read as a child—just as eager to stir emotion.

The author was no villain. Though his methods revealed cunning, I hesitated to accuse him of deceit. But his reasoning was weak—unsteady, ungrounded.

I smiled again, then laughed—though not with the same ease. Something stirred inside me.

I glanced at the title page: “Père Silas.” On the flyleaf, in clear, familiar handwriting: “From P. C. D. E. to L—y.”

At that, I laughed again—but differently. A sudden clarity burst through the fog. The connection was undeniable: Père Silas and Paul Emanuel. The penitent had confessed everything. Every word of our talk had been revealed. He had spoken of us—of our covenant, of his adopted sister.

How could such a bond be permitted? Fraternal fellowship with a Protestant? I imagined Père Silas disapproving, warning him, commanding restraint—in the name of God, in memory of all M. Emanuel held dear—ordering him to withdraw, to guard his heart.

These thoughts may seem harsh. But compared to the fear of M. Paul changing within himself, they felt almost welcome.

Later, I couldn’t tell how much of this I had guessed, and how much came from elsewhere. But help was not far off.

That evening, no sunset painted the sky. Clouds covered east and west. No blue mist softened the view. A damp fog crept in from the marshes, gray and heavy. Rain had fallen all afternoon, and now fell steadily.

No one would wander in the wet alleys. So I was surprised when Sylvie barked—her joyful bark. She was never so excited unless he was near.

Through the glass door and archway, I saw the long path. Sylvie dashed through the gloom, white as a rose. She ran, whined, leapt—disturbing birds in the bushes. Five minutes passed. Nothing followed. I returned to my books.

Then her bark stopped. I looked up. She stood nearby, tail wagging furiously, watching a spade digging fast in the mud.

There he was—bent over the soil, working hard, rain soaking the shrubs around him. He dug as if earning his bread by sweat alone.

In that sight, I saw unrest. He dug when troubled—when grief or guilt pressed upon him. He worked for hours, head down, lips sealed.

Sylvie tired. Then she bounded about, sniffing everywhere—until she spotted me.

She flew to the window, barking wildly, as if urging me to join her master. She had seen me walk with him before. She likely thought it my duty to go now, however wet.

Her noise grew louder. Finally, M. Paul looked up. He saw her, saw me. He whistled. She barked louder.

Exasperated, he dropped the spade, stepped forward, and pushed the door open.

Sylvie burst in, leaping into my lap, paws at my neck, nose and tongue exploring my face, mouth, eyes. Her tail scattered papers and books across the desk.

M. Emanuel approached, collected the mess, and tucked Sylvie under his coat. She nestled quietly, head peeking out. Tiny, beautiful—her dark eyes, silken ears, innocent face. I thought of Paulina de Bassompierre. Forgive the thought—it came naturally.

He petted her. Such affection was no surprise. She begged for love with her very being.

As he caressed her, his gaze drifted to the papers—then settled on the religious tract.

His lips moved. He hesitated. Did he promise never to speak to me again? If so, he broke it now—because he spoke.

“You haven’t read it yet, I suppose? It doesn’t seem very inviting?”

I said I had.

He waited—hoping, perhaps, for a comment. But I was silent. If any concession was needed, it was not mine to make. I was not the docile pupil of Père Silas. I was not bound by his rules.

His eyes met mine—gentle, concerned, tinged with sorrow. He longed to see emotion in me. I could not give it.

Then I remembered the pens. I took them from my desk and began sharpening them—carelessly. I cut my finger—half on purpose.

I knew this would shift his mood. He disliked seeing me struggle. My knife was dull, my hand clumsy. He always chided me.

“Maladroit!” he exclaimed. “You’ll turn your hands into mincemeat!”

He set Sylvie down, placed her beside his hat, and took the pens from me. With swift precision, he sliced, shaped, pointed each quill—like a machine.

“Did you like the little book?” he asked.

I suppressed a yawn. “Hardly know.”

“Did it move you?”

“I thought it made me sleepy.”

(A pause.)

“Allons donc! You’re taking that tone with me? Bad as I am—and I’ll confess all my faults—God gave you too much sensitivity, too much sympathy not to be deeply moved by such a tender appeal.”

“Indeed,” I replied sharply, “I wasn’t affected at all.”

To prove it, I pulled out a perfectly dry, clean handkerchief—still folded.

He launched into a series of sharp remarks—biting, playful. I listened, delighted. After two days of silence, hearing him speak again, in his old way, was music.

We shared chocolate comfits from a bonbonnière he always kept stocked. He liked to see even small gifts appreciated.

He watched us, then put down his knife. Holding the newly cut pens, he touched my hand.

“Tell me, petite sœur—speak frankly. What have you thought of me these past two days?”

I ignored the question. It filled my eyes with tears. I stroked Sylvie instead.

M. Paul leaned forward. “I called myself your brother,” he said. “But I don’t know what I am—brother? Friend? I can’t say. I know I think of you—I care for you—but I must stop. You are dangerous. My best friends warn me. They whisper caution.”

“You do right to listen. Be cautious.”

“It’s your religion—your strange, self-reliant creed. It seems to clothe you in some invincible armor. You are good—Père Silas says you are good. But your proud, fierce Protestantism—that is the danger. It shows in your eyes, in your gestures. Just now—when you handled that tract—my God! I thought Lucifer smiled.”

“Certainly I don’t respect that tract. So what?”

“Not respect it? But it is pure faith, love, charity! I hoped it would touch you. I placed it there with prayer. I must be a sinner—Heaven won’t hear my most heartfelt pleas. You scorn my offering. Oh, cela me fait mal!”

“Monsieur, I don’t scorn it—as your gift. Sit down. Listen to me. I am not a heathen. Not hard-hearted. Not unchristian. Not dangerous. I would not trouble your faith. You believe in God, Christ, the Bible—and so do I.”

“But do you really believe in the Bible? Do you accept Revelation? What limits are there to your wild, reckless beliefs? Père Silas hinted…”

By persuasion, I drew out his meaning—crafty slanders, Jesuitical lies.

That night, we talked seriously. He pleaded, argued. I could not argue—I lacked the skill. But I could speak in my own way—the way he understood. I defended my faith not with logic, but with truth. I let him see the depth of my belief, the reverence in my heart.

He left unsatisfied, but not angry. He understood now that Protestants were not the irreverent pagans his director had described. He glimpsed how we honored God—not with ritual, but with awe, with quiet devotion.

And in that quiet, I felt something shift—not in him, but in me. The distance had not vanished. But the silence was broken. And that, for now, was enough.

It was understandable for Paulina to withhold further letters from Graham until her father gave his consent. But Dr. Bretton could not live within a mile of the Hôtel Crécy and remain untouched by the pull of her presence. At first, both were determined to keep their distance—no grand gestures, no open declarations. Yet in silence, their hearts drew closer than either had intended.

Whatever was noble in Graham stirred at the sight of Paulina. His mind, once stirred only by fleeting admiration for Miss Fanshawe, now awakened fully under her influence. His intellect—sharp, eager, hungry for meaning—found its truest nourishment in her company. He spoke casually of a book he’d been reading; she responded with a quiet resonance that struck a chord deep within him. The harmony between them surprised even himself. Each word she spoke seemed to unlock something hidden inside him—a new depth of thought, a warmth of feeling he hadn’t known before. Her voice, her manner, her very way of thinking—everything pleased him. And she, in turn, found delight in his wit, his charm, his effortless grace. Their minds met like polished stones, fitting together perfectly. Where he had always carried a natural joy, she, though usually quiet and reflective, now sparkled with a lightness as bright as dawn. In his presence, she bloomed—her beauty deepened, her spirit lifted. The reserve that once shielded her, that delicate armor of restraint, began to melt. Graham brought with him a warmth that thawed her carefully guarded heart.

They talked of the past—the old days at the Bretton home. At first, tentatively, almost shyly, then more freely, with growing honesty. Graham had built a life of his own, earned his independence without relying on the help Lucy had refused him. Now, he spoke of “little Polly” with a tenderness that made each memory feel fresh and alive. His words carried the weight of truth, spoken from the heart—not filtered through someone else’s lens. How much better this was than if I had tried to recall those moments for him.

More than once, when we were alone, Paulina would confide in me how astonishing it was to see how vividly he remembered things. As he looked at her, memories surged forward—images so clear they felt like dreams come true. He told her she used to gather his head in her arms, stroke his thick hair, and whisper, “Graham, I *do* like you!” She would bring a footstool beside him and climb up to sit on his knee. Even now, he said, he could feel the soft touch of her small hands smoothing his cheek, or burying themselves in his hair. He recalled the gentle press of her tiny finger, placed half trembling, half curious, into the cleft of his chin. She’d call it “a pretty dimple,” then look into his eyes and ask why they pierced so deeply, saying he had a “nice, strange face—far nicer, far stranger, than either his mamma or Lucy Snowe.”

“Child as I was,” she mused, “I wonder how I dared be so bold. Now he seems sacred to me—his hair is beyond reach, and Lucy, I feel a kind of awe when I look at his firm jaw, his sharp Greek features. Women are called beautiful—but he is not like a woman. So what is he? Do others see him as I do? Do *you* admire him?”

“I’ll tell you what I do, Paulina,” I replied, once, to her many questions. “_I never see him_. I glanced at him twice or three times last year, before he recognized me—and then I closed my eyes. If he passed before me twelve times between sunset and sunrise, I wouldn’t know what shape had gone by, except from memory.”

“Lucy, what do you mean?” she whispered.

“I mean I value my vision—and fear losing it completely.”

It was best to speak plainly, to shut down the tender confessions that came from her lips like honey, yet sometimes fell upon my ears like molten lead. From then on, she spoke no more of his beauty to me.

Yet she couldn’t stop speaking of him altogether. Sometimes softly, in quiet phrases; other times with a sweetness in her voice that made music itself seem tame. But often, that music grated on me—like a knife against glass. I gave her stern looks, harsh words. But she, wrapped in cloudless joy, mistook my pain for caprice.

“Spartan girl! Proud Lucy!” she would say, smiling. “Graham says you’re the most peculiar, wildest little woman he knows—but still, you’re excellent. We both think so.”

“You both think you know nothing,” I said. “Please leave me out of your thoughts and conversations. I have my own life—one apart from yours.”

“But ours, Lucy, is a beautiful life—or will be. You shall share it.”

“I shall share no one’s life in this world as you understand sharing. I may have one friend—but I’m not sure. Until I am, I live alone.”

“But solitude is sadness.”

“Yes—it is. But life has worse than that. Deeper than sorrow lies heartbreak.”

“Lucy, I wonder if anyone will ever truly understand you.”

There is a certain selfishness in love—an eagerness to have a witness to one’s happiness, no matter the cost. Paulina had forbidden letters, yet Graham wrote. She had vowed no correspondence, yet she answered—sometimes just to scold. She showed me the letters, demanding I read them, with the imperiousness of a spoiled heiress and the stubbornness of a child. As I read Graham’s words, I understood her pride. They were strong and tender, modest and brave. To him, her letters must have seemed radiant. Though she didn’t write to show off her talent—or express her love—she had set herself the task of hiding her feelings, tempering his passion. But how could such letters serve that purpose? He was already dearer than life to her. Everything he said, wrote, thought, or looked—carried an invisible power. Her letters glowed with unspoken confession, burning from greeting to farewell.

“I wish papa knew,” she murmured, again and again. “I *do* wish papa knew.” But she also feared. She couldn’t keep Graham from telling him. She longed to speak openly, to settle the matter—yet dreaded the moment. She knew he would be angry at first. He might even dislike her. It would be a shock, a surprise. She couldn’t predict how it would affect him.

And indeed, her father—long calm, long blind—was beginning to stir. A light, once ignored, now forced its way into his awareness.

To her, he said nothing. But when she wasn’t looking, I saw him watching her—quietly, deeply.

One evening, Paulina was in her dressing room, writing—likely to Graham. She had left me in the library, reading. M. de Bassompierre entered. I rose to go, but he asked me to stay—gently, but firmly. He sat near the window, some distance from me. He opened a desk and took out what looked like a journal. For several minutes, he studied one entry closely.

“Miss Snowe,” he said, closing the book, “do you know how old my daughter is?”

“About eighteen, sir?”

“It seems so. This old notebook tells me she was born May 5th, eighteen years ago. Strange—I’d lost count. I thought of her as twelve, or fourteen. A child.”

“She is about eighteen,” I repeated. “She is grown. She won’t grow taller.”

“My little jewel,” he said, in a tone that cut through me like one of her own voices.

He sat silent, lost in thought.

“Sir,” I said, knowing his grief, though it was never spoken, “don’t grieve.”

“She is the only pearl I have,” he said. “And now others will see her worth. They will want her.”

I said nothing. Graham Bretton had dined with us that day—radiant, confident, full of a quiet fire. Under the glow of hope, something in him had unfolded—something that demanded attention. I believe he meant to reveal the source of his ambition, the reason behind his efforts. M. de Bassompierre, slow to notice, was quick to reason. Once he grasped the thread, he followed it through a maze of meaning.

“Where is she?” he asked.

“Upstairs.”

“What is she doing?”

“Writing.”

“She writes? Does she receive letters?”

“Only those she can show me. And—sir—she… _they_ have long wanted to talk to you.”

“Pshaw! They don’t think of me—an old father! I’m in the way.”

“No, sir—that can’t be. But Paulina must speak for herself. And Dr. Bretton must speak for himself.”

“It is a little late. Things are moving.”

“Sir, nothing is done until you approve. Only—they love each other.”

“Only!” he echoed.

I, caught in fate’s design, had become confidante and mediator. I pressed on: “Dr. Bretton has been on the verge of speaking to you—many times. But despite his courage, he fears you terribly.”

“He may well fear me. He has touched the best thing I have. Had he left her alone, she would have stayed a child for years. So. Are they engaged?”

“They cannot be engaged without your permission.”

“It is good of you, Miss Snowe, to speak with such propriety. But this troubles me. My little girl was all I had. No more daughters. No son. Bretton might have looked elsewhere. There are many rich, pretty women who would not, I daresay, refuse him. He has looks, manners, connections. Why must it be my Polly?”

“If he had never seen your ‘Polly,’ others might have pleased him—your niece, Miss Fanshawe, for instance.”

“Ah! I would have given him Ginevra with all my heart. But Polly—I cannot let him have her. No. I cannot. He is not her equal,” he said gruffly. “In what way is he her match? They talk of fortune! I am not greedy—but the world thinks of these things. And Polly will be rich.”

“Yes, sir. All Villette knows her as an heiress.”

“Do they talk of my little girl in that way?”

“They do, sir.”

He fell into silence. I ventured: “Would you consider any man Paulina’s match? Would you prefer another to Dr. Bretton? Would higher rank or greater wealth change your feelings?”

“You touch me there,” he said.

“Look at the aristocracy of Villette—would you accept any of them?”

“I should not—never a duke, baron, or viscount among them.”

“I hear many of them think of her, sir. Other suitors will come if Dr. Bretton is refused. Wherever you go, aspirants will follow. Independent of her wealth, Paulina charms most who meet her.”

“Does she? How? My little girl is not considered beautiful.”

“Sir, Miss de Bassompierre is very beautiful.”

“Nonsense—though I beg your pardon, Miss Snowe. I think you’re too fond of her. I like Polly—I like everything about her—but I am her father. Even I never thought of beauty. She is amusing, fairy-like, interesting to me. You must be mistaken.”

“She attracts, sir. She would attract even without your wealth or position.”

“My wealth and position! Are these bait for Graham? If I thought so…”

“Dr. Bretton knows these things well, sir. He values them—as any gentleman would—as *you* would, under the same circumstances. But they are not his motives. He loves your daughter deeply. He sees her finest qualities—and they move him nobly.”

“What! Has my little pet ‘fine qualities’?”

“Ah, sir! Did you see her that evening when so many distinguished men dined here?”

“I was struck—surprised—by her manner. Its maturity made me smile.”

“And did you see the French gentlemen gather around her in the drawing room?”

“I did. But I thought it mere amusement—like playing with a pretty child.”

“Sir, she carried herself with distinction. I heard the Frenchmen say she was ‘pétrie d’esprit et de graces.’ Dr. Bretton thought the same.”

“She is a good, dear child. And I believe she has character. When I was ill, Polly nursed me. They thought I would die. She grew stronger, kinder, as I weakened. And when I recovered, she was a sunbeam in my room—light, quiet, cheerful. And now—she is sought in marriage! I don’t want to lose her,” he groaned.

“You’ve known Dr. and Mrs. Bretton so long,” I suggested. “Giving her to him would feel less like separation than giving her to anyone else.”

He pondered, darkly.

“True. I’ve known Louisa Bretton for years. She and I are old friends. A sweet, kind girl—when young. You speak of beauty, Miss Snowe! _She_ was handsome—tall, straight, blooming—not like my elfin Polly. At eighteen, Louisa had the bearing of a princess. She is comely and good now. The lad resembles her. I’ve always thought so. I favored him. Now he repays me—with this theft! My treasure loved her old father dearly. It’s over now, I suppose—I am an encumbrance.”

The door opened. His “little treasure” entered. She wore evening dress—her face flushed, her eyes bright with the warmth of twilight. A hint of summer crimson lit her cheeks. Her curls tumbled down her neck like silk. Her white gown suited the June heat. Thinking me alone, she held a letter—folded, unsealed. I was to read it. When she saw her father, her step faltered. A flush spread across her face.

“Polly,” he said, softly, with a grave smile, “do you blush at seeing papa? That’s new.”

“I don’t blush—I never *do* blush,” she insisted, though another wave of color rose. “But I thought you were in the dining room. I wanted Lucy.”

“You thought I was with John Graham Bretton, I suppose? But he’s been called away. He’ll return soon. He can post your letter—save Matthieu a ‘course.’”

“I don’t post letters,” she said, slightly annoyed.

“What do you do with them? Come here and tell me.”

Her hand hesitated—whether to come or not—but she stepped forward.

“How long have you been writing letters, Polly? It seems like yesterday you were struggling with pot-hooks, writing with both hands.”

“Papa, these aren’t letters to send. Just notes—I give them to someone directly, just to satisfy.”

“The person? That means Miss Snowe, I suppose?”

“No, papa—not Lucy.”

“Who then? Mrs. Bretton?”

“No, papa—not Mrs. Bretton.”

“Who, then, my little daughter? Tell papa the truth.”

“Oh, papa!” she exclaimed, earnestly. “I will—I *will* tell you—everything. I’m glad to—glad, though I tremble.”

She *did* tremble—excitement, emotion, courage—all shook her.

“I hate hiding things from you, papa. I fear and love you above all but God. Read the letter. Look at the address.”

She laid it on his knee. He picked it up, read it—his hand trembling, his eyes glistening.

He folded it back and looked at her with a strange, tender, mournful wonder.

“Can *she* write so—the little girl who stood at my knee yesterday? Can she feel so?”

“Papa, is it wrong? Does it hurt you?”

“There’s nothing wrong, my innocent little Mary. But it pains me.”

“But papa, listen! You mustn’t be hurt by me. I’d give up everything—almost,” she corrected herself, “I’d rather die than make you unhappy. That would be too wicked!”

She shuddered.

“Does the letter please you? Must it be torn? For your sake, I’ll destroy it, if you command.”

“I command nothing.”

“Command something, papa. Speak your wish. Only—don’t hurt Graham. I can’t bear it. I love you, papa—but I love Graham too—because—because—it’s impossible not to.”

“This splendid Graham is a young rogue, Polly—that’s my current opinion. I don’t love him one bit. Ah! Years ago, I saw something in that boy’s eye I never quite understood—something his mother lacks—a depth that warned a man not to wade too deep. Now, suddenly, I find myself dragged over my head.”

“Papa, you haven’t fallen in—you’re safe on the bank. You can do as you please. You’re the master. You could lock me in a convent, break Graham’s heart tomorrow—if you chose to be cruel. Now, autocrat, now czar—will you do it?”

“Send him to Siberia, red whiskers and all. I don’t like him, Polly. I wonder you do.”

“Papa,” she said, “you’re being very naughty. I’ve never seen you so disagreeable, so unjust, so—almost vindictive.”

“Off with him!” Mr. Home snapped, clearly annoyed—bitter, even. “But if he goes, Polly will pack a bundle and follow. Her heart is won—won, and turned from her old father.”

“Papa, you’re wrong. It’s naughty, it’s wrong, to speak like that. I am *not* turned from you. No human being, no force on earth, can turn me.”

“Be married, Polly! Marry the red whiskers. Stop being a daughter—be a wife!”

“Red whiskers! What do you mean? You should guard against prejudice. You’ve told me before that all Scots, your countrymen, are victims of bias. Now it’s proved—when no distinction is made between red and deep nut-brown.”

“Leave the prejudiced old Scot. Go.”

She stood, staring at him. She wanted to show strength, superiority. She had expected this scene—knew his ways. But dignity failed her. Suddenly, her soul broke. She threw herself into his arms.

“I won’t leave you, papa. I’ll never leave you. I won’t hurt you—I’ll never hurt you!” she cried.

“My lamb! My treasure!” he murmured, rough but loving. He said nothing more—those two words were choked.

The room darkened. I heard a movement outside. Thinking it a servant with candles, I opened the door gently. No servant stood there. A tall man was removing his hat, slowly taking off his gloves—waiting, lingering.

He said nothing, but his eyes said: “Lucy, come here.” And I did.

A smile played over his face as he looked down at me. Only his own nature could express such agitation with a smile.

“M. de Bassompierre is there—is he not?” he asked, pointing to the library.

“Yes.”

“He noticed me at dinner? He understood me?”

“Yes, Graham.”

“So I am brought up for judgment—and she too?”

“Mr. Home is talking to his daughter.”

“Ha! These are sharp moments, Lucy!”

He was agitated—his hand trembled. A vital suspense gripped him—held his breath, quickened his pulse. Yet his smile never faded.

“Is he *very* angry, Lucy?”

“_She_ is very faithful, Graham.”

“What will be done to me?”

“Graham, your star must be fortunate.”

“Must it? Kind prophet! So cheered, I should be a coward indeed to quail. I think I find all women faithful, Lucy. I ought to love them—and I do. My mother is good; _she_ is divine; and _you_—you are true as steel. Are you not?”

“Yes, Graham.”

“Then give me thy hand, my little god-sister. It is a friendly little hand—and always has been. Now for the great venture. God be with the right. Lucy, say Amen!”

He turned. I waited—then said “Amen!” to please him. The old charm returned. I wished him success—and I knew he would win. He was born a victor, as some are born defeated.

“Follow me!” he said. And I followed him into Mr. Home’s presence.

“Sir,” he asked, “what is my sentence?”

The father looked at him. The daughter kept her face hidden.

“Well, Bretton,” said Mr. Home, “you have given me the usual reward of hospitality. I entertained you—you have taken my best. I was always glad to see you—you were glad to see the one precious thing I had. You spoke fair—and yet, I will not say you *robbed* me—but I am bereaved. What I have lost, _you_, it seems, have won.”

“Sir, I cannot repent.”

“Repent! Not you! You triumph, no doubt. John Graham, you descend partly from a Highlander and a chief—and there is a trace of the Celt in all you look, speak, and think. You have his cunning, his charm. The red—(Well then, Polly, the *fair*) hair, the tongue of guile, the brain of wile—all come by inheritance.”

It is not so for all. Yet, what then? His will shall be done—done it surely will, whether we bow to it or not. The force of creation moves forward; unseen and seen powers fulfill their purpose. Proof of life beyond must be given—and if need be, written in fire and blood. We see this record etched across nature: in fire and blood it crosses our own lives. Sufferer, do not faint before this burning truth. Tired traveler, strengthen your limbs, lift your eyes, press on. Pilgrims and fellow mourners, walk together. For most of us, the path through this world is dark and long—let our steps be steady, equal, unshaken. Let our cross be our banner. Our staff is His promise: “His word is tried, His way perfect.” Our hope now is His providence: “He gives the shield of salvation, whose gentleness makes great.” Our final home is His bosom, who “dwells in the height of Heaven.” Our crown is a glory that surpasses, that endures. Let us run so that we may win. Let us endure hardship as good soldiers. Let us finish our course, keep the faith, and trust that in the end we shall come out more than conquerors. “Art thou not from everlasting mine Holy One? WE SHALL NOT DIE!”

On a Thursday morning, we gathered in class, waiting for the literature lesson. The hour had arrived—we expected the master.

The students of the first class sat still, quiet as stone. Their neatly penned compositions, tied with ribbon, lay ready before them, awaiting the professor’s swift glance as he passed each desk. It was July, the morning bright and warm. The glass door stood slightly open, letting in a fresh breeze that stirred the plants at the lintel—leaves swaying, bending, seeming to whisper secrets.

M. Emanuel was not always punctual. We did not think much of his delay. But when the door finally opened, it was not he who entered—not with his usual fire and haste—but Madame Beck, calm and cautious, stepping into the room like a shadow.

She approached M. Paul’s desk, stood before it, drew her shawl tighter around her shoulders. With a fixed gaze and a low, firm voice, she began: “This morning there will be no literature lesson.”

After a pause of about two minutes, she continued:
“It is likely the lessons will be suspended for a week. I shall need at least that time to find a suitable replacement for M. Emanuel. In the meantime, let us fill the gaps wisely.”

She paused again, then spoke further:
“Your professor, ladies, intends, if possible, to take leave of you properly. At present, he lacks the time for such a farewell. He is preparing for a long journey. A sudden, urgent call of duty has summoned him far away. He has decided to leave Europe for an indefinite period. Perhaps he will speak to you himself. In place of the usual lesson, you will read English today under Mademoiselle Lucy.”

She bowed slightly, adjusted her shawl, and left the room.

A silence fell—then a murmur spread through the hall. I believe some girls wept.

Time passed. Whispers grew louder. Sobbing came here and there. Discipline slipped—like a thread unraveling. It felt as though vigilance had vanished, surveillance lifted. Habit and duty helped me regain control. I rose, spoke in my usual tone, ordered quiet, and enforced it. I made the English reading long and intense, keeping them occupied all morning.

I confess—I felt irritation toward those who wept. Their grief seemed hollow, only hysteric emotion. I told them so plainly. I almost mocked them. I was stern. Truthfully, I could not bear their tears, that gasping breathlessness. I could not stand it. One weak-hearted girl kept sobbing after the others had ceased. Duty compelled me to confront her—so sharply, so firmly—that she dared not continue, forced to suppress the storm within.

That girl might have hated me—except that, once school ended and her companions departed, I called her to stay. When they were gone, I did something I had never done before: I pulled her close, pressed her to my chest, kissed her cheek. But the moment I yielded to that impulse, I quickly sent her away—for upon that tender act, she wept even harder.

I filled every minute of that day with work. I would have stayed awake all night if I could have lit a candle. But the night brought no peace—only restless shadows and a deep weariness, leaving me ill-prepared for the next day’s torment: relentless gossip.

Of course, the news became the talk of everyone. At first, there was some restraint—then it vanished. Every mouth opened, every tongue wagged. Teachers, students, servants—all spoke of “Emanuel.” He, who had been part of the school since its beginning, suddenly leaving? Everyone found it strange.

They talked endlessly, over and over, until from the chaos of words and rumors, a few truths emerged. By the third day, I heard he would sail in a week—then, that he was bound for the West Indies. I looked at Madame Beck—her face, her eyes—searching for confirmation or denial. I studied her closely, but nothing revealed itself except composure, indifference.

“This departure is a great loss to me,” she said publicly—once in class, again at dinner, speaking aloud to Zélie St. Pierre. “I do not know how I shall fill this gap. I am used to my cousin—he has been my right hand. What shall I do without him? I opposed his decision, but M. Paul convinced me it was his duty.”

She repeated this everywhere, calmly, clearly.

Why was it his duty? I wanted to ask. I had the urge to grab her by the arm as she walked past me in class, to hold her fast and say: “Stop. Let us hear the full truth. _Why_ must he go into exile?” But she never looked at me. Never seemed aware I cared.

The week passed. No one mentioned him coming to say goodbye. No one seemed to care. No one asked if he would come. No one showed distress at the thought of him vanishing without a word. They chattered constantly—yet never touched on the heart of it.

Madame Beck, of course, could see him. Speak freely with him. What did she care whether he appeared in the classroom?

The week ended. We learned he was departing on a certain day—his destination: Basseterre, Guadaloupe. The reason for his journey involved a friend’s affairs, not his own. I believed that.

“Basseterre, Guadaloupe.” I barely slept during this time. But whenever I did, I woke abruptly—startled—as if someone whispered those names over my pillow, or saw them flash across the darkness in jagged red or violet light.

There was no help for what I felt. How could there be? M. Emanuel had been kinder to me lately—growing ever more gentle, more considerate. A month had passed since we settled our theological dispute, and since then, no quarrel had arisen. Our peace was not cold or distant. We had grown closer. He came often, spoke more freely, spent hours with me—calm, content, almost familiar. We shared gentle topics. He asked about my plans. I told him. He liked the idea of opening a school—it made me repeat it several times, though he called it an Alnaschar dream. The rift was healed. Understanding deepened. Feelings of unity, of hope, took root in my heart. Affection, respect, trust—each wove a stronger bond.

What peaceful lessons we had! No more mockery of my “intellect,” no more threats of public humiliation. Instead, silent kindness, gentle guidance, patient forbearance—forgiving, yet never praising. There were moments when he would sit beside me, saying nothing at all. When dusk came or duty called, he would leave with words like these: “Il est doux, le repos! Il est précieux le calme bonheur!”

One evening, less than ten days ago, he joined me walking in my private alley. He took my hand. I looked up. I thought he meant to catch my attention.

“Bonne petite amie!” he murmured softly. “Douce consolatrice!”

But in his touch, in his words, something new stirred—a strange thought, a deeper feeling. Was he becoming more than brother or friend? Did his gaze carry affection beyond kinship?

His eyes spoke volumes. His hand pulled me forward. His lips trembled—almost as if to speak. No. Not now. Then—into the twilight came interruption: two figures, ominous and dual—a woman’s and a priest’s—Madame Beck and Père Silas.

I shall never forget the look on the latter’s face. At first, it showed a Jean-Jacques sensibility, disturbed by the signs of intimacy just caught. Then, instantly, it darkened with the jealousy of the church. He spoke to me with solemnity. He looked at his pupil with severity. As for Madame Beck—she saw nothing. Nothing. Though her kinsman held the hand of the foreign heretic, refusing to let go, pressing it tightly, even in her presence.

After that, when the announcement of his departure came, I found it hard to believe. Only constant repetition, the belief of the hundred and fifty minds around me, forced me to accept it fully. And that week of silence—of blank, burning days—without a word from him—its passage I remember, but cannot describe.

The last day dawned. He would come. He would speak his farewell—or vanish without a sound, never to be seen again.

This choice seemed clear in every mind in the school. All rose at the usual hour. All ate breakfast as usual. All went to their duties, indifferent, as if nothing had changed.

So blind, so numb, so routine-bound was the house—I scarcely knew how to breathe in such suffocating stillness. Would no one give me a voice? Was there no wish, no word, no prayer I could echo with an “Amen”?

I had seen them united in demanding the smallest things—treats, holidays, skipped lessons. Yet now, not one would join in demanding a final meeting with a teacher they had loved—at least some of them—loved as they could love. But oh—what is the love of the crowd?

I knew where he lived. I knew where he could be reached. The distance was mere steps. If he’d been in the next room—uninvited, I could not have used my knowledge. To follow, to seek, to remind, to recall—these were powers I did not possess.

M. Emanuel might have passed within reach of my hand. Had he walked silently by, unnoticed, I would have let him go—silent and still.

Morning passed. Afternoon came. I thought it was over. My heart trembled. My blood churned. I was sick, barely able to remain at my post. Yet the world around me moved on, unconcerned. All seemed cheerful, free of worry, fear, or thought. Even the girls who had wept hysterically seven days before seemed to have forgotten everything.

Just before five o’clock—the hour of dismissal—Madame Beck summoned me to her chamber. She had received an English letter and needed me to translate it and write a reply. Before beginning, I noticed she quietly closed both doors of her room. She even shut and locked the window, though it was hot and air circulation was usually essential to her. Why this caution? A sharp suspicion, almost fierce distrust, rose in me. Was she trying to block sound? What sound?

I listened—more intently than ever. Like a winter wolf sniffing snow, scenting prey, hearing the tramp of a traveler far off. Yet I could listen and write at the same time. Midway through the letter, I heard it—a step in the vestibule. No bell rang. Rosine, no doubt acting on orders, had anticipated the call. Madame saw me stop. She coughed, made noise, raised her voice. The step moved on—toward the classrooms.

“Continue,” she said. But my hand was frozen, my ear captive, my thoughts stolen.

The classrooms were in another building, separated by a hall. Still, I heard the sudden stir—a whole group rising at once.

“They are putting away their work,” Madame said.

It was indeed time—but why such sudden silence? That instant stillness?

“Wait, Madame—I will see what it is.”

I put down my pen and left her. Left her? No—she followed, close as my shadow. I turned on the last step of the stairs.

“Are you coming too?” I asked.

“Yes,” she replied, meeting my gaze with a peculiar look—clouded, yet resolute.

We walked—not side by side, but she stepped behind me, following my footsteps.

He had come. Entering the first class, I saw him. There, once again, stood the familiar figure. I doubt they had tried to keep him away—but he was here.

The girls formed a semicircle. He walked among them, giving farewells, shaking hands, kissing cheeks. This custom—foreign, yet accepted—was solemn, prolonged.

I felt bitter that Madame Beck should follow me so closely—watching, trailing. My neck and shoulder burned under her breath. I was driven to fury.

He was nearing the end. The circle nearly complete. He turned. But Madame stepped forward suddenly—towering, spreading her drapery, blocking me. She eclipsed me. I was hidden.

She knew my weakness. She understood the paralysis of spirit that seized me in crisis. She rushed to her cousin, overwhelmed him with words, captured his attention, hurried him toward the glass door leading to the garden. I think he glanced back. If only I had met his eye—courage might have surged. There might have been a charge, a rescue. But already the room was in chaos. The semicircle broke into groups. I was lost among thirty others. Madame had her way. Yes—she took him away. He did not see me. He thought I was absent.

Five o’clock struck. The dismissal bell rang. The school scattered. The room emptied.

To my memory, there follows a darkness, a confusion in certain minutes—grief beyond expression, loss unbearable. _What_ should I do? Oh, _what_ should I do—when all my life’s hope was torn from my heart, ripped by the roots?

I did not know what I should do—until a child, the youngest in the school, burst into my silent storm with innocence.

“Mademoiselle,” she lisped, “I am to give you this. M. Paul said I must search the whole house—from attic to cellar—and when I found you, give you this.”

She handed me a note. The little dove dropped its olive branch on my knee.

No address. No name. Only these words:

“It was not my intention to say good-bye to you when I bid the others farewell, but I hoped to see you in class. I was disappointed. The meeting is delayed. Be ready for me. Before I sail, I must see you alone, speak with you at length. Be ready—my time is short, and now, taken up. Besides, I have private business I will not share with anyone—even with you.—PAUL.”

“Be ready?” Then tonight. But wasn’t he sailing tomorrow? Yes—of that I was certain. I had seen the ship’s departure date posted. Oh—I would be ready. But could this meeting truly happen? Time was short. The schemers were vigilant, active, hostile. The path to him was narrow as a gully, deep as a chasm—Apollyon stood guard, breathing flames. Could my Greatheart prevail? Could my guide reach me?

Who could say? Yet I began to gather courage. It seemed I felt his heartbeat still true to mine.

I waited. Apollyon came, dragging Hell behind him. I think if Eternity held torment, it would not be fire or despair. I think that on a certain day—among those days that never dawned nor set—an angel entered Hades. He stood, shone, smiled, delivered a prophecy of conditional pardon, sparked a fragile hope of bliss to come—not now, but at a day unknown. He revealed His glory, His promise—spoke—then rose, became a star, and vanished into Heaven. His gift? Suspense—a worse blessing than despair.

All that evening I waited, trusting the dove’s message, yet trembling beneath it. Fear pressed heavy. Cold and unmistakable, I knew it for the partner of a rare, truthful预感. The first hours stretched long and slow. In spirit, I clung to the tail of the last. They passed like drifting clouds—like wreckage before a storm.

They passed. The long, hot summer day burned away like a Yule log. Its crimson glow faded. I was left bent in cool blue shadows, watching the pale, ash-like gleams of night.

Prayers were over. Bedtime came. My roommates were asleep. I remained in the dim first class, forgetting rules I had never broken before.

How long I walked I cannot say. Hours, surely. Mechanically, I pushed benches aside, cleared a path down the center. There I paced—until I was certain all were asleep, out of hearing. Then, relying on Night, trusting Solitude, I let my tears fall. No longer sealed, no longer chained. They heaved my heart. They tore their way. In this house, what grief could be sacred?

Shortly after eleven—a very late hour in the Rue Fossette—the door opened quietly, not furtively. A lamp flame cut through the moonlight. Madame Beck entered, composed as if arriving at any ordinary hour. Instead of speaking to me, she went to her desk, took her keys, and pretended to search for something. She lingered too long. Too calm. My mood could not endure the pretense. Two hours ago, I had cast aside all usual fears and respect. Led by a touch, ruled by a word—no yoke could now be borne.

“It is past time for rest,” she said. “The rules have already been broken too long.”

She received no answer. I did not stop walking. When she blocked my path, I moved her aside.

“Let me persuade you to calm, Meess,” she said, trying to soften her voice. “Let me lead you to your chamber.”

“No!” I said. “Neither you nor anyone else shall persuade or lead me.”

“Your bed shall be warmed. Goton is still awake. She will make you comfortable. She will give you a sedative.”

“Madame,” I burst out, “you are a sensualist. Beneath your serenity, your peace, your decorum—you are an undenied sensualist. Warm your own bed. Take your sedatives, your sweet drinks, your spiced meats. If you suffer—if you are disappointed—and perhaps you are—nay, I _know_ you are—seek your own remedies. Leave me. _Leave me_, I say!”

“I must send someone to watch you, Meess. I must send Goton.”

“I forbid it. Let me be alone. Keep your hand off me, off my life, off my pain. Oh, Madame! In your hand lies both chill and poison. You envenom and paralyze.”

“What have I done, Meess? You must not marry Paul. He cannot marry.”

“Dog in the manger!” I said. For I knew she secretly desired him—had always wanted him. She called him “insupportable.” She railed at him as a “dévot.” She did not love him—but she wanted to marry him, to bind him to her interests. Into some of Madame’s deepest secrets I had penetrated—I know not how—by intuition, by inspiration. Through living with her, I had slowly learned: unless with an inferior, she must always be a rival. She was my rival—heart and soul—though hidden beneath the smoothest mask, known only to her and me.

For two minutes I stood over her, feeling I held her entire being in my power. In moments like this, when perception sharpened, her habitual disguise—her mask, her domino—became to me a net of holes. I saw beneath: a heartless, self-indulgent, ignoble soul. She retreated quietly—meek, composed, though deeply uneasy. “If you will not be persuaded to rest,” she said, “I must reluctantly leave you.” And she did—perhaps more relieved to escape than I was to see her go.

This was the only moment of raw truth between us—the only confrontation that ever occurred. It was never repeated. It did not change her manner toward me. I do not know if she sought revenge. I do not know if she hated me more for my blunt honesty. I think she armored herself with the secret philosophy of her strong mind, resolved to forget what pained her to remember. I know that until the end of our lives, there was never another word, never another hint of that fiery exchange.

And so, the night passed.

I stood transfixed, as if under the gaze of a serpent with three heads, unable to move from the circle of figures gathered beneath the trees. The ground seemed rooted beneath my feet. Above, the canopy of intertwined branches cast a deep shadow; the night murmured secrets of safety, and a single lamp flared briefly—just enough to reveal a hidden seat in the darkness—then vanished as if it had never been. Now, let me tell you what I have silently learned over these past two weeks of silence, through whispers and glances, about the reason for M. Emanuel’s sudden departure. The story is short, and not new: its beginning is money, its end—ambition.

Madame Walravens was as grotesque as a Hindu idol, yet to those who worshipped her, she carried the weight of one. She had once been immensely wealthy—very wealthy—and though now she lived without means, there was talk that she might soon regain her fortune. In Basseterre, Guadeloupe, she owned a vast estate, inherited at marriage sixty years before, long held in limbo after her husband’s financial ruin—but now, it was said, free of claims, and with proper management, capable of yielding great returns within a few years.

Père Silas cared for this potential revival not only for the sake of wealth, but for religion—for Magliore Walravens had been a devout daughter of the Church. Madame Beck, distantly related to the hunchback and aware that she had no family of her own, had long planned for every contingency, calculating like a mother would for her child. Though harshly treated by Madame Walravens, she never stopped seeking favor—not out of affection, but for gain. Thus, both the priest and the headmistress were united in their interest in securing the West Indian estate—each driven by self-interest, each sincere in their pursuit.

But distance was vast, and the climate dangerous. A man of integrity, devoted and trustworthy, was needed. Such a man existed—one whom Madame Walravens had kept in her service for twenty years, crushing his spirit and living off him like a parasite. This same man had been shaped by Père Silas—trained, bound by gratitude, habit, and faith. And Madame Beck knew him well, could influence him subtly. “My pupil,” said the priest, “if he remains in Europe, risks apostasy—he has become entangled with a heretic.” Madame Beck, too, had her private motive: what she could not possess, she did not wish others to claim. Better to destroy it than see it fall into another’s hands. As for Madame Walravens, she wanted her land and her money, and she knew Paul could be the most loyal steward—if he chose to be. So the three, each selfish in their own way, joined forces to press upon the one unselfish soul. They reasoned, pleaded, implored. They laid their hopes at his feet, placing their futures in his hands. They asked only a few years of sacrifice—after which, he could live for himself. One among them, perhaps, hoped he would not live at all.

No one ever offered M. Emanuel such a burden without him accepting it. He never refused a trust, nor turned away a duty. What sorrow he felt, what fears he carried—what plans he made for his own future—none knew, none questioned. His conversations with his confessor, I could guess at; the role of duty and piety in persuading him, I could imagine. But he left, quietly, without a word. That was all I knew.

Bent forward, my forehead resting on my hands, I sat among tree trunks and tangled brushwood. The conversation around me drifted faintly—I could hear it if I wished, but for a while, there was little reason to listen. They spoke of dresses, music, lights, the lovely night. I strained to hear someone say, “It’s calm weather for _his_ journey—the _Antigua_ will sail safely.” But no such words came. The ship, her course, her passenger—none were named.

Perhaps the idle chatter meant nothing to old Madame Walravens any more than it did to me. She seemed restless, turning her head from side to side, scanning the crowd, the trees—as if expecting someone, growing impatient with delay. “Où sont-ils? Pourquoi ne viennent-ils?” I heard her mutter more than once. At last, as if determined to demand an answer—since none had offered—she spoke aloud: “Messieurs et mesdames,” she said, “où donc est Justine Marie?”

“Justine Marie!” What did this mean? Justine Marie—the dead nun—where was she? Why, in her grave, Madame Walravens! You may go to her, but she shall not come to you.

That was what I would have said, had I dared. But no one else seemed surprised. No one blinked. The quietest reply met the strangest question—the kind that stirs spirits from tombs. “Justine Marie,” said one, “is coming. She is in the kiosk. She will be here soon.”

From that moment, the conversation changed—but remained light, casual, familiar. Hints, jokes, remarks passed between them, so fragmented, so dependent on unnamed people and unspoken events, that even when I listened intently—now, with a sense of fate—I could only grasp that some plan was in motion, involving this spectral Justine Marie—dead or alive. This gathering seemed to reach for her, clutching at her for some purpose. There was talk of marriage, of fortune—though I could not tell for whom. Perhaps Victor Kint. Perhaps Josef Emanuel. Both were unmarried. Once, I thought the teasing was aimed at a fair-haired young foreigner named Heinrich Mühler. Amidst the laughter, Madame Walravens still broke in with hoarse, irritable remarks—her impatience eased only by watching Désirée closely, threatening her with her cane whenever she moved.

“La voilà!” suddenly called one of the men. “Voilà Justine Marie qui arrive!”

That moment was mine. I recalled the painted nun on the panel—the sad tale of love, the vision in the garret, the ghost in the alley, the mysterious birth in the cradle.
