I felt a premonition, a certainty of revelation. Ah! When imagination runs wild, where can we stop? What bare winter tree, what humble hedge animal, cannot be transformed by fancy—a passing cloud, a flicker of moonlight—into something unearthly?

The weight of mystery pressed upon my heart. Until now, I had seen this specter only through a glass darkly. Now, I would see it face to face. I leaned forward. I looked.

“She comes!” exclaimed Josef Emanuel.

The circle parted, as if welcoming a new guest. At that instant, a torch passed nearby—its flame mingling with the pale moonlight, illuminating the scene perfectly. Surely those near me must have felt something of the tension I felt—though none showed it. Even the calmest must have held his breath. As for me—my life stood still.

It is over. The moment has passed. The nun has arrived. The revelation is complete.

The torch still blazes within a yard, held high by a park keeper. Its flame almost touches the figure of the Expected—there, standing full before my eyes. Who is she? What does she wear? How does she look?

There are many masks tonight in the park. As the hour grows late, a strange mood of revelry and mystery spreads—so much so that I scarcely blame you, reader, if you think she looks like the nun from the attic, dressed in black skirt and white veil, rising from the grave, a true ghost returned.

But no—those are lies. Let us be honest. Let us cut straight from the fabric of truth.

Though “homely” is a poor word to use. What I see is not plain. A girl from Villette stands there—fresh from her boarding school. She is very beautiful, with the natural grace of this country. She is well-fed, fair, plump. Her cheeks are round, her eyes bright, her hair thick. She is elegantly dressed. She is not alone—her escort consists of three people—two older ones, whom she calls “Mon Oncle” and “Ma Tante.” She laughs, she talks—cheerful, healthy, blooming—every inch the bourgeois beauty.

“So much for Justine Marie,” then—so much for ghosts and mystery. Not that the mystery is solved. This girl is certainly not my nun. The figure I saw in the garret and garden was taller by a span.

We’ve seen the city belle. We’ve glanced at the respectable uncle and aunt. Can we spare a glance for the third member of this group? Should we notice him? We ought to, reader—he has claims on us. We do not meet him for the first time.

I clenched my hands tightly. I drew in my breath deeply. I stifled the cry, swallowed the gasp, suppressed the start. I did not speak. I did not move. But I knew. Through the dimness left by nights of weeping, I recognized him. They said he was sailing on the _Antigua_. Madame Beck said so. She lied—or she once told the truth and failed to correct it when it became false. The _Antigua_ had sailed. And there stood Paul Emanuel.

Was I glad? A great weight lifted from me. Was it cause for joy? I cannot say. Ask first—what were the circumstances? How did this delay affect me? Were there not others who might care more?

Still—who is this girl, this Justine Marie? Not a stranger, reader. I know her by sight. She visits the Rue Fossette. She attends Madame Beck’s Sunday gatherings. She is related to both the Becks and the Walravens. Her name honors the sainted nun who would have been her aunt had she lived. Her surname is Sauveur. She is an heiress, an orphan, and M. Emanuel is her guardian—some say her godfather.

The family council wants this heiress married to one of their own. Which one? That is the vital question.

I felt glad now that the draught given me had filled me with a strength that made sleep and rest unbearable. I have always loved to uncover truth—to seek the goddess in her temple, to lift the veil, to dare the terrible gaze. O Titaness among gods! Your form, hidden behind shadows, often sickens us with uncertainty. But show us one line, one feature—clear, stark, undeniable—and though we gasp in terror, we drink in your divinity. Our hearts tremble, our blood surges like rivers shaken by earthquake—but we have gained strength. To see and know the worst is to rob Fear of her greatest power.

The Walravens’ party, now larger, grew lively. The men brought refreshments from the kiosk. All sat on the grass beneath the trees. They toasted, laughed, joked. M. Emanuel was teased—half kindly, half cruelly, especially by Madame Beck. Soon I understood: his voyage had been delayed—not by force, but by choice, against the advice of his friends. He had let the _Antigua_ sail. He had booked passage on the _Paul et Virginie_, set to depart two weeks later. They mocked him for it, demanding why he had chosen this. He gave only a vague answer: “a small matter of business I am determined to settle.” What was this business? No one knew. But one person seemed partly privy—there was a glance exchanged between him and Justine Marie. “La petite va m’aider—n’est-ce pas?” he said.

The reply came swiftly—God knows how fast!

“Mais oui, je vous aiderai de tout mon cœur. Vous ferez de moi tout ce que vous voudrez, mon parrain.”

And the dear “parrain” took her hand and kissed it reverently. At this, the fair-skinned young German, Heinrich Mühler, stirred uneasily—as if displeased. He muttered a few words. M. Emanuel actually laughed in his face—laughed with the cold triumph of a victor—and pulled his ward closer.

M. Emanuel was joyful that night. He seemed untouched by the change ahead. He was the life of the party—perhaps a little domineering, eager to lead in joy as in work—but at every turn, he proved his right to command. His wit sparkled, his stories delighted, his laughter rang clear. Restless as always, he moved among them, attending to everyone. Yet I saw whose favorite he was. I saw at whose feet he lay on the grass, whom he wrapped warmly, whom he watched with tender care—like a jewel, like a treasure.

Still, jests flew. Still, I gathered that while Paul was away, working for others, these others would guard the treasure he left behind. Bring back an Indian fortune, and they would give him a young bride and a rich inheritance. As for the sacred vow, the promise of constancy—forgotten. The vibrant present outweighed the past. And finally, his nun was truly buried.

So it must be. The revelation had come. My预感 had not deceived me. There is a kind of premonition that never errs. It was I who misread the sign—thinking she spoke of visions when, in truth, she foretold reality.

I might have lingered longer, pondered deeper. Some might say the evidence was weak, the proof uncertain. Some cautious doubters might demand more before believing a match between a poor, forty-year-old man and an eighteen-year-old heiress. But not I. No hesitation, no excuse, no cowardice in facing the inevitable. No paltering with the truth. No fleeing from the Fact—the swift, relentless, conquering Fact.

No. I embraced the whole plan. I seized it with urgency, wrapped it around me like a soldier clutches his banner. I summoned Conviction to fix it upon me—this truth, abhorrent yet accepted—with the strongest nails, the deepest blows. When the iron had entered my soul, I rose—renovated.

In my fervor, I said, “Truth, you are a good mistress to faithful servants! While a lie pressed on me, I suffered. Even when falsehood was sweet, flattering, warm to the heart, it consumed me with torment. Believing love was won, I feared its loss. Truth stripped away illusion, flattery, expectation. Here I stand—free!”

Now, all I had to do was carry this freedom to my room, to my bed, and see what I could make of it. The play was not quite over. I might have stayed, watched longer—the love scene beneath the trees, the woodland courtship. Had there been no love in it, my imagination—so generous, so creative—could have shaped it into passion, colored it with depth and fire. But I would not look. I had made my decision. I would not betray my nature. And then—something tore at my shawl, dug into my side, like a vulture with strong beak and talon. I must be alone to wrestle with it. I think I never truly felt jealousy until now.

This was not like enduring Dr. John and Paulina’s affection—when I closed my eyes, shut my ears, withdrew my thoughts, I still acknowledged its charm. This was an outrage. Love born of beauty was not mine. I had no claim to it. But another love—tentative, born of long acquaintance, forged in pain, marked by constancy, strengthened by pure affection, tested by intellect, perfected by his own hand—this love, which laughed at passion’s frenzy and fleeting heat—this love, I had a stake in. Anything that touched it, either to build or destroy, I could not witness indifferently.

I turned from the trees and the merry company beneath them. Midnight had long passed. The concert was over. The crowds thinned. I followed the tide. Leaving the radiant park and brightly lit Haute-Ville—still aglow, for this was to be a “nuit blanche” in Villette—I sought the dim lower quarter.

Dim, I should not say. For the beauty of moonlight—forgotten in the park—flowed here again, flooding the senses. High and calm, she rode, pure and unstained. The music, the mirth, the fire and color of lamps had drowned her for an hour. But now, her glory and silence triumphed. The rival lamps died. She moved on like a white fate. Drum, trumpet, bugle—once loud—were forgotten. With a pencil-light, she wrote records on earth and sky, destined for eternity. She and the stars seemed to me both symbols and witnesses of truth reigning supreme. The night-sky lit her reign. Like the slow progress of the heavens, her victory advanced—an eternal movement, past, present, and future.

These narrow streets are very still. I love them for their humility and peace. Occasionally, a homeward-bound citizen passes—but these are quiet walkers, gone quickly. I love Villette in this mood. I would not willingly return under a roof, but I must finish my strange adventure and quietly reclaim my bed in the dormitory before Madame Beck returns.

Only one street lies between me and the Rue Fossette. As I enter it for the first time, the sound of a carriage tears through the silence. It comes this way—fast. How loudly it rattles on the paved path! The street is narrow. I keep to the curb. The carriage thunders past—but what do I see—or think I see—through the window? Something white flutters—surely a hand waves a handkerchief. Was that signal meant for me? Am I known? Who could recognize me? It is not M. de Bassompierre’s carriage, nor Mrs. Bretton’s. Neither the Hôtel Crécy nor La Terrasse lies in that direction. Well, I have no time to wonder. I must hurry home.

Reaching the Rue Fossette, arriving at the pensionnat, all is quiet. No fiacre has yet brought Madame and Désirée. I left the great door slightly open. Will it still be so? Might the wind or some accident have slammed it shut, triggering the spring bolt? If so, entry would be impossible. My adventure would end in failure. I gently pushed the heavy door—would it yield?

Yes. As silent, as effortless, as if some helpful spirit had waited for a magic word, it opened into the vestibule. I stepped inside, breath held, quietly secured the door, climbed the stairs barefoot, reached the dormitory, and found my bed.

Ah! I reached it. I drew a free breath. Then—almost I shrieked—almost, but not quite. Thank Heaven.

Throughout the dormitory, throughout the house, reigned the stillness of death. All slept. So deep was the hush, it seemed no one dreamed. Nineteen beds held nineteen still forms, stretched out and unmoving. On mine—the twentieth—nothing should have lain. I had left it empty. It should have remained so. But what do I see between the half-drawn curtains? What dark, strange shape lies supine, long, and unnatural? Is it a thief who slipped through the open door? Does it wait in ambush? It looks very black. Not human. Could it be a stray dog, come from the street, curled up here? Will it leap at me if I approach? I must approach. Courage! One step!

My head spun. By the dim night-lamp, I saw stretched across my bed—the old phantom—the NUN.

A cry now might have undone me. Whatever the sight, I could afford neither panic, scream, nor faint. Besides, I was not overcome. Tempered by recent trials, my nerves rejected hysteria. Fresh from lights, music, and throngs, lashed by a new wound, I defied ghosts. In a moment, without a sound, I rushed to the haunted bed. Nothing leapt, nothing stirred. All motion was mine—so was all life, all substance, all strength. Instinct told me. I tore her up—the incubus! I held her aloft—the goblin! I shook her loose—the mystery! And down she fell—down in pieces—down in shreds—and I stamped upon her.

Again—behold the leafless tree, the unstable horse, the cloud, the moonbeam. The long nun was merely a long bolster wrapped in a black stole, artfully covered with a white veil. The garments—strange as it sounds—were genuine nun’s robes. Someone had arranged them to deceive. Where did they come from? Who devised this trick? These questions remained.

Tied to the headband was a slip of paper. In pencil, it read:

“The nun of the attic bequeaths to Lucy Snowe her wardrobe. She will be seen in the Rue Fossette no more.”

And who, then, was the woman who haunted me? I had seen her three times. No woman I knew matched her height. She was not tall for a woman. Nor could any man I knew have created this deception.

And yet—she was real. Or was she?

The morning after that extraordinary Midsummer night unfolded like a promise fulfilled—a day so radiant and perfect it seemed nature itself had paused to celebrate. The sun rose with a smile, golden and unburdened, painting the sky in hues of molten amber. Roses bloomed as if scattered by an unseen hand, their petals drifting across the garden like whispered secrets. Dew-kissed hills glistened under the clear azure dome, and time moved forward with lightness, as though even the hours were dancing on tiptoe. It was, without question, one of the most beautiful days summer could offer. Yet I wonder—was I the only soul in the Rue Fossette who truly noticed?

For while others saw only the brilliance of the world outside, my thoughts turned inward, caught in the quiet aftermath of what had passed. A single absence now marked every corner of the house: a vacant seat at matins, an untouched coffee cup at breakfast, a bolster left neatly folded in a bed, still wearing its cap and nightgown. And when Ginevra Fanshawe’s music teacher arrived as usual, she found no pupil waiting—no sign, no word, no trace.

A frantic search began. Every room, every corridor, every hidden nook was scoured. But nothing remained but silence. Miss Fanshawe had vanished—gone as silently and completely as a star swallowed by night.

The staff were stunned; Madame Beck, especially, looked shattered. Her composure cracked, her face pale as marble. This was not just a loss—it was a blow to her pride, to her control, to the very order she had built around her life. How had it happened? No window was open, no glass broken, no door left ajar. Everything was locked, secure. The mystery deepened.

But I remembered. I recalled the soft closing of a certain heavy door—the one near the attic—drawn shut, yes, but never bolted. I remembered the thundering carriage that had passed in the night, the flicker of a handkerchief waved from a passing window. And then, the ghost in the garret—the nun who came to visit me in the dead of night.

Now, all the pieces fell into place.

I knew, beyond doubt, what had occurred.

When I shared my conviction with Madame Beck, she did not argue. She already knew of M. de Hamal’s pursuit of Ginevra—had discussed it long ago with Mrs. Cholmondeley, placing the burden of responsibility squarely upon her shoulders. Now, both women turned to M. de Bassompierre for answers.

By then, the Hôtel Crécy was already abuzz with news. Ginevra had written to her cousin Paulina, hinting at marriage. The de Hamal family had responded. M. de Bassompierre set out in pursuit—but too late.

A week later, a letter arrived for me. I read it slowly, carefully, for it held more than mere news—it carried the voice of a heart full of mischief, triumph, and love.

> I’m off! Gone like a shot. Alfred and I planned this almost from the start—we never meant to marry in the ordinary way. Alfred has too much spirit for that—and so do I. *Dieu merci!*
>
> You know, Alfred—who once called you ‘the dragon’—has seen so much of you lately that he’s actually grown fond of you. He hopes you won’t miss him now that he’s gone. He apologizes for any trouble he may have caused—especially that time he startled you in the grenier, catching you mid-letter. He couldn’t resist. But you once frightened *him* too—rushing in for a shawl just as he lit his cigar.
>
> Do you now understand? M. le Comte de Hamal was the nun in the attic. He came to see me. Here’s how: he enters the Athénée—where his nephews study—and from there, climbs our wall. Then, using the great tree over the grand berceau, he scales the buildings until he reaches the garret. One night, he fell, broke branches, nearly broke his neck, and barely escaped Madame Beck and M. Emanuel walking below.
>
> From the grande salle, the climb to the attic is easy. The skylight stays half-open for air—and that’s how he entered.
>
> I told him about our legend of the nun. That inspired his disguise. He wore the black gown and white veil—not just for fun, but to avoid being caught. He thinks you and M. Paul are excellent ghost-seers—brave men both. But I must say, I marvel less at your courage and more at your silence. How could you endure those visits—night after night—without screaming or alerting the whole house?
>
> And how did you like the nun as a bedfellow? *I* dressed her up—didn’t I do well? Did you shriek? I would’ve gone mad. But you—you’re made of iron and leather. You feel nothing. You’re a real old Diogenes.
>
> So, dear grandmother—aren’t you furious I ran away? I assure you, it was pure joy. I did it partly to spite Paulina and Dr. John—to show them that even I, with all my supposed wildness, could get married just as easily as they.
>
> M. de Bassompierre was furious at first—threatened legal action, said Alfred had kidnapped a minor. I had to play the melodrama—kneel, cry, drench three handkerchiefs. But he gave in. What could he do? I’m married. That’s that. He still insists it’s not legal because I’m not of age. As if that matters! I’m just as married as if I were a hundred. We’ll be married again properly—Mrs. Cholmondeley will oversee my trousseau—and there’s hope he’ll give me a decent portion.
>
> Only I wish he’d do it generously, without conditions. He wants Alfred to swear never to gamble again. They say he has a weakness for cards—but I don’t care. I know he’s my darling, my angel.
>
> Alfred’s genius in planning our escape was remarkable. He chose the fête night—when Madame Beck would be at the concert. I watched you leave the dormitory at eleven. How you returned alone, on foot—I can’t imagine. Was it you we met in the narrow Rue St. Jean? Did you see me wave my handkerchief from the carriage?
>
> Adieu! Rejoice in my happiness. Congratulate me. Believe me, dear cynic and misanthrope, yours, in the best of health and spirits,
>
>
> P.S.—Remember, I’m a countess now. Papa, mamma, and the girls at home will be thrilled. *‘My daughter the Countess!’* *‘My sister the Countess!’* Bravo! Sounds better than *Mrs. John Bretton*, doesn’t it?”

And so ended the tale of Ginevra Fanshawe’s flight.

The reader might expect some tragic reckoning, some bitter fall from grace. But life, as it often does, spared her the harshest consequences.

Later, near the end of her honeymoon, she visited Madame Beck and summoned me to the salon. She burst into my arms, laughing, radiant—her cheeks flushed, her curls fuller than ever. She wore a white bonnet, a Flanders veil, orange blossoms, and a dress that suited her perfectly.

“I’ve got my portion!” she exclaimed—always practical, always grounded in the tangible. “Uncle de Bassompierre is reconciled. He calls Alfred a ‘nincompoop’—but that’s just his Scotch bluntness. Paulina envies me. Dr. John is jealous—so jealous he might shoot himself! And I’m so happy—I think I have everything I want… except maybe a carriage and a hotel. Oh, and I must introduce you to *mon mari*!”

Alfred stepped forward from the inner salon, where he’d been speaking with Madame Beck. He offered me a polite apology for his ghostly visits, ending with a smile: “The best excuse for all my misdeeds stands right there,” he said, pointing to his bride.

She sent him back and turned to me, enveloping me in her exuberant energy. She showed me her ring proudly, calling herself “Madame la Comtesse” again and again, asking how it sounded. I said little. I gave her only the dry, reserved side of myself—just as she expected. She liked my sarcasm, my detachment. The more impassive I appeared, the louder she laughed.

Soon after, Alfred left the army—persuaded by family and friends to seek a quieter life. A diplomatic post was secured for him, and the couple departed abroad.

I thought she’d forget me. But she didn’t.

For years, letters came—irregular, impulsive, sometimes extravagant. At first, they spoke only of her and Alfred. Then, gradually, Alfred faded. In his place emerged a new figure: *Alfred Fanshawe de Bassompierre de Hamal*. A child—her son—whose every milestone became a spectacle. His first tooth, his first illness—each event was magnified into a crisis of epic proportions.

I received letters filled with agony, fear, and desperate pleas for sympathy. I wrote back with concern at first—then realized it was all theater. The child weathered each storm with heroic resilience. Five times he was said to be “in articulo mortis.” Five times he revived.

Over time, murmurs arose about debts, about financial strain. Alfred the First faced troubles—some of honor, some of pride. Ginevra, as before, never faced them alone. She called for help, demanded support, and somehow always found it.

She fought life not with strength, but with charm—with wit, with drama, with relentless appeal. And through it all, she suffered little.

Not because she was immune to pain—but because she never let it settle. She lived, laughed, loved, and fled—always fleeing toward the next adventure, the next escape, the next moment of joy.

And so, in the end, she remained—unbroken, unrepentant, and gloriously alive.

Must I now account for the Freedom and Renovation I claimed on that night of celebration? Must I confess how I tested the two steadfast companions I brought home from the glowing park, when they vowed to liberate me from love’s chains?

I tried them the very next day. They had spoken boldly of strength when they rescued me from my bondage, but when I asked not for words, but deeds—for proof of comfort, of relief—Freedom excused himself, saying he was poor and unable to help. And Renovation? He never spoke again. He had died in the night, suddenly, silently.

Left alone, I could only hope that my imagination had run too fast, too far. I clung to the idea that jealousy might have distorted my vision, and I sought solace in the memory of its cruel tricks. But after a brief, futile struggle, I found myself once more bound by the old torment of uncertainty—pulled back into the same cage of suspense, strained anew by the weight of waiting.

Would I see him before he leaves? Will he remember me? Does he plan to come? Will today—or this very hour—bring him? Or must I endure once more the slow, gnawing ache of prolonged absence, the sudden, brutal agony of final parting—the silent, fatal wrench that severs hope and doubt alike, shaking life itself? And the hand that delivers such violence cannot be soothed, for distance stands between us like an unyielding wall.

It was the Feast of the Assumption. No classes were held. The boarders and teachers had gone on a long walk into the countryside, to take their afternoon meal at a farmhouse after morning mass. I did not go with them. Only two days remained before the *Paul et Virginie* sailed, and I clung to what little time was left—like a survivor clinging to the last raft after shipwreck.

There was some carpentry work to do in the first classroom—repairs to benches or desks. Holidays were often used for such tasks, which couldn’t be done when pupils filled the rooms. Sitting alone, I meant to go out to the garden and leave the room clear, but I was too weary to move. Then I heard footsteps approaching.

Foreign workers always come in pairs: I suspect it would take two Labassecourien carpenters to hammer a single nail. As I tied on my bonnet—until then hanging loosely from my fingers—I noticed something odd: only one worker approached. I also observed that he wore shoes, not wooden clogs. I assumed it must be the master carpenter, coming to inspect before sending his men.

I wrapped my scarf around me. He entered. I turned away from the door. A strange tremor passed through me—a fleeting sensation, too quick to name. I turned. There stood the man I thought was the artisan. But as I looked toward the doorway, I saw a figure fill it—and my eyes fixed upon M. Paul.

How many prayers we offer in silence, begging for miracles, only to receive no answer. Yet once in a lifetime, one perfect gift falls into our hands—unasked, unexpected, radiant and complete, minted fresh from the heart of fulfillment.

M. Emanuel wore the clothes he likely intended for travel—a velvet-trimmed surtout. I thought he was ready to depart immediately, though I believed two days still remained until the ship sailed. He looked well, cheerful, kind—gentle even. He stepped forward eagerly, reaching me in seconds. His presence was warm, open, full of affection. Perhaps it was the joy of being a groom that lit his face. Whatever stirred him, I could not meet his light with shadow. If this were my last moment with him, I would not waste it in coldness or pride. I loved him deeply—too deeply to let jealousy stand between us, even in farewell. One kind word, one soft look from him, would sustain me for all the years ahead; it would be comfort in solitude, a healing draught. I would accept it. I would taste it—no matter how much pride might protest.

The meeting would be brief, of course. He would say the same thing to me as to each student—take my hand for a few minutes, touch my cheek once, gently, and then—nothing more. Then the final parting, the vast separation, the chasm I could never cross to reach him—across which, perhaps, he would not even glance to recall me.

He took my hand in one of his, and with the other lifted my bonnet. He looked into my face. His bright smile faded. His lips seemed to form words without sound—like a mother seeing her child changed by illness or sorrow. A pause came.

“Paul! Paul!” called a woman’s voice behind him, urgent and sharp. “Come into the salon. I have so much to say to you—conversation for the whole day. Victor is here. Josef is here. Come, Paul, come to your friends.”

Madame Beck, drawn by watchfulness or some hidden instinct, pressed close—so close she nearly stood between us.

“Come, Paul!” she repeated, her gaze slicing through me like a steel stylus. She pushed against her kinsman. I thought he would yield. I thought he would leave. The pain pierced deeper than I could bear—something I could no longer hide. I cried out—

“My heart will break!”

What I felt was not metaphor. It was real, crushing grief. But then—another flood opened. One breath from M. Paul, the whisper, “Trust me!”—lifted a weight, opened a passage. I sobbed deeply, shivered, trembled—but also found relief. I wept.

“Leave her to me,” said Madame Beck calmly. “It is a crisis. I will give her a cordial, and it will pass.”

To be left with her and her cordial felt like being handed over to a poisoner with her cup. But M. Paul answered sharply, low, and firm:

“Laissez-moi!”

In that harsh tone, I heard a strange music—rough, fierce, yet alive.

“Laissez-moi!” he repeated, nostrils flaring, muscles quivering.

“But this will never do,” Madame insisted, sternly.

Even fiercer, he replied:
“Sortez d’ici!”

“I’ll send for Père Silas,” she threatened, “right now.”

“Femme!” he shouted—not in his usual deep voice, but in a high, trembling key. “Femme! Sortez à l’instant!”

He was roused—and I loved him in that rage more than ever before.

“What you do is wrong,” she persisted. “It is typical of men like you—unreliable, emotional. Impulsive, unwise, inconsistent. An act unworthy of someone steady and resolute.”

“You do not know what I am capable of,” he said. “But you will learn. Time will teach you. Modeste,” he added, softer now, “be gentle. Be compassionate. Look at this face—see how it suffers. You know I am your friend, and the friend of your friends. Despite your mockery, you know I can be trusted. I have no hesitation in sacrificing myself—but my heart aches for what I see. It _must_ have comfort. _Leave me!_”

This time, in the “_leave me_,” there was a command so bitter, so absolute, I wondered how even Madame Beck could resist. But she stood firm. Her eyes met his—hard, unflinching. She opened her mouth to speak. I saw fire rise in M. Paul’s face. I don’t know how he moved—it wasn’t violent, but precise, courteous. He offered his hand. It barely touched hers. She spun away, fled from the room, vanished in a heartbeat. The door closed behind her.

The storm passed quickly. He smiled, telling me to wipe my eyes. He waited quietly, offering soothing words now and then. Soon I sat beside him again—calmer, not desperate, not desolate. Not friendless, not hopeless, not longing for death.

“It made you very sad to lose your friend?” he asked.

“It kills me to be forgotten, Monsieur,” I whispered. “These long days—no word from you. I feared you’d leave without saying goodbye.”

“Must I tell you what I told Modeste—that you do not know me? Must I show you my character? You _will_ have proof that I can be a loyal friend? Without proof, this hand will not rest in mine, nor trust my shoulder as shelter? Very well. The proof is here. I’ve come to justify myself.”

“Say anything, teach anything, prove anything, Monsieur,” I said. “I can listen now.”

“Then, first—you must go with me into the town. I came specifically to bring you.”

Without question, without hesitation, I retied my bonnet. I was ready.

We walked along the boulevards. He made me sit several times on benches beneath the lime trees. He didn’t ask if I was tired—he simply watched, and knew.

“All these weary days,” he echoed my words, mimicking my voice, my accent, with a playful warmth I had come to love. “All these weary days”—he paused—“I have not forgotten you for one hour. Faithful women make a mistake: they think themselves the only faithful ones in the world. I, too, once dared not count on faith from anyone. But look at me.”

I raised my eyes—happy now, truly happy, or they wouldn’t have mirrored my soul.

“Well,” he said after a moment’s study, “there is no denying that signature: Constancy wrote it. Her pen is of iron. Was it painful to see?”

“Severely painful,” I admitted. “Take your hand, Monsieur. I cannot bear its pressure any longer.”

“Elle est toute pâle,” he murmured, speaking to himself. “This face—she hurts me.”

“Oh,” I said, almost involuntarily, “I am not pleasant to look at—?”

I couldn’t help it. The words slipped out. I had always feared how I appeared—this fear gripped me now with fresh force.

A softness fell over his face. His violet eyes glistened beneath thick lashes. He rose abruptly. “Let us walk on.”

“Do I displease your eyes _much_?” I ventured, gaining courage. It mattered—deeply.

He stopped. He gave me a short, strong answer—one that silenced me, humbled me, yet satisfied me utterly. From that moment, I knew what I was to _him_. What I was to the world beyond, I ceased to care about.

Was it weak to place so much value on his opinion of my appearance? Perhaps. I fear it was. But if so, I confess I carried my share of weakness. I admit a deep dread of displeasing him—and a quiet desire to please him, just a little.

Where we walked, I scarcely knew. The journey was long, yet felt short. The path was lovely, the day radiant. M. Emanuel spoke of his voyage—he expected to stay three years. Upon returning from Guadaloupe, he hoped to be free of obligations, clear of debt. And what, he asked, did I plan to do during his absence?

Once, he reminded me, I had spoken of becoming independent—of running my own small school. Had I given up that dream?

“No,” I said. “I’m doing everything I can to save enough to make it real.”

He didn’t like leaving me in the Rue Fossette. He feared I’d miss him too much—feel lonely, grow sad.

That was certain. But I promised to try to endure.

“Still,” he said, voice low, “there is another reason to leave your current home. I want to write to you sometimes. It wouldn’t be wise to risk losing letters. In the Rue Fossette—well, our Catholic discipline, though reasonable, might under certain circumstances be misused—perhaps abused.”

“But if you write,” I insisted, “I _must_ have your letters. Ten directors, twenty directresses—none shall keep them from me. I am a Protestant. I will not submit to such control. Monsieur, I _will not_.”

“Doucement—doucement,” he replied. “We’ll find a way. We have resources. Soyez tranquille.”

And so he paused.

We were returning now. We reached the middle of a clean, quiet faubourg. The houses were small but neat. Before a white doorstep of a tidy house, M. Paul stopped.

“I call here,” he said.

He didn’t knock. Instead, he took a key from his pocket, unlocked the door, and stepped inside. He ushered me in and closed the door behind us. No servant appeared. The vestibule was small, freshly painted, tastefully decorated. At the end, a French window framed by vines—tendrils and green leaves brushing the glass. Silence filled the house.

He opened an inner door, revealing a tiny parlor—small, but charming. The walls glowed with a soft blush. The floor was polished. A square carpet covered the center. A round table gleamed like the mirror above the hearth. A small couch, a delicate chiffonnière with crimson silk curtains half-open, showing porcelain shelves. A French clock, a lamp, biscuit figurines. The window recess held a green stand with three pots of blooming plants. In a corner stood a guéridon with a marble top, bearing a workbox and a glass of violets in water. The lattice was open—fresh air flowed in, mingling with the sweet scent of flowers.

“Pretty, pretty place!” I exclaimed. M. Paul smiled, pleased by my delight.

“Must we wait here?” I whispered, half awed by the stillness.

“We’ll explore a few other corners of this little shell,” he said.

“May I wander freely?” I asked.

“Yes,” he said quietly. “I dare.”

He led me. I saw a small kitchen with a stove, oven, bright brasses, two chairs, a table. A cupboard held a compact set of earthenware.

“There’s a china coffee service in the salon,” he said, pointing to six green-and-white dinner plates, four dishes, cups and jugs to match.

Up the narrow, clean staircase, I glimpsed two neat bedrooms. Then, back downstairs, we halted before a larger door.

Producing a second key, M. Emanuel fitted it into the lock. He opened it, gesturing for me to enter first.

“Voici!” he declared.

I found myself in a modest but clean room—bare compared to the others, but orderly. The floorboards were scrubbed, though bare of carpet. Two rows of green benches and desks lined the walls, with a central aisle leading to a teacher’s platform, chair, and table. Behind it hung a tableau. Maps adorned the walls. A few hardy plants bloomed in the windows. This was a miniature classroom—complete, neat, inviting.

“It’s a school?” I asked. “Who teaches here? I’ve never heard of a school in this faubourg.”

“Will you kindly distribute a few prospectuses for a friend of mine?” he asked, pulling papers from his coat and placing them in my hands.

I read them—neatly printed:

And what did I say to M. Paul Emanuel?

Some moments are impossible to recall. Certain emotions—joy, grief, wonder—when revisited, feel like whirlwinds, blurred by speed, dim as spinning wheels.

I cannot remember the thoughts or words of the ten minutes after that revelation—no more than I can trace my earliest memories. But the first clear image is of myself speaking rapidly, repeating over and over:

“Did you do this, M. Paul? Is this your house? Did you furnish it? Did you print these papers? Do you mean me? Am I the directress? Is there another Lucy Snowe? Tell me—say something!”

But he remained silent. His quiet smile, his amused gaze downward, his posture—all remain vivid.

“How is it? I must know everything—_everything_,” I pleaded.

The packet of papers dropped to the floor. He had extended his hand—and I had seized it, oblivious to all else.

“Ah,” he said, “you said I had forgotten you all these weary days. Poor old Emanuel! These are the thanks he gets for spending three weeks trudging from painter to upholsterer, cabinet-maker to charwoman—thinking only of Lucy and Lucy’s cot!”

I hardly knew what to do. First, I stroked the soft velvet on his cuff. Then, I gently rubbed the hand beneath it. It was his foresight, his kindness—his quiet, powerful goodness—that overwhelmed me. The certainty of his constant care struck me like a divine light. And his look—his tender, loving gaze—shook me beyond words. Yet I forced myself to focus on practical matters.

“The trouble!” I said. “And the cost! Did you have money, M. Paul?”

“Plenty!” he said warmly. “Selling my teaching position gave me a good sum. I decided to spend part of it on the richest treat I’ve ever known or ever will know. I’ve dreamed of this hour day and night. I didn’t come near you before because I didn’t want to spoil it. Reserve isn’t my virtue or vice. If I had placed myself in your power, and you’d begun asking—Where have you been, M. Paul? What have you been doing? What is your secret?—my deepest secret would have spilled out. Now,” he continued, “you will live here. You will have a school. You will work while I am away. You will think of me. You will care for yourself—for my sake. And when I return—”

He paused.

I promised to do all he asked. I vowed to work hard, willingly. “I will be your faithful steward,” I said. “I trust that when you return, the account will be ready. Monsieur, monsieur—you are _too_ good!”

Words failed me. My feelings surged, but language broke and froze in the attempt. He watched me, still. Gently, he raised his hand to stroke my hair. It brushed my lips. I pressed it close—paid tribute. He was my king. That hand had bestowed royal favor. To honor it was both joy and duty.

Evening settled over the quiet faubourg. M. Paul claimed my hospitality. He had been walking since morning and needed refreshment. He said I should offer him chocolate in my gold-and-white china set. He went out to order from the restaurant. Then he arranged the small guéridon and two chairs on the balcony outside the French window, beneath the vine-covered trellis.

With shy joy, I served him. I arranged the tray, poured the chocolate, presented the rolls and fruit—cherries and strawberries nestled in green leaves. Our meal was simple, but it was more than a feast to us both. I took immense pleasure in tending him.

Behind us lay gardens. Beyond, fields stretched under the sky. The air was still, mild, fresh. Above the poplars, laurels, cypresses, and roses, the moon rose—so beautiful, so serene, that the heart trembled beneath her glow. A star shimmered beside her, pure and unassuming, like a quiet love.

Nearby, in a large garden, a fountain jetted upward. A pale statue leaned over the play of water.

M. Paul spoke to me. His voice blended with the soft sigh of wind, the murmur of water, the rustle of leaves—a lullaby of twilight.

Happy hour—stay a moment! Lower your plumes, rest your wings. Bow to me, brow of Heaven! White Angel, let your light linger. Leave its reflection on future clouds. Bequeath your peace to the time that needs a ray in memory!

Our meal was simple, but it was what we both desired more than any banquet. I delighted in caring for M. Paul. I asked if his friends—Père Silas, Madame Beck—knew of his plans, if they had seen my house.

“Mon amie,” he said, “only you and I know what I’ve done. This joy is ours alone—unsullied, untouched. To be honest, there’s a refinement of pleasure in this secrecy I wouldn’t wish to share. Besides” (with a smile), “I wanted to prove to Miss Lucy that I _could_ keep a secret. How often has she teased me for lacking dignity and caution? How many times has she saucily hinted that all my affairs are the secret of Polichinelle?”

It was true. I hadn’t spared him on that point—or any other. Magnificent-minded, noble-hearted, dear, flawed little man—you deserved honesty, and from me, you always received it.

I pressed further: Who owned the house? Who was my landlord? How much was the rent?

He instantly gave me written details—everything foreseen, prepared.

The house was not his—I suspected that. He was not the sort to buy property. I suspected a lack of thrift in him—he could earn, but not save. He needed a treasurer. The building belonged to a man in the Basse-Ville—a man of means, M. Paul said. He startled me by adding: “A friend of yours, Miss Lucy. Someone who holds you in great respect.”

To my delight, I learned the landlord was none other than M. Miret—the irritable yet kind-hearted bookseller who had kindly found me a seat that fateful night in the park. It seemed M. Miret was wealthy and respected, owning several houses in this faubourg. The rent was modest—half what it would cost for a similar house nearer the city center.

“And if fortune does not favor you,” M. Paul added, “though I believe it will, I have the comfort of knowing you’re in good hands. M. Miret won’t be greedy. Your first year’s rent is already in your savings. After that, Miss Lucy, you must trust God—and yourself. But now—what will you do for pupils?”

“I must distribute my prospectuses.”

No man can foresee the future. Love does not speak in prophecy. Fear often conjures up shadows that are not real. Those three years of separation—how I had dreaded them! The pain they would bring seemed as certain as death itself. I knew the path they would take; I felt sure of every ache they would leave behind. Like a colossal Juggernaut rolling forward, heavy and unstoppable, I saw it looming ahead—its broad wheels crushing all beneath. And I, kneeling low, already felt the crushing weight before it came. Yet—strange though it may seem, and yet true, as so many lives have shown—the worst torment was not the passage of time, but the fear of it. The great chariot moved on, loud and solemn, yet passed silently, like a shadow gliding across the noonday sky. Only a chill dimness remained. I looked up—chariot and driver were gone. The worshipper still lived.

M. Emanuel was away for three years. Reader, those were the happiest years of my life. You may think this odd—but hear me out.

I began my school. I worked—oh, how I worked! I took upon myself the care of his estate, determined, if God would allow, to give an honest account. Pupils came first from modest homes, then from more distinguished ones. Midway through the second year, fate offered me an unexpected gift: one hundred pounds, delivered by letter from England. It came from Mr. Marchmont, cousin and heir to my dear departed mistress. He had just recovered from a serious illness, and this sum was meant as penance—a quiet offering to his conscience, troubled by papers found after her death—papers that named or praised Lucy Snowe. Mrs. Barrett had given him my address. How deeply he had wronged himself, I never asked. I accepted the money without question, and put it to good use. With it, I bought the house next door. I could not abandon the home M. Paul had chosen, where he had left me, and where he expected to find me again. My small boarding school grew into a full-fledged pensionnat—and it thrived.

But my success was not due only to my own effort, nor to any special talent. It was born of a changed world, a transformed life, a heart set free. The force that stirred me came from afar—from an island in the Indian Ocean. At our parting, I had been left with a legacy—not of gold, but of hope. That promise shaped my present, guided my future. It gave me strength to endure, to work, to strive, to be brave and patient. Nothing could easily shake me now. Few things held power to disturb, frighten, or sadden me. Most things brought joy—even the smallest details sparkled with charm.

Do not suppose this warmth lasted only because of a distant dream or a farewell vow. A generous hand kept it burning. I was spared coldness, scarcity, anxiety. I was never made to fear poverty or doubt. With every ship that crossed the sea, he wrote. He wrote as freely as he gave, as fully as he loved—without restraint, without pretense. He sat down to write simply because he wanted to. Because he loved Lucy. Because he had so much to say. Because he was faithful, thoughtful, tender, true. There was no falsehood in him. No hollow words. No excuses disguised as kindness. Apology never touched his lips—never crept into his letters. He gave no stone for bread, no scorpion for honey. His words were nourishment, living water, sustenance for the soul.

And did I feel gratitude? Heaven knows! I believe no creature who has been so steadfastly cared for, so nobly treated, could fail to be grateful—through every breath, until death.

He honored his faith, yet respected mine. He was not one to turn lightly from belief. But he never sought to change me. “Remain a Protestant,” he said. “My little English Puritan—I love your Protestantism. I admire its strength, its dignity. Though I cannot accept its rites, it is the only creed for ‘Lucy.’” Even Rome could not make him bigoted, nor the Church’s influence mold him into a rigid zealot. He was born honest, not deceitful; simple, not cunning; free, not enslaved. His tenderness had made him pliable under a priest’s guidance; his affection, devotion, and fervent piety sometimes blinded him—causing him to sacrifice fairness to himself for the sake of duty, or to serve selfish ends. But these flaws, rare and costly, may one day be counted among virtues rather than sins.

Now the three years are over. M. Emanuel’s return is fixed. It is autumn. He will be here before November’s mists descend. My school flourishes. My home is ready. I have built him a small library, filling its shelves with the books he left in my care. I have nurtured plants he loved—though I was never skilled in gardening—and some still bloom in honor of him.

I thought I loved him when he left. Now I love him deeper, more truly. He is more mine than ever before.

The sun crosses the equinox. Days grow shorter. Leaves turn brittle. But—he is coming. Frosts touch the night. November sends its fog ahead. The wind sighs with autumn’s sorrow. But—he is coming.

The skies grow dark and heavy. Clouds form strange shapes—arches, radiant lines. Dawn rises in splendor—purple and golden, like a king in regal robes. The heavens blaze with fire, wild as battle at its fiercest, crimson as victory in its pride.

I know the signs of the sky. I have read them since childhood. God, watch that sail! Protect it! The wind turns westward. Peace, peace, Banshee—do not wail at every window! It will rise… it will grow louder… it screams long and loud. No matter how I wander through the house tonight, I cannot silence the storm. As hours pass, it grows stronger. By midnight, all who wait awake hear it—and fear it. This storm raged for seven days. It ceased only when the Atlantic was littered with wreckage. It calmed only when the deep had drunk its fill. Not until the angel of tempest had done his terrible work would he fold his thunderous wings, still trembling with fury.

Peace, be still!

Oh, a thousand hearts on waiting shores wept and prayed for that voice—but it was not spoken. Not until the silence came—until the sun returned—did some realize it was not night, but dawn.

Here pause. Pause now. Enough has been said. Let no gentle heart be disturbed. Let hopeful minds imagine what they will. Let them see reunion—joy reborn from terror, rescue from danger, deliverance from dread, the fulfillment of longing. Let them picture happiness restored, love renewed, life unfolding in peace.

Madame Beck lived well and prospered. Père Silas lived long and peacefully. Madame Walravens reached ninety before she passed.

Farewell.
